

######## LENS health-app — 10 proposals ########
SUMMARY: LENS: application-level system health monitoring. The book's position is clear and repeated — "production observation must be ~free; the hot path timestamps and records, analysis happens off-path" (Ch. 76 §76.2) — but it is asserted, not built. Ch. 76 (3,386 words, the capstone) spends its techniques on CI regression gates, replay and simulation; the production-monitoring half is a one-screen ASCII diagram plus one bullet. The metrics stack is entirely absent (Prometheus/StatsD/OpenTelemetry/Grafana/exposition/scrape/sidecar: 0 files; DDSketch/t-digest: 0; interval/snapshot histograms: 0); HdrHistogram appears in 15 files but only ever as a benchmark-loop tool (Ch. 3 §3.4.3, App. L.2) or a pointer back to Ch. 3. Liveness exists as three bullets in Ch. 74 §74.4.3 (shm heartbeat + SIGCHLD, restart policy, "a hardware or kernel watchdog") with no progress-based stall detection, no idle-vs-stuck discrimination, no /dev/watchdog or hiccup detector, and no queue-depth/consumer-lag telemetry ("consumer lag", "high-water", watermark: 0). The pre-trade risk gate and the triggers that fire the kill-switch are referenced from 11 files but never implemented anywhere (fat-finger, order-rate limit, self-match, drawdown, circuit breaker: 0–1 hits, all in appendices). Order/position reconciliation is absent ("reconcil": 2 unrelated hits; drop-copy named 6 times as a feed, never as a reconciliation source). Feed/session health beyond sequence gaps is absent (crossed/locked book, staleness, A/B skew, TestRequest, reject rate: 0). SLO/SLI/alert design is absent (word-bounded SLO/SLI: 0; "alert" appears only as 12 one-phrase imperatives). Shadow mode/canary/parity checking is absent ("shadow" = warm-up traffic or CET everywhere; "canary" = stack protector). The control plane is stated as a principle ("keep it off the hot path", 21 lines) with no channel design or health API. I propose 10 items: three new chapters (hot-path telemetry; pre-trade risk gate & automated kill-switch triggers; order/position reconciliation could fold into the latter), five new sections (stall detection in Ch. 74, feed/session health in Ch. 54, shadow/canary in Ch. 73, control plane + readiness gate in Ch. 74), one Ch. 76 section on SLOs/alerting, and one low-priority resource-gauge section.

CONSISTENCY FINDINGS (in the chapters owning this lens, from the author's accuracy request): (1) Ch. 76 §76.2 diagram uses pre-reorg numbers — "TSC/PTP, Ch.16/50" should be Ch. 17/58; "eBPF/bpftrace (Ch.51)" → Ch. 61; "deterministic replay (Ch.62-63)" → Ch. 74–75; "capture (Ch.63)" → Ch. 75; "§64.4.1/§64.4.3" → §76.4.1/§76.4.3. (2) Ch. 71 §71.2 diagram: "isolated core, Ch.40" → 42; "TSC (Ch.16)" → 17; "batched, Ch.36" → 37; "disk/socket (Ch.44/45)" → 47/48; "SPSC ring (Ch.33)" → 34. (3) Ch. 74 §74.2.1 diagram "decode (Ch.48)" → Ch. 53. (4) Ch. 76 §76.1 "Sixty-three chapters built a toolkit" and Ch. 1 §1.2.3 "which is why the book has 64 chapters" are stale (76). (5) README.md line 5 says "seven appendices (A–G) follow" and Ch. 76's closing paragraph lists only A–G; there are twelve (A–L). (6) Appendix F defines Drop-copy with "(Ch. 74)" but Ch. 74 never mentions drop-copy. (7) Ch. 46 §46.4.2, Ch. 51 §51.4.1 and Ch. 38 §38.4 send readers to "Ch. 53" for FIX heartbeats / FIX session state machines; Ch. 53 contains zero occurrences of "heartbeat" or "session". (8) Appendix C.2 recommends `nosoftlockup nmi_watchdog=0` while Ch. 74 §74.4.3 relies on "a hardware or kernel watchdog" as the last-resort liveness guarantee — the two should be reconciled (Proposal 2 covers the replacement safety net).

--- [health-app #1] Zero-Overhead Telemetry: Counters, Gauges & Latency Histograms on the Hot Path
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part XII — immediately after Ch. 71 (Zero-Overhead Logging), as its metrics sibling, before Ch. 72 (Secure Programming); renumber 72–76 by the existing script. (Fallback if no renumber: a new §76.2.1–§76.2.3 block inside Ch. 76, but the material is too large for a section.) | gap=scattered | priority=high | area=health-monitoring-app
  HFT: The per-stage p99.9 is the number the desk watches live during the open and the first signal of a regression, yet Ch. 76 only asserts that production observation is '~free' and never shows how a trading process records, snapshots and exports a latency distribution without taxing the tick-to-trade path. Every other Part XII operational concern (logging, capture, hot reload) has a measured, codegen-verified chapter; metrics — the thing operators actually look at — has none.
  SCOPE: Counter taxonomy and cost: per-thread cache-line-padded counters (Ch. 31 §31.3/Ch. 33) updated with plain stores or `memory_order_relaxed` `fetch_add` (Ch. 30 §30.3: ~0.3 ns uncontended vs tens of ns contended), gauges, high-water marks; aggregation by a monitoring thread on a housekeeping core (Ch. 42) — measured per-increment cost and the HITM signature when done wrong // Per-stage latency recording: `rdtscp` stamps at stage boundaries (Ch. 17) carried in the shm message header (Ch. 50/74) so every stage sees the NIC RX hardware timestamp; recording raw stamps into the Ch. 71 SPSC ring vs. `hdr_record_value` directly in-thread — measured cost of each (ring write ~5 ns vs histogram record and its counts-array cache footprint) // Interval histograms: double-buffered/swap recorders (`hdr_interval_recorder`), snapshot-and-reset without stalling the writer, readout cadence; mergeable sketches (DDSketch, t-digest, circllhist) vs HdrHistogram — relative-error guarantees, memory bounds, merging across shards/hosts; when a sketch beats a histogram // Exporting via shared memory: a fixed-layout, versioned metrics segment published with a seqlock (Ch. 35) and read by a sidecar process — no syscall, socket, or formatting in the trading process (the Ch. 71 deferred-formatting rule applied to metrics) // The sidecar/exporter on a housekeeping core: Prometheus/OpenMetrics text exposition, StatsD UDP push, OpenTelemetry OTLP; cardinality discipline (per-symbol labels explode); what to export at 1 s vs what stays as raw per-message stamps in the capture (Ch. 75) // A trading metric set: per-stage p50/p99/p99.9/max, wire-to-wire tick-to-trade per venue (Ch. 58), decode/book-update rates, drops and gaps (Ch. 53/54), queue depths and consumer lag, order/ack/reject/fill counts, venue round-trip, position and P&L gauges // Measure the instrumentation tax: tick-to-trade distribution with telemetry on vs off (HdrHistogram + `perf stat`), quantifying the observer effect Ch. 5/61 describe; sampling strategies (1-in-N, every-Nth message, time-boxed) when per-message recording is too dear // Verify the codegen: the record call compiles to a few stores (the §71.5 discipline for metrics); confirm no `call` to a formatting or allocating routine // Pitfalls: a contended `std::atomic` counter shared across cores, `system_clock` stamps, text formatting or `snprintf` in the hot thread, unbounded label cardinality, a histogram whose counts array evicts the order book (Ch. 7/25), exporting via a socket from the hot thread
  EXISTING: chapters/76-production-profiling-end-to-end-case-study.md §76.2 (diagram + first bullet), §76.3 bullet 'Decompose continuously', §76.6 exercise 1: States the mental model (TSC/PTP stamps at stage boundaries, per-stage HdrHistograms recorded off the hot path, 'counters: drops, gaps, queue depths, restarts') in one paragraph; no mechanism, cost measurement, snapshotting or export.; chapters/03-microbenchmarking-done-right.md §3.4.3 Tail-latency capture with HdrHistogram: HdrHistogram API in a benchmark loop (hdr_init/record/value_at_percentile); benchmark context only.; chapters/appendix-L-benchmark-harness.md L.2: CO-corrected HdrHistogram harness; benchmark context only.; chapters/31-multithreading-concurrency-foundations.md §31.3 sharded counter; §31.4 bullet 'Per-thread state aggregated off the hot path': One bullet: per-thread padded counters summed periodically by a monitoring thread, 'applies to metrics, P&L accumulation, histograms (Ch. 3)'. The seed of the chapter, undeveloped.; chapters/30-cpp-memory-model-atomics.md §30.3 table; §30.4.1 relaxed: Measured cost of relaxed vs seq_cst stores and contended fetch_add; recommends relaxed for counters/statistics.; chapters/71-zero-overhead-logging.md §71.2–§71.5: The SPSC ring + deferred formatting + binary record design that the telemetry transport should reuse; measured 5–20 ns hot-path cost; codegen verified. Logging only — no counters/histograms/export.; chapters/17-timekeeping-tsc-rdtsc-clocks.md §17.2.2, §17.3: Measured timestamp costs (raw rdtsc ~6 ns, fenced rdtscp ~12 ns, vDSO clock_gettime ~18 ns) — the input cost for per-stage stamping.; chapters/61-ebpf-bpftrace-xdp.md §61.3, §61.5: Probe-type overhead table (tracepoint/USDT cheap, uprobe expensive); 'for continuous observability use the low-overhead tools/sampling deliberately (Ch. 76)' — points at a chapter that doesn't develop it.; chapters/58-clock-synchronization-ptp.md §58.4.2: 'Build it into continuous monitoring (Ch. 76)… track its distribution, alert on regression' — again deferred to Ch. 76.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il across chapters/*.md: Prometheus=0, StatsD=0, OpenTelemetry=0, Grafana=0, InfluxDB=0, exposition=0, scrape=0, sidecar=0, DDSketch=0, 't-digest'/tdigest=0, 'interval histogram'=0, hdr_interval=0, hdr_reset=0, 'histogram.*snapshot'=0. 'metrics' = 3 lines (Ch. 2 derived PMU metrics, Ch. 65 comparison metrics, Ch. 31 bullet); 'gauge' = 1 line (Ch. 17, TSC 'cycle-rate gauge'); 'telemetry' = 8 lines, all Ch. 64 (in-network telemetry) and Ch. 70 (offloading telemetry to a DPU) — none designs app telemetry. 'HdrHistogram' = 35 lines in 15 files: Ch. 3/App L implement it as a benchmark tool; all other hits are 'HdrHistogram (Ch. 3)' pointers. 'instrumentation' = 9 lines, all about sanitizer or eBPF instrumentation, none about a metrics layer.

--- [health-app #2] Progress-Based Liveness: Stall Detection for Busy-Poll Threads, Consumer-Lag Monitoring & the Watchdog Chain
  kind=expand-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 74 Process Topology & The Deterministic State Machine — expand §74.4.3 'Supervisor/watchdog, heartbeats and liveness' from three bullets into a full technique section (or split into §74.4.3 liveness/progress and a new §74.4.3b backlog monitoring); add a measurement to §74.3 and pitfalls to §74.5. | gap=partial | priority=high | area=health-monitoring-app
  HFT: A pinned busy-poll thread that stops making progress looks identical to one waiting for the next tick — it burns 100% CPU, never exits, and happily writes a heartbeat if the heartbeat is in its loop — so the Ch. 74 heartbeat alone misses the most dangerous failure mode: a live process with stranded working orders and a stale book during the open. Progress sequences and backlog age are the signals that catch it seconds before the kill-switch would otherwise have to.
  SCOPE: Heartbeat vs progress: a heartbeat written from inside the poll loop proves the loop spins, not that it processes; publish per-stage progress sequences (last-consumed input seq, last-published output seq) in the shm header (Ch. 50/74) and have the supervisor compare producer vs consumer sequences — a growing delta with a flat consumer is the stall predicate // Idle vs stuck: use the feed's own cadence (exchange heartbeats/sequence advance, A/B line activity — Ch. 53/54) as the reference so 'no progress while input advanced' is the test; quiet-market and auction-halt false positives // Queue-depth and consumer-lag telemetry: ring occupancy and sequence delta of Ch. 34/37/50 rings as sampled gauges and high-water marks; age of the oldest unprocessed message (now − its NIC RX hardware timestamp, Ch. 55/58) as the backlog metric that precedes a latency breach; exported via the telemetry segment // The watchdog chain: in-process watchdog thread on a housekeeping core → supervisor (Ch. 74) → kernel `softdog` / hardware `/dev/watchdog` (`WDIOC_KEEPALIVE`, `WDIOC_SETTIMEOUT`), what each layer catches and what it costs; reconciling with Appendix C.2's `nosoftlockup nmi_watchdog=0` (what replaces the kernel's own detectors on an isolated core) // A hiccup detector (jHiccup-style): a pinned thread recording gaps between successive `rdtscp` reads into an HdrHistogram, surfacing scheduler/SMI/THP/IRQ stalls on the hot core as a live production metric rather than a cyclictest-at-setup number (Ch. 45 §45.3) // Self-counters from inside the process: `getrusage(RUSAGE_THREAD)` ru_nivcsw/ru_minflt/ru_majflt deltas, `/proc/self/task/<tid>/schedstat` and `/proc/interrupts` deltas for the owned core, read periodically off-path — turning Ch. 41/42/45's one-time 'verify the pin took' into continuous verification // Measure it: detection latency vs false-positive rate across heartbeat/progress timeouts (extending the §74.3 detect row); cost of publishing a progress sequence per message (one relaxed store); hiccup-detector overhead on the sibling-idle vs sibling-busy core (Ch. 43) // Pitfalls: heartbeating via a syscall or socket from the hot thread; the heartbeat inside the stuck loop; a watchdog whose reset path can itself block; supervisor sharing the hot core; treating a quiet market as a dead process (and the reverse)
  EXISTING: chapters/74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.4.3 (three bullets), §74.3 table 'Detect' row and bullet 'Tune the heartbeat', §74.5 'Heartbeat that only catches exits', §74.6 exercise 5: Shm heartbeat slot + SIGCHLD, restart policy with backoff, 'a hardware or kernel watchdog (and an application watchdog)' named in one sentence. No progress/stall discrimination, no queue-depth monitoring, no watchdog device API, no hiccup detector.; chapters/73-hot-reload-live-reconfiguration.md §73.4.2 bullet 'Version the config': '… and detect a stuck reader' — one phrase.; chapters/37-the-disruptor-pattern.md §37.4.1 'Wrap protection via the slowest consumer', §37.5 'Mismatched ring size': Backpressure and burst headroom as design properties; not monitored as telemetry.; chapters/71-zero-overhead-logging.md §71.3 last paragraph, §71.6 'Ignoring back-pressure': Ring-full policy (drop-and-count / overwrite); counts drops but no depth monitoring.; chapters/42-thread-interrupt-pinning.md §42.4.1 'Verify it stuck', §42.4.2 'Verify the core is quiet': One-time verification with `perf stat -e cpu-migrations`, `/proc/interrupts`, `Cpus_allowed`; not continuous or in-process.; chapters/45-realtime-scheduling-kernel-tuning.md §45.3 cyclictest, §45.4.2 'don't starve migration/watchdog threads': Jitter measured at setup; kernel watchdog mentioned only as something not to starve.; chapters/appendix-C-system-tuning-checklist.md C.2 line 33 `nosoftlockup nmi_watchdog=0`; C.5 line 72 'periodic check… pre-trading-day readiness gate': Disables the kernel's lockup detectors for jitter; the replacement safety net is never described.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il: 'progress counter'=0, 'make progress'=0, 'stall detect'=0, softdog=0, '/dev/watchdog'=0, hung_task=0, deadman/'dead-man'=0, jHiccup=0, 'jitter detector'=0, schedstat=0, getrusage=0, RUSAGE_THREAD=0, minflt=0, '/proc/self'=0, 'consumer lag'=0, 'high-water'/'high water'=0, watermark=0, 'fill level'=0, 'duty cycle'=0, 'busy fraction'=0. 'watchdog' = 8 lines in 5 files (Ch. 74 three bullets; Ch. 45 kernel threads; App C disabling it; Ch. 73 teaser). 'hiccup' = 3 lines (Ch. 3 CO example, Ch. 74 false-positive remark). 'queue depth' = 1 line (the Ch. 76 diagram word). 'stuck' = 11 lines, only Ch. 74 ties it to detection and only via the heartbeat timeout. 'backlog' = 1 line (netdev_max_backlog sysctl).

--- [health-app #3] The Pre-Trade Risk Gate & Automated Kill-Switch Triggers
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part XII — after Ch. 74 (Process Topology, which names the Risk role and the kill-switch mechanism) and before Ch. 75 (Capture); the chapter Ch. 1/69/72/74/76 and Appendices J/K all point at but none contains. Renumber 75–76. | gap=scattered | priority=high | area=health-monitoring-app
  HFT: Pre-trade risk is legally mandatory on every order path and the book budgets 40–200 ns for it (Ch. 1 §1.2.3, Ch. 76 §76.3), yet no chapter builds the gate or the runtime sanity checks that automatically fire the kill-switch it treats as architecture. For the reader this is the application-health monitoring with the highest financial stakes: the triggers are what stand between a stuck strategy and an unbounded position.
  SCOPE: The check inventory mandated by SEC 15c3-5 / MiFID II RTS 6 and venue rules: max order size and notional, price collars vs last/NBBO, position and open-exposure limits, order-rate and message-rate throttles (venue limits, App K.4), duplicate-order and self-match prevention, per-symbol/strategy enable flags, credit limits, cancel-on-disconnect // Data structures for a sub-100 ns gate: flat per-symbol limit arrays indexed by symbol id (Ch. 25), scaled-integer notional with `__builtin_mul_overflow` (Ch. 27/72), TSC-based token-bucket / sliding-window rate limiter (Ch. 17/28), branchless limit comparisons (Ch. 13/28), single-writer per-shard position state published via seqlock (Ch. 35/74), limits hot-reloaded as validated snapshots (Ch. 73) // Placement and its latency: in-line in the strategy vs a separate risk process on the order ring (Ch. 50/74) vs in the gateway vs in FPGA fabric (Ch. 69) — measured gate cost at each placement against the Ch. 76 §76.3 budget row (~40 ns p50 / ~120 ns p99.9) // Automated kill-switch triggers (the health-monitoring half): P&L drawdown and position-beyond-limit, reject/cancel-rate anomalies, unacked-order aging, runaway order rate vs baseline, stale or gapped feed (Ch. 54), stall/heartbeat loss (Proposal 2), venue throttle hits, PTP desync (Ch. 58); hysteresis and debounce so one bad tick does not flatten the book // Safe-state sequencing: per-shard/venue cancel-all and mass-cancel messages, venue-side cancel-on-disconnect as the backstop, flatten vs hold policy, operator override and re-arm; independent confirmation of safe-state via drop-copy (Proposal 4) // Measure it: gate cost distribution in the tick-to-trade budget; time-to-safe-state from trigger to last cancel ack; trigger false-positive rate on replayed sessions (Ch. 75) including the open // Testing the gate: fuzz and stress it (Ch. 40), replay-based regression of trigger decisions (Ch. 76 §76.4.1), chaos tests (kill the strategy process — do its orders die? kill the gateway — does cancel-on-disconnect fire?) // Pitfalls: checks that allocate or lock; limits read through a torn config (Ch. 73); the gate living in the process that can crash (Ch. 74 §74.5); throttles bolted on after a venue reject storm; floating-point money math; a kill-switch that needs the dead component to cooperate
  EXISTING: chapters/74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.2.1 'Risk' role bullet; §74.4.4 Kill-switch/safe-state; §74.5; §74.7 reference to SEC 15c3-5: Mechanism given a failure (gateway cancels the dead strategy's orders); triggers listed only as 'a dead strategy, a risk breach, a stuck process, an operator command'; no check implementation.; chapters/72-secure-programming-low-latency.md §72.4.3 'Checked arithmetic where it matters', 'Validate price/quantity ranges at the wire gate': Overflow-safe notional/position math and range validation at the wire — the arithmetic, not the gate or its limits.; chapters/69-fpga-acceleration.md §69.4.1 'Pre-trade risk and order entry in fabric', §69.4.3, §69.5, exercises 4–5: Risk check as a candidate for fabric; 'price/size limits, position checks' named; hardware kill-switch verification.; chapters/54-reliable-multicast-feed-recovery.md §54.4.3 'Kill-switch integration', §54.5 'No escalation on recovery failure': One trigger (unrecoverable gap) escalating to safe-state.; chapters/appendix-J-market-structure-primer.md J.3 line 38, J.6, J.7 step 5: Lists STP and pre-trade checks (position limits, max order size, price collars) as domain facts, cites 15c3-5, defers latency budgeting to Ch. 76.; chapters/appendix-K-exchange-connectivity.md K.4 lines 41–42: Venue message-rate throttles and the 15c3-5 mandate; 'your order-submission logic (Ch. 76) must respect these' — Ch. 76 does not cover it.; chapters/76-production-profiling-end-to-end-case-study.md §76.3 table row 'Pre-trade risk': Budget numbers only, citing Ch. 72/74.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il: 'fat finger'/'fat-finger'=0, 'order rate'=0, 'circuit breaker'=0, drawdown=0, PnL=0, 'sequence reset'=0, 'price collar' only in App J/K. 'self-match'=0 and self-trade=1 line (App J). 'rate limit'/'rate-limit' = 11 lines but only App K.4 is an order throttle; the rest are NAK rate-limiting (Ch. 54) and egress pacing (Ch. 59). 'cancel-on-disconnect' = 2 lines and 'mass-cancel' = 1 line, all in Ch. 74 §74.2.1/§74.4.4. 'pre-trade' = 15 lines in 7 files, none containing a check implementation. 'risk check' = 8 lines, all cross-references. 'kill-switch' = 37 lines in 11 files, every one a pointer to Ch. 74 §74.4.4. 'notional' = 13 lines, all overflow/scaled-integer math (Ch. 27/72). 'P&L' = 11 lines, all about numerics, SIMD, or backtesting — none as a monitored trigger.

--- [health-app #4] Order-State, Fill & Position Reconciliation Against the Venue
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Inside the proposed risk-gate chapter if adopted (as its 'reconciliation' technique section); otherwise a new §74.4.6 in Ch. 74 after §74.4.5, since §74.2.1 already assigns working-order state and sequence numbers to the gateway. | gap=absent | priority=high | area=health-monitoring-app
  HFT: A strategy's view of its own orders and position is the input to every risk check, and it silently diverges from the venue's truth whenever an ack is lost, a session reconnects, or a fill arrives after a crash — exactly the Ch. 74 scenarios. Without reconciliation against drop-copy the kill-switch flattens the wrong position and the gate enforces limits on a fiction.
  SCOPE: The order state machine (App J.3: New→Ack/Reject→partial/full fill→Cancel/Replace→terminal) as data in a flat, pool-backed table keyed by client order id (Ch. 24/25), with per-order sent/acked/last-fill timestamps for unacked-order aging // Internal vs venue truth: reconciling working orders and positions against the drop-copy session (an independent feed), venue order-status requests, and start-of-day/periodic clearing or back-office positions; session sequence-number reconciliation (resend/gap-fill) after reconnect // Divergence signals as telemetry and triggers: unacked count and max age, fills for unknown orders, acks for cancelled orders, position drift between strategy view and drop-copy, cancel-reject loops — exported (Proposal 1) and wired to the kill-switch (Proposal 3) // Determinism: reconciliation outcomes fed back as explicit inputs to the deterministic core (Ch. 74 §74.2.2) and the drop-copy stream captured (Ch. 75) so a replay reproduces the reconciled state bit-for-bit // Measure it: reconciliation lag (drop-copy arrival vs internal update, hardware-timestamped — Ch. 55/58), hot-path cost of per-order bookkeeping (a few stores), time-to-detect a simulated lost ack / ghost fill in the simulation harness (Ch. 76 §76.4.3) // Pitfalls: node-based maps for the order table (Ch. 25); reconciliation that locks the table the hot path writes; trusting internal state after a session reconnect; drop-copy sharing the NIC/path of order entry (correlated failure, App K.6); reconciling in the strategy process rather than the surviving gateway/risk process
  EXISTING: chapters/74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.2.1 'Order gateway' bullet: 'owns… sequence numbers, working-order state… can cancel-on-disconnect / kill-switch the dead strategy's orders' — the only mention of order-state tracking; no reconciliation.; chapters/appendix-F-glossary.md line 12 'Drop-copy': Defined as 'a real-time copy of your order/fill activity fed to risk and compliance systems' with pointer '(Ch. 74)' — but Ch. 74 never mentions drop-copy (consistency bug).; chapters/appendix-J-market-structure-primer.md J.3 'The state machine', J.6 'Order gateway / OMS': Order lifecycle states and 'tracks their state machine, handles acks/fills/cancels' as a role description.; chapters/73-hot-reload-live-reconfiguration.md §73.4.4 'Draining cutover': Retiring a strategy with open orders must drain — assumes open-order tracking exists.; chapters/38-coroutines-async-models.md, 52-advanced-tcp-internals-tuning.md, 57-flow-steering-receive-scaling.md, 72-secure-programming-low-latency.md §38.4.1, §52.3/§52.4.2, §57.4.2, §72.1: Drop-copy named as a connection/flow type (coroutine session, GRO-off RX path, flow-steering target, untrusted input) — never as a reconciliation source.
  EVIDENCE: grep -n -i 'reconcil' chapters/*.md = 2 lines, both unrelated (Ch. 8 exercise 'Reconcile with exercise 1's verdict'; Ch. 50 'detect-and-recreate (or reconcile)' a shm segment). 'drop-copy' = 6 lines in 5 files, none describing its use; grep -i 'drop-copy' 74-*.md = 0 despite App F's '(Ch. 74)'. 'working order' = Ch. 74 only (2 lines); 'order state' / 'unacked' / 'unacknowledged' / 'outstanding order' / 'in-flight order' = 0 in the trading sense (the 'unacknowledged' hits are TCP Nagle in Ch. 51/52). 'position' in the trading sense appears only inside pre-trade/overflow mentions (Ch. 72 §72.4.3, Ch. 74 §74.2.1, Ch. 76 table, App J).

--- [health-app #5] Feed- and Session-Health Signals: Silence vs Quiet Market, A/B Skew, Book Invariants & Venue-Session Heartbeats
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 54 Reliable Multicast & Feed Recovery — new §54.4.4 'Feed- and session-health monitoring beyond gaps' after §54.4.3 (and fix the dangling 'FIX heartbeats — Ch. 53' pointers in Ch. 46 §46.4.2, Ch. 51 §51.4.1 and Ch. 38 §38.4.1 to point here). | gap=partial | priority=high | area=health-monitoring-app
  HFT: Ch. 54 catches the feed failures that announce themselves with a sequence gap; the ones that do not — a line that simply stops, a book that goes crossed, a venue session whose heartbeats stop being answered — leave the strategy quoting on a frozen picture of the market with no gap to detect. Distinguishing a dead feed from a quiet market in sub-second time is the single most valuable health check a feed handler has.
  SCOPE: Silence vs quiet market: the exchange's heartbeat/sequence-advance cadence per channel as the liveness reference; per-instrument last-update age; time-of-day expected-rate baselines; auction-halt awareness so a halted symbol is not a dead feed // A/B line skew as a leading indicator: per-sequence arrival delta between lines and which line 'wins' arbitration (Ch. 53 §53.4.3) — a line that stops winning or lags by microseconds is degrading before it gaps // Book data-quality invariants as cheap checks in the decode loop: crossed/locked book (bid ≥ ask), negative or absurd sizes, price outside a collar, sequence regression, timestamps going backwards — one predicted branch each (Ch. 13), counted, and flipping the per-instrument stale flag of §54.4.3 // Recovery-channel health: retransmission request latency, snapshot age, NAK rate (§54.5) as exported gauges // Order-entry session health: FIX Heartbeat/TestRequest (35=0/35=1) intervals and semantics, OUCH/SoupBinTCP heartbeats, logon/logout and sequence-reset/gap-fill state, session-level (35=3) vs business (35=j) reject rates, venue throttle counters, per-venue ack round-trip distribution (hardware timestamps — Ch. 55/58) — exported (Proposal 1) and wired to triggers (Proposal 3) // Measure it: detection latency of a silently dead line vs a quiet market across threshold choices on a replayed session (Ch. 75); codegen and cost of the invariant checks in the decode loop (Ch. 53 §53.5 style) // Pitfalls: heartbeats sent from the hot thread via a syscall; declaring the feed dead during an auction; alert storms from one market-wide halt (ties Proposal 6); trusting a silent line because the other is live
  EXISTING: chapters/54-reliable-multicast-feed-recovery.md §54.2, §54.3 'Gap rate', §54.4.1–§54.4.3, §54.5: Gap detection, per-instrument stale policy, recovery off-path, NAK storms, escalation on recovery failure — all triggered by sequence discontinuity; nothing time-, rate- or invariant-triggered.; chapters/53-zero-copy-wire-market-data-decoding.md §53.2.3, §53.2.4, §53.4.3, §53.4.4: A/B arbitration, gap detection, drops at NIC/socket/app with 'instrument and alert on drops'; no skew metric, no book invariants, zero occurrences of 'heartbeat' or 'session' despite being the referenced home for FIX heartbeats/session state machines.; chapters/51-socket-optimization-tcp-tuning.md §51.4.1 'Disable slow-start-after-idle + warm connections', §51.4.2 'Detect drops': Heartbeats only as cwnd-warming traffic ('FIX heartbeats — Ch. 53'); drop detection advice.; chapters/46-keeping-the-hot-path-warm.md §46.4.2 'Pre-establish connections': Heartbeats as connection warming; points to Ch. 53.; chapters/appendix-J-market-structure-primer.md J.3, J.4, J.5: Order lifecycle, feeds/snapshots, protocol field quick reference — domain facts, no health monitoring.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il: 'locked book'=0, staleness=0, 'last update'/'last-update'=0, 'expected rate'=0, 'line lag'/'A/B lag'/'arbitration latency'=0, TestRequest=0, 'heartbeat message'=0, 'sequence reset'=0, 'reject rate'=0, 'market is quiet'=0 ('quiet market' = 1 line, Ch. 1 prose). 'crossed' = 3 lines, all cache-line/wire crossings (Ch. 12/58). 'FIX session' = 1 line (Ch. 38 pointer to Ch. 53). grep -ci heartbeat 53-zero-copy-wire-market-data-decoding.md = 0 while Ch. 46:130, Ch. 51:39/98/117 and Ch. 38:123 cite 'Ch. 53' for FIX heartbeats/session state machines. --- [health-app #6] Tick-to-Trade SLOs, Alert Design & the Operator's View: Alerting Without Paging Storms
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 76 Production Profiling & End-to-End Case Study — new §76.4.4 'From metrics to alerts' after §76.4.3 (or as the closing section of the proposed telemetry chapter if that is adopted); add 'alerting on means / paging on every gap' to §76.5. | gap=absent | priority=medium | area=health-monitoring-app
  HFT: The book defines the north-star metric (tail latency, tick-to-trade) but never turns it into an operational objective with a threshold, a window and a response — so a reader instruments everything (Ch. 76) and then either pages on every microburst at the open or alerts on an average that hides the breach. Alert design is where the measured distribution becomes an operational decision.
  SCOPE: Defining the SLIs for a trading system: wire-to-wire p99.9 per venue and shard (Ch. 58/76), per-stage p99.9, drops and gaps (Ch. 53/54), unacked-order age (Proposal 4), reject rate, PTP offset (Ch. 58), hiccup-detector max (Proposal 2); SLOs as budgets per session phase (pre-open, open, continuous, close) because the open is a different regime // Alert design: alert on symptoms (budget breach, safe-state entered, feed dead) not causes; multi-window conditions (p99.9 over 1 s and over 10 s); hysteresis/debounce; grouping and dedupe across shards and venues so one fabric event is one page; severity tiers (page / ticket / dashboard-only); the auto-remediation ladder (shard pause → kill-switch) and what still warrants a human // Where alert evaluation runs: in the sidecar/monitoring host, never in the trading process; the alert latency budget relative to the kill-switch's automatic action (if the machine already flattened, the page is informational) // Dashboards for a desk: latency heatmaps over time-of-day, per-stage stacked percentiles, feed/session/shard status board, position and exposure vs limits — what must be legible at 09:30:00 in one glance; the Ch. 1 §1.5 rule that no panel shows a mean // Measure it: alert precision/recall by replaying captured incidents (Ch. 75) through the rule set; time-to-detect vs time-to-safe-state; page volume during a replayed open with naive vs grouped rules // Pitfalls: alerting on means or single samples; paging on every gap at the open; threshold flapping; rules that require the dead process to report; dashboards that poll the trading box
  EXISTING: chapters/76-production-profiling-end-to-end-case-study.md §76.2, §76.4.1 'Track the trend', §76.5 'No continuous production monitoring': 'Alert on regression', 'a trend dashboard catches slow drift' — phrases, no alert design, thresholds or SLO framing.; chapters/58-clock-synchronization-ptp.md §58.3, §58.4.1 'Monitor the sync continuously', §58.4.3 holdover, §58.5: 'Alert if it exceeds a threshold' for PTP offset — the most developed alert in the book, still one sentence.; chapters/44-cache-allocation-intel-rdt.md §44.4.2 'Monitor continuously (CMT/MBM)': 'Alert when the hot group's occupancy dips' — one sentence.; chapters/51-socket-optimization-tcp-tuning.md and 53-zero-copy-wire-market-data-decoding.md §51.4.2 'Detect drops'; §53.2.4/§53.4.4 'Detect drops at every layer': 'Instrument and alert on drops, especially during the open' — imperative only.; chapters/01-the-latency-mindset.md §1.5 'Reporting (or optimizing) the average': 'If a dashboard… reports average latency…' — the only dashboard-design remark.

--- [health-app #7] Shadow Mode, Canary Shards & Live Parity Checks for a New Strategy or Build
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 73 Hot Reload & Live Reconfiguration — new §73.4.5 'Shadow and canary rollout' after §73.4.4 (strategy reload with warm-up): 'you warmed it; now how do you trust it before it trades?' Cross-reference from Ch. 76 §76.4.1 as the production counterpart of the CI gate. | gap=absent | priority=medium | area=health-monitoring-app
  HFT: A CI replay gate (Ch. 76) proves a build behaves on yesterday's tape; only a shadow run proves it on today's market under today's microstructure, and only a canary shard bounds the blast radius when it is wrong. Every live strategy change otherwise goes from 'passed CI' to 'trading the whole book' in one atomic swap.
  SCOPE: Shadow mode: run the candidate build/strategy on the live feed (same shm ring, Ch. 50/74) with order emission hard-suppressed (a separate process with no gateway credentials — Ch. 72/74 privilege separation), comparing its decisions to production's at the order ring: decision parity rate, timing from identical input timestamps, divergence budget and auto-stop // Canary shards: promote the candidate on one symbol/venue shard (Ch. 74 §74.4.5) under tighter risk limits (Proposal 3), compare per-shard SLIs (Proposal 6) against control shards, roll back via the atomic swap (§73.2) or drain (§73.4.4) // Parity harness: the deterministic core (Ch. 74 §74.2.2) makes shadow-vs-production diffs exact; classifying expected divergences (new logic) vs defects; replaying the shadow window offline (Ch. 75) to explain each divergence // The latency cost of a co-resident shadow: L3 and memory-bandwidth contention (Ch. 44 CAT/MBA), SMT sibling (Ch. 43), NUMA placement (Ch. 16) — measure production's tail with and without the shadow and partition accordingly // Measure it: parity rate and divergence latency on a replayed open; production p99.9 delta with the shadow running; time from canary promotion to full rollout vs rollback // Pitfalls: a shadow that can emit an order; shadow on the hot core; comparing decisions on differently-ordered inputs; promoting on means or on a quiet session; canary limits identical to production
  EXISTING: chapters/73-hot-reload-live-reconfiguration.md §73.4.4 'Dynamic-library / strategy code reload', 'Draining cutover'; §73.5 'Reloading code without warming it': Load, warm, validate, swap or drain — validation is config schema checks; no live comparison before cutover.; chapters/76-production-profiling-end-to-end-case-study.md §76.4.1 correctness/latency gates, §76.4.3 simulation harness: Offline replay vs golden; closed-loop simulation; nothing live or in production.; chapters/74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.4.5 sharding, §74.2.2 deterministic core: The mechanisms (shards, determinism) a canary/shadow scheme would use; not applied to rollout.; chapters/40-concurrency-correctness-tooling.md §40.4.2 'Differential / replay fuzzing': 'Feed the same input to two implementations… and diff' — a test-time idea, not production shadowing.; chapters/46-keeping-the-hot-path-warm.md §46.2, §46.4.1: 'Shadow traffic' means synthetic warm-up messages — a different concept sharing the word.
  EVIDENCE: grep -n -i shadow = 49 lines in 15 files: every non-CET hit is warm-up traffic (Ch. 46/73/74/App C) or 'scalar shadow of SIMD predication' (Ch. 13/28); ASan 'shadow map' (Ch. 40). 'dark launch'=0, 'A/B test'=0, 'blue/green'/'blue-green'=0, 'rollout'=0, 'rollback'=0. --- [health-app #8] The Control Plane: Commands, Health Queries & Operator Actions Without Touching the Hot Path
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 74 Process Topology & The Deterministic State Machine — new §74.4.6 after §74.4.5, expanding the single bullet 'Control plane separate from data plane' in §74.4.1 into a design; the sidecar-facing API ties to the telemetry segment (Proposal 1). | gap=partial | priority=medium | area=health-monitoring-app
  HFT: Every operational action in Part XII — reload a limit, pause a symbol, flatten, kill, dump state — needs a way into a process whose only thread is a pinned busy-poll loop that must never block or syscall, and every supervisor and dashboard needs a way to ask it how it is. The book says 'keep the control plane off the data plane' ten times and never shows the channel.
  SCOPE: Channel design: a command slot/ring in shared memory polled by the hot thread once per loop iteration (one acquire load, measured ~1 ns) vs a Unix-domain/TCP admin socket serviced by a blocking housekeeping thread (Ch. 41 §41.4.2) that translates requests into shm commands; never a signal handler, syscall or socket on the hot thread // Command set and semantics: pause/resume per shard/symbol, flatten, set-limit (routed through Ch. 73's validate-before-swap), dump stats and a state snapshot (from the telemetry segment), trigger an off-path core/heap dump (§74.4.5), enable a USDT/eBPF probe (Ch. 61), drain-and-stop; idempotency and acknowledgement // Health and readiness API for supervisor and orchestration: liveness / readiness / trading-armed tri-state published in the shm header (read by the supervisor, §74.4.3, and the sidecar); an HTTP or gRPC health endpoint lives only in the sidecar on a housekeeping core // Security and audit: authenticated and authorized admin channel (UDS peer credentials, capabilities — Ch. 72 §72.4.6), an append-only audit log of operator actions (Ch. 71/75), commands captured as inputs to the deterministic core (§74.2.2) so a replay reproduces operator intervention // Measure it: command-to-effect latency distribution; hot-path cost of the per-iteration command check in the tick-to-trade budget; `bpftrace` syscall audit (Ch. 61 §61.4.1) proving the hot thread issues none while commands flow // Pitfalls: `SIGUSR1` handlers that do work; the admin socket in the hot thread's epoll set (Ch. 47); commands that take a lock the hot path shares (Ch. 32/45 priority inversion); control messages multiplexed onto the data ring; health that reports 'up' for a process that is stuck (Proposal 2)
  EXISTING: chapters/74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.4.1 bullet 'Control plane separate from data plane': 'Slow, rare control (config — Ch. 73, commands, kill-switch) can go over a separate channel' — one sentence.; chapters/50-inter-process-communication.md §50.2, §50.5, §50.6 checklist: 'Kernel IPC (pipes/sockets/msg-queues) fine for control-plane; shared-memory ring for the data path' — the principle, no design.; chapters/41-context-switching-mitigation.md §41.4.2 'Block on cold/background paths', §41.6 checklist: Control/admin threads should block on housekeeping cores.; chapters/73-hot-reload-live-reconfiguration.md §73.4.3 'Shared-memory config segments': A control/admin process writes validated config into shm — the config half of a control plane, no commands or health.; chapters/38-coroutines-async-models.md §38.4.1 'Where it fits in trading': 'admin/control connections' listed as coroutine-friendly I/O.; chapters/appendix-C-system-tuning-checklist.md C.4 line 57, C.5 line 72: Control plane on housekeeping cores; 'pre-trading-day readiness gate' phrase.

--- [health-app #9] The Readiness Gate: What Must Be True Before the First Real Order (Startup, Re-Arm & Continuous Re-Verification)
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 74 Process Topology & The Deterministic State Machine — expand §74.4.4 'Fast restart with warm-up' with a readiness state machine (or a new §74.4.7 after the control-plane section), tying together Ch. 46 (warm), Ch. 58 (PTP), Ch. 54 (feed), Ch. 73 (limits) and Appendix C.5's one-line 'pre-trading-day readiness gate'. | gap=partial | priority=medium | area=health-monitoring-app
  HFT: The book's restart story ends at 'warm, then go live' and its tuning checklist ends at 'verify with cyclictest', but a trading process that goes live with an un-synced clock, an un-reconciled position or a half-recovered book is a correctness and compliance failure at full speed. The readiness gate is the health check that turns twelve chapters of preconditions into one enforceable predicate.
  SCOPE: Readiness as an explicit state machine (cold → warming → syncing → reconciled → armed → trading → disarmed), published in the shm health header (Proposal 8) and gating the order path with a single predicted branch (Ch. 13) // The gate's conditions encoded as checks, each with its chapter: box invariants (isolated cores quiet via `/proc/interrupts` deltas, governor, THP mode, `mlockall` succeeded, huge pages reserved — App C/Ch. 42/45), clock (PTP offset within bound — Ch. 58; TSC calibrated — Ch. 17), feeds (A and B live, no open gaps, snapshot applied, book not crossed — Ch. 54/Proposal 5), sessions (logged on, sequence numbers reconciled, heartbeats answered), state (positions reconciled to clearing/drop-copy — Proposal 4; risk limits loaded and validated — Ch. 73/Proposal 3), warm (Ch. 46 shadow traffic complete, pages touched), capture running (Ch. 75) // The daily cycle: pre-open gate, intraday re-arm after a restart (§74.4.4) or recovery (Ch. 54), end-of-day disarm; the box-invariant checks re-run continuously as metrics (Proposal 2) rather than a one-time script (App C.5 'Re-verify after every change') // Measure it: time-to-ready distribution broken down by condition (extending the §74.3 detect→restart→recover→warm→live table); first-real-tick latency with vs without the gate; false-ready incidents found by replaying a session's startup (Ch. 75) // Pitfalls: 'process is up' treated as ready; trading before PTP lock (compliance timestamps invalid — Ch. 58/App K.5); gate checks executed on the hot thread; a readiness flag without hysteresis flapping during the open; warming skipped to shorten time-to-ready (Ch. 46/§74.3)
  EXISTING: chapters/74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.3 table (Detect→Restart→Recover→Warm→Live), §74.4.4 'Fast restart with warm-up': '…warm the process… then go live' — the go-live step is a single phrase with no conditions.; chapters/46-keeping-the-hot-path-warm.md §46.4.1–§46.4.2, §46.6 checklist: Warming and pre-established connections as startup steps; no gate.; chapters/appendix-C-system-tuning-checklist.md C.4 line 61 ('belongs in the launch/readiness…'), C.5 line 72 ('a periodic check (and ideally a pre-trading-day readiness gate)'): Names the gate in two phrases; no content.; chapters/44-cache-allocation-intel-rdt.md §44.6 checklist line 122: '…verified in the readiness check' — one phrase.; chapters/58-clock-synchronization-ptp.md §58.4.1, §58.5, §58.6 checklist: PTP offset must be monitored; not stated as a go-live precondition.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il: 'go-live'=0, 'pre-open'=0, 'armed'/'arm the'/'disarm'=0 in the trading sense, 'readiness gate'=1 line (App C:72), 'readiness check'=1 line (Ch. 44:122), 'startup checklist'=0. 'readiness' = 26 lines, 23 of them the epoll readiness-vs-completion model (Ch. 38/47/48/52). No chapter enumerates the conditions that must hold before the first order; Ch. 74 §74.4.4's 'then go live' is the only transition described.

--- [health-app #10] Runtime Resource Gauges for Zero-Allocation Systems: Pool Occupancy, Allocation and Page-Fault Alarms

######## LENS health-os-net — 9 proposals ########
SUMMARY: LENS: system health monitoring at the OS, hardware and network level. Overall finding: the book treats the box and the network as things you tune once (Ch. 6, 42, 45, Appendix C) and verify once (Appendix C.5 "verify the settings actually took"), and reduces production health to one-line "monitor X and alert" bullets — `ethtool -S rx_missed` (Ch. 51/53/55, App I.8), the PTP offset (Ch. 58 §58.4.1), and CMT/MBM occupancy (Ch. 44 §44.4.2, the only OS-level signal the book actually polls continuously). Ch. 76 §76.2's "continuous performance monitoring in production" is application-level only (per-stage TSC/PTP histograms; "counters: drops, gaps, queue depths, restarts"). Grepping /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/*.md for the OS/network signal vocabulary an SRE-minded trading engineer expects returns zero hits for: softnet_stat/time_squeeze/netdev_budget//proc/softirqs/ksoftirqd; turbostat-as-monitor, thermal_throttle/core_throttle_count/PROCHOT/PL1/aperf/mperf; MSR_SMI_COUNT; rtla/osnoise/timerlat/schedstat/perf sched/trace-cmd; /proc/vmstat/compact_stall//proc/pressure/buddyinfo/kswapd/kcompactd; numa_miss/numa_foreign/numa_maps; minflt/majflt/getrusage/VmLck; AER/EDAC/MCE/ECC/rasdaemon/LnkSta/retrain; rcu stall/soft lockup/dmesg//dev/kmsg/TSC unstable/clocksource watchdog; node_exporter/Prometheus/telegraf/collectd/StatsD/OpenTelemetry; SO_RXQ_OVFL/nstat/dropwatch/kfree_skb/skb_drop_reason; rte_eth_stats/imissed/rx_nombuf/onload_stackdump; IGMP//proc/net/igmp/ip maddr/querier/snooping; carrier_changes/link flap/sFlow/gNMI/SNMP/LLDP/streaming telemetry; smartctl/nvme smart-log/iostat/blktrace; anomaly/change-point/canary; pre-open/preflight/go-no-go/runbook/playbook. Partial hits exist only for: `/proc/interrupts` as a one-off verification (Ch. 42 §42.4.2, Ch. 45 §45.4.1), `irqbalance`/config drift as pitfall sentences (Ch. 42 §42.5, Ch. 44 §44.5, Ch. 45 §45.5, App C.72 which literally asks for "a pre-trading-day readiness gate" that does not exist), `hwlat` named once (App C.69), `numastat`/`HugePages_*`/`cpupower *-info` as one-off checks (App C.70), khugepaged/`numa_balancing`/swap as configuration pitfalls (Ch. 15 §15.5, Ch. 16 §16.5, Ch. 23 §23.5), RAPL/thermal as BIOS knobs (Ch. 6 §6.4.1), the ptp4l log token `s2` shown but never explained (Ch. 58 §58.3), and a single "device negotiated to a lower width/gen" pitfall (Ch. 66 §66.5). Nine proposals follow: two new chapters (host/hardware health telemetry; NIC/kernel-stack/bypass/fabric health telemetry) placed in Part XII before the capstone, one new appendix (a timed pre-open readiness gate and market-open playbook), and six expansions of existing chapters (Ch. 45 residual-noise tracers and SMI counting; Ch. 58 PTP servo/clock-health states; Ch. 54 IGMP membership and dark-feed detection; Ch. 66 PCIe AER/link-training health; Ch. 6 frequency/thermal/C-state residency telemetry; Ch. 75 capture-storage health). Fleet-collection architecture (what reading /proc, /sys, MSRs and `ethtool -S` costs a trading box, collector placement, baselining/anomaly detection) is folded into the host-health chapter rather than proposed separately. --- [health-os-net #2] Network Health Telemetry: NIC, Kernel-Stack, Bypass-Stack & Fabric Signals
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part XII — Observability & Operations in Production; immediately after the proposed host-health chapter and before the end-to-end case study (currently Ch. 76). Forward-reference it from Ch. 51 §51.5, Ch. 53 §53.4.4 ('detect drops at every layer'), Ch. 55 §55.5/§55.6, Ch. 54 §54.3 and Appendix I §I.8, which today each carry a one-line 'monitor `ethtool -S` and alert'. | gap=scattered | priority=high | area=health-monitoring-os-net
  HFT: Chapters 51, 53 and 55 already tell the reader to 'monitor `ethtool -S` and alert' but never explain what each counter means, which layer dropped the packet, or the signals that precede a drop — pause frames, `time_squeeze`, a flapping transceiver, a lost IGMP membership. A feed that goes dark or lossy at the open is the costliest production failure in trading, and nearly every one is preceded by a counter that was already moving.
  SCOPE: A taxonomy of `ethtool -S` counters by the layer that dropped the packet: `rx_missed_errors` (ring full — host too slow), `rx_no_buffer_count`/`rx_nombuf`, `rx_fifo_errors`/`rx_over_errors` (NIC-internal FIFO — PCIe backpressure, ties Ch. 66), `rx_dropped` (kernel), `rx_crc_errors`/`rx_length_errors`/`rx_frame_errors` (link and optics), per-queue `rx_queue_N_drops`; the driver dialects (ixgbe/i40e/ice, mlx5 `rx_out_of_buffer`/`rx_discards_phy`/`rx_pci_signal_integrity`, sfc) and `ethtool --include-statistics`/`-S --groups` // Pause frames and link health outside RoCE: `rx_pause`/`tx_pause` on a market-data port (the switch asking you to stop = microburst upstream — contrast Ch. 64's PFC treatment), `ethtool -a`, `/sys/class/net/<if>/carrier_{changes,up_count,down_count}`, negotiated speed/duplex/FEC drift (`ethtool <if>`, `ethtool --show-fec`), transceiver DOM telemetry (`ethtool -m`: Rx optical power, temperature — a dying SFP shows rising CRC errors before it flaps), `ip monitor link`, and the NIC's own PCIe link width/speed (ties Ch. 66) // Kernel RX-path health below the socket: `/proc/net/softnet_stat` (`dropped`, `time_squeeze`, `received_rps`) against `net.core.netdev_budget`/`netdev_budget_usecs`, `/proc/softirqs` NET_RX per CPU (is NET_RX running on a hot core?), `ksoftirqd` CPU time, `/proc/net/snmp` + `nstat -az` (`UdpInErrors`, `UdpRcvbufErrors`, `IpInDiscards`, `IpReasmFails`), the drops column of `/proc/net/udp`, `ss -ump`/`ss -tmi` `drops` and `skmem` // Exact per-socket drop accounting with `SO_RXQ_OVFL` (the kernel's overflow count delivered as a `cmsg` on every `recvmsg`/`recvmmsg`) — the only drop signal the receive loop itself can see in-band — plus `perf trace -e skb:kfree_skb` / `dropwatch` with `skb_drop_reason` to attribute a drop to its cause, and `tcpdrop`/`tcpretrans` for order sessions // Bypass-stack counters the kernel no longer keeps for you: DPDK `rte_eth_stats` (`imissed`, `ierrors`, `rx_nombuf`) and `rte_eth_xstats_get`, Onload `onload_stackdump lots`/`ef_vi` statistics (`rx_nodesc_drops`), ExaNIC `exanic-config` port counters; who polls them, how often, and how they are published (ties Ch. 62) // Multicast delivery health: IGMP membership state (`/proc/net/igmp`, `ip maddr show`), querier presence and snooping timeouts, membership loss after NIC reset or bond failover, per-group last-packet liveness against the venue's heartbeat cadence, and A-vs-B arrival skew as a fabric-path change detector (builds on the proposed Ch. 54 expansion) // Fabric and path telemetry: switch port counters and buffer-occupancy/drop telemetry via sFlow/gNMI-OpenConfig streaming telemetry (SNMP as the fallback), LLDP for cabling attestation, detecting a failover to the longer redundant route (Appendix K.6) with hardware-timestamped one-way latency canaries (ties Ch. 58, 60) // Exchange-session health signals on the order path: gateway heartbeat RTT and ack-latency trend per session, `TCP_INFO`/`ss -ti` retransmits and zero-window events, reject-rate and throttle-utilization counters (Appendix K.4) // Measure it: a controlled microburst that overflows the RX ring on the reference box, then the same event shown (or not) in `ethtool -S`, `softnet_stat`, `SO_RXQ_OVFL`, `nstat` and the feed handler's sequence gaps — with the cost of polling each source (firmware round-trip for `ethtool -S` vs procfs vs in-band cmsg) measured on a housekeeping core // The market-open watch list: which counters to snapshot and zero at T-5 minutes, which per-second deltas page a human during the open, and how they become a pre-open gate (ties the proposed Appendix M)
  EXISTING: /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/53-zero-copy-wire-market-data-decoding.md §53.2.4 line 56, §53.4.4 lines 177-180, §53.7 exercise 4: RX ring sizing (`ethtool -G`), `rx_missed`/`rx_no_buffer_count`, UDP `RcvbufErrors` via `/proc/net/snmp`/`ss -u`, sequence gaps — 'instrument all three, alert on any'; no counter semantics, no softirq layer, no per-socket `SO_RXQ_OVFL`; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/51-socket-optimization-tcp-tuning.md §51.5 lines 109-115: 'Detect drops at every layer' bullet (NIC/socket/application) and undersized-RX-buffer pitfall; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/55-nic-features-offloads.md §55.5 line 102, §55.6 exercises 3 and 5, checklist line 125: Monitor `ethtool -S` rx_missed/rx_no_buffer; per-queue `ethtool -S` used once in an exercise to verify flow steering; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/64-lossless-ethernet-rdma-congestion.md §64.3 line 47: PFC pause-frame counts measured — but only in the RoCEv2 context; pause frames on a market-data port are never discussed; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/60-network-fabric-switching.md §60.4.3: Switch hardware timestamping, replication and taps — no switch counters, buffer telemetry, sFlow/gNMI or LLDP; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/61-ebpf-bpftrace-xdp.md §61.4.1 lines 87-93: BCC tool list (`runqlat`, `offcputime`, `biolatency`, `tcplife`) as ready-made observability — no drop-reason tracing; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/appendix-I-tooling-cookbook.md I.8 lines 130-142: `ethtool -g/-G/-c/-C/-S|grep drop/-T`, `ss -tim`, `tcpdump`, `ptp4l`, `phc2sys` one-liners; 'the `ethtool -S` drop counters are the first thing to check after a market-open incident' — reactive, not monitored; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/54-reliable-multicast-feed-recovery.md §54.3: Application-level gap-rate and recovery-latency instrumentation (raw vs post-arbitration gaps) — the app side of feed health, with no transport/NIC signals

--- [health-os-net #3] Hunting Residual Noise on Isolated Cores: `rtla osnoise`/`timerlat`, `hwlat`, SMI Counting & ftrace
  kind=expand-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 45 Real-Time Scheduling & Kernel Tuning: add §45.4.3 after §45.4.2 (RT scheduling classes and priorities), extend §45.3 'Measure it' with an osnoise/timerlat run beside the `cyclictest` table, and point §45.5's 'find the last spike' and Appendix C.5's 'hunt residual jitter sources' at it. Cross-reference from Ch. 6 §6.4.1 (SMIs) and Ch. 42 §42.5 (stray IPIs). | gap=partial | priority=high | area=health-monitoring-os-net
  HFT: Ch. 45 takes the tail from ~3 ms to ~2 µs and then says 'chase the remaining spikes with `cyclictest -b`/ftrace' without showing how — and those remaining spikes are exactly the p99.99 events that cost a trade at the open. `rtla osnoise`/`timerlat` and SMI counting are how RT engineers attribute a 20 µs spike to its source in minutes, and the book does not mention them.
  SCOPE: The osnoise tracer: `rtla osnoise top`/`hist -c <isolated cpus> -P f:1 -d 1h`, the per-CPU noise budget decomposed into NMI/IRQ/softirq/thread sources, `osnoise/stop_tracing_total_us` and `stop_tracing_us` thresholds that freeze the trace buffer with the offending stack on the first excursion // The timerlat tracer: `rtla timerlat hist -c <cpu> -P f:95 --dma-latency 0 -T <us>` — IRQ-to-thread wake-up latency with automatic trace capture on threshold, the mainline (5.17+) replacement for `cyclictest -b`, and how its numbers relate to Ch. 45's `cyclictest` max // `hwlat_detector` for the latency the OS cannot see (SMIs, firmware), cross-checked against `MSR_SMI_COUNT` (MSR 0x34) deltas via `turbostat --show SMI` or `rdmsr`; ######## LENS debug-diag — 12 proposals ########
SUMMARY: LENS: full debugging & diagnostics reporting. What the book already does well: Ch. 5 establishes the methodology (observer effect, reproduce-then-debug, -g/split-dwarf, rr reverse debugging, core dumps at principle level, eBPF as the production probe); Ch. 40 covers TSan/ASan/UBSan + stress/fuzz/model-checking; Ch. 61 covers bpftrace/USDT/probe overhead; Ch. 71 the lock-free binary logger; Ch. 74.2.2/74.4.3/74.4.5 the deterministic state machine, heartbeats/supervisor and "cores without stalling survivors"; Ch. 75.4.5 and 76.4.1-2 deterministic replay, golden-output gates and bisection. Deterministic replay as a debugging tool is therefore genuinely covered and needs no new chapter — only divergence-localization tooling (proposal 9). What is missing or scattered, verified by grepping all 89 files: (1) the entire crash path — async-signal-safe fault handlers, sigaltstack, an in-memory flight recorder dumped on fault, coredump_filter/MADV_DONTDUMP/PR_SET_DUMPABLE, systemd-coredump, minidumps/Breakpad-Crashpad, the symbolization pipeline (zero hits for sigaction/sigaltstack/SA_SIGINFO/coredump_filter/Breakpad/debuginfod/build-id; 'minidump' appears only as a passing reference); (2) one-off tail-spike forensics as a workflow and the hardware/kernel tracers for it — Intel PT/magic-trace, osnoise/timerlat/irqsoff, perf snapshot/overwrite mode, SMI counting (zero hits for intel_pt/osnoise/timerlat/perf sched/MSR_SMI; cyclictest -b + ftrace appears once in Ch. 45); (3) live introspection without stopping the hot thread — gdb non-stop/Python pretty-printers/process_vm_readv/ptrace scope (zero hits for gdb outside Ch. 5, ptrace, pretty-printer, lldb, process_vm_readv); (4) rr's hard limits with multi-core pipelines, shared memory and kernel bypass; (5) unwinding mechanics and their measured cost (frame pointers vs DWARF CFI vs LBR vs SFrame, libunwind/backtrace(), std::stacktrace — zero hits for libunwind/SFrame/stacktrace); (6) watchdog-triggered diagnostics before restart; (7) a structured incident diagnostics bundle (zero hits for dmesg/journalctl/sosreport/perf archive); (8) memory-corruption hunting without sanitizers (perf_event_open hardware breakpoints, guard pages, poison, _GLIBCXX_ASSERTIONS/libc++ hardening modes — zero hits); (9) replay-divergence localization and determinism auditing (ASLR/pointer-hash nondeterminism, per-event state digests); (10) debugging the NIC/FPGA hardware path and correlating pcaps with host traces; (11) user-space tracing frameworks (LTTng/uftrace/Perfetto/Tracy — zero hits); (12) the C++ diagnostics facilities themselves (std::source_location, std::stacktrace, C++26 <debugging> and contracts — zero hits, absent from Appendix H). Two new chapters (crash handling; latency-spike forensics), one new appendix (incident bundle), and nine targeted sections/expansions are proposed. CONSISTENCY FINDINGS noticed while reading (outside the gap list but relevant to the author's 'consistency and accuracy' request): the Phase-3 renumbering script evidently did not remap cross-references inside fenced diagrams/code blocks written in the unspaced 'Ch.NN' style — ~140 such refs across 34 files, many now wrong (Ch. 76.2 diagram cites 'Ch.51' for eBPF, 'Ch.62-63' for replay, 'Ch.63' for capture, '§64.4.1' for the gate; Ch. 74.2.2 diagram cites 'Ch.44-52', 'Ch.16', 'Ch.48'; Ch. 75.2 diagram cites 'Ch.33/36/45/50'; Ch. 61.3 code comment 'Ch.39!'; Ch. 45.3 table 'Ch.40/41/5'; Ch. 54 diagram 'Ch.69' for TCP; Ch. 73 diagram '§61.4.3'). Appendix H rows also carry stale numbers (std::print 'Ch. 59, 71'; coroutines 'Ch. 37, 40'; std::execution 'Ch. 40'; saturating arithmetic 'Ch. 60'; <bit> 'Ch. 27'). README line 5 and the Preface still say 'seven appendices (A–G)' although twelve (A–L) exist.

--- [debug-diag #1] Crash Handling, Core Dumps & Post-Mortem Diagnostics: Fault Handlers, Flight Recorders and Dumps That Don't Stall the Box
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part XII — Observability & Operations in Production, as a new chapter between Ch. 74 (Process Topology & The Deterministic State Machine) and Ch. 75 (Capture, Persistence & Replay Storage); it absorbs and expands §74.4.5 and is the production-machinery counterpart to Ch. 5's methodology (Ch. 5 §5.3/§5.4.2 forward-reference it). | gap=scattered | priority=high | area=debugging-diagnostics
  HFT: A strategy process that faults at the open with 40 GB of hugepage-backed book and queue state will, by default, spend tens of seconds synchronously writing a core onto the same NVMe the capture writer uses, stalling every surviving process on the box while its open orders sit unprotected. The fault path has to be engineered like the hot path — pull orders first, record the last microseconds without allocating, dump only what forensics needs — and leave an artifact that symbolizes and replays to the exact triggering message.
  SCOPE: Async-signal-safe fault handling: `sigaction` with `SA_SIGINFO|SA_ONSTACK|SA_NODEFER`, `sigaltstack` so a stack overflow is still handled, handler code restricted to `write(2)` of pre-formatted buffers — no malloc/printf/locks/`std::print`, and why glibc `backtrace()` is not async-signal-safe (first-call `dlopen` of libgcc_s) and must be pre-warmed or replaced by a frame-pointer walk; reading `si_addr`/`si_code`/`ucontext_t` registers; SIGSEGV vs SIGBUS (MAP_SHARED truncation, hugetlbfs) vs SIGILL/SIGFPE; `std::set_terminate` and `noexcept` violations ending in `abort` (ties Ch. 20); re-raising with `SIG_DFL` so the kernel still writes the core // Fail-safe ordering in the dying process: notify the kill-switch/safe-state (Ch. 74.4.4) and poison the single-writer shm segment header (Ch. 74.4.2, Ch. 50) before any dumping; what the handler must never do while holding a seqlock writer slot or a NIC ring (Ch. 35, 62); handler-side budget measured in µs // The in-memory flight recorder (black box): the Ch. 71 per-thread binary ring placed in a `MAP_SHARED` `/dev/shm`/hugetlbfs segment so the last N seconds survive the process and a sidecar persists them on fault; alternatively `write(2)` of the raw ring from the handler (no formatting); which fields the black box must carry (last consumed capture sequence numbers → the replay cursor into Ch. 75), `MADV_DODUMP` to force it into the core // Core-dump mechanics and their cost: `RLIMIT_CORE`, `kernel.core_pattern` piping (`%e %p %t %h`, `core_pipe_limit`, pinning the pipe handler to housekeeping cores — Ch. 42), `/proc/PID/coredump_filter` bitmask (anon private/shared, file-backed, hugetlb, ELF headers) to exclude the 30 GB shared-memory data plane and hugepage mempools (Ch. 15, 26, 62), `madvise(MADV_DONTDUMP)` on reconstructible buffers, `PR_SET_DUMPABLE`/`fs.suid_dumpable` — why dropping privileges (Ch. 72.4.6) silently makes the process non-dumpable, `systemd-coredump` storage/compression limits; measured: wall time and survivors' p99.9 while a 1/8/64 GB core is written (I/O storm, page-cache and memory-bandwidth pollution) // Minidumps and out-of-process dumpers: Breakpad/Crashpad-style minidumps (stacks + registers + selected regions), why an out-of-process handler is the right model for a process whose heap may be corrupt, `gcore`/`process_vm_readv`-based live dumps of a hung-but-alive process, dump size vs. forensic value trade-off, measured dump time per method // The symbolization pipeline: build-id (`readelf -n`), `.gnu_debuglink`, `-gsplit-dwarf`/`.dwo`/`dwp`, `debuginfod`, `eu-unstrip`, `gdb set debug-file-directory`/`set sysroot`, `llvm-symbolizer`/`addr2line -i` for inlined frames, `perf buildid-cache`; a per-release symbol archive keyed by build-id so a stripped production binary's core, perf.data and flight recorder all symbolize elsewhere (Ch. 5.4.1 states the principle; this is the mechanism) // Reading a core from an optimized LTO/PGO binary: inlined/collapsed frames, `<optimized out>` recovery via registers (Ch. 4/5), gdb Python pretty-printers for the SPSC ring (Ch. 34), seqlock snapshot (Ch. 35) and flat order-book arrays (Ch. 25), batch scripts that auto-extract the flight recorder and the last input sequence number from the core and hand it to replay (Ch. 75) // Secrets and dumps (ties Ch. 72.4.5): session credentials in cores and flight recorders, `MADV_DONTDUMP`/`mlock`'d secret pages excluded from `coredump_filter`, redaction in the core_pattern handler; retention and access control for crash artifacts // Pitfalls: handlers that allocate or lock (deadlock in the dying process), missing `SA_ONSTACK` (double fault on stack overflow), dumping hugepage regions, cores written to the capture NVMe (Ch. 75.5 writer stalls), the pipe handler on a hot core, non-dumpable after setuid, `SIGKILL` reflexes that forfeit the artifact, trusting a core's view of a race (the core is the end state — pair with replay)
  EXISTING: chapters/05-debugging-low-latency-optimized-code.md §5.3 (bullet 'Core dumps for the crash you can't reproduce'), §5.4.2 ('Core-dump post-mortem'), §5.5, §5.7: Core dumps as a post-mortem tool, 'minidumps / partial cores (Ch. 74)' mentioned in one phrase, archive debug info (-g, -gsplit-dwarf, objcopy --only-keep-debug). No handler code, no coredump_filter, no pipeline mechanics.; chapters/74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.4.5 'Core dumps without stalling survivors; symbol/venue sharding': One paragraph: write cores off the hot disk, bound/compress, core_pattern piping to an async handler, 'capture a minimal dump'. No coredump_filter, no measurement, no signal handling.; chapters/71-zero-overhead-logging.md §71.4.1–§71.4.2: The lock-free binary ring is the flight-recorder substrate, but it is drained to a consumer thread only; nothing on surviving a crash, shm placement, or dumping the ring on fault (grep shm|crash|survive|dump in Ch. 71: 0 relevant hits).; chapters/72-secure-programming-low-latency.md §72.4.5, §72.4.6: Secrets out of logs/captures; privilege drop and seccomp — but not the PR_SET_DUMPABLE consequence for cores.; chapters/09-object-layout-alignment-padding.md §9.6: Only mention of a fault signal in the book: split-lock SIGBUS aside.

--- [debug-diag #2] Latency-Spike Forensics: Attributing the One-Off Tail Event with Intel PT, osnoise, perf Snapshot Mode and Trigger-on-Outlier Capture
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part XII, as a new chapter between Ch. 75 (Capture, Persistence & Replay Storage) and Ch. 76 (Production Profiling & End-to-End Case Study) so Ch. 76 stays the capstone; it operationalizes Ch. 1 §1.4.3's jitter table and consumes Ch. 61 (eBPF), Ch. 71 (logger), Ch. 75 (capture) and Ch. 58 (PTP) — Ch. 45 §45.6 ex. 5 ('find the last spike') and Appendix C §C.5 should point at it. | gap=scattered | priority=high | area=debugging-diagnostics
  HFT: The event that costs money is the single 40 µs stall at 09:30:00.412 that happens once a week; averages, flame graphs and even per-stage histograms tell you that it happened and roughly where, but not why. Attributing a one-off to its mechanism — an IPI, a THP compaction, an SMI, a cold line — needs a forensic workflow and the hardware/kernel tracers built for it (Intel PT, osnoise/timerlat, perf snapshot mode), none of which the book currently names.
  SCOPE: The forensic method: from a p99.99 outlier in the per-stage histograms (Ch. 76.2) to a mechanism — reconstruct one timeline across clocks (TSC → CLOCK_MONOTONIC → PHC/PTP → dmesg/ftrace timestamps via `PTP_SYS_OFFSET_PRECISE` and `CLOCK_BOOTTIME`, Ch. 17/58), pin down the core, the thread and the ±100 µs window, then test each suspect against its fingerprint // Kernel-side suspects and fingerprints: IRQ/softirq (`/proc/interrupts`+`/proc/softirqs` deltas, `irq:*` tracepoints), IPIs and TLB shootdowns (`tlb:tlb_flush`, `smp_call_function`), the tick returning on a `nohz_full` core (second runnable task — Ch. 45.5), RT throttling, `kworker`/`khugepaged`/THP compaction (`compaction:*`, Ch. 15), page faults, preemption (`sched:sched_switch` prev_state, `perf sched timehist`, `runqlat`), C-state exits (`power:cpu_idle`) and frequency transitions (`power:cpu_frequency`, `turbostat`), CE/MCE storms (`rasdaemon`); the purpose-built `osnoise` and `timerlat` tracers (kernel 5.14+) that attribute noise on an isolated CPU to NMI/IRQ/softirq/thread/hardware, and `irqsoff`/`preemptoff`/`preemptirqsoff` for the longest disabled sections // Firmware/hardware suspects: SMIs via `hwlat` and `MSR_SMI_COUNT` (0x34, `rdmsr`/`turbostat`), split locks (`split_lock_detect`), AVX frequency-license transitions (Ch. 29), SMT sibling activity (Ch. 43), DDIO eviction (Ch. 56), NUMA-remote lines (Ch. 16); the single cache/TLB-miss storm via PEBS (`perf mem`, `mem_load_retired.*:pp`, `-d`) sampled only inside the slow window // Hardware control-flow tracing for a single rare path: Intel Processor Trace — `perf record -e intel_pt//u`, snapshot mode (`--snapshot` + SIGUSR2), AUX-buffer sizing, `perf script --itrace=i0ns` with cycle-accurate `cyc` packets, `ptwrite`, `gdb record btrace pt`; the Jane Street `magic-trace` workflow (trigger on a slow iteration, keep the last ~10 ms of control flow); PT vs `--call-graph lbr` vs sampling for a *one-off* event; AMD alternatives and their limits; measured overhead of PT armed in snapshot mode on the hot core // Trigger-on-outlier capture (the spike trap): the hot thread compares its own `rdtsc` iteration delta to a threshold and sets a flag/writes a ring record (no syscall); a sidecar on a housekeeping core converts the flag into a PT snapshot, a 1 s `perf record -C <hotcore>` from a circular `--overwrite` buffer, `/proc/interrupts` and `osnoise` deltas and a flight-recorder dump; design and measured cost of keeping `perf record --overwrite -C` armed continuously // Application-side evidence: per-stage `rdtsc` stamps (Ch. 76), the binary log/flight recorder (Ch. 71), USDT markers (Ch. 61), `trace_marker` writes to merge app events into the ftrace timeline (a syscall — off-path only) and `perf script`/Perfetto import for one unified view // Correlating with the wire: NIC drop/pause counters and hardware RX timestamps vs first software touch to separate fabric/NIC delay from host delay (Ch. 55/58/64), the tap pcap aligned by PTP (Ch. 60/75) — 'was the stall before or after the packet reached our NIC', using the exchange sequence number as the join key // Case study walking one 40 µs spike from histogram to `khugepaged` compaction (or an IPI from a housekeeping-core `mprotect`), with every command and counter shown; a second case where the spike turns out to be the tool itself // Pitfalls: reading `/proc/interrupts` or running `perf` on the hot core, `trace_marker` from the hot path, clock confusion (TSC vs PHC vs MONOTONIC), truncated PT AUX buffers, sampling at 999 Hz for a 40 µs event (it will not be in the samples), treating a single max as noise instead of a defect (Ch. 1), `nmi_watchdog=0` hiding hard lockups on isolated cores (Appendix C)
  EXISTING: chapters/01-the-latency-mindset.md §1.4.3 'Identifying and attacking sources of jitter' (table): The taxonomy of jitter sources with typical signatures and the chapters that fight them — a map, not a diagnostic workflow.; chapters/45-realtime-scheduling-kernel-tuning.md §45.3 (last paragraph), §45.5 ('Pinning/isolating without the firmware layer'), §45.6 exercise 5 'Find the last spike': Names `cyclictest -b` (break-trace) + ftrace + `/proc/interrupts` and SMIs as the firmware culprit in a few sentences; no tracer names (osnoise/timerlat/irqsoff), no workflow, no application-level spike.; chapters/appendix-C-system-tuning-checklist.md §C.5 verification pass: One checklist item: hunt residual jitter with perf/ftrace/`hwlat`, check `/proc/interrupts`, `ps -eLo psr`.; chapters/61-ebpf-bpftrace-xdp.md §61.3, §61.4.1: bpftrace histograms, `runqlat`, `offcputime`, syscall audit — the eBPF half of the toolbox; no PT/ftrace/osnoise, no correlation method.; chapters/02-measure-first-profiling-pmu.md §2.4.2 (last paragraph), §2.5: Off-CPU flame graphs in one sentence; LBR mentioned only as a call-graph method; no Intel PT.; chapters/76-production-profiling-end-to-end-case-study.md §76.2, §76.3: Per-stage histograms and 'deep profiling offline on replay' — but a one-off kernel/firmware spike does not reproduce in replay; the chapter has no path from 'spike seen' to 'cause named'.; chapters/15-virtual-memory-tlb-huge-pages.md §15.6 exercise 5: Correlate khugepaged compaction spikes with `perf record` on compaction events — one instance of the technique.

--- [debug-diag #3] Live Introspection Without Stopping the Hot Thread: Out-of-Process Reads, gdb Non-Stop Mode and Pretty-Printers for Lock-Free State
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 5 — Debugging Low-Latency & Optimized Code, new §5.4.4 after §5.4.3 'Production tracing without perturbing the hot path' (Ch. 5 is the shortest chapter in Part I at ~3,360 words and currently says only 'never attach'); the gdb Python material is reused by the crash chapter (proposal 1) and the watchdog section (proposal 6). | gap=absent | priority=medium | area=debugging-diagnostics
  HFT: When the book is wrong at 10:14 and the process is still trading, the engineer needs to look inside it now, and the only tool the book currently gives is 'never attach'. Out-of-process reads of versioned shared state and non-stop debugger modes let you inspect a live isolated process at zero cost to its tail, and the same pretty-printers then pay off on cores and replays.
  SCOPE: Why `gdb -p` is dangerous, measured: ptrace attach SIGSTOPs every thread in all-stop mode, breakpoints are process-wide `int3` patches every thread hits, and a stopped hot thread holds a NIC ring/seqlock writer slot (Ch. 35, 62); attach/detach stall on the hot thread's p99.9 and the stall propagated to shm consumers (Ch. 50) // gdb non-stop mode (`set non-stop on`, `set target-async on`, `set scheduler-locking`, `interrupt`/`continue -a` per LWP) and `gdbserver --attach --multi` to inspect a housekeeping thread while the hot thread keeps running; LLDB equivalents; what still perturbs the process (breakpoints in shared code, `info proc mappings` walks, symbol loading in the inferior) // Read-only out-of-process introspection: `process_vm_readv`/`/proc/PID/mem` snapshot reads with no stop (measured cost and zero target perturbation), torn-read hazards and pairing reads with seqlock versions (Ch. 35); exposing hot state deliberately — order book, queue heads/tails, counters — in a `MAP_SHARED` stats segment the sidecar reads lock-free (the Ch. 50/73 machinery as a debugging channel); `/proc/PID/task/TID/{stat,status,sched,schedstat,wchan}` as 'what is my hot thread doing right now' (state, cpu, nr_involuntary_switches, voluntary_ctxt_switches) // gdb Python API: pretty-printers and frame filters for the SPSC ring (Ch. 34), seqlock snapshot (Ch. 35), flat/open-addressing containers and the order book (Ch. 25), `std::atomic` members, CRTP/inlined frames (Ch. 19); --- [debug-diag #4] Record/Replay Debuggers Meet Kernel Bypass: rr's Hard Limits, Multi-Core Alternatives and When the Deterministic Core Is the Better Recorder
  kind=expand-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 5 §5.4.2 'Record/replay, reverse debugging, and core-dump analysis' — add a 'What rr cannot record (and what to use instead)' block plus a comparison table; add the corresponding warning to §5.5 and a glossary refinement in Appendix F ('rr'). | gap=partial | priority=medium | area=debugging-diagnostics
  HFT: Ch. 5 sells rr as the gold standard, but a feed handler polling an ef_vi ring or a strategy reading a peer's shared-memory queue is exactly what rr cannot record, and a multi-threaded pipeline loses its timing bugs when rr serializes it onto one core. Readers need the limits spelled out and the alternative — rr the replay harness, not production — or they will spend a week discovering it.
  SCOPE: rr's recording model and the consequences for a trading pipeline: all threads serialized onto one core (a Disruptor pipeline — Ch. 37 — runs sequentially, so timing bugs change or vanish; chaos mode `-h` to perturb schedules), requirement for a precise retired-conditional-branch PMU counter (`perf_event_paranoid`, unavailable in most VMs/clouds, AArch64 support caveats — Appendix A), `rdtsc`/`rdtscp` and `cpuid` trapped (slow and reordered — Ch. 17), the syscall-buffer `LD_PRELOAD` interaction with seccomp (Ch. 72.4.6), `mlockall`/`SCHED_FIFO` under rr's scheduler (Ch. 45) // Memory rr cannot see: DMA'd NIC rings and mmap'd BARs (Ch. 62, 66) and `MAP_SHARED` segments written by a peer process (Ch. 50) are nondeterministic inputs from rr's viewpoint — replay diverges or recording is refused; the practical rule: never rr the live bypass feed handler, rr the replay harness (Ch. 75 journal → Ch. 74 core) instead — the two-tier approach // Measured: rr slowdown on a single-threaded ITCH decode replay (Ch. 53), trace size per minute of market data, `rr pack` for archiving traces with the binary, chaos-mode reproduction rate of a planted race vs plain stress (Ch. 40.4.1) // Alternatives and complements: Undo LiveRecorder/UDB (multi-core recording, shared-memory support), Pernosco omniscient queries over rr traces ('where did this value come from'), gdb `record btrace pt` (Intel PT control-flow-only recording that works multi-core — ties the spike-forensics chapter), gdb `record full` cost; comparison table rr vs UDB vs btrace vs deterministic-core replay vs core dump on works-with-bypass / multi-core / production overhead / what you can inspect // Pitfalls: declaring a race fixed because it vanished under rr's serialization, recording with syscallbuf disabled and changing timing, 'rr needs a hardware performance counter' in a VM, recording the production process, trusting replay of a core that read shared memory the recorder did not own
