--- [debug-diag #5] Unwinding and Stack-Capture Cost: Frame Pointers vs DWARF CFI vs LBR vs SFrame, libunwind/backtrace(), and `std::stacktrace` on the Hot Path
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 22 — Build Toolchain for Speed, new §22.4.4 'Debug info, frame pointers and unwind tables: what the production binary should carry' after §22.4.3 (PGO and BOLT); add rows for `std::stacktrace` and `std::source_location` to Appendix H.2 and the relevant flags (`-fasynchronous-unwind-tables`, `-gsplit-dwarf`, `-gz`, `-g1`/`-gline-tables-only`, `-Wa,--gsframe`) to Appendix D.4/D.7; Ch. 2 §2.4.2 and Ch. 5 §5.4.1 cross-reference it. | gap=scattered | priority=medium | area=debugging-diagnostics
  HFT: Every profile, flame graph, crash backtrace and error-path log in the book depends on unwinding, and the choice between frame pointers, DWARF tables, LBR and SFrame is a hot-path codegen decision with a measurable cost and a large effect on whether a production incident can be diagnosed at all. The book tells readers to keep frame pointers in profiled builds but never measures the trade or explains the alternatives.
  SCOPE: Measured cost of `-fno-omit-frame-pointer` on the hot path (one register lost, push/mov per non-leaf call) on the decode and book-update loops, `-momit-leaf-frame-pointer`, why Fedora/Ubuntu now ship frame pointers, and the decision rule for a trading binary // How each unwinder works and what it costs per frame: frame-pointer walk (~ns/frame), `.eh_frame`/`.eh_frame_hdr` binary search via libunwind/`_Unwind_Backtrace` (µs for deep stacks, and the tables `-fasynchronous-unwind-tables` keep even with `-fno-exceptions` — Ch. 20.5), glibc `backtrace()` and its first-call `dlopen` (not async-signal-safe — proposal 1), LBR snapshots (`perf record --call-graph lbr`, 16–32 entries, Intel only), kernel ORC and the new SFrame format (kernel 6.x, GCC 14 `-Wa,--gsframe`, perf SFrame unwinding) as the low-cost userspace unwinder; `perf record --call-graph dwarf` copying 8 KB stack chunks per sample — the real overhead number // Debug-info choices that cost nothing at runtime but decide debuggability: `-g` vs `-g1`/`-gline-tables-only` for symbolization-only, DWARF 4 vs 5, `-gsplit-dwarf` + `dwp`, `-gz` compressed sections, `-fdebug-types-section`, debug-info size vs link time vs `pahole`/profiler fidelity (Ch. 9, 2) // In-process stack capture for diagnostics: `std::stacktrace::current()` (C++23; libstdc++ 12 with `-lstdc++exp`, libc++ not yet) with a `pmr` allocator to avoid hot-path allocation, deferred symbolization, measured capture cost vs `backtrace()` vs a frame-pointer walk; logging a stack on a rare error path into the Ch. 71 binary ring without blowing the budget // Symbolization throughput and tooling: `addr2line -i` vs `llvm-symbolizer` vs `eu-addr2line` on a 1 M-sample `perf.data`, inlined-frame reconstruction, `perf report --inline`, build-id matching (ties proposal 1) // Verify the codegen: the prologue with and without frame pointers, `.eh_frame`/`.sframe` section sizes (`readelf -S`), confirming a stripped-with-`.gnu_debuglink` binary still symbolizes // Pitfalls: `-g` believed to change codegen (it does not) vs frame pointers believed free (they are not), DWARF unwinding in a signal handler, `perf --call-graph dwarf` on the hot core, stripped binaries with no build-id archive (Ch. 5.5), LBR truncation on deep call chains
  EXISTING: chapters/02-measure-first-profiling-pmu.md §2.4.2 (first note), §2.5 ('The observer effect'): 'Build with -fno-omit-frame-pointer (or rely on DWARF/LBR unwinding)'; 'prefer LBR/frame-pointer unwinding over DWARF on the hot path' — the recommendation without the mechanism or the numbers.; chapters/22-build-toolchain-for-speed.md §22.5 ('Forgetting -fno-omit-frame-pointer for profiling'), §22.6 checklist: 'Keep frame pointers (small cost) on profiled builds, or use DWARF/LBR' — cost asserted, not measured.; chapters/05-debugging-low-latency-optimized-code.md §5.4.1 'Keep debug info in optimized builds': -g is codegen-neutral; -gsplit-dwarf / objcopy --only-keep-debug; archive per release. --- [debug-diag #6] Diagnose Before You Restart: Watchdog-Triggered Snapshots and Hung-Process Forensics on Isolated Cores
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 74 — Process Topology & The Deterministic State Machine, new §74.4.6 immediately after §74.4.5, extending the supervisor protocol of §74.4.3 (heartbeats/restart) with the diagnostic step the chapter currently skips; cross-link from Ch. 45 §45.5 (RT throttling) and the crash chapter (proposal 1). | gap=partial | priority=medium | area=debugging-diagnostics
  HFT: A strategy that stops heartbeating at 09:31 may be spinning on a corrupted queue, throttled by the RT scheduler, blocked in a syscall it should never have made, or paused by a colleague's gdb — and a supervisor that just restarts it destroys the only evidence while leaving the cause in place for tomorrow. Ch. 74 teaches detection and restart; the diagnostic step between them, with its time budget, is missing.
  SCOPE: A hang taxonomy on an isolated core and its `/proc` fingerprint: spinning on a stale sequence (R state, user time climbing, IPC high — lost wakeup/ABA, Ch. 34/37), blocked in a syscall it should never make (S/D state, `wchan`), RT-throttled (Ch. 45.5), stopped by a debugger or SIGSTOP (T state — proposal 3), livelocked producer/consumer, waiting on a dead single-writer's shm sequence (Ch. 50/74.4.2) // The supervisor's protocol on heartbeat miss, ordered by intrusiveness and budgeted in time: (1) read-only — `/proc/PID/task/*/{stat,status,sched,schedstat,wchan,stack}`, the shm heartbeat/sequence/stats segment (Ch. 50), `/proc/interrupts`+`softirqs` deltas for its core, `perf stat -C <core>` for 100 ms (IPC separates spinning from stalled from idle); (2) non-stopping — `perf record -C <core>` for 1 s, `bpftrace -p` on sched/syscall tracepoints (Ch. 61), an Intel PT snapshot (proposal 2); (3) stopping — `eu-stack -p` / `gdb -batch -p -ex 'thread apply all bt'` (ptrace stop, now acceptable), `gcore` with `coredump_filter` (proposal 1), SIGUSR1-triggered self-dump of the flight recorder; (4) the kill — `SIGABRT`/`SIGQUIT` (core) vs `SIGKILL` (nothing), chosen deliberately // Where kernel watchdogs stand on a tuned box: `nmi_watchdog=0`/`nosoftlockup` (Appendix C) remove hardlockup/softlockup detection from the hot cores and a busy-polling RT thread is indistinguishable from a soft lockup anyway; `hung_task` sees only D state; `kernel.softlockup_panic`; SysRq `l`/`t` (NMI backtrace of all CPUs) as last resort — so the application heartbeat is the only hang detector and must be budgeted accordingly // Deadline design: heartbeat interval vs detect latency vs false positives (Ch. 74.3) with the protocol's cost added — snapshots in µs, `perf record` in seconds, `gcore` in seconds — while the kill-switch (Ch. 74.4.4) has already fired; running every collector on a housekeeping core at low priority // Measured: wall time of each protocol step; effect of a ptrace/`gcore` stop on shm readers (single-writer discipline means they read stale but consistent state); false-positive rate vs heartbeat period under a market-open burst // Pitfalls: `SIGKILL` reflex (no core, no handler), killing before collecting (evidence lost) or collecting too long (session lost), collectors on the hot cores, diagnostics written to the capture disk (Ch. 75.5), a watchdog that cannot tell 'hung' from 'stopped by the on-call engineer'
  EXISTING: chapters/74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.4.3 'Supervisor/watchdog, heartbeats and liveness', §74.3 (detect stage), §74.4.4, §74.6 checklist: Heartbeats in shm catch hangs, restart policy with backoff/cap/escalation, hardware/kernel watchdog as safety device, tune the heartbeat for detect-vs-false-positive. Nothing on what to collect from the hung process before restarting it.; chapters/45-realtime-scheduling-kernel-tuning.md §45.5 first bullet: RT throttling descheduling a busy-poll thread 'looks mysterious until you find it' — the hang fingerprint is not given.; chapters/appendix-C-system-tuning-checklist.md §C.2: `nosoftlockup nmi_watchdog=0` recommended for jitter; the diagnostic consequence (no kernel hang detection on hot cores) is not discussed.; chapters/50-inter-process-communication.md §50.4.2, §50.5: Shm header carries PIDs/liveness for stale-segment detection — not used for diagnosis.

--- [debug-diag #8] Memory-Corruption Forensics Without Sanitizers: Hardware Data Breakpoints via perf_event_open, Guard Pages, Poison and Hardened-Library Modes You Can Afford in Production
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 5 — Debugging Low-Latency & Optimized Code, new §5.4.5 'Finding the corrupting write in a zero-allocation design' (after the proposed §5.4.4 live introspection); the hardened-mode cost rows also belong in Ch. 72 §72.3's hardening-vs-latency table and Appendix D.7, with cross-references from Ch. 24 §24.5 (lifetime bugs in pools). | gap=partial | priority=medium | area=debugging-diagnostics
  HFT: Zero-allocation arena and pool designs (Ch. 24) remove the allocator's accidental safety net, so a buffer overrun or a stale lock-free node silently corrupts a neighbouring order instead of crashing — and the sanitizers cannot run in production (Ch. 40.5, 72.3). Hardware data breakpoints, poison/guard-page canary builds and the hardened standard-library modes let you catch the corrupting write on a live or production-faithful build at a cost you can actually measure and afford.
  SCOPE: 'Who wrote this word?' on a live process: x86 debug registers DR0–DR3 through `perf_event_open(PERF_TYPE_BREAKPOINT, HW_BREAKPOINT_W, bp_len 1/2/4/8)` attached to a running PID (`perf record -e mem:0xADDR:w -p PID` or a sidecar), zero cost until the write happens, then a sample with the writer's IP and stack; watching a ring-slot header, a seqlock counter or a pool free-list head; the four-register limit, per-thread inheritance, kernel/kgdb sharing, and the `ptrace(PTRACE_POKEUSER)` alternative // Guard pages and redzones in arenas and pools (Ch. 24): `mprotect(PROT_NONE)` separators in canary builds — the cost is VMAs and TLB reach (Ch. 15), not instructions; poison-on-free patterns checked on alloc/free, `MALLOC_PERTURB_`/`MALLOC_CHECK_`/`mcheck` for the setup-phase glibc heap, canaries in queue slots validated by the consumer (Ch. 34), quarantine via `MADV_FREE`/`PROT_NONE`; turning a use-after-free of a reclaimed lock-free node (Ch. 36) into an immediate fault at the use site // Hardened standard-library modes and their measured hot-path cost: libstdc++ `_GLIBCXX_ASSERTIONS` and GCC 14 `-fhardened`, libc++ `_LIBCPP_HARDENING_MODE_{FAST,EXTENSIVE,DEBUG}`, `-D_FORTIFY_SOURCE=3`, `-ftrivial-auto-var-init=pattern|zero` (uninitialized reads become deterministic), a minimal production UBSan subset with `-fsanitize=bounds -fsanitize-trap` (no runtime) — measured on the decode and book-update loops (Ch. 53/25), which checks the optimizer folds away (Ch. 72.4.2) and which it cannot; ARM MTE / `-fsanitize=hwaddress` as the hardware route (Appendix A) // Reading corruption in a core or replay (ties proposal 1, Ch. 5.4.2): recognizing poison and canary patterns, adjacent-object overflow signatures, ABA'd pointers (Ch. 34), stale seqlock snapshots; gdb `find`/`x` to locate the pattern, `pahole` (Ch. 9) to map an offset back to a field; rr hardware watchpoints to find the write in a replay // Measured: hardened-mode cost per access and on the whole tick-to-trade path, `-ftrivial-auto-var-init` cost, guard-page VMA count vs `mmap`/`fork` time, breakpoint overhead on the watched process (≈0 until hit) and the sampling cost on hit // Pitfalls: poison values that look like valid scaled prices (Ch. 27), debug registers already taken (perf, kgdb), breakpoints not inherited by threads created later, ASan-only testing that hides the race (Ch. 40.5), hardening `DEBUG` modes that allocate on the hot path, guard pages inside a hugepage region (they split it — Ch. 15)
  EXISTING: chapters/05-debugging-low-latency-optimized-code.md §5.4.2 'Hardware watchpoints and conditional breakpoints': gdb `watch` under rr/replay only — 'never the live hot path'; no perf_event_open breakpoints, no production use.; chapters/72-secure-programming-low-latency.md §72.4.2 'Memory safety in zero-allocation/arena designs; bounds checks that compile away', §72.3 table: Notes arena designs remove per-object guard pages and manual lifetimes; advocates `std::span`/`.at()` checks the optimizer folds away; stack protector/FORTIFY measured. No hardened-library modes, no poison/guard-page techniques, no corruption forensics.; chapters/40-concurrency-correctness-tooling.md §40.2, §40.5: ASan redzones/shadow map as a test-time tool; 'running sanitizers in production' is a listed anti-pattern — the production-safe alternatives are not given.; chapters/24-custom-allocators.md §24.5 'fragmentation, lifetime bugs': Names lifetime bugs as the pool pitfall without debugging technique.

--- [debug-diag #9] Replay-Divergence Forensics and Determinism Auditing: Per-Event State Digests, Bisecting to the First Diverging Input, and Linting the Core for Hidden Nondeterminism
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 74 — Process Topology & The Deterministic State Machine, new §74.4.7 (after the proposed §74.4.6) titled 'Divergence forensics and determinism auditing', completing §74.2.2's pattern with its debugging tooling; Ch. 76 §76.4.1 ('any divergence is a behavior change') and Ch. 5 §5.4.2 cross-reference it. | gap=partial | priority=medium | area=debugging-diagnostics
  HFT: Ch. 74–76 make bit-identical replay the foundation of debugging, backtesting and CI, but when a replay diverges at event 1,983,211 of a five-hour capture the book offers no way to find out why — and the usual culprits (pointer-hashed maps under ASLR, a stray clock read, a missing timer input) are invisible until you can localize the first bad event. Per-event digests and a determinism lint turn 'the gate failed' into 'this input, this line'.
  SCOPE: Localizing a divergence: a per-input-event state digest (incremental checksum of the fields each `apply` mutated, or a periodic full-state hash) recorded beside the outputs in the journal (Ch. 75); diffing two runs' digest streams finds the first diverging input, and checkpointed replay (Ch. 75.4.4 snapshots) turns the search into a bisection over the input sequence; a 'stop at first divergence' replay mode that drops into gdb/rr at that event (Ch. 5.4.2); an output-journal diff tool keyed by sequence number // The determinism lint — nondeterminism sources the golden gate catches too late: clock reads (`clock_gettime`/`rdtsc` — Ch. 17; `-Wl,--wrap` tripwires), `rand`/`std::random_device`, address-dependent behaviour under ASLR (`std::hash<T*>`, pointer-keyed `unordered_map` iteration order — Ch. 25, sorting or comparing unrelated pointers), uninitialized reads (`-ftrivial-auto-var-init=pattern`, MSan), thread-id/affinity dependence, `std::sort` instability vs `stable_sort`, `getenv`, config read outside the input stream (Ch. 73), CPUID-dispatched SIMD paths and FMA contraction differences between replay and production hosts (Ch. 27.4.3, 29) // Capture-completeness audit: every core input class in the journal — timers, fills, config swaps, operator commands, recovery/snapshot events (Ch. 54) — the most common divergence is a missing input class; detect by diffing the core's input sequence counter against the journal // Build and environment parity for replay: same build-id enforced by the replay harness, reproducible builds (Ch. 72.4.7), a fixed ISA level or forced scalar path for the deterministic core, `-ffp-contract=off` and strict FP (Ch. 27), ASLR on in both environments (or digests that never hash addresses) // Measured: hot-path cost of the per-event digest (hashing a few mutated words vs the whole book), checkpoint frequency vs bisection time on a full-day capture, the digest's detection latency for a planted `now()` read vs the end-of-run golden diff // Pitfalls: digesting padding bytes (Ch. 9 — uninitialized padding differs run to run), hashing pointer values, digesting outputs only (divergence surfaces late), 'replay matched this time' taken as proof, a replay box with different CPU features than production
  EXISTING: chapters/74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.2.2 (the DSM pattern), §74.5 ('A non-deterministic "deterministic" core'), §74.6 exercise 2 'Prove determinism': Names causes in one sentence (now()/clock_gettime, random seed, 'map iteration order or thread scheduling', I/O inside the core) and an exercise that replays twice and asserts identical outputs. No localization method, no ASLR/pointer-hash discussion, no digests.; chapters/76-production-profiling-end-to-end-case-study.md §76.4.1 'Correctness gate (determinism)', §76.4.2 'Bisect regressions with replay', §76.5: Bit-identical golden gate; bisection across *builds* (not across *inputs*); 'replay diverges and both gates become meaningless' as a pitfall.; chapters/75-capture-persistence-replay-storage.md §75.5 'Non-deterministic replay': Divergence from a hidden clock read or an unrecorded input — stated, not diagnosed.; chapters/40-concurrency-correctness-tooling.md §40.4.2 'Differential / replay fuzzing': Diff two implementations' outputs — the idea in one bullet.; chapters/27-fixed-point-floating-point-arithmetic.md §27.4.3, §27.5: FP determinism (-ffp-contract, x87, -ffast-math) is covered well for the numeric side.

--- [debug-diag #10] Debugging the Hardware Path: In-Fabric Logic Analyzers and Stage Timestamps, NIC/PCIe Health Counters, and Correlating Tap Captures with Host Traces
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 69 — FPGA Acceleration, new §69.4.4 'Debugging the hardware path' after §69.4.3, replacing the single sentence in §69.5 ('Debugging is hardware-level — signal taps, simulation'); the NIC-side bullets cross-reference Ch. 55 §55.5, Ch. 62 §62.5 ('losing kernel tooling') and Ch. 66 §66.5, and the general timeline-alignment method lives in the spike-forensics chapter (proposal 2). | gap=scattered | priority=medium | area=debugging-diagnostics
  HFT: When the FPGA order path or the bypass NIC is suspected, the engineer has a pcap from the tap and a host log and no method for lining them up, and the book's only guidance is that hardware debugging is 'signal taps, simulation'. A per-packet ledger from tap to tap, plus the NIC/PCIe health counters and in-fabric probes, is how you prove whether the microseconds were lost in the fabric, the card, the PCIe link or your code.
  SCOPE: Per-packet nanosecond ledger across the boundary: tap/mirror pcap with PTP hardware timestamps (Ch. 58/60) → NIC RX hardware timestamp (Ch. 55) → first host software touch (TSC↔PHC via `PTP_SYS_OFFSET_PRECISE`, Ch. 17/58) → app stage stamps (Ch. 76) → TX hardware timestamp (`SOF_TIMESTAMPING_TX_HARDWARE`, ef_vi TX stamps) → tap egress; assertions in RTL as the hardware Ch. 40 // Measured: each boundary's contribution on the reference path with hardware timestamps, ILA resource and timing overhead, the cost of an `ethtool -d`/register read (never on the hot core) // Pitfalls: comparing timestamps from undisciplined clocks, the tap's own delay, pcap drops at the capture host masquerading as fabric loss, kernel `tcpdump` on a bypassed NIC (blind — Ch. 62.5), 'time on the FPGA' measured without the PCIe crossings (Ch. 66/70)
  EXISTING: chapters/69-fpga-acceleration.md §69.3 ('Measure with hardware timestamps ... or an external timing tap'), §69.5 ('Toolchain complexity', 'Inadequate verification'), §69.6 checklist: 'Debugging is hardware-level (signal taps, simulation)' in one clause; verification by simulation/formal/HIL named, not developed.; chapters/58-clock-synchronization-ptp.md §58.4.1 ('Relate the PHC to the TSC'), §58.5 ('Forgetting phc2sys'): Cross-timestamping PHC↔TSC and the confusion of unsynced software timestamps vs hardware-stamped packets — the mechanism, not the debugging workflow.; chapters/60-network-fabric-switching.md §60.3, §60.4.3: Taps, mirror/SPAN, layer-1 replication for capture and per-hop measurement.; chapters/62-kernel-bypass-userspace-networking.md §62.5 'Losing kernel tooling', §62.6 exercise 4: tcpdump/netstat are blind under bypass; 'the vendor APIs / DPDK have capture hooks' — unnamed.; chapters/53-zero-copy-wire-market-data-decoding.md §53.4.4, §53.2.4: Detect drops at NIC/socket/app layers with `ethtool -S`; the drop-counter semantics and other NIC diagnostics are not covered.; chapters/66-pcie-host-device-boundary.md §66.6 checklist, §66.7 references: 'lspci -vv' to check link width/generation — one checklist item; AER/health not mentioned.

--- [debug-diag #11] User-Space Tracing Frameworks and Unified Timelines: LTTng-UST, uftrace, Perfetto/Tracy, ftrace Integration and Trace Formats vs the In-House Binary Logger
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 61 — eBPF, bpftrace & XDP / AF_XDP, new §61.4.4 'Beyond bpftrace: user-space tracing frameworks and unified timelines' after §61.4.1 (the chapter already owns production tracing); Ch. 71 §71.4.2 (binary logging) and the spike-forensics chapter (proposal 2) cross-reference it, and Appendix I.5 gains the one-liners. | gap=absent | priority=low | area=debugging-diagnostics
  HFT: Most shops end up with an in-house binary logger (Ch. 71) and a pile of bpftrace one-liners, then discover during an incident that nobody can put the application's records, the kernel's scheduler events and the NIC's interrupts on one timeline. The established user-space tracers and unified-trace UIs solve exactly that at a measurable cost, and the reader should know which ones can stay armed in production and which belong only on the replay box.
  SCOPE: The design space and measured per-event cost on the hot thread: LTTng-UST (per-CPU lock-free ring buffers, CTF output, `lttng enable-event -u`, snapshot/flight-recorder session mode that keeps only the last N MB in memory until triggered), uftrace (function entry/exit via `-pg`/`-finstrument-functions` or dynamic patching; overhead per call and why it is a replay-box tool), Tracy (frame/zone profiler, ~ns zones, live viewer), Perfetto track events and the Perfetto UI as a universal timeline, Chrome JSON — vs the Ch. 71 binary logger and USDT markers (§61.4.1) // Merging app and kernel timelines: ftrace `trace_marker` writes (a syscall — housekeeping threads only), `trace-cmd record -e sched -e irq` with app markers, `perf script` → Perfetto import, LTTng kernel+UST sessions on one clock, aligning TSC-stamped app records with `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` kernel traces (Ch. 17) — one view of a tick-to-trade iteration across user, kernel and IRQ // Kernel-side ftrace beyond the one-liner: `function_graph` to time a syscall the hot path should never have made (Ch. 41), `irqsoff`/`preemptoff`, `trace-cmd`/KernelShark, tracefs instances to keep production tracing isolated, buffer sizing and per-CPU rings // Trace formats and retention: CTF (`babeltrace2`), Perfetto protobuf, `perf.data`, pcap-ng — sizes per minute at feed rates, converting between them, keeping traces alongside captures (Ch. 75) and in the incident bundle (proposal 7) // Where each belongs: continuous-in-production (USDT + bpftrace, LTTng snapshot mode, the Ch. 71 ring), armed-but-dormant (LTTng flight recorder, perf `--overwrite`), replay-box only (uftrace, Tracy with full instrumentation);   EXISTING: chapters/61-ebpf-bpftrace-xdp.md §61.3, §61.4.1, §61.5: USDT/uprobe/tracepoint overhead, bpftrace one-liners, BCC tools, continuous profilers (Parca, Pyroscope). --- [debug-diag #12] The Standard Library's Diagnostics Toolkit: `std::source_location`, `std::stacktrace`, C++26 `<debugging>` and Contracts — What They Cost and What Stays in the Production Binary
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 20 — The Cost of Abstractions, new §20.4.4 'Assertions, contracts and the diagnostics library on the hot path' after §20.4.3 (Disabling RTTI), with a matching 'Verify the codegen' addition in §20.5; add rows to Appendix H.2/H.3 (`std::source_location`, `std::stacktrace`, `<debugging>`, contracts, `std::unreachable`) and a pointer from Ch. 71 §71.4.2 (format-site IDs) and Ch. 5 §5.4. | gap=absent | priority=medium | area=language-features
  HFT: The C++ standard now ships the diagnostics primitives trading codebases used to hand-roll — source locations for log sites, stack capture, debugger traps, and soon contracts with selectable enforcement — and each has a specific hot-path cost and a right place (production, replay box, or test). The book's modern-C++ convention and its Appendix H matrix currently skip this entire family.
  SCOPE: `std::source_location` (C++20) as the zero-cost replacement for `__FILE__`/`__LINE__` macros in log and assert records: `consteval`-generated format-site IDs for the Ch. 71 binary logger, what it costs when the call is not inlined, and the codegen to verify (a few immediates) // `std::stacktrace` (C++23): `stacktrace::current()` cost per capture, `basic_stacktrace` with a `pmr` resource so a rare error path captures a stack without a hot-path allocation (Ch. 24), deferred `description()` symbolization (allocates — never on the hot path), toolchain status (libstdc++ 12 + `-lstdc++exp`/`-lstdc++_libbacktrace`, libc++ absent) and the `backtrace()`/libunwind fallback; use in the terminate/crash handler — and why it is not async-signal-safe (proposal 1) // C++26 `<debugging>` (P2546): `std::breakpoint()`, `std::breakpoint_if_debugging()`, `std::is_debugger_present()` (reads `/proc/self/status` TracerPid on Linux — a syscall, so cache it at startup) — trap into the debugger only on the replay box while the production binary runs through; the `__builtin_trap`/`int3` codegen // Contracts (C++26, P2900): `pre`/`post`/`contract_assert`, evaluation semantics ignore/observe/enforce/quick_enforce chosen per build, the contract-violation handler as a diagnostics hook (flight-recorder write, kill-switch), GCC 15/Clang experimental `-fcontracts` status; measured cost of `enforce` on the decode and book paths vs a hand-written check vs `assert`/`NDEBUG`; the 'assertion that becomes an optimization hint' trick via `[[assume]]`/`__builtin_assume` in release (Ch. 21) and its UB hazard when the assumption is false // `std::unreachable()` (C++23) and `__builtin_unreachable` vs `__builtin_trap` for impossible states; `-fsanitize=undefined -fsanitize-trap=undefined` as the trap-only, runtime-free production subset; always-on cheap invariant checks (Ch. 40.4.1) vs debug-only checks — the production assertion policy // Verify the codegen: a `pre` condition and a `contract_assert` under each semantic, `source_location` folded to immediates, `std::stacktrace::current()` call shape // Pitfalls: `assert` with side effects vanishing under `NDEBUG`, `std::stacktrace` symbolization on the hot path, `is_debugger_present()` per message, `enforce` semantics in the inner loop without measuring, `[[assume]]` on a condition that is only usually true, `std::print` to stderr from a signal/terminate handler
  EXISTING: chapters/20-the-cost-of-abstractions.md §20.2, §20.4.1–20.4.3, §20.5: Exceptions, `noexcept` (a throw → `std::terminate`), `std::expected`, RTTI — error handling cost; no assertions, contracts, source_location or stacktrace.; chapters/21-aliasing-restrict-type-punning.md §21.4.1 ('assume/__builtin_assume_aligned are cousins'): `[[assume]]` (C++23) as an optimizer hint — not as a release-mode assertion policy.; chapters/40-concurrency-correctness-tooling.md §40.4.1 'Property/invariant checks under stress': Invariant asserts under test — not the production assertion policy or cost.; chapters/appendix-H-cpp-feature-matrix.md H.2–H.5 tables: Rows for expected/span/mdspan/flat_map/print/format, consteval/concepts/coroutines/[[likely]]/[[assume]], atomic_ref/jthread/barrier/execution/hive, bit_cast/<bit>/byteswap/endian/saturating/from_chars — none for source_location, stacktrace, <debugging>, contracts or unreachable.; chapters/appendix-D-compiler-flag-reference.md D.7, D.8: Sanitizer flags and `-DNDEBUG` in the representative build line — no discussion of what assertions stay on.

SUMMARY: LENS: implementation languages for low-latency trading systems (user-requested). Verdict first: Appendix B does not do justice to the question and should be promoted — not into one chapter but into a short Part.

WHAT APPENDIX B ACTUALLY IS (/home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/appendix-B-beyond-cpp.md): 2,514 words in five content sections of 300–455 words each (B.1 Rust 389, B.2 C/Zig 300, B.3 managed runtimes 379, B.4 taming the JVM 455, B.5 FFI 378). It contains 0 fenced code blocks (the 76 chapters average 3 each plus benchmark tables) and exactly one quantitative figure (Azul C4 "sub-100µs"). Its thesis is right and consistent with the book — the GC is the dividing line; match language to latency tier; put the FFI boundary at the tier line; prefer shared-memory to per-call interop — but it asserts roughly a dozen performance claims (GC pauses as tail events, JIT warm-up/deopt, barrier cost, FFI-call-per-tick cost, Rust zero-cost, Zig comptime parity) with no measurement, no Godbolt diff and no harness. That violates the book's own convention ("every performance claim is backed by a measurement") and leaves the survey at blog-post depth on every topic the user named: Rust hot-path idioms, inline/hand-written asm (absent from the whole book), Java mechanics (Agrona/JMH/safepoints never appear), C#/.NET (one phrase), Go (one paragraph), OCaml/Jane Street (0 hits), Python/Julia research pipelines (one clause), interop mechanics (tool names only), cross-language benchmarking (nothing).

(5) B.1 says Rust "eliminates by construction much of what Ch. 40/72 spends effort detecting", yet Ch. 72 (memory safety) never mentions Rust or memory-safe languages — a one-way link. (6) Accuracy nits inside B: `MemorySegment`/the FFM API was finalized in Java 22 (JEP 454), not "Java 21+" (21 shipped it as a preview); Rust `std::simd` is nightly-only (portable_simd) — stable code uses `core::arch` intrinsics — B presents it as available.

--- [languages #1] Rust for the Hot Path: Ownership, `unsafe`, and Codegen Parity with C++
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Chapter 1 of a new Part 'Beyond C++: Languages, Interop & the Metal', inserted between Part XI (after Ch. 70 SmartNICs & DPUs) and Part XII (renumbering 71–76 → 76–81 so the case study stays last). Absorbs and supersedes Appendix B §B.1. Prerequisites: Ch. 4, 14, 19–21, 24, 29–30, 34, 36, 38. | gap=partial | priority=high | area=implementation-languages
  HFT: Rust is the only mainstream language that shares C++'s no-GC, AOT, hardware-access proposition while removing the memory-safety bug class that Ch. 72 shows is both a vulnerability and a tail-latency hazard in zero-allocation designs, and HFT shops are already shipping Rust feed handlers and order gateways. A reader deciding whether a new component or a hostile-input parser belongs in Rust needs the same measure-and-verify-codegen evidence the book demands of every C++ technique, not a prose assurance that Rust is 'credible'.
  SCOPE: Mapping the book's C++ toolkit onto Rust: RAII/ownership vs Ch. 23–24 (arena crates `bumpalo`/`typed-arena`, custom `GlobalAlloc` and the `Allocator` API for Ch. 24 pools), `#[repr(C)]`/`#[repr(align(64))]`/`crossbeam_utils::CachePadded` vs Ch. 9/33, `std::sync::atomic` + `Ordering` as the same C++20 memory model (Ch. 30), `&mut` as a built-in `restrict` (Ch. 21) and what that buys the vectorizer // The zero-cost question, measured: bounds checks and when they are elided (iterator chains vs indexed loops, `get_unchecked`, `assert!` hoisting), `#[inline(always)]`/`#[cold]`, `std::hint::{black_box, unreachable_unchecked, assert_unchecked}`, the `likely`/`unlikely` gap; --- [languages #2] Hand-Written & Inline Assembly: When the Compiler Can't Be Beaten — and Proving It
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Chapter 2 of the new 'Beyond C++' Part (after the Rust chapter). Alternative if the Part is not created: a new chapter at the end of Part III after Ch. 22 (Build Toolchain), since it is the natural sequel to Ch. 4 'Reading the Machine'. Prerequisites: Ch. 4, 11, 17, 29, 30, 32, 66. | gap=absent | priority=high | area=implementation-languages
  HFT: The last few cycles of a tick-to-trade path — a timing probe, a doorbell write, a TSX region, a PMU read — sometimes require instructions the compiler cannot emit or will not order correctly, and a wrongly-written asm statement silently corrupts registers or reorders memory in exactly the way Ch. 30 warns against. Readers need a disciplined answer to 'should I drop to assembly here?', with the measurement showing the compiler usually wins and the maintenance bill when it does not.
  SCOPE: GCC/Clang extended asm anatomy: output/input operands, constraint letters (`r`, `m`, `a`, `+r`, `=&r` early-clobber), the `memory` and `cc` clobbers, `volatile`, `asm goto`, `asm inline`; what the optimizer can and cannot do around an asm statement (no scheduling across it, no CSE/hoisting of `volatile` asm) — the hidden cost behind Ch. 30's compiler-barrier warning // Intrinsics first: why `__rdtsc`/`_mm_lfence`/`_mm_pause`/`__builtin_bswap` (as used in Ch. 17, 28, 32, 53) beat asm — the compiler still allocates registers and schedules around them — measured on the Ch. 17 timing probe // The short list where asm is still required on x86-64: `rdpmc` user-space PMU reads (Ch. 2) via `perf_event_open` mmap, `cpuid` serialization, `xbegin`/`xend` (TSX/RTM where enabled), `umonitor`/`umwait` on toolchains lacking intrinsics, `movdir64b`/`clwb`/`sfence` doorbell sequences for the Ch. 66 write-combining MMIO path, `vzeroupper` control at AVX/SSE boundaries (Ch. 29), exact instruction placement for a timing probe // Whole-function asm: `.S` files vs `__attribute__((naked))`, the System V ABI contract (Ch. 4 — callee-saved registers, red zone, 16-byte stack alignment, `endbr64` under CET — Ch. 72), hand-unrolled/hand-scheduled kernels (a FIX checksum, a `bswap`-heavy SBE field burst, a branchless price-level search) checked with `llvm-mca`/uiCA (Ch. 11) and Agner Fog's tables before running // Measure it: compiler output vs hand asm for three kernels on the reference box — expect the compiler to win or tie on two; show the one case where scheduling or `cmov`/`setcc` selection wins N cycles, and the cost of losing inlining and LTO around the asm island // Keeping asm honest: unit tests against the C++ reference, a CI guard that re-diffs compiler output on every toolchain upgrade (Ch. 22), sanitizer and TSan blind spots (Ch. 40), Appendix A portability (an asm island is per-ISA) // Rust `asm!`/`global_asm!` and C `__asm__` equivalents; the same constraints model, the same rules // Pitfalls: missing `memory` clobber (loads/stores reordered around the asm), wrong constraint (silent register corruption), forgetting `volatile` (asm hoisted or deleted as dead), clobbering the red zone, `-fno-omit-frame-pointer` interactions, breaking `-fcf-protection`/CET expectations on hand-written entry points

--- [languages #4] Language Interop & ABI Boundaries: the C ABI, Zero-Copy Across FFI, and the Cost of a Cross-Language Call
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Chapter 4 of the new 'Beyond C++' Part; supersedes Appendix B §B.5. Prerequisites: Ch. 4, 9, 20, 22, 26, 50, 53; ties Ch. 74–75 (the polyglot seam) and Appendix D. | gap=scattered | priority=high | area=implementation-languages
  HFT: Every real trading stack is polyglot — a C++ or Rust core beside Python research, Java gateways, Go tooling and vendor C SDKs — and the boundary between them is where copies, per-call overhead and undefined behaviour creep back into a path that was tuned to be copy-free. The book already teaches the shared-memory contract across processes (Ch. 50) but never across languages, and never puts a number on what a cross-language call costs.
  SCOPE: The C ABI as lingua franca: the `extern "C"` hourglass pattern, opaque handles, POD-only structs with `static_assert(sizeof/offsetof)` parity enforced on both sides (`#[repr(C)]`, `ctypes.Structure`, `MemoryLayout`), `-fvisibility=hidden` plus export lists (Appendix D.4), symbol versioning and `-fno-semantic-interposition` at a library boundary (Ch. 22) // What may not cross the boundary: C++ exceptions (Ch. 20 — UB across C ABI; `noexcept` wrappers), Rust panics (`panic=abort`, `catch_unwind`, `extern "C-unwind"`), `std::string`/`std::vector`/`std::function` layouts, C11 `_Atomic` vs `std::atomic` layout compatibility; ownership-transfer protocols (who frees), borrowed-span lifetimes // Zero-copy across the boundary: pointer+length (`std::span` ↔ `&[T]` ↔ Python buffer protocol/`memoryview` ↔ `MemorySegment`), the Arrow C Data Interface, and shared-memory rings/journals as the preferred cross-language data plane (Ch. 50 §50.4.2 offsets and versioned layouts, Ch. 75) with SBE/FlatBuffers/Cap'n Proto schema codegen emitting C++, Java, Rust and Python accessors from one schema (Ch. 53) // Measure the call: a null call via direct C, pybind11/nanobind, ctypes/cffi, JNI, Panama downcall (with `critical`/`isTrivial` linker options), cgo, Rust `extern "C"` and a `cxx` bridge — ns per call and what each does to the caller (callee-saved spills, `vzeroupper`, GC safepoint polling, thread-state transitions); why one FFI call per tick is a tier violation; a new Appendix E row // Per-tick vs per-batch boundary design; GC'd runtimes calling into the C++ core (object pinning, moving collectors, safepoints during the native call), reentrancy, thread-local state and affinity across the boundary (Ch. 42) // Tooling: `bindgen`/`cbindgen`/`cxx`/`autocxx`, `nanobind`, `jextract`, `cgo`; ABI-compatibility testing (`libabigail`/`abi-compliance-checker`), sanitizers across mixed-language builds (Ch. 40/72) // Pitfalls: struct padding drift between compilers (Ch. 9), enum size assumptions, `bool`/`size_t` width mismatches, lifetime of a borrowed slice after the owner reallocates, exceptions silently terminating at the boundary, benchmarking the FFI call with the GC parked

--- [languages #5] Choosing a Language per Latency Tier & Benchmarking Across Languages with Equal Rigour
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Chapter 5 (closing chapter) of the new 'Beyond C++' Part — the methodological capstone that extends Ch. 3/Appendix L across languages and makes Appendix B's 'match the language to the tier' framing quantitative. The reader needs the tier table with real floors and a benchmarking protocol that is as unforgiving across languages as the book is within C++.

--- [languages #6] Research-to-Production: Python, Julia and the C++ Core
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=A §x.4 section of the proposed tier/benchmarking chapter (proposal 5). If the new Part is not created: a new §75.4.6 in Ch. 75 (Capture, Persistence & Replay Storage) directly after §75.4.5 'Feeding captures into replay, simulation and backtests', with a pointer from Ch. 76 §76.4.3. | gap=absent | priority=medium | area=implementation-languages
  HFT: Signals are discovered in Python or Julia and traded in C++, and the gap between the two implementations is a silent source of both P&L surprises and false latency regressions in the Ch. 76 CI gates. The book already builds the capture/replay seam (Ch. 74–75) that makes the hand-off possible but never says what the research side runs or how data crosses to it without copies.
  SCOPE: The two-language problem: research in Python (numpy/pandas/Polars) or Julia versus production C++; parity risk when the research book-builder and the production decoder are different code (different signals, Ch. 27 float-vs-scaled-int price drift) and the 'one library, two bindings' pattern — the C++ core exposed via pybind11/nanobind so backtests (Ch. 75.4.5) run the production decode // Zero-copy data exchange: Arrow/Parquet as the capture-to-research format (Ch. 75.4.2 binary journals → columnar), the numpy buffer protocol over the Ch. 50/75 shared-memory journals, kdb+/q and columnar time-series stores as the industry's research tier // Julia's 'no two-language problem' claim measured: time-to-first-execution/compile latency, GC pauses, `@inbounds`/`StaticArrays`, why it sits in the research tier // Numba/Cython/JAX for research speed vs production parity; model export (ONNX/TensorRT — Ch. 67) as a clean boundary for ML inference // Pitfalls: the GIL anywhere near a latency-sensitive loop, pandas semantics drifting from the C++ decoder (timestamps, NaN handling), re-implementing the Ch. 25 order book in Python and trusting its outputs as golden records

######## LENS design-patterns — 10 proposals ########
SUMMARY: LENS: useful design patterns for low-latency trading systems. The book already treats several patterns thoroughly and these were NOT proposed: Disruptor + self-correcting batching (Ch. 37 §37.4.1-2, Ch. 47 §47.4.2, Ch. 48 §48.4.3), seqlock and double-buffering (Ch. 35 §35.4, Ch. 73 §73.2), deterministic state machine + ordered-log replay / event sourcing (Ch. 74 §74.2.2, Ch. 75), hot/cold splitting (Ch. 8 §8.2.2, Ch. 12 §12.4.2), CRTP/policy-based design (Ch. 14 §14.4.2, Ch. 19 §19.4.1-3), object pools/free-lists/intrusive containers and the LOB case study (Ch. 24 §24.4.2, Ch. 25 §25.4.3-4), the single-writer principle (Ch. 31 §31.4.2, Ch. 35, Ch. 74 §74.4.2), exchange-side snapshot+increment recovery (Ch. 54). Smaller user-listed items were folded into proposals rather than listed alone: snapshot+delta/late-join for internal consumers -> Sequencer chapter; triple-buffering/latest-value mailbox and smart-batching-vs-drop -> Flow Control chapter; strong types and std::inplace_vector -> Type-Driven Design and Slot-Map proposals; actors/active object and reactor/proactor -> Threading Topologies chapter.

WHAT IS MISSING (10 proposals, ranked): four HIGH - (1) the Sequencer / total-order-broadcast architecture with replicated deterministic consumers and hot standby (absent: 'sequencer' appears once, as a glossary line in Appendix J); (2) idempotent order entry with correlation tokens and a preallocated in-flight order table (absent: 0 hits for ClOrdID/duplicate order/PossDup/at-least-once; 'idempoten' only in Ch. 65 pointing at market-data chapters); (3) flow control - backpressure/overflow policies/conflation/staleness (scattered across Ch. 34, 37, 54, 71, 75 with four different ad-hoc drop policies; conflation and latest-value registers absent); (4) threading topologies and the hot-loop skeleton - run-to-completion vs staged pipeline vs fan-out, polling many sources fairly (scattered: one bullet each in Ch. 31, 37, 39, 41, 47, 62). Five MEDIUM - timers/timing wheels (0 hits for timing wheel/timerfd), type-driven design (type-state, static DI/test seams, FSM encodings, strong types - 0 hits for typestate/dependency injection/mock), the software pre-trade risk gate (built only in FPGA in Ch. 69, budgeted at ~40 ns in Ch. 76, never designed), slot maps/generational handles as a Ch. 25 section (cited in six chapters, never built or measured), and an Appendix M anti-pattern catalogue (76 per-chapter pitfall sections, zero consolidation in appendices A-L). One LOW - a slot-ownership/zero-copy handoff section in Ch. 37.

PROPOSED PLACEMENT: a coherent mini-Part, 'Part XII - Low-Latency Architecture & Design Patterns', inserted after Ch. 70 (end of Part XI) and before the current Part XII (Observability & Operations), which becomes Part XIII; the existing scripted renumber + cross-ref remap (Phase 3 tooling) makes the insertion mechanical. Suggested chapter order: Threading Topologies & the Hot Loop -> Flow Control -> The Sequencer Architecture -> Idempotent Order Entry & the In-Flight Table -> Timers & Deadlines -> Type-Driven Design -> The Pre-Trade Risk Gate (applied capstone). Ch. 74 (Process Topology & Deterministic State Machine) could optionally migrate into this Part as its anchor, since the sequencer, order-entry and risk chapters all build on §74.2.2. If the mini-Part is not adopted, the alternative placements given per proposal still work (Threading Topologies after Ch. 37 in Part VI; Risk Gate before Ch. 74; Type-Driven Design as Ch. 19 §19.4.4-5; Slot Maps as Ch. 25 §25.4.4; Ownership as Ch. 37 §37.4.3).

--- [design-patterns #1] The Sequencer Architecture: Total-Order Broadcast, Replicated Deterministic Consumers & Hot Standby
  kind=new-chapter | placement=New Part XII — Low-Latency Architecture & Design Patterns (inserted after Ch. 70, before the current Operations Part, which becomes Part XIII). Third chapter in the new Part, after Threading Topologies and Flow Control and immediately before the order-entry chapter. Builds on Ch. 34/35/37/50/54/74/75; should be cross-referenced from Ch. 74 §74.2.2 ('inputs are an ordered, captured stream') and from Appendix J's one-line 'Sequencer' glossary entry. | gap=absent | priority=high | area=design-patterns
  HFT: Most trading firms and venues are built around a sequencer: one total order of events that every component consumes, so the gateway, risk, strategy and journal agree bit-for-bit on state and a standby can take over at sequence N with no reconciliation. The book already depends on 'a single ordered log' (Ch. 74–75) but never explains who produces that order, how replicas are kept identical, or what the extra hop costs on the tick-to-trade path.
  SCOPE: The sequencer pattern: one process (or a switch/FPGA) stamps a global sequence number on every input — market data after A/B arbitration (Ch. 54), order acks/fills, timer fires, operator commands, config changes (Ch. 73) — and fans the single sequenced stream out over multicast or shared-memory rings (Ch. 50) to every consumer // Replicated deterministic consumers: gateway, risk, strategy, journal and GUI all fold the same stream through a Ch. 74 state machine so every replica holds identical state; state-machine replication vs request/response, and why trading systems converged on the ordered log // Input merge and ordering rules: sequencing multiple sources (A/B feeds, several venues, timers) into one order; the sequencer's own loop (Ch. 62 run-to-completion), its single-writer discipline (Ch. 31/35) and throughput ceiling; sharding sequencers by instrument group when one stream cannot carry the load // Hot standby and failover: a passive replica consuming the same stream takes over at sequence N with no state transfer; sequencer failover (primary/backup with sequence-based handover, heartbeats, fencing) compared with Raft-based Aeron Cluster (Ch. 65) and consensus-free designs; measuring takeover time and its tail (extends Ch. 74 §74.3) // Rewind and late-join: the retransmit/rewind service and snapshot+delta catch-up for a restarted internal consumer (periodic state checkpoints in shared memory plus replay of the sequenced ring from the checkpoint sequence) — generalizing Ch. 54's exchange-side recovery and Ch. 75 §75.4.4 checkpoints // Cost accounting: the extra hop a sequencer adds (shared-memory ring write + read, tens to low hundreds of ns — Appendix E) vs direct feed→strategy; which paths go through the sequencer and which bypass it; hardware sequencing on switches (Ch. 60) and FPGAs (Ch. 69) // Exchange-side view: the matching engine as a sequencer — gateways → sequencer → matcher → publishers; price-time priority is defined by the sequencer's order (Appendix J.1) // Measure it: tick-to-trade latency with and without the sequencer hop; replica-divergence test (hash of state at sequence N across replicas); failover-time distribution under load // Pitfalls: ordering by wall-clock across hosts (PTP is not a sequencer — Ch. 58), multiple writers racing to stamp, consumers that read the clock (Ch. 74 §74.5), unbounded rewind buffers (Ch. 65 §65.5), a sequencer without a fenced standby as the single point of failure
  EXISTING: /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/appendix-J-market-structure-primer.md J.6 Roles, line 72: One glossary line: 'Sequencer — in some designs, a single process that stamps a total order on events for deterministic replay (Ch. 74, 75)'. Nothing more anywhere in the book.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.2.2 line 57; §74.3 line 75: Assumes 'every input … is a record in a single ordered log (Ch. 75)' but never says how multiple sources are merged into that order or how replicas stay identical; §74.3 measures restart/failover time without a hot-standby replica.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/65-transport-beyond-tcp.md §65.4.1 line 64; §65.5 line 96; references line 132: One sentence that Aeron Cluster provides Raft-based replicated state machines; a pitfall about not assuming total order from a transport.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/75-capture-persistence-replay-storage.md §75.4.4 lines 95–99: Sequences every captured record and checkpoints state — the persistence side, not the live fan-out/replication architecture.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/54-reliable-multicast-feed-recovery.md §54.2 lines 31–39; §54.4.2 lines 80–81: Snapshot + buffered-increment catch-up for exchange feeds — the external version of what internal consumers need from a sequencer.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il 'sequencer' → only appendix-J (one glossary line). 'total.order'/'total-order' → Ch. 30 (seq_cst memory ordering), Ch. 65 line 96, App J. 'state.machine.replication', 'deterministic consumer', 'hot standby', 'hot-standby', 'active-active', 'active/passive', 'leader', 'Paxos' → 0 files. 'Raft|consensus' → Ch. 65 only (lines 64, 132). 'replicated' → Ch. 16/31 (reference data replicated per node), Ch. 65. 'ordered log' → Ch. 74 line 57 only. 'stamp' in Ch. 50/74/75 → only 'timestamp' and the word 'seq' in the Ch. 74 diagram. 'matching engine' → App J/F definitions only, never as a design.

--- [design-patterns #2] Idempotent Order Entry: Correlation Tokens, the Preallocated In-Flight Order Table & Session Recovery
  kind=new-chapter | placement=New Part XII patterns mini-part, fourth chapter, directly after the Sequencer chapter and before Timers (it consumes the timer facility for ack timeouts and the sequencer for replayable gateway state). Cross-reference from Ch. 74 §74.2.1 ('order gateway owns … working-order state'), Ch. 73 §73.4.4 (draining open orders) and Appendix J.3 (order lifecycle). | gap=absent | priority=high | area=design-patterns
  HFT: A lost ack is an inconvenience; a duplicated order is a real position and real money, so order-entry code must be idempotent and must correlate thousands of asynchronous venue responses to the right order in nanoseconds. The book covers the gateway as a fault domain and the order lifecycle as a diagram, but never designs the in-flight table, the token scheme, or the reconnect/resend semantics that make order entry both fast and exactly-once.
  SCOPE: Order identity: client order IDs / OUCH tokens / FIX ClOrdID as monotonic per-session integers that encode slot index + generation (Ch. 28 field packing), never strings or hash keys on the hot path; venue uniqueness rules and the hazards of ID reuse within a session // The in-flight order table: a preallocated fixed-capacity array of order slots (Ch. 24 pool) indexed directly by the token, with a bitmap free-list (Ch. 28) and generation checks that reject late or duplicate responses; O(1) correlation of acks, partial fills, rejects, cancel-acks and unsolicited cancels to the originating slot // The order state machine as code: New → PendingAck → Working → PartiallyFilled → Filled/Cancelled/Rejected plus PendingCancel/PendingReplace; enum+switch, table-driven and std::variant encodings and their codegen (Ch. 14 §14.4.3, Appendix J.3); invalid venue transitions treated as untrusted input (Ch. 72) // Exactly-once effects: at-least-once delivery (TCP reconnect, FIX ResendRequest/PossDupFlag, OUCH token replay) vs at-most-once risk; client-side dedup by token, idempotent cancel, 'unknown order' handling, and why a blind resend after a timeout is how you send two orders // Timeouts and liveness: unacked-order timers (Timers chapter), session heartbeat/TestRequest (Ch. 51 §51.4.1), cancel-on-disconnect as the venue-side safety net (Ch. 74 §74.4.4), mass-cancel and order-status/mass-status requests for reconciliation, drop-copy as the independent source of truth // Gateway restart without orphaning orders: the in-flight table in a shared-memory segment (Ch. 26/50) so a crash-only restart (Ch. 74 §74.2.4) re-attaches and reconciles; session sequence-number recovery (FIX MsgSeqNum/SeqReset, OUCH replay) // Measure it: per-order gateway budget (token assign → encode → send; ack → slot lookup → transition) as a distribution; token→slot index vs hashed string-ID map (Ch. 25 §25.3 harness); reconnect-to-reconciled time // Pitfalls: std::unordered_map<std::string, Order> keyed by ClOrdID on the hot path, reusing tokens within a session, correlating by (symbol, price, qty) instead of token, treating rejects as exceptional (Ch. 20), resend storms after reconnect, trusting venue responses without bounds/transition validation

--- [design-patterns #3] Flow Control on the Hot Path: Backpressure, Overflow Policies, Conflation & Staleness
  kind=new-chapter | placement=New Part XII patterns mini-part, second chapter (after Threading Topologies, before the Sequencer). Builds on Ch. 34 (bounded rings), Ch. 37 (gating), Ch. 54 (slow consumer), Ch. 71/75 (drop policies). Should become the single home that Ch. 34 §34.4.1, Ch. 37 §37.4.1, Ch. 71 §71.6 and Ch. 75 §75.5 cross-reference instead of each restating its own policy. | gap=scattered | priority=high | area=design-patterns
  HFT: Every trading system has a consumer slower than the market-open burst — a GUI, a risk aggregator, a journal, a strategy mid-computation — and what happens to the backlog decides whether the fast path stays fast and whether anyone trades on stale prices. The book says 'never block the producer' in four chapters with four different drop policies but has no unified treatment, and conflation — the standard answer in market-data distribution — is never mentioned.
  SCOPE: The policy taxonomy for a full channel: throttle/block the producer (never on the tick-to-trade path), drop-newest, drop-oldest/overwrite, conflate per key, sample/degrade — and which consumer class gets which (strategy, risk, gateway, journal, GUI/monitoring) // Conflation: per-symbol latest-value slots plus a dirty bitmap (Ch. 28 tzcnt iteration) so a slow consumer sees the newest state of every instrument instead of a backlog; depth-1 conflation as the latest-value register/mailbox (wait-free single-writer/single-reader exchange, triple-buffering vs seqlock — Ch. 35) for top-of-book publication // Ring sizing math: (peak − sustained) × burst duration from captured market-open data (Ch. 53 §53.2.4, Ch. 75), headroom vs cache footprint (Ch. 7), and why 'a bigger buffer' is latency debt; Disruptor gating (Ch. 37) vs failing push (Ch. 34) vs overwrite rings // Staleness and deadlines: tag every message with the NIC hardware timestamp (Ch. 55/58) and skip or down-weight ticks older than a budget; age-aware dequeue in the strategy; deadline-aware order submission (never quote on information already microseconds stale) // Signaling overflow without blocking: drop counters and high-water marks published off-path (Ch. 71), alarm on the first drop at the open, record drops in the capture (Ch. 75) so replay and backtests see what production saw // Measure it: a market-open microburst replayed into a fast and a slow consumer under each policy — the fast consumer's tail must be unchanged; loss/lateness distribution of the slow consumer; conflation's message-count reduction vs information loss // Pitfalls: unbounded queues (Ch. 34 §34.5), blocking the producer (Ch. 71/75), silent drops, conflating the order/fill stream (never conflate state transitions, only snapshots), std::deque growth on the hot path, backpressure arriving as TCP window shrink from a slow gateway (Ch. 52)
  EXISTING: /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/34-lock-free-data-structures.md §34.4.1 lines 123, 133; §34.5 line 154: Bounded ring 'push fails when full → caller handles it'; 'handle fullness explicitly' — the policy is left to the reader.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/37-the-disruptor-pattern.md §37.4.1 line 136; §37.5 line 154; checklist line 175: Wrap protection gates the producer on the slowest consumer — 'natural backpressure' (producer stalls), right for a pipeline but wrong for a GUI/monitor consumer.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/71-zero-overhead-logging.md §71.3 line 62; §71.6 line 119; exercise 4 line 132: Drop-newest vs overwrite-oldest, never block the producer — for the log ring only.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/75-capture-persistence-replay-storage.md §75.3 line 54; §75.5 line 116; checklist line 138: Capture ring: 'drop-and-flag, never stall the trade'.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/54-reliable-multicast-feed-recovery.md §54.5 line 100: Slow consumer causes self-inflicted gaps — the symptom is named, no policy design.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/20-the-cost-of-abstractions.md lines 13, 129, 149: 'A full queue' listed as an expected error to return via std::expected.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/35-seqlocks-single-writer-publication.md §35.1 line 13: Snapshot-not-stream publication — the latest-value idea without the conflation framing.

--- [design-patterns #4] Threading Topologies & the Hot Loop: Run-to-Completion, Staged Pipelines, Fan-Out & Polling Many Sources
  kind=new-chapter | placement=New Part XII patterns mini-part, first chapter (the architecture-level framing the rest of the Part assumes). Alternative if the mini-part is not adopted: Part VI immediately after Ch. 37 (The Disruptor Pattern), since it generalizes §37.4.1's pipeline/fan-out/diamond graphs. Cross-reference from Ch. 31 §31.4.1, Ch. 41 §41.4.1, Ch. 62 §62.4.3 and Ch. 39 §39.4.1. | gap=scattered | priority=high | area=design-patterns
  HFT: The first architectural decision in a tick-to-trade system is how many threads touch a message and in what order they poll their inputs — it fixes the latency floor more firmly than any micro-optimization later in the book. The book states that single-threaded run-to-completion is lowest latency (Ch. 62, one bullet) and that pipelines are built from Disruptor barriers (Ch. 37), but offers no decision framework, no hot-loop skeleton, and nothing on polling several sources fairly.
  SCOPE: The three topologies and their latency cost model: single-thread run-to-completion (poll → decode → book → strategy → risk → send on one isolated core, zero hops), staged pipeline (one stage per core, each hop a cross-core cache-line transfer + wakeup — Appendix E HITM numbers, Ch. 39 §39.3 'cost of a hop'), parallel fan-out/shard-by-symbol (Ch. 31) — when a split pays (per-stage work exceeds hop cost) vs when it only adds tail // Pipelining vs parallelism: throughput-stage splitting (decode | strategy | journal) vs symbol sharding; Amdahl/USL (Ch. 31 §31.2) applied to a tick-to-trade chain; mapping the topology onto the core budget (Ch. 41–43), NUMA nodes (Ch. 16) and NIC queues (Ch. 57) // The hot-loop skeleton: a non-blocking run-to-completion loop that polls N sources (ef_vi/DPDK or AF_XDP rings — Ch. 62/61, shared-memory rings from other processes — Ch. 50, the timer queue, the control channel) in a fixed priority order, bounded work per iteration, housekeeping (stats publication, heartbeats — Ch. 74) time-sliced by TSC (Ch. 17) and never on the critical path // Fairness and starvation across sources: priority (order acks before market data, control before both), round-robin vs strict priority, bounded batch per source (Ch. 37/47 batching), how a flooded source starves another; measuring per-source service latency // Actors without a framework: shared-nothing stage threads communicating only via SPSC rings (Ch. 31/34) as the C++ 'active object'; single-writer ownership of every datum (Ch. 31 §31.4.2, Ch. 35) as the rule that keeps every topology lock-free // Reactor/proactor and event-loop frameworks vs the hand-rolled hot loop: where epoll loops (Ch. 47), coroutines (Ch. 38) and senders/receivers (Ch. 39) belong (cold, many-connection paths) and why the critical path stays a hand-written loop // Measure it: the same tick-to-trade work as run-to-completion vs 2-stage vs 3-stage pipeline — p50/p99.9 per topology; the hop cost isolated; interference of housekeeping on the hot loop's tail // Pitfalls: splitting stages for 'modularity' and paying a hop per message, oversubscription (Ch. 31 §31.5), blocking anywhere in the loop (Ch. 41), unbounded per-iteration work, work-stealing pools on the hot path, std::thread pools and futures for latency-critical handoffs
  EXISTING: /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/31-multithreading-concurrency-foundations.md §31.2 line 19–44; §31.4.1 line 106; §31.4.2: Shared-nothing partitioning, 'design for one writer', USL — the principle, not the topology decision or the loop structure.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/37-the-disruptor-pattern.md §37.4.1 lines 128–137: Pipeline, parallel fan-out and diamond dependency graphs via sequence barriers — within one ring.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/62-kernel-bypass-userspace-networking.md §62.4.1 lines 80–81; §62.4.3 line 98: DPDK lcores run-to-completion; 'single-threaded, run-to-completion on a pinned core … no handoffs — the lowest latency' — one bullet, no comparison or measurement.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/41-context-switching-mitigation.md §41.4.1 line 126: 'One hot thread per core; no oversubscription'.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/39-std-execution-senders-receivers.md §39.3 lines 62, 102; §39.4.1 line 72; exercise 3 line 117: 'Cost of a hop' (scheduler hop tens–hundreds of ns) and a custom scheduler over a pinned-core poll loop.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/47-linux-native-io.md §47.4.1 line 120: One epoll event loop per thread, sharded — the many-connection (cold) loop.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/57-flow-steering-receive-scaling.md §57.3 lines 43–55: Cross-core RX handoff cost measured — the NIC-to-core side of the same hop.

--- [design-patterns #5] Timers & Deadlines Without Jitter: Timing Wheels, Deadline Queues & Time as an Input
  kind=new-chapter | placement=New Part XII patterns mini-part, fifth chapter, after the order-entry chapter (which consumes its ack-timeout timers) and before Type-Driven Design. Builds on Ch. 17 (TSC) and Ch. 74 §74.2.2 ('time is an input'). Cross-reference from Ch. 53 §53.4.3 (reorder-window timeout), Ch. 74 §74.4.3 (heartbeat timeouts) and Ch. 46 §46.4.1 (warming cadence). | gap=absent | priority=medium | area=design-patterns
  HFT: A trading system is full of deadlines — an order unacknowledged for 500 µs must be treated as lost, a quote must be refreshed or pulled, a heartbeat must go out — and each is a timer that must fire within nanoseconds of its deadline without a syscall or a wakeup. The book makes timers inputs to the deterministic core and tunes heartbeat timeouts, but never shows how to build a jitter-free timer facility on a busy-poll core.
  SCOPE: Why kernel timers are wrong on a busy-poll core: timerfd, epoll_wait timeouts, nanosleep, std::this_thread::sleep_for and signal timers all mean syscalls, wakeups and context switches (Ch. 41); the alternative is polling a user-space timer queue from the hot loop with rdtsc (Ch. 17) once per iteration // Timer-queue data structures: hierarchical timing wheels (O(1) insert/cancel/expire, bounded per-tick work) vs binary/4-ary heaps vs sorted intrusive lists (Ch. 25) — cost, cache footprint and worst-case per-poll work; cancellation via generational handles (Ch. 25 slot-map section) so a cancelled timer cannot fire on a reused slot // The timers a trading system needs: order-ack and cancel-ack timeouts (order-entry chapter), quote refresh/requote and order-rate throttles (token bucket on the TSC — Appendix K.4 venue limits), session heartbeats/TestRequest (Ch. 51 §51.4.1), reorder-window gap timeouts (Ch. 53), stale-quote pulls, auction/close schedule events; coalescing timers to a tick granularity and the latency it costs // Time as an input (Ch. 74): timer fires enter the deterministic core as sequenced events and are captured (Ch. 75) so replay reproduces the exact timeline; the edge converts TSC/PTP time into events; the core never reads a clock // Multi-process timers: who owns the clock — the sequencer stamps time (Sequencer chapter) or each process edge does; drift between processes; PTP-disciplined CLOCK_REALTIME vs TSC for deadlines that span hosts (Ch. 58) // Measure it: fire-time jitter (requested vs actual) of a polled timing wheel vs timerfd/epoll_wait(timeout) vs nanosleep on an isolated core, as a distribution; per-poll overhead of the wheel with 10^4 live timers; interference of timer servicing on the hot loop's tail // Pitfalls: std::chrono::steady_clock::now() (a vDSO clock_gettime) sprinkled through the hot path, allocating timer nodes (Ch. 23), unbounded work when many timers expire in one tick, timers that read now() inside the deterministic core (Ch. 74 §74.5), SCHED_DEADLINE/cyclictest-style periodic wakeups for a busy-poll design (Ch. 45)
  EXISTING: /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.2.2 lines 43–57; §74.4.3 lines 81, 119; exercise 5 line 159: Timers as inputs to the pure core; heartbeat-timeout tuning for liveness detection — no timer implementation.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/17-timekeeping-tsc-rdtsc-clocks.md §17.4.1: Reliable TSC timestamping — the clock, not the timer queue built on it.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/53-zero-copy-wire-market-data-decoding.md §53.4.3 lines 170, 178, 227: 'Bounded reorder window + timeout' — a timer requirement stated without a mechanism.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/46-keeping-the-hot-path-warm.md §46.4.1 line 121: Shadow-traffic cadence must beat L1 decay — a periodic-timer need without a timer.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/45-realtime-scheduling-kernel-tuning.md §45.2 line 44: SCHED_DEADLINE dismissed as 'less common for busy-polling HFT'.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/59-precise-scheduled-transmission.md §59.2 line 32; references line 121: Departure-time scheduling of packets (EDT/LAUNCHTIME) — the NIC-side timer, not application timers; notes EDT 'replaced token buckets'.

--- [design-patterns #6] Type-Driven Design: Type-State, Strong Types, Compile-Time State Machines & Static Dependency Injection
  kind=new-chapter | placement=New Part XII patterns mini-part, sixth chapter, after Timers. Explicitly builds on Ch. 19 (CRTP/policies), Ch. 14 (variant/visit), Ch. 18 (constexpr tables) and Ch. 27 (the Price strong-type bullet). If a new chapter is not wanted, the same material fits as Ch. 19 §19.4.4 'Static dependency injection and no-op policies' and §19.4.5 'Type-state and compile-time FSMs' plus a Ch. 27 §27.4.4 on strong types. | gap=partial | priority=medium | area=design-patterns
  HFT: The most expensive bug in a trading system is a correct-looking message with the wrong unit, side or lifecycle state, and the cheapest place to catch it is the compiler — if the types are designed so the mistake cannot be expressed. Ch. 19 uses CRTP and policies for speed; this chapter uses the same machinery for correctness and testability (type-state, strong types, injected fakes) and shows how to encode the order and session state machines so they are both fast and un-misusable.
  SCOPE: Strong types for the domain: Price, Qty, Side, SymbolId, Seq, Nanos as zero-cost wrappers (enum class, single-member structs, phantom/tag parameters) that make mixing scales or units a compile error (Ch. 27 §27.6), verified in asm to be a bare register (Ch. 4); C++20 concepts to constrain them; what -Wconversion and [[nodiscard]] add // Type-state: encoding lifecycle in the type — Session<Disconnected> → Session<LoggedOn>, Order<Pending> → Order<Working> — so 'send on an unconnected session' or 'fill a cancelled order' does not compile; move-only transitions, std::expected for runtime-only transitions (Ch. 20); where type-state stops paying (dynamic state still needs a runtime FSM) // Compile-time and table-driven state machines: enum+switch, transition tables (constexpr 2-D arrays — Ch. 18), std::variant of state types + std::visit (Ch. 14 §14.4.3), template/CRTP FSM libraries (Boost.SML-style); codegen comparison (jump table vs branch chain vs cmov — Ch. 13/14) on the order and FIX-session state machines (Appendix J.3) // Static dependency injection: venue, clock, queue, logger and risk gate as template parameters (Ch. 19 §19.4.3) so tests substitute a fake venue/clock at zero runtime cost — the test seam without virtual; if constexpr + concepts for optional capabilities; explicit instantiation to control bloat (Ch. 19 §19.6) // No-op policies as feature removal: NullLogger, NoRisk (shadow mode), NoCapture that compile to nothing via EBO/[[no_unique_address]] (Ch. 9) vs runtime feature flags (one predictable branch — Ch. 13) vs per-venue binaries; the build matrix this implies (Ch. 22) // Static plugin architecture: strategies as a compile-time type list dispatched by if constexpr/fold expressions vs std::variant of strategies (closed set, selected once at startup) vs dlopen (Ch. 73 §73.4.4) — cost, reload story and testability of each // Measure/verify: asm diff of strong-typed vs raw-integer price math; dispatch cost of variant-FSM vs switch-FSM vs virtual; confirm injected fakes leave no trace in the production instantiation // Pitfalls: strong types that are not trivially copyable (lose register passing), std::variant with a non-trivial alternative (Ch. 14 §14.4.3 caveats), template bloat and compile times, type-state for state that is actually dynamic, std::function-based DI on the hot path (Ch. 14 §14.6)
  EXISTING: /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/19-template-metaprogramming-zero-cost.md §19.2 lines 25–45; §19.4.1–3 lines 135–168; §19.6 line 224: CRTP StrategyBase template-method, policy-based OrderBook<Storage,Locking,Alloc>, 'empty policies are truly free' (NoLock), compile-time configuration per venue, runtime selection at setup — performance framing only; no testability/injection, type-state or FSM encodings; plugins mentioned only as the case where CRTP does not fit.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/14-indirect-calls-virtual-dispatch.md §14.4.3 lines 193–219: std::variant<AddOrder, CancelOrder, Trade> + std::visit as closed-set event dispatch — not state encoding.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/27-fixed-point-floating-point-arithmetic.md §27.4.1 line 127; §27.6 line 195; exercise 2 line 204: 'Wrap it in a strong type (struct Price { int64_t ticks; })' — one bullet, one pitfall line and an exercise.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/18-compile-time-mechanics.md §18.4.2 line 162: Compile-time dispatch tables indexed by message type.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/73-hot-reload-live-reconfiguration.md §73.4.4 lines 97, 105, 121: dlopen strategy reload with warm-up — the runtime-plugin end of the spectrum.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/appendix-J-market-structure-primer.md J.3 lines 33–40: The order state machine described conceptually.

--- [design-patterns #7] The Pre-Trade Risk Gate in Software: Limit Tables, Position Accumulators, Throttles & the Kill-Switch Path
  kind=new-chapter | placement=New Part XII patterns mini-part, last chapter (the applied capstone that composes the hot loop, flow control, in-flight table, timers and type-state). Alternative: the Operations Part immediately before the Process Topology chapter (Ch. 74), whose 'Risk' role bullet (§74.2.1) and §74.4.4 kill-switch would then point at it. Cross-reference from Ch. 69 §69.4.1 (the FPGA version) and Ch. 76 §76.3's ~40 ns risk row. | gap=partial | priority=medium | area=design-patterns
  HFT: Every order must pass a mandated pre-trade risk check, and in a ~640 ns tick-to-trade budget the gate gets about 40 ns — so its data layout, arithmetic and process placement are hot-path engineering problems, not compliance paperwork. The book budgets the gate (Ch. 1, 76), names it as a role (Ch. 74) and builds it in FPGA fabric (Ch. 69), but never designs the software version most readers will ship.
  SCOPE: What SEC Rule 15c3-5 / MiFID II RTS 6 require on the order path (max order size/notional, price collars/bands, position and credit limits, message-rate limits, duplicate-order detection, kill-switch) and what a software gate must do in ~40–120 ns (Ch. 76 §76.3) // Data layout for 40 ns: per-symbol and per-account limit records in flat arrays indexed by SymbolId/account slot (Ch. 25), hot/cold split of limits vs audit fields (Ch. 8), sized to a cache line (Ch. 9), replicated per NUMA node (Ch. 16), hot-reloaded via seqlock/RCU snapshots (Ch. 73) // Branchless checks: price-collar and size checks as min/max/mask compositions (Ch. 13/28), overflow-safe notional math in scaled integers with __int128/__builtin_mul_overflow (Ch. 27/72), a single predictable reject branch; what the asm must look like (Ch. 4) // Position and exposure accumulators: single-writer per-account state updated on fills (order-entry chapter), published to monitoring via seqlock (Ch. 35); open + in-flight exposure vs filled position; --- [design-patterns #8] Slot Maps, Generational Handles & Relocatable Object Graphs (indices, not pointers)
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 25 (Hot-Path-Hostile STL & Cache-Friendly Containers): new §25.4.4 'Slot maps and generational handles' inserted before the LOB case study (which becomes §25.4.5 and should adopt the slot map for its orderID→Order table), with a matching benchmark added to §25.3. One-line forward references from Ch. 24 §24.4.2, Ch. 34 §34.4.3, Ch. 36 §36.4, Ch. 50 §50.4.2 and Ch. 72 §72.4.2, each of which currently mentions the idiom in passing. Add std::inplace_vector to Appendix H. | gap=scattered | priority=medium | area=design-patterns
  HFT: Order books, in-flight order tables, timer queues and session tables all need stable identities for millions of short-lived objects without allocation, pointer chasing or ABA — the slot map with generational handles is the idiom low-latency shops actually use. Six chapters of the book cite 'indices instead of pointers' or 'generation counters' as the answer, but the structure is never built, shown or measured.
  SCOPE: The slot map: dense value array + sparse indirection table + free-list of slot indices + per-slot generation counter; Handle{uint32 index, uint32 generation} packed into 64 bits (Ch. 28); O(1) insert/erase/lookup, erase-by-swap keeping values contiguous for iteration (Ch. 8) // Validation on dereference: a generation mismatch catches use-after-free/stale handles with one predictable compare (Ch. 13) — the cheap safety net Ch. 72 §72.4.2 asks for; debug-mode poisoning; when to skip the check on trusted internal paths and verify it compiles away (Ch. 4) // Why indices beat pointers on the hot path: 32-bit handles halve footprint (Ch. 9), tables are relocatable (grow/rehash — Ch. 25 §25.4.1 trade-offs), work across process address spaces in shared memory (Ch. 50 §50.5 offsets rule), and need no ABA tags (Ch. 34 §34.4.3) or SMR (Ch. 36 §36.4) // Entity-style storage: handles as the identity of orders/levels/sessions with SoA field arrays indexed by the same slot (Ch. 8 §8.2.1) — the book's orderID→Order table, the in-flight order table (order-entry chapter), timer slots (Timers chapter) and per-symbol strategy state as instances // Fixed-capacity companions: std::inplace_vector (C++26, P0843; boost::static_vector / etl::vector fallback) as the zero-allocation vector for slot payloads and per-level order lists // Measure it: lookup/insert/erase ns and cache misses for slot map vs std::unordered_map<uint64_t, Order*> vs pointer-linked pool on the LOB workload (reuse §25.3's harness); handle-check cost with vs without the generation compare; 32-bit vs 64-bit handle footprint effect on L1 misses // Pitfalls: handing out raw pointers into the dense array across an erase (invalidated by swap), generation wrap-around on hot slots, mixing handles from different slot maps (strong-type the handle — Type-Driven Design chapter), unbounded growth where fixed capacity was the point (Ch. 24 §24.5)
  EXISTING: /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/25-hotpath-hostile-stl-cache-friendly-containers.md §25.4.3 line 138; §25.4.1 line 121; §25.4.4 / exercise 4 line 176: One bullet 'Index handles over pointers … a common low-latency idiom (slot maps, generational indices …)'; 'use indices or an intrusive design if you need stable identity'; 'a flat orderID→Order map' in the LOB — no implementation or measurement.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/34-lock-free-data-structures.md §34.4.3 line 140; checklist line 172: Index-based pools/rings 'sidestep ABA' — mention only.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/36-safe-memory-reclamation.md §36.4 lines 150, 161, 176: 'Object pools with integer indices instead of pointers' as the no-reclamation escape — mention only.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/50-inter-process-communication.md §50.4.2 line 145; §50.5 line 153: Offsets/indices, never pointers, in shared memory — the cross-process reason without the data structure.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/72-secure-programming-low-latency.md §72.4.2 line 89; checklist line 172: 'Generation counters / tagged handles instead of raw pointers (an index + generation, validated on deref)' — the safety reason, no implementation.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/24-custom-allocators.md §24.4.2 lines 146–154: Free-list pools — the allocation half of a slot map.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/07-memory-hierarchy-and-caches.md §7.4 line 185: 'Indices-into-arrays instead of pointers' to avoid pointer chasing.
