--- [design-patterns #9] Appendix M — Hot-Path Anti-Pattern Catalogue (symptom → counter → fix → chapter)
  kind=new-appendix | placement=New Appendix M after Appendix L. Consolidates every chapter's §N.6 'Pitfalls & anti-patterns' the way Appendix C consolidates tuning knobs and Appendix I consolidates commands; each entry links to the chapter that measures it. Cross-reference from the Preface's 'How each chapter is built' and from Ch. 76 §76.4.1 (regression gates) as the code-review checklist. | gap=scattered | priority=medium | area=design-patterns
  HFT: When a tick-to-trade path regresses from 800 ns to 1.2 µs the cause is almost always one of a few dozen well-known hot-path sins, and a reviewer needs them in one ranked list with the counter that exposes each — not spread over 76 pitfalls sections. The book names the anti-patterns individually ('the dominant anti-pattern', 'the headline disaster', 'the cardinal rule') but has no catalogue, and none of the twelve appendices consolidates them.
  SCOPE: Organized by where the sin lives: allocation (hidden malloc in handlers, std::string/std::vector growth, coroutine frames — Ch. 23, 38); dispatch (std::function, megamorphic virtual, shared_ptr refcounts — Ch. 14, 25); control flow (exceptions for expected errors, seq_cst by default — Ch. 20, 30); sharing (locks on the hot path, shared mutable counters, false sharing — Ch. 31–33); queues (unbounded, blocking producer, no overflow policy — Ch. 34, 71, Flow Control chapter); kernel (syscalls, sleep, blocking epoll_wait, clock_gettime inside the core — Ch. 41, 47, 74); memory (THP/khugepaged, first-touch faults, pointer chasing, NUMA-remote — Ch. 7, 15, 16, 23); input (trusting wire lengths — Ch. 53, 72); architecture (stage hops for modularity, clock-ordered events, non-deterministic core — Topology/Sequencer chapters) // Each entry in a fixed format: the pattern as it looks in source, the symptom in the latency distribution (p99.9 spike, bimodal tail), the detector (perf counter, bpftrace one-liner, strace -c, sanitizer, -Wpadded, operator-new counter — Appendix I), the fix, and the deriving chapter/section // A ranked 'first ten to look for in code review' list and a CI-enforceable subset (steady-state allocation count = 0, syscalls per message = 0, no std::function symbols in the hot translation unit — ties Ch. 76 §76.4.1) // The mirror column — patterns that are fine off the hot path, to stop over-application: exceptions at startup, std::map for config, std::thread pools for research, std::print in the logger consumer (Ch. 71) // Language-level traps that masquerade as design: implicit conversions in price math (Ch. 27), auto capturing expression-template references (Ch. 19 §19.6), UB-as-optimization surprise (Ch. 21), volatile as a synchronization tool (Ch. 30)

--- [design-patterns #10] Zero-Copy Handoff Ownership: the Slot Lifecycle Across Stages and Processes
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 37 (The Disruptor Pattern): new §37.4.3 'Slot ownership and zero-copy handoff across stages' after §37.4.2 Batching, with pointers from Ch. 50 §50.4.1 (cross-process rings) and Ch. 49 §49.5 / Ch. 48 §48.5 (buffer lifetime until completion). Alternatively a section of the new Threading Topologies chapter. | gap=scattered | priority=low | area=design-patterns
  HFT: Zero-copy pipelines are only correct if every byte has exactly one owner at every instant, and a stage that keeps a pointer one sequence too long corrupts a message already on its way to the exchange. The book establishes slot reuse (Ch. 37), buffer-lifetime-until-completion (Ch. 48/49) and fixed-slot cross-process rings (Ch. 50) separately, but never states the single ownership protocol they all implement.
  SCOPE: The slot lifecycle as an explicit state machine: free → claimed (producer owns) → published (consumers may read) → consumed by all gated stages → free; which sequence transition grants and revokes each right (Ch. 37 §37.2, Ch. 35 release/acquire) // Ownership transfer without reference counts: a stage holds a std::span/view into a slot only until it advances its sequence; views passed downstream (decode → book → strategy) with lifetime bounded by the last stage's barrier; move-only, [[nodiscard]] slot handles that make 'kept a pointer past publish' a compile error (Type-Driven Design chapter) // Cross-process variant: slot ownership in a shared-memory ring (Ch. 50) where a crashed owner never releases — detecting and reclaiming orphaned claimed slots on restart (Ch. 74 §74.2.4), header/PID checks, and why offsets (Ch. 50 §50.5) make views relocatable // DMA-owned buffers: NIC RX descriptors (Ch. 55/62) and zero-copy TX (MSG_ZEROCOPY, io_uring SEND_ZC — Ch. 49) as externally owned slots whose release is a completion event; unifying completion-driven release with sequence-driven release // When to copy instead: small fixed-size messages (Ch. 49 §49.5 small-message trap) copied into the ring vs large payloads referenced by (offset, length) descriptors into a byte-ring (Ch. 50 §50.4.1); measuring the crossover // Pitfalls: refcounted shared_ptr slots (Ch. 25 §25.5), views escaping into timers or logs (Ch. 71 must copy), reusing a slot before the slowest consumer or the NIC completion (Ch. 37 §37.5, Ch. 48 §48.5), double publish, consumers mutating a published slot
  EXISTING: /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/37-the-disruptor-pattern.md §37.2 lines 35, 41; §37.4.1 line 136; §37.5 line 154: Claim → write → release-publish; slots reused in place; wrap protection against overwriting unread slots — the mechanics without an ownership-protocol statement.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/49-zero-copy-modern-kernel-fast-path.md §49.1 line 17; §49.2 line 38; §49.5 lines 86–90: 'Zero-copy changes the buffer ownership model'; reusing a zero-copy buffer before completion is the cardinal bug — kernel-completion ownership only.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/48-io-uring-deep-dive.md §48.5 line 127; checklist line 152: The buffer must stay valid until the CQE.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/50-inter-process-communication.md §50.4.1 line 137; §50.5 line 156: Fixed POD slots, offset+length descriptors into a byte-ring; a producer that died mid-write leaves the ring indeterminate.; /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/53-zero-copy-wire-market-data-decoding.md §53.3 line 87; §53.4.2: Parse-in-place views over the receive buffer — the consumer side of the ownership question.

--- [cpp-features #1] Concepts, Constrained Overload Sets & Compile-Time Dispatch (tag dispatch vs `if constexpr` vs concepts)
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Part III, Ch. 19 (Template Metaprogramming & Zero-Cost Abstractions): new §19.4.4 'Concepts and constrained overload sets' after §19.4.3 Policy-based design, with a matching asm block in §19.5 and a pitfalls bullet in §19.6; update the Appendix H 'Concepts (Ch. 19)' row so it points at real content. | gap=partial | priority=high | area=language-features
  HFT: A tick-to-trade path resolves protocol, book-storage and strategy choices at build time, and concepts are the C++20 tool that makes that dispatch readable and checkable without reintroducing a vtable. Today the book names concepts only as a source of 'readable errors' while its dispatch techniques are SFINAE-era, so a C++20 reader has no model for which mechanism to pick or how to verify it compiles to the same code.
  SCOPE: Defining concepts from `requires`-expressions and the standard concepts (`std::integral`, `std::invocable`, `std::ranges::contiguous_range`); subsumption and how constrained overloads rank. // The four compile-time dispatch mechanisms on one feed-handler example — tag dispatch, `if constexpr`, SFINAE/`enable_if`, concept-constrained overload sets — identical codegen (prove with §19.5 diff), different build-time and diagnostic cost. // Concept-checked static interfaces: a `Strategy`/`BookStorage` concept at the template boundary replacing a CRTP base, and C++23 deducing `this` as the CRTP alternative (Appendix H names it, nothing shows it). // Concepts for protocol dispatch: constraining `decode<Msg>` overloads on message traits (fixed vs variable length, endianness) so untaken decoders are never instantiated (ties Ch. 53). // Measure it: `-ftime-trace` build time and diagnostic length for SFINAE vs concepts on the same constraint; runtime identical. // Pitfalls: ambiguous overloads when constraints do not subsume, `requires` on non-template functions, concepts that check syntax not semantics, over-constraining generic hot-path code. // Toolchain: GCC 10+/Clang 10+ (`__cpp_concepts`), `std::enable_if` fallback (Appendix H.3).

--- [cpp-features #2] Compile-Time Protocol Decoders: From Schema to Code with NTTPs, `consteval` Parsing & C++26 Static Reflection
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part III, between Ch. 19 and Ch. 20 (prerequisites Ch. 18–19 and Ch. 9; leads into Ch. 53 decoding and Ch. 71 binary logging). Alternative if a new chapter is unwanted: §18.4.3 + a §53.4.5 'Generating the decoder at compile time'. | gap=absent | priority=high | area=language-features
  HFT: The decode path is the hottest loop in the system and its correctness depends on field offsets matching the venue's schema; generating that table inside the compiler makes the fast path provably in sync with the schema at zero runtime cost. The book repeatedly promises 'an SBE/protocol decoder whose field layout is resolved at compile time' but never shows how, and the author explicitly asked for deeper C++ metaprogramming.
  SCOPE: Why the external generator (sbe-tool, FIX codegen) is a build step the compiler can own: schema → `constexpr` field table → accessors in one TU, with `static_assert`s against the real layout and schema version. // Class-type non-type template parameters (C++20) and `fixed_string`: schema snippets and field names as template arguments; `consteval` parsing of an SBE/ITCH field list into offsets, sizes and byteswap flags. // Generating overlay accessors with templates + `if constexpr`, and verifying they lower to the same `mov`+`bswap` as the hand-written §53.5 code. // C++26 static reflection (P2996): `^^T`, splicers `[: :]`, `std::meta::nonstatic_data_members_of`, `define_aggregate`; one struct definition yielding decoder, encoder, binary-log serializer (Ch. 71 argument capture) and enum-to-string tables; expansion statements (`template for`, P1306). // Compile-time layout contracts: `static_assert(offset_of(^^Msg::price) == 30)`, cache-line sizing (Ch. 9), schema drift caught at build time instead of at market open. // Measure it: build time (`-ftime-trace`/ClangBuildAnalyzer) and binary size vs generated code; runtime identical; `allocs/call == 0`. // Toolchain status and fallbacks: Clang P2996 branch/EDG for reflection, GCC/Clang for NTTPs and consteval; what to do today vs when reflection lands; new Appendix H rows for reflection and expansion statements. // Pitfalls: giant `consteval` parses blowing build time (Ch. 18 §18.5), schema/venue drift, reflection-generated code that is opaque in a debugger (Ch. 5).
  EXISTING: chapters/18-compile-time-mechanics.md §18.1 line 15; §18.4.2 lines 139–165: Promises a compile-time SBE decoder (Ch. 53) and shows constexpr lookup tables (digit pairs, tick sizes) — but no schema-driven generation.; chapters/53-zero-copy-wire-market-data-decoding.md §53.2.2 line 41; §53.5 lines 185–196: 'Generated decoders (SBE)' defers to external schema-driven code generators; codegen verification of hand-written memcpy+bswap.; chapters/71-zero-overhead-logging.md §71.4.1 line 80: Variadic-template argument capture with compile-time record size — the serializer reflection would generate.; chapters/appendix-J-market-structure-primer.md J.5 line 58: SBE schema is XML generating fixed-layout structs.

--- [cpp-features #4] Value Categories, Copy Elision & the Calling Convention: How Your Types Travel Between Functions
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Part III, Ch. 20 (The Cost of Abstractions): new §20.4.4 'Passing and returning values: elision, moves and the SysV ABI', a matching asm block in §20.5 and pitfalls in §20.6; cross-reference from Ch. 4 §4.2.1 (register model) and Ch. 9 (layout). | gap=scattered | priority=high | area=language-features
  HFT: Order and quote objects cross several function boundaries per tick, and whether a 16-byte `Order` or an `expected<Fill, Err>` travels in registers or through memory is decided by the type's layout and special members, not by the optimizer. --- [cpp-features #5] Object Lifetime for Zero-Copy: Implicit-Lifetime Types, `std::start_lifetime_as` and the Wire-Overlay Problem

--- [cpp-features #8] Non-Owning Callables & Cheap Type Erasure: `std::function_ref`, `move_only_function`, In-Place Functions and `std::any`
  kind=expand-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 14 (Indirect Calls, Virtual Dispatch & Devirtualization): extend §14.2.3 'std::function and type-erasure overhead' and add §14.4.5 'Lighter type erasure: function_ref, move_only_function, in-place function', extending the §14.3 benchmark and the §14.5 asm; add Appendix H rows. | gap=partial | priority=medium | area=design-patterns

--- [cpp-features #10] Appendix N — Undefined Behaviour Catalogue for Performance Programmers (what the optimizer assumes, and what the flags that buy it back cost)
  kind=new-appendix | placement=New appendix after Appendix D/M, cross-referenced from Ch. 21 §21.6, Ch. 40 §40.2, Ch. 72 §72.2.2 and Appendix D.7. | gap=scattered | priority=medium | area=language-features
  HFT: The hot path courts UB deliberately — signed price math, overlays off the wire, `[[assume]]` — because each UB rule is an optimization the compiler is licensed to make, and readers need the price of switching any rule off before a sanitizer finding turns into 'just add -fwrapv'. The book treats UB in four places as aliasing, races, sanitizer output and vulnerability, never as a single cost-benefit catalogue.
  SCOPE: Signed-integer overflow: induction-variable widening, strength reduction and vectorization that depend on it; `-fwrapv`/`-fno-strict-overflow`/`-ftrapv` and their measured cost on a scaled-price loop (Ch. 27); `__builtin_*_overflow` and C++26 `std::add_sat` where checks are wanted (Ch. 72 §72.4.3). // Strict aliasing (Ch. 21): what `-fno-strict-aliasing` costs in reloads and lost vectorization, and when a codebase should pay it anyway. // Null-pointer dereference and `-fno-delete-null-pointer-checks`; `[[assume]]`/`__builtin_unreachable` as programmer-introduced UB (ties Appendix M). // Uninitialized reads: `-ftrivial-auto-var-init=zero|pattern` cost on hot stack buffers, C++26 erroneous behaviour (P2795) and `[[indeterminate]]`. // Infinite loops without side effects (P2809 C++26 change), shifts ≥ width, out-of-range enum/bool values, misaligned access (Ch. 9), pointer provenance and one-past-the-end comparisons. // Object-lifetime UB (overlays, arenas, `launder`, `start_lifetime_as`) and data races as UB (Ch. 30) with the seqlock's `atomic_ref` fix (Ch. 35). // Detection and containment: `-fsanitize=undefined` in CI, `-fsanitize-trap`/`-fsanitize-minimal-runtime` for near-production builds (Ch. 40, 72), `-Wall -Wextra -Wconversion -Wstrict-aliasing=2`, static analysis. // Each entry: the UB, the optimization it licenses, the measured cost of the 'defined' flag, and where in the book it bit.
  EXISTING: chapters/21-aliasing-restrict-type-punning.md §21.2 lines 19–46; §21.6; §21.8 line 223: Strict aliasing as UB; `-fno-strict-aliasing` only as a documentation pointer, no cost.; chapters/40-concurrency-correctness-tooling.md §40.2 line 25: UBSan's catch list (signed overflow, aliasing, null, shifts, enum).; chapters/72-secure-programming-low-latency.md §72.2.2 line 35; §72.4.3 line 98: UB as vulnerability class; `__builtin_add_overflow` for risk math.; chapters/30-cpp-memory-model-atomics.md §30.1 line 13: Data race = UB.; chapters/appendix-D-compiler-flag-reference.md D.7 line 78: `-fsanitize=undefined` in CI.
  EVIDENCE: 'undefined behavio' appears in 6 files (Ch. 21, 30, 40, 72, App F, README) with no consolidated list. '-fwrapv|-fno-strict-overflow|-ftrapv|-fno-delete-null|trivial-auto-var-init|-fsanitize-trap|sanitize-minimal|P2809|erroneous behavio|indeterminate' → 0. '-fno-strict-aliasing' → 2 hits, both reference pointers (Ch. 21 lines 223/232), no measurement.

--- [cpp-features #11] `std::source_location` and `std::stacktrace`: Site Metadata and Crash Context Without Hot-Path Cost
  kind=expand-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 71 (Zero-Overhead Logging) §71.4.1/§71.4.2 for `source_location`-based site capture, and Ch. 5 (Debugging) §5.4.2 plus Ch. 74 §74.4.4 for `std::stacktrace` in safe-state/watchdog paths; Appendix H rows for both. | gap=absent | priority=low | area=debugging-diagnostics
  HFT: Binary loggers and `expected`-based error channels need to say where an event happened without paying for strings, and `std::source_location` provides that for free in C++20 while `std::stacktrace` provides the crash-time 'how did we get here' the operations chapters call for. Neither appears in the book, so Ch. 71's site-ID mechanism is still described as 'a macro + static registration'.
  SCOPE: `std::source_location::current()` as a defaulted argument: zero-runtime-cost file/line/function capture replacing `__FILE__`/`__LINE__` macros in the binary logger's site table (§71.4.1) and in `expected` error payloads (Ch. 20); verified as immediates in the asm. // Compile-time site IDs from `source_location` + `consteval` hashing vs macro registration; `.rodata` size and relocation implications. // `std::stacktrace` (C++23): `stacktrace::current()` cost (libbacktrace unwinding, tens of µs, allocates), linking (`-lstdc++exp`/`-lstdc++_libbacktrace`, libc++ status), and where it belongs — kill-switch/safe-state handlers, watchdog reports, core-dump companions (Ch. 74) — never the hot path. // Debug-info prerequisites for useful traces in optimized builds (`-g`, `-fno-omit-frame-pointer`, Ch. 5/Appendix D); offline symbolization. // Measure it: `source_location` argument cost (0 ns, immediates) vs `stacktrace::current()` (µs + allocation). // Pitfalls: `source_location` in inlined/templated wrappers reporting the wrong site, `stacktrace` from a signal handler (not async-signal-safe), leaking build paths into logs (Ch. 72 §72.4.5).
  EXISTING: chapters/71-zero-overhead-logging.md §71.2 line 40; §71.4.1 line 79; §71.5 line 107: Format-site ID 'via a macro + a static registration, or a consteval mechanism'; site ID as an immediate.; chapters/05-debugging-low-latency-optimized-code.md §5.4.2 line 55: gdb `bt` on a core dump; no in-process stack capture.; chapters/74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.4.5: Core dumps without stalling survivors.
  EVIDENCE: 'source_location|stacktrace|__builtin_FILE|__builtin_LINE|__PRETTY_FUNCTION__|libunwind|__builtin_return_address' → 0. 'backtrace' → 1 hit (gdb `bt`, Ch. 5 line 55). '__FILE__|__LINE__' → 0 (Ch. 71 describes the site ID abstractly).

--- [cpp-features #12] C++20/23 Atomics Additions: `atomic_ref`, `wait`/`notify`, `atomic_flag::test`, Lock-Freedom and 16-Byte CAS
  kind=expand-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 30 (The C++ Memory Model & Atomics): new §30.4.3 'Beyond `std::atomic<T>`: atomic_ref, wait/notify and wide atomics' with measurements in §30.3 and asm in §30.5; cross-reference Ch. 32 (futex), Ch. 34 §34.4.3 (DWCAS), Ch. 35 §35.5 (seqlock payload), Ch. 50 (shared memory). | gap=partial | priority=low | area=language-features
  HFT: Shared-memory IPC segments and seqlock-published quotes are plain structs by necessity, and `atomic_ref` is the only standard way to read them race-free; meanwhile a 16-byte tagged-pointer CAS that silently falls back to a libatomic lock turns a lock-free queue into a mutex. The book names these primitives in Appendix H but never shows their cost or their traps.
  SCOPE: `std::atomic_ref<T>`: atomics on fields of wire/shared-memory structs and seqlock payloads without changing their layout; `required_alignment`, lifetime rules, libstdc++ 10 / libc++ 19. // `std::atomic<T>::wait`/`notify_one`/`notify_all` and `std::atomic_flag::test` (C++20): the spin-then-futex implementation in libstdc++/libc++, measured wake latency vs busy-poll (Ch. 32, 41), and when it is acceptable off the hot core. // Lock-freedom you cannot see: `is_always_lock_free` static_asserts for every atomic placed in shared memory (Ch. 50); 16-byte atomics (`cmpxchg16b`, `-mcx16`, `__atomic_load_16`) and the libatomic lock fallback GCC emits silently for loads; `std::atomic<double>::fetch_add`. // `std::atomic<std::shared_ptr<T>>` (C++20) is lock-based in practice — measured, as the counterexample behind Ch. 73 §73.5's config-swap advice. // `std::atomic_signal_fence` vs `atomic_thread_fence` vs `asm volatile("":::"memory")` (Ch. 3's ClobberMemory): what each orders. // Startup-phase synchronization with `std::latch`/`std::barrier`/`counting_semaphore` (coordinating Ch. 46 warm-up) and `std::jthread`/`stop_token` for control threads — explicitly off the hot path. // Verify the codegen: `lock cmpxchg16b` vs `call __atomic_compare_exchange_16`; `atomic_ref` load lowering to a plain `mov` on x86.
  EXISTING: chapters/30-cpp-memory-model-atomics.md §30.6 line 175; checklist line 197: Warning that large `std::atomic<T>` may use a hidden lock; check `is_always_lock_free`. No 16-byte/libatomic/-mcx16 detail.; chapters/34-lock-free-data-structures.md §34.2 line 39; §34.4.3 line 139: DWCAS via `cmpxchg16b` for tagged pointers; no compile-flag or fallback discussion.; chapters/35-seqlocks-single-writer-publication.md §35.5 line 157: `atomic_ref` named as the standard-blessed fix for the seqlock data-race formality.; chapters/73-hot-reload-live-reconfiguration.md §73.5 line 106: `std::atomic<std::shared_ptr>` recommended with a 'measure the refcount RMW' caveat.; chapters/appendix-H-cpp-feature-matrix.md H.4 rows: Versions for atomic_ref, jthread, latch/barrier.
  EVIDENCE: 'atomic_ref' → 7 hits (Ch. 35 ×2, App H ×4, README) — no code. '\.wait(|notify_one|notify_all|atomic_flag' → 0 in chapters. 'mcx16|libatomic|__atomic_load_16|atomic_signal_fence|atomic<double' → 0. 'is_always_lock_free|is_lock_free' → 4 hits (Ch. 30/33 warnings only). 'std::latch|std::barrier|std::jthread|counting_semaphore|stop_token' → Appendix H rows only.

--- [cpp-features #13] Fixed-Capacity & Stable-Address Containers: `std::inplace_vector` (C++26), `std::hive`, static_vector and small_vector
  kind=expand-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 25 (Hot-Path-Hostile STL & Cache-Friendly Containers): rename §25.4.3 to 'Intrusive, fixed-capacity and small-buffer containers' and expand it; use `inplace_vector` for per-level order lists or repeating groups in the §25.4.4 order-book case study; Appendix H rows. | gap=partial | priority=low | area=language-features
  HFT: Per-price-level order lists, per-tick batches and per-message repeating groups are 'usually ≤ N' sequences that must never allocate, and `std::inplace_vector` is the first standard container built for exactly that. The container chapter names small-vector libraries in a single bullet and its own order-book case study uses no fixed-capacity container.
  SCOPE: `std::inplace_vector<T, N>` (C++26, P0843): the `vector` interface with inline storage and no allocator — `try_push_back`/`unchecked_push_back`, constexpr-ness, trivially copyable when `T` is, overflow behaviour (throw vs `try_`), and the hot-path `[[unlikely]]` capacity branch (ties Ch. 72 §72.4.2). // Today's equivalents: `boost::container::static_vector`, `etl::vector`, `absl::InlinedVector`/`llvm::SmallVector`/`boost::small_vector` (inline-then-spill), `std::array` + count — layout, size and spill behaviour compared. // `std::hive` (C++26, P0447; `plf::colony`): stable addresses with O(1) erase/insert and skipfield iteration — a candidate for order pools where intrusive lists need stable nodes (vs Ch. 24 pools). // Choosing capacity: size to the p99.9 occupancy (Ch. 1), overflow policy (reject vs spill), memory footprint (Ch. 9). // Measure it: push/pop/iterate latency distributions for `inplace_vector` vs reserved `std::vector` vs small_vector at N = 8/64/1024; `allocs/call == 0` proof (Ch. 23). // Verify the codegen: capacity-check lowering; a trivially-copyable `inplace_vector<Order, 8>` returned in registers or via `sret` (ties the ABI section). // Pitfalls: a 4 KiB `inplace_vector` on the stack (stack page faults, Ch. 23/26), copying large inline containers by value, exception-on-overflow inside a `noexcept` path.
  EXISTING: chapters/25-hotpath-hostile-stl-cache-friendly-containers.md §25.4.3 line 137; §25.4.4; §25.8 line 195: One bullet on SBO/small-vector types (boost::small_vector, absl::InlinedVector, llvm::SmallVector); order book uses flat arrays + intrusive FIFOs.; chapters/23-memory-management-fundamentals.md §23.4.1 line 121: SBO thresholds.; chapters/72-secure-programming-low-latency.md §72.4.2 line 90: 'Fixed-capacity inline buffers (small_vector, a bounded ring)' as overflow-proof types.; chapters/appendix-H-cpp-feature-matrix.md H.4 `std::hive` row: Version row only.
  EVIDENCE: 'inplace_vector' → 0. 'static_vector|fixed_vector|etl::' → 0; 'fixed-capacity' → 3 hits (Ch. 71 ring, Ch. 72 line 90). 'small_vector|SmallVector|InlinedVector' → 3 hits, all in one Ch. 25 bullet (line 137) and its reference (line 195). 'std::hive|plf::|colony' → 1 Appendix H row.

--- [cpp-features #14] `std::simd` (C++26) and Portable SIMD Wrappers: Standard Vector Types Without Intrinsics
  kind=expand-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 29 (SIMD & Vectorization): new §29.4.4 '`std::simd` and portable wrappers' after §29.4.3, with a codegen check in §29.5; Appendix H row for `<simd>`. | gap=partial | priority=low | area=language-features
  HFT: Batch-side work — risk aggregation, book-wide analytics, backtests — is where the book says SIMD pays, and readers today must choose between unportable intrinsics and a third-party wrapper; `std::simd` is the standard answer arriving with C++26 and Ch. 29 only name-drops its experimental predecessor. Showing it compiles to the same `ymm`/`zmm` code as intrinsics completes the chapter's 'verify the codegen' promise.
  SCOPE: `std::simd<T, Abi>` / `basic_simd` (C++26, P1928, `<simd>`; `std::datapar` naming in the latest drafts): `simd_mask`, `where`/`select`, `reduce`, `simd_cat`/`split`, ABI tags (`fixed_size`, `native`, `compatible`) and how width follows `-march`. // Interop: `std::simd` ↔ intrinsics (`__m256`), loads/stores with `element_aligned`/`vector_aligned`/`overaligned` flags, `std::mdspan` layouts (Ch. 8) feeding lanes. // A worked kernel: branchless top-of-book aggregation / risk sum in scalar, `std::simd`, Highway and intrinsics — same codegen? (verify `vpaddd`/`vpmaxsq`/`vfmadd` emission). // Runtime dispatch with `std::simd` + `target_clones` (Appendix M) for mixed AVX2/AVX-512 fleets; the downclocking caveat (§29.6). // Toolchain: libstdc++ `<experimental/simd>` (GCC 11+) vs `<simd>` (GCC 15/16), libc++ status, `__cpp_lib_simd`; fallbacks (Highway, xsimd, Eve). // Pitfalls: `fixed_size<N>` not matching the native width, masked operations lowering to blends rather than predication on AVX2, reductions reordering FP (Ch. 27 determinism).
  EXISTING: chapters/29-simd-vectorization.md §29.2 line 39; §29.4.2 line 117; checklist line 195; §29.8 line 203: Names `std::experimental::simd`, Highway, xsimd, Eve as preferable to raw intrinsics; no API shown, no measurement.; chapters/appendix-A-arm-graviton.md A.2 line 34: Portable wrappers recommended for NEON/SVE portability.; chapters/appendix-B-beyond-cpp.md B.1 line 17: Rust's `std::simd` mentioned.
  EVIDENCE: 'std::simd' → 2 hits (Ch. 29 checklist line 195, Appendix B Rust note). 'experimental::simd' → 3 name-drops (Ch. 29 ×2, App A). 'simd_mask|where(|fixed_size|element_aligned|basic_simd|datapar|P1928|__cpp_lib_simd' → 0. No Appendix H row for `<simd>`.


######## LENS sw-engineering — 10 proposals ########
SUMMARY: LENS: software-engineering lifecycle for low-latency code (testing, CI gates, build/toolchain reproducibility, static analysis & review, invariants-as-code, IPC schema versioning, release/deployment, fleet config & drift, fault injection/postmortems). Method: read chapters/README.md in full, then read Ch. 3 §3.4.4, Ch. 5 §5.3–5.4, Ch. 18 (build-time passages), Ch. 22 (full), Ch. 40 (full), Ch. 50 §50.4.2, Ch. 53 §53.2, Ch. 72 §72.4.6–72.4.8, Ch. 73 (versioning/cutover passages), Ch. 74 §74.2.2/§74.3/§74.4.3–74.4.5, Ch. 75 §75.4.5, Ch. 76 (full), Appendix C (full), Appendix D §D.7, Appendix I §I.9, Appendix L (full); then grepped ~150 distinctive terms across all 89 files (word-bounded where needed; e.g. "ABI" and "SLO" produced false hits inside "available"/"slow" and were re-run with -w).

FINDINGS. The book's lifecycle story is strong on the *architecture that enables* verification (deterministic state machine Ch. 74, capture Ch. 75, hot reload Ch. 73, crash-only/supervisor Ch. 74) and on *dynamic* correctness tooling (Ch. 40: TSan/ASan/UBSan, stress, fuzzing, model checking) — but the engineering process around that architecture is largely absent. Zero hits anywhere for: CMake, Bazel, Nix, ccache/sccache, unity builds, hermetic, SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH, Conan/vcpkg; clang-tidy, Coverity/CodeQL/cppcheck/scan-build/-fanalyzer; unit test, property-based/RapidCheck, linearizability, deterministic simulation (FoundationDB/loom style), mock/fake/test double; blue/green, standby, rollback, canary deploy, deploy window, conformance/certification, RTS 6; Ansible/Puppet/Salt, Redfish/dmidecode, golden image; chaos/fault injection/game day/runbook/postmortem-as-process. "Regression gate in CI" exists only as a 5-bullet section in the capstone (Ch. 76 §76.4.1) plus one bullet each in Ch. 3 §3.4.4 and Ch. 75 §75.4.5 — principle stated, mechanics (statistics, deterministic proxies, runner hardware, codegen tests, bisect automation, policy) missing. Shared-memory layout versioning is two bullets in Ch. 50 §50.4.2 with no evolution mechanics; SBE schema versioning/extension is never mentioned in Ch. 53. Performance invariants-as-code are scattered (static_assert(sizeof) Ch. 9, is_always_lock_free Ch. 30, null_memory_resource Ch. 24, operator-new instrumentation Ch. 38, consteval validation Ch. 18) with no home. PGO/BOLT profile *operation* (merge, storage, staleness, mismatch diagnostics) is absent despite Ch. 22/D.6 covering the three-step build. Ten proposals follow: five new chapters (testing strategies; performance CI & regression gates; build systems/hermetic toolchains/reproducible builds; static analysis, performance linting & code review; release engineering/controlled deployment), one new chapter-or-section on fault injection/game days/latency postmortems, and four targeted expansions (Ch. 18 performance contracts; Ch. 50 schema evolution; Appendix C fleet config-as-code & drift detection; Ch. 22 PGO/BOLT profile lifecycle).

CONSISTENCY ISSUES NOTICED IN PASSING (the user asked for consistency review; these are outside the gap list but concrete): (1) cross-references inside fenced code/diagram blocks were evidently not remapped by the Phase-3 renumbering script — e.g. 76-production-profiling-end-to-end-case-study.md lines 26–32 cite "Ch.16/50", "Ch.51", "Ch.62-63", "Ch.63", "§64.4.1", "§64.4.3" (should be 17/58, 61, 74–75, 75, §76.4.1, §76.4.3); 74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md lines 48–49 "Ch.44-52", "Ch.16"; 73-hot-reload-live-reconfiguration.md line 27 "§61.4.3"; 61-ebpf-bpftrace-xdp.md line 74 "§51.5"; 18-compile-time-mechanics.md line 112 "§17.5"; 28-bit-manipulation-integer-tricks.md line 81 "§27.6"; 30-cpp-memory-model-atomics.md line 98 "§29.5"; 55-nic-features-offloads.md line 45 "Ch.48"; appendix-D-compiler-flag-reference.md line 97 "Ch.63". A sweep of all fenced blocks for `Ch\.\d+`/`§\d+\.` is warranted. (2) Stale counts: Ch. 1 line 97 says "the book has 64 chapters"; Ch. 76 line 13 says "Sixty-three chapters built a toolkit" and line 146 refers to "Appendices (A–G)"; README.md line 5 says "seven appendices (A–G) follow" though A–L exist and are listed below it. (3) Ch. 76 §76.5 cites "Ch. 72's ASan point" for profiler overhead where Ch. 40 is the sanitizer chapter (Ch. 72 §72.3 does discuss hardening cost, so this may be intentional, but it reads as a mis-pointer).

--- [sw-engineering #1] Testing Latency-Critical Code: Unit, Property-Based & Deterministic Simulation Testing of the Deterministic Core
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part XII — after Ch. 75 (Capture, Persistence & Replay Storage) and before Ch. 76 (the capstone), so it can build on Ch. 74's deterministic state machine and Ch. 75's captures and feed Ch. 76's gates; cross-reference back to Ch. 40 (which stays the dynamic-tooling chapter) and forward from Ch. 40's 'Leads into'. | gap=partial | priority=high | area=other
  HFT: Most money-losing failures in a trading system are logic and sequencing bugs in the order book, feed arbitration and strategy state machine — not data races — and the book currently teaches how to *architect* a deterministic, replayable core (Ch. 74–75) but not how to test it before the open. A reader finishing Part XII has a replay harness and sanitizers but no guidance on unit/property/simulation testing of an order book, on test seams that cost zero nanoseconds, or on reproducing a distributed failure from a seed.
  SCOPE: Test architecture for functional-core/imperative-shell (Ch. 74 §74.2.2): testing `apply(state, input) → (state', outputs)` in isolation with scripted input logs; golden-master tests cut from captures (Ch. 75); table-driven order-book tests (Ch. 25 case study) with explicit expected BBO/depth after each message // Test doubles for the impure shell without hot-path cost: injectable `TimeSource`/clock as a template policy vs virtual seam (Ch. 19 vs Ch. 14), fake NIC/ring (Ch. 50), fake venue (acks/fills/rejects/cancel-on-disconnect), and verifying in the asm (Ch. 4) that the seam compiles away at -O2 // Property-based testing (RapidCheck) of the order book, decoders and allocators: invariants (best bid < best ask, level quantity = Σ orders, replay idempotence, encode∘decode = id), generators for realistic ITCH/SBE sequences, shrinking to a minimal failing sequence // Deterministic simulation testing (FoundationDB/TigerBeetle style): a single-threaded simulator with a controlled scheduler and virtual time driving feed handler, strategy, gateway and risk 'processes', seeded PRNG so any failure reproduces from its seed, built-in fault injection (drop/dup/reorder/delay/crash/clock step) — contrasted with Ch. 40's model checkers (exhaustive, small primitives) and stress (sampled) // Checking lock-free structures beyond TSan: linearizability/history checking of SPSC/MPMC queues (Ch. 34) and seqlock readers (Ch. 35) against a sequential reference under randomized interleavings; differential tests vs a mutex-based oracle; loom-style exhaustive interleaving on bounded tests // Zero-allocation / zero-syscall / no-virtual as test assertions: an `operator new` counting fixture (ties Ch. 23/38), `std::pmr::null_memory_resource` as proof (Ch. 24), `strace -c`/seccomp SIGSYS trap mode in tests to assert the steady state makes no syscalls (ties Ch. 41/72) // Fuzzing beyond parsers (extends Ch. 40 §40.4.2 / Ch. 72 §72.4.8): structure-aware fuzzing of message *sequences* into the state machine, fuzzing the replay/journal reader (Ch. 75) and config loader (Ch. 73), corpus seeding from captures, continuous fuzzing in CI with crash triage // Test build matrix and flakiness policy: -O0/-Og vs -O2 vs sanitizer builds and the 'bug only at -O2' case (Ch. 5 §5.4.1); running the suite on an ARM runner (Appendix A) and on several core counts; quarantine rather than retry for timing-dependent tests; mutation testing (mull) and branch coverage on the decode path as effectiveness measures // Measure it: time-to-detect for a planted sequence bug under unit vs property vs simulation testing; cost of the test-time seams at -O2 (ns/op before/after, asm diff)
  EXISTING: chapters/40-concurrency-correctness-tooling.md §40.4.1–40.4.3: Stress testing, fuzzing (parser-focused) and model checking (CDSChecker/GenMC/Relacy); one bullet 'Property/invariant checks under stress' — no unit-test or property-based framework, no DST; chapters/74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.2.2: States the core is 'pure, testable, and replayable' and that time/randomness are inputs — the enabling design, but testing it is not developed; chapters/76-production-profiling-end-to-end-case-study.md §76.4.3: Simulation harness that models venue responses for closed-loop strategy/P&L testing and 'inject the rare and the hostile' — strategy-level, not a deterministic system simulator or test method; chapters/05-debugging-low-latency-optimized-code.md §5.3: 'Deterministic stress to force rare bugs' via adversarial captured inputs under sanitizers; chapters/24-custom-allocators.md §24.2: `std::pmr::null_memory_resource` 'to prove no allocation' — an invariant-as-test idea mentioned once
  EVIDENCE: grep -il across chapters/*.md: 'unit test' 0 files; 'property-based|RapidCheck|QuickCheck|proptest' 0; 'deterministic simulation|simulation test|FoundationDB|Jepsen|loom' 0; 'linearizab' 0; '\bmock\b|mocking|test double|stub' 0 relevant (only 'fake' in Ch. 11/App. D in unrelated senses); 'gtest|GoogleTest|Catch2|doctest' 0; 'controlled scheduler|deterministic scheduler|virtual time|fake clock|inject.*clock' 0 (the one Ch. 17 hit for 'template.*Clock' is unrelated prose); 'golden' appears only in Ch. 75/76 for replay fixtures. 'test harness' 0. Read Ch. 40 in full: it covers tools, not test design; Ch. 74 §74.2.2 read in full: 'testable' asserted in one sentence.

--- [sw-engineering #2] Performance Engineering in CI: Benchmarks as Tests, Latency-Regression Gates & Automated Bisection
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part XII — immediately before Ch. 76 (and after the proposed Testing chapter), so Ch. 76 §76.4.1 can shrink to a pointer and the capstone keeps its end-to-end walkthrough; cross-reference Ch. 3 §3.4.4 and Appendix L, and add the CI skeleton to Appendix L as §L.5. | gap=partial | priority=high | area=other
  HFT: Ch. 76 says 'performance is a state you defend' and that a regression gate must be stable or it gets disabled, but gives five bullets of principle and no mechanics; readers who try to gate p99.9 on a shared CI VM will get a flapping gate and abandon it. The techniques that make latency gates trustworthy — noise calibration, deterministic counter proxies, codegen assertions, tuned runners and automated bisection — are exactly what separates shops that keep their 800 ns from shops that drift to 1.2 µs.
  SCOPE: Benchmarks as tests: wiring Google Benchmark into the test runner (ctest/labels), `--benchmark_out=json`, `compare.py` with Mann–Whitney U at a chosen α, per-benchmark baselines stored as artifacts; A/A runs to calibrate the noise floor before setting any tolerance; bootstrap confidence intervals on p99/p99.9 rather than means // Deterministic proxies that make gates stable on shared CI: `perf stat -e instructions:u,branches:u,branch-misses:u` (near-deterministic retired counts), cachegrind instruction counts, `llvm-mca`/uiCA cycle estimates for hot loops — what each proxy misses (memory-bound tails, frequency effects) and how to layer proxy gates (every PR) under wall-clock gates (nightly, dedicated box) // Codegen tests: FileCheck-style assertions on `-S`/`objdump` output of hot TUs (no `call malloc`/`operator new`, no `syscall`, no indirect `call *`, `cmov` present, `vzeroupper`/AVX-512 width as intended), `-Wframe-larger-than=`, `.text`/`.rodata` size budgets, `nm` symbol-count gates (ties Ch. 4, 12, 13, 29, Appendix I.9) // The performance CI runner: dedicated bare-metal tuned per Appendix C (isolated cores, governor, turbo off, SMT off, IRQs away), serialized jobs, a pre-flight `cyclictest`/noise smoke test that fails the run (not the code) when the box is noisy, identical build flags and `-march` to production (Ch. 22, Ch. 76 §76.5) // The replay-based tick-to-trade gate as the top of the pyramid (Ch. 74–76): choosing golden captures (market-open microburst windows — Ch. 53), per-stage HdrHistogram diff at p99/p99.9 with tolerance bands, golden-output bit-identity, how many replay iterations the quoted percentile needs (Ch. 3 §3.4.4) // Trend storage and drift alerts: per-commit distributions in a time series, N-run moving-window alerts for slow drift, retaining `perf.data`/profiles per commit for `perf diff`, `.text` size over time (Ch. 12) // Automated bisection: `git bisect run` driving the replay harness; bisecting toolchain, kernel and microcode changes separately from source (a build matrix keyed by compiler version — Ch. 22) so a compiler bump is treated as a reviewed performance event // Gating policy and ownership: blocking vs advisory gates, per-stage ns budgets checked into the repo as code (Ch. 1 §1.4.2 made mechanical), exemptions with expiry, who owns a regression, the perf review that accompanies a hot-path PR (ties the static-analysis/code-review proposal) // Build-time regression gates kept separate from runtime ones: `-ftime-trace`/ClangBuildAnalyzer totals per TU (Ch. 18–19) so the iterate loop stays fast // Measure it: gate false-positive rate vs runner tuning and iteration count (A/A tests on a shared VM vs a tuned bare-metal box), and instruction-count vs wall-clock variance on the same benchmark
  EXISTING: chapters/76-production-profiling-end-to-end-case-study.md §76.4.1 and §76.4.2 (bisect bullet), §76.5: Golden capture + bit-identical output gate, distribution-vs-baseline latency gate, 'stable measurement environment', 'track the trend', one bullet on replay-based bisection; no implementation, statistics, proxies, runner spec or policy; chapters/03-microbenchmarking-done-right.md §3.4.4: `compare.py` Mann–Whitney bullet and a single 'Gate regressions in CI … built out in Ch. 76' bullet; chapters/75-capture-persistence-replay-storage.md §75.4.5: One bullet: replay in CI asserting golden outputs and no per-stage regression; chapters/appendix-L-benchmark-harness.md L.1–L.4: Google Benchmark + HdrHistogram skeleton, repeatable-run environment and pre-trust checklist — no CI integration, baselines or gating; chapters/04-reading-the-machine-asm.md §4.4.3: Diffing codegen across flags/compilers 'to catch a compiler upgrade silently changing your hot loop — the asm-level complement to Ch. 3's regression gates' — manual, not automated

  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part III — immediately after Ch. 22 (Build Toolchain for Speed), closing Part III; Ch. 22 keeps the flag/LTO/PGO/BOLT model and points here for how the build is *organized, pinned, reproduced and sped up*. --- [sw-engineering #4] Static Analysis, Performance Linters & Code Review for the Hot Path
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part III — after the proposed Build Systems chapter (or directly after Ch. 22 if that chapter is not adopted); alternatively Part I after Ch. 5 as a methodology chapter.   HFT: Every rule this book teaches — no allocation, no syscalls, no virtual dispatch, no exceptions on the hot path, keep structs on a cache line — is currently enforced only by vigilance and by Ch. 40's *runtime* tools, which cannot see a path that did not execute. Mechanical enforcement (clang-tidy and custom AST checks) and a performance-aware review protocol are how a team keeps a tuned tick-to-trade path from regressing one innocuous PR at a time; the book has zero mention of clang-tidy or any static analyzer.

--- [sw-engineering #5] Performance Contracts: Encoding Latency Invariants in Code (compile-time, link-time and test-time)
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 18 (Compile-Time Mechanics) — new §18.4.3 'Performance contracts: asserting latency invariants at build time', expanding the single 'Compile-time validation' bullet in §18.4.1; cross-reference from Ch. 9 §9.3, Ch. 24 §24.2, Ch. 30 checklist, Ch. 38 §38.5 and the proposed Testing/Static-Analysis chapters; add a one-page consolidated table to Appendix D or I. | gap=scattered | priority=medium | area=design-patterns
  HFT: The book repeatedly tells readers to 'lock it with a static_assert' (Ch. 9), 'confirm is_always_lock_free' (Ch. 30), 'prove no allocation' (Ch. 24) and 'verify it folded' (Ch. 18), but these are scattered one-liners; a reader has no single pattern for turning a hard-won latency property into a build-time contract that fails the PR instead of the open. Invariants-as-code is the cheapest regression defense in the book — zero runtime cost — and it is the design pattern the user asked for.
  SCOPE: The layout contract: `static_assert(sizeof/alignof/offsetof)` tables for hot structs and wire overlays (Ch. 9 §9.3, Ch. 53), `static_assert(std::is_trivially_copyable_v<T>)`/`is_standard_layout_v` for anything `memcpy`'d or placed in shared memory (Ch. 50), `hardware_destructive_interference_size` pinned to 64 (Ch. 33) // The concurrency contract: `static_assert(std::atomic<T>::is_always_lock_free)` for every hot-path atomic (Ch. 30), power-of-two capacity asserts (Ch. 34/37), `noexcept` as a documented no-throw contract with `static_assert(noexcept(...))` on hot APIs (Ch. 20) // The constant-folding contract: `consteval` for 'must be compile-time' (Ch. 18 §18.2), `__builtin_constant_p` + `[[gnu::error]]`/`__attribute__((error))` to fail the build when an expected constant did not fold, `[[assume]]`/`__builtin_unreachable` as optimizer contracts (Appendix H.3) and verifying them in the asm (Ch. 4) // The resource contract: `-Wframe-larger-than=` on hot TUs, `std::pmr::null_memory_resource` to prove zero allocation in a scope (Ch. 24), debug-build `operator new`/syscall counters that `abort()` on the hot path (ties Ch. 23/38/41), `[[nodiscard]]` on `std::expected` returns (Ch. 20) // C++26 contracts (`pre`/`post`/`contract_assert`) and their semantics modes: where a checked lines), `is_always_lock_free` differences across targets, `-DNDEBUG` silently removing runtime contracts you relied on

--- [sw-engineering #6] Versioning and Evolving Shared-Memory Layouts & IPC Message Schemas Across Independently Deployed Processes
  kind=expand-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 50 (Inter-Process Communication) — grow the two §50.4.2 bullets into a new §50.4.3 'Schema evolution: versioning, compatibility windows and rolling upgrades'; add a short §53.2.5 in Ch. 53 on SBE schemaId/version/blockLength and extension semantics; cross-reference Ch. 73 §73.4.2 (config versioning) and Ch. 74 (process-per-role), and the proposed Release Engineering chapter. | gap=partial | priority=medium | area=design-patterns
  HFT: Process-per-role (Ch. 74) exists so a strategy can be restarted or upgraded while the feed handler and gateway keep the venue session alive — which is only possible if a newer strategy can attach to the older processes' shared-memory rings without silent corruption. Ch. 50 names the hazard in two bullets ('version it … treat layout changes like protocol changes') but never shows how to evolve a layout or roll one process forward during the day.
  SCOPE: Why layouts drift: compiler/flag/ABI differences between separately built processes (Ch. 9, 22 §22.5), field additions, cache-line re-padding (Ch. 33 §33.5 'padding that breaks serialization'); treating every shm slot and header as a wire format with explicit widths, endianness and `static_assert(offsetof)` tables (Ch. 53) // Versioning mechanics: segment-level magic/version header (Ch. 50 §50.4.2) vs per-message version/type byte vs per-field presence; layout fingerprints (hash of a `pahole`/DWARF dump or C++26 static reflection) embedded in the header and checked at attach; failing loudly on mismatch vs negotiating // Evolution rules that keep readers compatible: append-only fields with reserved padding, never reuse/renumber, widen by adding not changing, SBE-style `schemaId`/`version`/`blockLength` and extension fields that old readers skip (Ch. 53 SBE generators), FlatBuffers/Cap'n Proto evolution semantics as comparison, and what each costs in decode latency (Ch. 53 §53.3) // Rolling upgrades in a process-per-role system (Ch. 74): the compatibility window (new writer, old readers and vice versa), dual-version readers, migrating a live segment by publishing a new segment and cutting over (Ch. 73 double-buffering, Ch. 36 reclamation), sequence-number and open-order state continuity across the swap // Testing the contract: golden layout snapshots in CI (`pahole` diff as a gate), round-trip tests old-reader/new-writer and new-reader/old-writer (ties the proposed Testing chapter), fuzzing the attach/validate path (Ch. 72 §72.4.1) // Config schema versioning tie-in (Ch. 73 §73.4.2–73.4.3): recording schema and config version with every decision for replay (Ch. 75), replaying old captures through new schemas // Measure it: decode/attach cost of a versioned-with-extensions slot vs a fixed slot; cutover-induced gap during a live segment migration // Pitfalls: implicit padding differences between GCC/Clang or `-march` builds, `bool`/enum width changes, `#pragma pack` on one side only (Ch. 9), ABI-breaking standard-library types inside shared structs

--- [sw-engineering #7] Release Engineering for Trading Systems: Controlled Deployment, Standby Cutover, Rollback, Canary/Shadow and Venue Conformance
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part XII — after Ch. 74 (Process Topology & The Deterministic State Machine) and before Ch. 75, since it uses Ch. 73's hot reload, Ch. 74's supervisor/warm-restart/sharding and Ch. 46's shadow-traffic gate; Appendix K §K.4 (exchange access) and Appendix J §J.3 (order lifecycle) gain cross-references; Appendix C §C.5's 'pre-trading-day readiness gate' points here. | gap=absent | priority=high | area=other
  HFT: A trading system changes every day and cannot drop a tick while doing so; the book covers in-process hot reload (Ch. 73) and crash recovery (Ch. 74) but says nothing about how a new *binary* reaches production — no standby cutover, no rollback, no canary, no deploy windows, and no venue conformance testing, which every exchange requires before an order-entry change goes live. These are the steps where most real production latency incidents and regulatory findings originate.
  SCOPE: The deploy calendar as a latency decision: what may change intraday (config and strategy `.so` via Ch. 73) vs only in pre-open/post-close/weekend windows (binary, kernel, NIC driver/firmware, BIOS); change freezes around expiries/rebalances/macro events; venue-specific windows and weekend DR test sessions // Blue/green and hot-standby cutover for process-per-role: start the new version beside the old on reserved cores (Ch. 42), attach to the data planes under a compatibility window (Ch. 50 versioning), warm it (Ch. 46), run it in shadow (live feed in, orders suppressed via the dry-run gate of Ch. 46 §46.4.1) and diff its decisions against the live process using determinism (Ch. 74), then hand over the role atomically // The hard part — order-gateway/session handover: FIX/OUCH session ownership, sequence-number continuity, open-order and position state transfer via shared memory (Ch. 50), cancel-on-disconnect semantics, keeping the venue session up while the process behind it changes (Appendix J.3, K.4) // Rollback as a first-class, rehearsed path: keep N-1 warm, define triggers (per-stage p99.9 from Ch. 76 monitoring, reject/gap rates, P&L guards), roll back config vs binary vs shm layout version separately, measure rollback time as a distribution (Ch. 74 §74.3 method) // Canary and staged rollout across shards (Ch. 74 §74.4.5): one symbol/venue shard first with a control shard on the mirrored feed, compare distributions not means (Ch. 3), widen on green; --- [sw-engineering #8] Fleet Configuration as Code & Drift Detection for Tuned Hosts (the pre-trading-day readiness gate)
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Appendix C (System Tuning Checklist) — new §C.6 'Keeping the fleet converged: configuration as code, drift detection and the readiness gate', between the current C.5 verification pass and the references; add a pointer from Ch. 6 §6.4.3 and Ch. 76 §76.5 (test/prod drift).   HFT: Appendix C itself says the most common 'why is it still jittery' is a setting that was meant to be applied but was not, and Ch. 76 names test/production drift as the cardinal pitfall — yet the book gives no mechanism for declaring a tuned host, converging a fleet to it, or detecting that a kernel update, BIOS flash or re-enabled daemon silently undid the tuning before the open. For a colo fleet of identical boxes, configuration-as-code plus a readiness gate is the OS-level health check that keeps the measured latency true.
  SCOPE: Declaring Appendix C as code: the kernel cmdline, sysctls, IRQ affinities, `tuned` custom profile, hugepage reservation, THP mode, governor, cgroup/cpuset layout and NIC `ethtool -G/-C/-K` settings as an Ansible/Salt role or a systemd unit tree checked into the application repo and versioned with the release (Ch. 76 §76.5 'same tuning') // Firmware and microcode as inventory: capturing and pinning BIOS settings (Redfish/`racadm`/`ipmitool`), BIOS and BMC versions (`dmidecode`), CPU microcode revision (`/proc/cpuinfo`, `intel-microcode` package pinning — a microcode update can change mitigation/predictor behavior and latency, Ch. 6), NIC firmware/driver versions (`ethtool -i`), kernel package pinning; treating any of these changing as a performance event (Ch. 22/76) // Drift detection: a readiness probe that diffs live state (`/proc/cmdline`, `/sys/devices/system/cpu/isolated`, `cpupower idle-info`/`frequency-info`, `/proc/irq/*/smp_affinity`, `/proc/meminfo` HugePages_*, `/sys/kernel/mm/transparent_hugepage/enabled`, `/sys/devices/system/cpu/vulnerabilities/*`, `ethtool -c/-g/-k`, `numactl -H`, PTP lock state — Ch. 58) against the declared manifest and against a golden host, run before every session and after every reboot (extends C.5 'verify the settings actually took') // Fleet benchmark fingerprinting: run the Appendix L harness and `cyclictest` on every box nightly, compare distributions across hosts (Ch. 3 statistics) to find the outlier host before the open; store per-host baselines // Immutable vs mutable hosts: golden images (PXE/kickstart) with the tuning baked in vs converging mutable hosts; reboots as the only sanctioned way to change boot-time parameters; rollback of a tuning change // Noisy-agent control as code: the allowlist of daemons and their cpusets (C.3 'turn off / pin background services') enforced declaratively, with the probe failing on any process found on an isolated core (`ps -eLo psr`) // Measure it: inject a single drift (e.g. `irqbalance` re-enabled, THP back to `always`, governor reset) and show the probe catches it and `cyclictest`/tick-to-trade tails degrade; time to converge a fresh host from image + role

--- [sw-engineering #9] Resilience Verification: Fault Injection, Game Days & Latency-Incident Postmortems
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part XII — after the proposed Release Engineering chapter (i.e. after Ch. 74 and before Ch. 75), or, if a smaller footprint is preferred, as new §74.4.6 'Fault injection and game days' in Ch. 74 plus new §76.4.4 'Latency-incident postmortems' in Ch. 76; cross-reference Ch. 54 (gap recovery), Ch. 58 (PTP holdover), Ch. 5 (production tracing), Ch. 1 §1.4.3 (jitter table). | gap=scattered | priority=medium | area=other

  SCOPE: A fault catalogue for the trading stack with the chapter that owns each: process crash/hang/slow (Ch. 74), feed gap/dup/reorder/corrupt and A-line loss (Ch. 53–54), NIC link flap and switch port events (Ch. 55, 60), PTP grandmaster loss/holdover and clock steps (Ch. 58), capture disk full/slow (Ch. 75), hostile shm writer (Ch. 50, 72), venue reject storms/throttles/cancel-on-disconnect (Appendix J.3, K.4), kernel OOM, THP compaction storm (Ch. 15), SMI (Ch. 6), a noisy neighbor on an isolated core (Ch. 44–45) // Injection mechanisms: `tc netem`/`iptables` on a test NIC, `ethtool`/`ip link` down, `kill -STOP/-SEGV`, seccomp SIGSYS traps, `dm-delay`/FUSE for storage stalls, killing `ptp4l`/stepping the PHC, `stress-ng` on a sibling, a hostile writer process, and the deterministic simulator's fault schedule (proposed Testing chapter) — each with its observability signature (Ch. 76 §76.2 counters, Ch. 61 eBPF) // Asserting the right outcome: safe-state/kill-switch reached (Ch. 74 §74.4.4), recovery tail measured detect → restart → warm → live (Ch. 74 §74.3), no torn state (Ch. 73 tear test), gap recovery off the hot path (Ch. 54), no survivor stall (Ch. 74 §74.4.5) — as repeatable, scripted tests in CI and on the staging fleet // Game days and venue DR tests: scheduled drills in a shadow environment, weekend exchange disaster-recovery sessions, failing a whole box or site (Appendix K.6 path diversity), measuring human + system recovery time, turning each drill into runbook entries and new automated checks // Latency-incident postmortems: blameless process; reconstructing the timeline from capture (Ch. 75) + PTP hardware timestamps (Ch. 58) + per-stage histograms (Ch. 76) + eBPF traces (Ch. 61) + `hwlat`/`/proc/interrupts` (Appendix C); a root-cause taxonomy with signatures (extending Ch. 1 §1.4.3's jitter table: SMI, THP, cron, IRQ migration, NIC firmware, microcode, turbo, compiler bump); ######## LENS hft-domain — 15 proposals ########
SUMMARY: HFT-practitioner lens: the book is exceptionally deep on the *platform* under a tick-to-trade system (microarchitecture, OS isolation, NIC/fabric/bypass, concurrency, capture/replay) and on the *inbound* half of the domain (decoding, A/B arbitration, recovery, the LOB), but the *outbound* and *operational* halves of a trading system are named as boxes and never built. Three absences would stop an expert reader cold: (1) the order-entry gateway and session layer — FIX/SoupBinTCP/FIXP session mechanics, sequence persistence, encode fast path, throttles, cancel-on-disconnect, order state machine — are absent, and Ch. 38, 46 and 51 all cross-reference 'FIX session state machines / FIX heartbeats (Ch. 53)' although Ch. 53 covers decoding only; (2) pre-trade risk and position keeping are budgeted at ~40 ns in Ch. 76 and listed as a role in Ch. 74/J.6 but never implemented (0 hits for fat-finger, duplicate-order, STP beyond one sentence, position keeping, P&L); (3) trading-specific health monitoring (feed staleness vs quiet market, crossed books, session heartbeat misses, reject/order-to-trade anomalies, position reconciliation, PTP drift) exists only as scattered counters in Ch. 53/54/58/74/76 with no model or automated safe-state. Second tier (medium): the sequencer/total-order architecture and hot-standby replication (one line in J.6, one Aeron Cluster bullet), engineering of signal computation (O(1) incremental statistics, book features — alpha is declared out of scope but its latency engineering is not covered), quoting/market-making mechanics including options chains (0 hits for mass quote, requote, vol surface), multi-venue normalization/symbology/SOR (one bullet in 25.4.4), an exchange simulator + matching-engine case study (Ch. 76.4.3 is 227 words), hot-path timer management (0 hits for timer wheel/timerfd), market-data conflation for slow consumers (only the verb 'conflates'), columnar tick storage (0 hits for kdb/Parquet/Arrow), Python<->C++ research-to-production workflow (B.5 has two bullets), and crypto/web-API venues (every 'TLS' hit is thread-local storage). Low: regulation-to-engineering map (RTS 6, CAT absent) and venue feed anatomy (MoldUDP64/SoupBinTCP/MDP channels/EOBI/PITCH/SIP). Structurally, proposals 1-2 and 5-8 would sit most naturally in a new Part 'Trading-System Components' between current Parts XI and XII (so the capstone remains last); fallback placements inside Part XII are given per proposal. Consistency/accuracy findings recorded while reading (not proposals): README legend says 'seven appendices (A–G)' while twelve (A–L) exist and Ch. 76's closing paragraph likewise lists A–G; Ch. 1 says '64 chapters' and Ch. 76 'Sixty-three chapters' (76 exist); pre-reorg chapter numbers survive in Ch. 76's §76.2 diagram (Ch.16/50, Ch.51, Ch.62-63, Ch.63, §64.4.1, §64.4.3), Ch. 54's §54.2 diagram ('Ch.69' for the TCP chapter), Ch. 53's code comments and results block ('Ch.20,27', 'Ch.27', '§48.2.2', '§48.5'), Appendix J ('Ch. 34's seqlock' should be 35; 'Ch. 56, Appendix K.5' for PTP should be 58; 'Ch. 53, 60'/'Ch. 72, 60' for untrusted input — 60 is the fabric chapter) and Appendix K ('Ch. 56' for PTP should be 58, 'Ch. 68' for FPGA should be 69, throughout).

--- [hft-domain #1] The Order-Entry Gateway & Session Layer: FIX/Binary Sessions, Sequence Recovery, Encode Fast Path and Throttles
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part XII, new chapter immediately after Ch. 74 (Process Topology — which defines the gateway as the process owning the venue session) and before Ch. 75; or first chapter of a proposed new Part 'Trading-System Components' inserted between Part XI (after Ch. 70) and Part XII. Prerequisites: Ch. 51–52 (TCP), 53 (encodings), 62.4.2 (userspace TCP), 74, 75; leads into the pre-trade-risk chapter and Ch. 76. | gap=scattered | priority=high | area=other
  HFT: The gateway is the last software hop before the wire and the one process that must never lose a sequence number or orphan an order; its encode latency, throttle behaviour and ack round-trip sit directly on the tick-to-trade budget. The book treats the gateway as a box that owns 'the venue session, sequence numbers, working-order state' (Ch. 74) without ever building it, and three chapters point FIX session mechanics at Ch. 53, which covers only decoding.
  SCOPE: Session protocols as state machines: FIX session layer (Logon/Logout, Heartbeat/TestRequest, MsgSeqNum, ResendRequest/SequenceReset-GapFill, PossDupFlag) vs binary sessions (SoupBinTCP under OUCH, Cboe BOE, CME iLink 3 / FIXP negotiation, sequence and retransmit); what a 'FIX engine' is and why general-purpose engines allocate and parse on the hot path // Sequence-number persistence and recovery: journaling inbound/outbound sequence numbers (ties Ch. 75) so a crash-only gateway restart (Ch. 74.2.4) resynchronizes without duplicate or orphaned orders; rebuilding working-order state from venue replay and drop-copy // The encode fast path: pre-built per-order-type message templates with only variable fields patched (price/qty/ClOrdID/sending time), `std::to_chars` and digit-pair tables for FIX numerics (Ch. 53.4.1), incremental BodyLength/CheckSum, SBE/OUCH struct fill; verified in asm (Ch. 4) as a handful of stores // Order identity and lookup: ClOrdID generation schemes, dense order tables indexed by local id, flat orderID→slot map (Ch. 25.4.1) for O(1) ack/fill/cancel-reject matching // The order state machine (J.3) under real venue behaviour: fill-before-ack, unsolicited cancels, cancel-reject races, cancel-replace chains, busts/trade corrections, 'cancel in flight' semantics; keeping it a pure replayable core (Ch. 74.2.2) // Throttles and rate limits: venue messages-per-second limits (Appendix K.4), a TSC-based token-bucket limiter on the hot path, prioritizing cancels over new orders under throttle, mass-cancel/purge-port semantics // Liveness and failover: cancel-on-disconnect, session heartbeats vs TCP keepalive (Ch. 51/52), connection warming (Ch. 46.4.2), reconnect to a secondary gateway port (Appendix K.6) with sequence re-sync; measured failover tail (Ch. 74.3) // Measure it: order→ack round-trip distribution with NIC hardware timestamps (Ch. 58), encode latency, gateway hop cost (shm ring → TCP send, Ch. 50/62), kernel TCP vs Onload/userspace stack for the session (Ch. 62.4.2), throttle-induced queueing // Venue certification/conformance testing, test symbols, and the venue simulator (ties the exchange-simulator proposal)
  EXISTING: 74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.2.1, §74.4.4: Gateway named as the role owning the venue session; cancel-on-disconnect/kill-switch in one bullet; no session or encode mechanics.; appendix-J-market-structure-primer.md J.3, J.5, J.6: Order lifecycle, protocol quick reference and roles — orientation only, no engineering.; 53-zero-copy-wire-market-data-decoding.md §53.2.1, §53.4.1: OUCH/FIX/SBE described from the decode side; `to_chars` for outbound formatting is one bullet.; 51-socket-optimization-tcp-tuning.md §51.4.1, §51.5: TCP_NODELAY/slow-start for order entry; 'FIX heartbeats — Ch. 53' cross-reference to coverage that does not exist.; 38-coroutines-async-models.md §38.4.1 (line 123): 'FIX session state machines (Ch. 53)' as a coroutine use case — again pointing at non-existent coverage.; 62-kernel-bypass-userspace-networking.md §62.4.2: Userspace TCP stack for order entry — transport only.; appendix-K-exchange-connectivity.md K.4, K.6: Ports, throttles, redundant gateways — orientation.; appendix-F-glossary.md F.1: Defines Order gateway, Drop-copy, OUCH with pointers to Ch. 74/53 where they are not developed.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il / -n -i over chapters/*.md: 'FIX engine' → 0 files; 'logon|logout' → 0; 'TestRequest|test request|SequenceReset|sequence reset|PossDup' → 0; 'MsgSeqNum|session sequence' → 0 (the 3 hits for 'sequence.*(session|login)' in 72/74/75 are about replay); 'resend ?request|gap ?fill' → only 53/54 (market-data retransmission); 'cancel.on.disconnect' → 74 only (2 hits); 'ClOrdID|client order id|order id' → 25 once (orderID map), 10 incidental; 'encod(e|ing) (the )?order|order encod' → 01 and 76, one phrase each; 'BOE|Binary Order Entry' → 53 once (SBE spelled out); 'token bucket|leaky bucket|rate.limit' → hits are NAK rate-limiting (54), pacing (59), DDIO (56); 'certif|conformance' → 0; 'order.*ack|ack latency' hits (55/62/52/51) are TCP ACKs/RDMA completions, not venue acks; 'heartbeat' → 74 (supervisor), 46/51 (keepalive, citing Ch. 53), 45, 73, K.

--- [hft-domain #2] Pre-Trade Risk & Position Keeping on the Hot Path
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part XII, directly after the proposed Order-Entry Gateway chapter (between current Ch. 74 and Ch. 75), or second chapter of the proposed 'Trading-System Components' Part. Builds on Ch. 27 (scaled integers, tick tables), 72.4.3 (checked arithmetic), 35/73 (publishing limits lock-free), 74 (risk as a separate fault domain, kill-switch), 69 (risk in fabric); feeds Ch. 76's ~40 ns budget row. | gap=scattered | priority=high | area=other
  HFT: Every order pays the risk check, it is legally mandatory, and it is the one stage that cannot be optimized away — so it must be engineered to tens of nanoseconds with the same discipline as the decoder. Ch. 76 budgets '~40 ns' for it and Ch. 74/J.6 list it as a role, but the book never shows the data structures, arithmetic or position bookkeeping that achieve that number.
  SCOPE: The regulatory floor as a check list: SEC 15c3-5 controls (max order size/notional, price collars vs reference price, credit/capital thresholds, duplicate-order detection, restricted symbols) and MiFID II RTS 6 pre-trade controls, mapped to concrete per-order checks // Data layout for a ~40 ns check: per-instrument and per-account limit records as dense arrays indexed by instrument id, sized to a cache line (Ch. 9), hot/cold split (Ch. 8), limits published via seqlock/RCU and hot-reloaded (Ch. 35/36/73) // Arithmetic: notional in `__int128` or `__builtin_mul_overflow` (Ch. 27.4.1, 72.4.3), collar comparison against last trade/NBBO in scaled integers, branchless accumulation of pass/fail bits (Ch. 13/28) so the common pass path is a few predicted branches // Position keeping: applying acks, partial fills, cancels and busts; counting in-flight (pending) exposure at send time rather than fill time; netting across strategies; mark-to-market P&L in scaled integers; publishing position snapshots to strategy and monitoring (Ch. 35) // Self-trade prevention and wash-trade checks across strategies sharing a symbol; order-to-trade ratio and message-rate limits treated as risk controls (ties gateway throttles) // Where the check runs and the trust model: inline in the strategy process vs a separate risk process (IPC hop cost, Ch. 50/74) vs on FPGA/SmartNIC (Ch. 69/70); why no strategy may bypass it // Kill-switch triggers and safe-state: loss limits, position breach, stale data (Ch. 54.4.3), heartbeat loss; automatic mass-cancel through the surviving gateway (Ch. 74.4.4); staged responses (pause instrument → pull quotes → flatten) // Measure it: per-check nanoseconds and tail, cost of the risk hop, deterministic replay of risk decisions through the Ch. 74 core, fault injection with fat-finger orders and malformed fills (Ch. 72)
  EXISTING: 76-production-profiling-end-to-end-case-study.md §76.3 table: 'Pre-trade risk | limit/position/kill-switch check | ~40 ns / ~120 ns' — a number with no implementation.; 72-secure-programming-low-latency.md §72.4.3: Checked arithmetic on notional/position/exposure 'cheap enough for the pre-trade risk check (Ch. 69)'.; 27-fixed-point-floating-point-arithmetic.md §27.2.1, §27.4.1, §27.6: Scaled-integer prices/notional, `__int128` intermediates, tick-size bands.; 74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.2.1, §74.4.4, §74.7: Risk as a separate process; kill-switch in the gateway; 15c3-5 as a reference.; 69-fpga-acceleration.md §69.4.1, §69.4.3: Fast pre-trade risk check in fabric as a hardware kill-switch.; appendix-J-market-structure-primer.md J.3, J.6: STP and pre-trade risk in one sentence each.; appendix-K-exchange-connectivity.md K.4: 15c3-5 mandates (max size, collars, position limits) as orientation.; 35-seqlocks-single-writer-publication.md §35.1: 'the current position' listed as a seqlock-published snapshot.

--- [hft-domain #3] Trading-System Health, Safety Monitoring & Automated Safe-State
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part XII, after Ch. 71 (Zero-Overhead Logging) and before Ch. 72, or immediately before Ch. 76 so the capstone's 'continuous monitoring' model has a chapter to cite. Builds on Ch. 3 (HdrHistogram), 17 (TSC), 33 (padded per-core counters), 50 (shm), 53–55 (drop counters), 58 (PTP offset), 61 (eBPF), 71, 74 (supervisor/kill-switch). | gap=scattered | priority=high | area=health-monitoring-app
  HFT: A system that is fast but unaware it is blind, stale, throttled or out of sync loses money faster than a slow one; the health signals that matter (gaps, crossed books, PTP drift, reject spikes, position drift) are domain-specific and must be collected without touching the hot path. The book has the ingredients scattered — drop counters, supervisor heartbeats, PTP offset, per-stage histograms — but no chapter assembles them into a health model with automated safe-state responses.
  SCOPE: A layered health model for a trading system: box (C-states/isolation intact, IRQ leakage, THP compaction — Ch. 6/42/45), NIC/fabric (rx_missed, rx_no_buffer, PFC pauses, switch drops — Ch. 53/55/60/64), clock (PTP offset, servo state, holdover — Ch. 58), feed (per-line and post-A/B gap rate, reorder depth, snapshot cycles — Ch. 53/54), book (crossed/locked book, stale BBO, impossible levels), session (heartbeat/TestRequest misses, sequence mismatch, reject rate, throttle hits), order flow (order-to-trade ratio, cancel latency, in-flight count, runaway-rate detection), positions (vs drop-copy reconciliation), process (Ch. 74 heartbeats/restarts), per-stage latency SLOs (Ch. 76) // Feed staleness vs a quiet market: exchange heartbeat/idle messages, cross-feed and cross-venue comparison, per-instrument staleness deadlines (ties the timer proposal) — telling 'no data' from 'no trades' without false alarms // Zero-cost hot-path instrumentation: per-core cache-line-padded counters (Ch. 33), TSC-stamped last-event times (Ch. 17), HdrHistogram recording into pre-allocated memory (Ch. 3), all exposed through a shared-memory stats segment (Ch. 26/50) read by a monitoring process on a housekeeping core — no syscalls, no formatting (Ch. 71) // Export and alerting off the hot core: the monitor process scraping shm → Prometheus/StatsD/OpenTelemetry; what must never run on isolated cores (agents, exporters, perf daemons); eBPF probes for OS/network health (Ch. 61) and their overhead budget // Automated machine-speed responses with hysteresis: thresholds → pause instrument (Ch. 54.4.3) → pull quotes → mass-cancel/safe-state (Ch. 74.4.4); operator dashboards for humans vs the kill-switch for machines; avoiding flapping and alert storms at the open // Measure it: tail cost of instrumentation on vs off; detection latency for an induced feed stall, NIC drop, PTP step and session loss; false-positive rate vs threshold and hysteresis // Pitfalls: monitoring that perturbs (observer effect, Ch. 5), averaging away the tail (Ch. 1), a 'healthy' process with a dead feed, counters that false-share, exporters on the hot core
  EXISTING: 76-production-profiling-end-to-end-case-study.md §76.2 diagram, §76.4.1: One diagram line 'counters: drops, gaps, queue depths, restarts'; trend dashboard for latency regression only.; 74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.4.3, §74.4.4: Supervisor heartbeats (catch hangs), restart policy, kill-switch — process liveness only.; 53-zero-copy-wire-market-data-decoding.md §53.2.4, §53.4.4: Detect drops at NIC/socket/app layers; 'instrument and alert'.; 54-reliable-multicast-feed-recovery.md §54.3: Gap-rate measurement and recovery latency.; 58-clock-synchronization-ptp.md §58.3, §58.5: PTP offset/jitter measurement and holdover.; 61-ebpf-bpftrace-xdp.md §61.4.1: Low-overhead production tracing — tools, not a health model.; appendix-I-tooling-cookbook.md I.8: ethtool/ss/tcpdump one-liners.

--- [hft-domain #4] The Sequencer Architecture & Hot-Standby Replication
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part XII, immediately after Ch. 74 (Process Topology) as its architectural sibling, before the proposed gateway chapter; prerequisites Ch. 34/37 (rings, Disruptor), 50/51 (shm, multicast), 58 (hardware timestamps), 65 (Aeron/Aeron Cluster), 74, 75. | gap=partial | priority=medium | area=design-patterns
  HFT: The sequencer / total-ordered event bus is the dominant architecture in exchanges and many HFT shops because determinism, replay and hot-standby all fall out of one decision, at the price of one extra hop that must be measured. The book introduces the deterministic core and a point-to-point shared-memory topology, but mentions the sequencer in one line of Appendix J and Aeron Cluster in one bullet, with no treatment of replication or gateway failover.
  SCOPE: The sequencer pattern: every input (ticks, order requests, fills, timers, config) passes through one sequencer that assigns a global sequence and republishes a total-ordered stream (shm ring in-box — Ch. 50; UDP multicast/Aeron across boxes — Ch. 51/65); all components are deterministic consumers of that one stream — contrasted with Ch. 74's point-to-point ring topology // What total order buys: one capture log is the whole system's truth (Ch. 75), replay is trivial, hot-standby replicas hold identical state by construction, causality (which tick caused which order) is auditable // What it costs: the extra hop (shm ~100 ns vs multicast µs), the single-writer throughput ceiling, batching-vs-latency at the sequencer (Ch. 37.4.2), slow-consumer policy (ties the conflation proposal); measured tick-to-trade with and without the sequencer // Hot-standby strategies and gateways: replicas consuming the same sequenced stream, per-sequence state hashes to detect divergence, failover protocol (who owns the venue session, sequence-number handover, cancel-on-disconnect interplay), fencing tokens against split-brain; failover tail measured as in Ch. 74.3 // Sequencer implementation: single pinned thread (Ch. 42), lock-free MPSC ingress (Ch. 34), hardware-timestamp ordering of multi-NIC inputs (Ch. 58), in-line journaling (Ch. 75) so the sequence log is the capture, sequencer failover/election // Consensus where durability demands it: Raft/Aeron Cluster replicated state machines (Ch. 65) vs leaderless sequencer+journal; when consensus latency is acceptable (off the order path) and when it is not // Pitfalls: non-deterministic consumers silently diverging from replicas, the sequencer as single point of failure, assuming cross-box clock agreement, re-coupling fault domains through the shared stream
  EXISTING: appendix-J-market-structure-primer.md J.6: 'Sequencer — in some designs, a single process that stamps a total order on events' — one bullet.; 74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.2, §74.3, §74.4.1: Process-per-role with point-to-point shm rings; failover measured as restart time, no replication.; 65-transport-beyond-tcp.md §65.4.1: Aeron Cluster 'Raft-based fault-tolerant replicated state machines' — one bullet.; 37-the-disruptor-pattern.md §37.2: In-process total order via sequences; LMAX lineage.; appendix-K-exchange-connectivity.md K.6: Redundant gateways that 'reconnect and resynchronize' — one line.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il / -n -i: 'sequencer' → appendix-J only (1 line); 'total order|totally ordered|global order' → 30 (memory model), 65 (a pitfall about transports), J; 'replicated state machine|state.machine replication' → 65 once; 'hot.standby|standby|failover|active.passive|primary.*secondary' → 74 (restart framing, 2), K (2), 76 (2, failover as a tested condition), 65 (1) — no replication mechanics; 'event.sourc' → 74/75/76/G as literature pointers; 'leader|follower|election' → no HA hits (27/19/14 are unrelated words).

--- [hft-domain #5] Strategy Engine & Incremental Signal Computation
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part V (Numerics & Data Parallelism) as a new chapter after Ch. 29, or in the proposed 'Trading-System Components' Part after Pre-Trade Risk.   HFT: The decision stage is where Ch. 76's p99.9 lives, and its cost is set by how features are updated — recomputing a window on every tick versus O(1) incremental updates is the difference between hundreds of nanoseconds and tens. The book declares alpha out of scope but never covers the engineering of signal computation, which is pure latency craft, and gives no guidance on structuring a strategy framework.

  EXISTING: appendix-J-market-structure-primer.md J.6: 'Strategy / signal — the alpha logic — outside this book's scope'.; 19-template-metaprogramming-zero-cost.md §19.4.2: Expression templates for 'moving-window computations' — one sentence.; 27-fixed-point-floating-point-arithmetic.md §27.2.3, §27.6: EWMA as a denormal example; Kahan summation in one pitfall bullet.; 29-simd-vectorization.md §29.1: 'computing a vector of signals or P&L across many instruments' as a SIMD example.; 73-hot-reload-live-reconfiguration.md §73.4.1, §73.4.4: Strategy parameter publication and dynamic-library strategy reload.; 74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.2.2: The pure core pattern the strategy should follow.
