--- [hft-domain #9] Hot-Path Timer Management: Deadlines, Timer Wheels and Time as Input
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 74 (Process Topology & The Deterministic State Machine), new §74.4.6 after 'Core dumps … sharding', with a Measure-it addition to §74.3; alternatively Ch. 41 §41.4.3 beside 'Busy-poll vs block'. Cross-link from Ch. 17 (TSC), 47 (epoll timeouts) and the gateway/health proposals. | gap=absent | priority=medium | area=design-patterns
  HFT: Every order has a deadline and every session has a heartbeat, so a trading core maintains thousands of timers and must check them at nanosecond cost without a syscall or a clock read inside the deterministic core. The book treats time only as a measurement instrument (Ch. 17) and as 'an input' (Ch. 74); timer data structures, deadline checking in the poll loop and `timerfd` are never covered.
  SCOPE: The timer workload of a trading system: order/quote TTLs, cancel-if-no-ack, session heartbeat/TestRequest intervals, feed-staleness deadlines, throttle windows, periodic snapshots and stats — thousands of armed timers checked at nanosecond cost with no syscall // Data structures: hierarchical timing wheel vs binary/4-ary heap vs sorted intrusive list; O(1) insert/cancel for TTL-style timers, wheel cascading cost, pooled timer nodes (Ch. 24) and their layout (Ch. 8) // Driving timers from the busy-poll loop: TSC deadline checks (Ch. 17) every iteration or every N iterations, the cost of the check itself, coalescing expirations into batches (Ch. 37.4.2) // Timers as deterministic inputs: expirations become sequenced events (Ch. 74.2.2) so replay reproduces them; virtual time for simulation and replay (exchange-simulator proposal); the 'no clock read inside the core' rule in practice // Kernel timers where blocking is acceptable: `timerfd` in epoll/io_uring loops (Ch. 47/48), timer slack (`prctl(PR_SET_TIMERSLACK)`), `nohz_full` interactions (Ch. 45), `clock_nanosleep` vs spin-until-deadline (Ch. 32/41) // Measure it: per-iteration timer-check overhead, expiry latency/jitter distribution for 10k armed timers (wheel vs heap), `timerfd` wake latency vs spinning // Pitfalls: `system_clock` on the hot path, cascade spikes at wheel boundaries, TTL cancel storms, TSC-vs-wall-clock drift for venue-facing deadlines
  EXISTING: 74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.2.2: 'each timer fire' listed as an input event; time must be an input, never read in the core — no mechanism.; 17-timekeeping-tsc-rdtsc-clocks.md §17.2–17.4: Clock sources and timestamp cost — measurement, not scheduling.; 47-linux-native-io.md §47.4.1: epoll_wait timeouts (4 hits) — no timer facility.; 41-context-switching-mitigation.md §41.2, §41.4: Kernel timer tick/IRQ as noise to remove.; 53-zero-copy-wire-market-data-decoding.md §53.4.3: Reorder-window timeout — needs exactly this facility.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il / -n -i: 'timer wheel|timing wheel|hierarchical timer' → 0; 'timerfd' → 0; 'timer (heap|list|queue|management|facility|subsystem)|application.level timer|software timer' → 0; 'deadline' → 59 (hardware LAUNCHTIME), 45, 01 only; 'timeout' → 53 (reorder window), 47 (epoll), 74 (heartbeat), 46, 48, 32; 'timer' (word) → 45/42/41 (timer IRQ/tick), 17 (measurement overhead), 74 (timer as input), 75/76 (timer events captured).

--- [hft-domain #10] Market-Data Conflation & Fan-Out to Slow Consumers
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 35 (Seqlocks & Single-Writer Publication), new §35.4.3 'Conflated / latest-value publication for slower consumers', plus a Measure-it addition to §35.3; cross-link from Ch. 37.4.2 (batching), 54.5 (slow consumer) and 71.6 (ring-full policy). | gap=absent | priority=medium | area=design-patterns
  HFT: At the open a feed produces far more updates than a risk engine, GUI or downstream strategy can consume, and the standard answer is conflation — publish the latest state per instrument and let slow readers skip intermediate values — so that no slow consumer ever backpressures the hot path. The book covers single-writer snapshots and consumer batching but never the conflation structures or catch-up policies that make high-throughput fan-out safe.
  SCOPE: Two consumer classes: must-see-every-delta (book builder, Ch. 25) vs latest-state-is-enough (strategies away from the touch, risk, UI, loggers, downstream systems); why one ring size cannot serve both (Ch. 34/37 backpressure) // Conflation structures: per-instrument latest-value slots (seqlock) plus a dirty bitmap (Ch. 28) and a deduplicating dirty queue so a slow reader handles each instrument once per catch-up; last-write-wins vs merged semantics (summed volumes, counted trades) // Catch-up policies when a consumer falls behind: latest-only, drop-and-count, snapshot-and-resubscribe; bounded memory; never blocking the producer (Ch. 71.6) // Fan-out topologies: one writer → N readers via shm (Ch. 50) vs multicast/Aeron (Ch. 51/65) vs layer-1 replication (Ch. 60); coherence effects of many readers on the writer's lines (Ch. 33) // Throughput engineering: conflation ratios at the open (Ch. 53 microburst), staleness of the conflated view (ns since last update) as a health metric (health proposal) // Measure it: publish cost with 1/10/100 readers; staleness distribution of a conflated reader under burst vs overflow of a full-delta queue; dirty-set scan cost // Pitfalls: conflating deltas that must not be merged (order adds/deletes), losing trade prints, unbounded dirty queues, slow readers head-of-line blocking fast ones
  EXISTING: 35-seqlocks-single-writer-publication.md §35.1–35.2: Seqlock for one writer / many readers of a BBO snapshot — the building block, no conflation.; 37-the-disruptor-pattern.md §37.4.2, §37.5: Consumer batching and wrap protection as natural backpressure.; 71-zero-overhead-logging.md §71.6: Drop vs overwrite-oldest policy for a full log ring.; 54-reliable-multicast-feed-recovery.md §54.5: Slow consumer self-inflicting gaps.; 60-network-fabric-switching.md §60.4.1: Layer-1 fan-out replication (physical).
  EVIDENCE: grep -il / -n -i: 'conflat' → 11 and 23 only, both the verb 'conflates' (unrelated); 'slow (reader|consumer|subscriber)' → 54 (2, as a cause of gaps); 'latest.value|last.value|snapshot cache|value cache' → 35 once (phrase), 63 unrelated; 'fan.out|fanout' → 60 (layer-1), 65 (transport), 51 (multicast), 37 (parallel consumers) — transport-level only; 'load shed|coalesc' → NIC interrupt coalescing (55), GRO (52), NAK coalescing (54) — nothing about data conflation.

######## LENS os-kernel-depth — 14 proposals ########
SUMMARY: LENS: operating-system and kernel depth (Part VII isolation/scheduling, Part VIII kernel I/O, Appendix C). Method: read chapters/README.md in full, then grepped ~300 distinctive terms across /home/user/ClaudeTeaches/chapters/*.md (grep -il and grep -n -i), reading every matching passage in Ch. 5, 6, 15, 16, 17, 23, 26, 41, 42, 44, 45, 61, 62, 66, 72, 74, 75, 76 and Appendices C and I before grading a gap.

WHAT THE BOOK DOES WELL FROM THIS LENS: the isolate-pin-tickless-RT recipe (Ch. 41–46, App C) is coherent and measured; mlock/pre-fault discipline (Ch. 23/26), clocksource/vDSO (Ch. 17), RDT/MBA (Ch. 44), eBPF/bpftrace (Ch. 61), seccomp-at-the-boundary (Ch. 72) and process supervision/core dumps (Ch. 74) are all genuinely covered. Candidates I dropped as already covered: Intel MBA (Ch. 44 §44.4.2 is thorough), rcu_nocbs/nohz_full basics, numa_balancing (Ch. 16 §16.5), THP-vs-explicit (Ch. 15), mlockall/RLIMIT_MEMLOCK (Ch. 26), io_uring polling (Ch. 48).

THE GAPS (14 proposals, 4 high): (1) the whole "how a sleeping thread wakes" story is absent — hrtimers, timer slack, clock_nanosleep/TIMER_ABSTIME, timerfd all have zero hits, yet every non-busy-poll thread (heartbeats, scheduled sends, re-warm loops, supervisor) depends on it and cyclictest itself measures it; (2) a systematic catalog of residual kernel noise sources on an isolated core (kthreads/workqueues/ksoftirqd, RCU expedited IPIs, NMI/softlockup watchdogs, khugepaged/kcompactd/kswapd/KSM, vmstat, writeback, clocksource watchdog, self-inflicted TLB shootdowns) exists only as scattered one-liners in Ch. 45 §45.2/§45.5 and App C.2/C.3; (3) kernel latency tracing — ftrace is name-dropped in ten files but never taught; trace-cmd, KernelShark, rtla/osnoise/timerlat, perf sched, perf trace have zero hits; (4) continuous OS/network health monitoring (per-thread ctxt-switch and fault counters, /proc/softirqs, softnet_stat, C-state residency, EDAC/MCE, PTP offset time series, readiness gate) is absent — Ch. 76 monitors application latency only and Ch. 42/53/55 use the relevant counters as one-off checks. Medium: scheduler internals (EEVDF, SCHED_DEADLINE depth, PREEMPT_RT in mainline 6.12 and the should-we-run-RT decision), VMs/containers/Kubernetes (KVM, SR-IOV, cgroup v2 cpu.max — all zero hits), UIO/VFIO userspace-driver mechanics, host lifecycle (boot/kexec/systemd unit directives/rlimits/capabilities/kernel & livepatch choice), advanced mmap tricks (mremap double-mapped ring, MCL_ONFAULT, MADV_POPULATE/COLLAPSE, userfaultfd, stack pre-fault), PM QoS /dev/cpu_dma_latency, LSM/audit/seccomp-SSBD/lockdown latency cost, storage-path internals for capture. Low: CXL/tiered memory as a NUMA hazard.

CONSISTENCY/ACCURACY FINDINGS (OS-related, found while grepping): (a) 139 cross-references written without a space ("Ch.NN", mostly inside ASCII diagrams and code-comment blocks) were missed by the Phase-3 renumber remap and still carry the OLD chapter numbers — e.g. Ch. 62 §62.2 "KERNEL STACK (Ch.44)" should be Ch. 47; Ch. 45 §45.3 "(Ch.40)" for IRQ routing should be Ch. 42 and "Ch.41" for HT should be Ch. 43; Ch. 76 §76.2 "TSC/PTP, Ch.16/50" should be Ch. 17/58, "capture (Ch.63)" should be Ch. 75, "§64.4.1/§64.4.3" should be §76.4.1/§76.4.3; Ch. 61 §61.3 "(Ch.39!)" should be Ch. 41. A regex pass for `Ch\.[0-9]` (no space) over chapters/[0-9]*.md will list them all. (b) README.md line 5 says "seven appendices (A–G) follow" while the index lists twelve (A–L). (c) Ch. 45 §45.1/§45.2 describes the default scheduler as "CFS"; under the Preface's "recent mainline kernel" assumption that is EEVDF (since 6.6) — the sched_min_granularity-style tuning intuitions differ. (d) Ch. 45 says isolcpus is "semi-deprecated" but App C.2 still presents it as the foundation without mentioning isolcpus=managed_irq or cpuset partitions — the two should agree on the recommended interface.

--- [os-kernel-depth #1] Timers, Sleeping & Wake-Up Latency: hrtimers, Timer Slack, clock_nanosleep and timerfd
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part VII — OS, Scheduling & Isolation, immediately after Ch. 41 (Context Switching & Its Mitigation) and before Ch. 42 (Pinning); Ch. 41's busy-poll-vs-block section becomes its prerequisite. | gap=absent | priority=high | area=other
  HFT: Not every thread busy-polls: the control plane, heartbeat writers, scheduled senders, re-warm loops and the supervisor all sleep and wake, and the default 50 µs timer slack or a tick-aligned expiry quietly turns a 1 ms heartbeat into a 1.2 ms one and a scheduled send into a late one. Today the reader is told to busy-poll (Ch. 41) and to run cyclictest (Ch. 45) but is never given a model of how a sleep becomes a wake-up, so semi-hot threads are left to std::this_thread::sleep_for.
  SCOPE: The Linux timer stack: hrtimers vs the timer wheel, CONFIG_HZ and the tick vs high-resolution expiry, how nanosleep / epoll_wait(timeout) / futex timed waits / std::this_thread::sleep_for become an hrtimer plus a wake-up IPI — and what cyclictest is actually measuring. // Timer slack: the 50 µs default for SCHED_OTHER threads (prctl(PR_SET_TIMERSLACK), /proc/<pid>/timerslack_ns), zero slack under SCHED_FIFO/RR/DEADLINE; measuring the slack-induced tail on a 1 ms housekeeping loop. // Drift-free periodic loops with clock_nanosleep(CLOCK_MONOTONIC, TIMER_ABSTIME) vs relative sleeps; timerfd (TFD_TIMER_ABSTIME, TFD_TIMER_CANCEL_ON_SET) in epoll and io_uring (IORING_OP_TIMEOUT / linked timeouts) event loops. // Spin-then-sleep hybrids for semi-hot threads (sleep until T-x µs, then busy-wait on the TSC) — the pattern behind software-scheduled sends (Ch. 59) and shadow-traffic cadence (Ch. 46). // Wake-up path anatomy: timer interrupt → try_to_wake_up → reschedule IPI to the target core → scheduler → cache-cold resume; kernel.timer_migration; why an armed timer on a nohz_full core brings the tick back. // Clock choice for deadlines: CLOCK_MONOTONIC vs CLOCK_REALTIME vs CLOCK_TAI vs CLOCK_BOOTTIME, PTP-disciplined wall-clock deadlines (Ch. 58), and TSC-based deadline math (Ch. 17). // User-space hierarchical timing wheels for order-timeout and heartbeat bookkeeping with zero syscalls on the hot path (ties Ch. 74 heartbeats, Ch. 53 FIX heartbeats). // Measure it: wake-latency histograms on the reference box for SCHED_OTHER with/without slack, SCHED_FIFO, timerfd-in-epoll, and spin-wait; perf sched timehist and sched:sched_wakeup tracepoints to attribute the gap. // Pitfalls: usleep granularity, sleeping on a nohz_full core, a non-RT heartbeat thread whose wake-up IPIs land on the hot core, std::condition_variable::wait_for slack, timers armed from the hot thread.
  EXISTING: 41-context-switching-mitigation.md §41.2, §41.4.2: Busy-poll vs block, futex ping-pong context-switch measurement; sleeping is only ever 'the thing to avoid'.; 17-timekeeping-tsc-rdtsc-clocks.md §17.2.2: Clocksource/vDSO cost of clock_gettime; one mention of nanosleep; no timer or wake-up mechanics.; 45-realtime-scheduling-kernel-tuning.md §45.3: cyclictest used as the jitter instrument but what it measures (hrtimer expiry → wake) is not explained.; 59-precise-scheduled-transmission.md §59.2–§59.4: Hardware/qdisc launch-time (SO_TXTIME/ETF); no software wait-until-T technique.; 03-microbenchmarking-done-right.md harness code: sleep_until used in the harness without discussion.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il across chapters/*.md: 'hrtimer' 0 files; 'timer slack'/'timer_slack' 0; 'prctl' 0; 'clock_nanosleep' 0; 'timerfd' 0; 'TIMER_ABSTIME' 0; 'timer wheel'/'timing wheel' 0; 'high-resolution timer' 0; 'usleep' 0; 'nanosleep' only 17-timekeeping; 'sleep_for' only 35/36 (test code); 'sleep_until' only 03; 'condition_variable'/'wait_for'/'timed wait' 0; 'CONFIG_HZ' only 45 (named, not explained); 'perf sched' 0. Ch. 59 grep for sleep|spin|wait until|clock_nanosleep returned nothing.

--- [os-kernel-depth #2] The Kernel Noise Catalog: Finding and Silencing Every Residual Interrupt on an Isolated Core
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part VII, immediately after Ch. 45 (Real-Time Scheduling & Kernel Tuning) and before Ch. 46 (Keeping the Hot Path Warm); Appendix C.2/C.3 one-liners link back to it as the chapter that explains 'why'. | gap=scattered | priority=high | area=health-monitoring-os-net
  HFT: After isolcpus/nohz_full/rcu_nocbs the hot core is quiet 'except' for a dozen residual sources — an NMI every 10 s, an expedited-RCU IPI, a TLB shootdown from the logger unmapping a buffer, an NVMe managed IRQ — each a 5–50 µs spike that lands exactly once in a p99.99 that trades. The book names tick/RCU/kworker at a high level and lists knobs in Appendix C, but a reader hunting 'the last spike' has no catalog that maps spike → source → fingerprint → knob.
  SCOPE: A taxonomy of what still runs on an isolcpus/nohz_full core, each with its fingerprint and its knob: per-CPU kthreads (ksoftirqd/N, migration/N, rcuc/N, watchdog/N, kworker/N:*), unbound workqueues (/sys/devices/virtual/workqueue/cpumask), isolcpus=domain,managed_irq,nohz flags and the housekeeping cpumask. // RCU in depth: rcu_nocbs plus rcuo* kthread affinity, rcu_nocb_poll, expedited grace periods and the IPIs they send to every core (rcupdate.rcu_normal=1, rcu_normal_after_boot), and the residual 1 Hz nohz_full tick. // Watchdogs: the perf-NMI hardlockup detector (fires on every core every ~10 s), the softlockup watchdog/N kthreads, kernel.watchdog_cpumask as the surgical alternative to nmi_watchdog=0; the clocksource watchdog (tsc=reliable / tsc=nowatchdog) and 'TSC unstable' fallbacks to HPET (ties Ch. 17). // Memory-management daemons: khugepaged with transparent_hugepage/defrag and khugepaged/defrag modes, kcompactd, kswapd and reclaim watermarks (vm.min_free_kbytes, watermark_scale_factor), KSM (/sys/kernel/mm/ksm/run), numa_balancing (Ch. 16), vm.stat_interval/vmstat_update, dirty-page flusher kworkers (vm.dirty_*). // IPIs you cause yourself: TLB shootdowns from munmap/mprotect/madvise(DONTNEED) by any thread sharing the mm, membarrier, perf_event enable/disable IPIs, vm.drop_caches, cpufreq governor polling, MCE polling (machinecheck/check_interval), smp_call_function from drivers. // Interrupts that ignore smp_affinity: managed MSI-X vectors (NVMe, NICs) that spread across all CPUs unless isolcpus=managed_irq or queue counts are limited (ties Ch. 42, 55, 75); kernel.timer_migration; IRQ affinity hints. // Hardware-level noise the OS cannot see: SMIs (hwlat), thermal throttling, correctable ECC errors (EDAC/rasdaemon), PCIe AER storms. // Measure it: a reproducible 'silence scorecard' for the reference box — per-core /proc/interrupts and /proc/softirqs deltas, perf stat -C <core> -e irq_vectors:*,irq:*,sched:sched_switch, rtla osnoise summary, ps -eLo psr,cls,rtprio,comm for stray residents — before and after each knob. // Pitfalls: nohz_full with two runnable tasks (Ch. 45), isolcpus without managed_irq, cmdline isolcpus vs cgroup v2 cpuset.cpus.partition=isolated mismatch, housekeeping cores so overloaded that offloaded RCU/workqueue work backs up and stalls the hot core's syscall-free loop anyway.
  EXISTING: 45-realtime-scheduling-kernel-tuning.md §45.2, §45.4.1, §45.5, §45.6 ex. 5: Names tick, RCU callbacks, kworkers, CFS load-balancing at a high level; pitfalls mention 'an un-offloaded kthread', 'starving migration/watchdog'; exercise says 'use cyclictest -b + ftrace' in one line.; 42-thread-interrupt-pinning.md §42.4.2: IRQ affinity, irqbalance, /proc/interrupts; kernel threads deferred to Ch. 45.; appendix-C-system-tuning-checklist.md C.2, C.3, C.5: Single-line knobs: nosoftlockup nmi_watchdog=0, skew_tick=1, rcu_nocb_poll, vm.stat_interval, writeback tuning, 'hunt residual jitter with perf/ftrace/hwlat'.; 15-virtual-memory-tlb-huge-pages.md §15.4.1, §15.5: khugepaged compaction jitter; no defrag modes, no kcompactd/kswapd.; 16-numa-architecture.md §16.5: numa_balancing as a migration-stall source; 'usually disabled'.; 26-memory-mapping.md §26.5: One clause: munmap 'reintroduces faults (and TLB shootdowns across cores)'.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il: 'kcompactd' 0; 'kswapd' 0; 'ksm'/'ksmd' 0; 'workqueue' 0; 'ksoftirqd' 0; 'softirq' only 45; 'migration/' 0; 'rcuo' 0; 'rcu_normal'/'RCU offload' 0; 'hardlockup' 0; 'nmi_watchdog'/'softlockup'/'NMI' only appendix-C; 'watchdog_cpumask' 0; 'clocksource watchdog'/'tsc=reliable' 0; 'defrag' 0 (incl. khugepaged/defrag, transparent_hugepage/defrag); 'managed_irq'/'kthread_cpus' 0; 'dirty_writeback'/'dirty_expire'/'dirty page' 0; 'vm.stat_interval' only appendix-C; '/proc/softirqs' 0; 'irq_vectors' 0; 'EDAC'/'rasdaemon'/'mcelog' 0.

--- [os-kernel-depth #3] Kernel Latency Tracing: ftrace, trace-cmd & KernelShark, rtla (osnoise/timerlat), hwlat and perf sched
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part VII, paired with the Kernel Noise Catalog chapter (directly after it, before Ch. 46); alternative placement is Part I after Ch. 5 (Debugging) as the kernel-side companion to Ch. 5's user-space toolkit. Appendix I gains an 'ftrace/rtla/perf sched' recipe section either way. | gap=partial | priority=high | area=debugging-diagnostics
  HFT: Ch. 45 and Appendix C both end with 'if cyclictest still shows spikes, use ftrace to find the culprit' — but ftrace is never taught, and rtla's osnoise/timerlat (the tools built precisely for isolated-core jitter attribution) do not appear at all. Finding the last 20 µs spike on a production hot core is the single most common OS-level diagnostic task in an HFT infra team, and the book currently leaves the reader with bpftrace one-liners and a name-drop.
  SCOPE: tracefs anatomy (/sys/kernel/tracing): current_tracer, set_event, per-CPU ring buffers, trace_pipe, tracing_cpumask (trace only the isolated core), trace_clock=x86-tsc to align with Ch. 17 timestamps, and trace_marker writes from a housekeeping thread to correlate application events with kernel events. // The latency tracers: irqsoff, preemptoff, preemptirqsoff, wakeup/wakeup_rt/wakeup_dl, hwlat; tracing_max_latency and tracing_thresh; function_graph with set_ftrace_filter/set_graph_function to get a syscall's in-kernel timeline. // rtla timerlat (per-core wake-up latency with automatic trace capture on threshold, --dma-latency, user-space -u mode) and rtla osnoise (noise attributed per source: IRQ, NMI, softirq, thread), rtla hwnoise — the modern replacement for cyclictest -b plus hand-driven ftrace. // trace-cmd record -e sched -e irq -e irq_vectors -M <mask> → trace-cmd report / KernelShark timelines; the cyclictest -b <us> --tracemark break-trace workflow. // perf sched record/latency/timehist/map for run-queue and wake-up latency; perf trace as strace-without-ptrace; strace/ltrace overhead and why never near the hot path; perf ftrace. // Event sources beyond scheduling: irq_vectors:* (call_function, reschedule, local_timer), tlb:tlb_flush, exceptions:page_fault_*, power:cpu_idle, x86_fpu:*, mce:*; using them as perf stat counters for production-safe monitoring (ties health monitoring). // Always-on application tracing alternatives: LTTng-UST per-CPU ring buffers vs USDT+bpftrace (Ch. 61) vs the book's own binary logger (Ch. 71); --- [os-kernel-depth #4] OS & Network Health Monitoring: Continuously Proving the Box Is Still Quiet
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part XII — Observability & Operations, between Ch. 75 (Capture) and Ch. 76 (Production Profiling & Case Study), so the capstone's 'continuous monitoring' section can point to it; alternative: directly after Ch. 71 (Logging). | gap=scattered | priority=high | area=health-monitoring-os-net
  HFT: A box that was quiet at 06:00 is not necessarily quiet at 09:31: a package update re-enabled irqbalance, a new daemon landed on core 5, PTP lost its grandmaster, the NIC started dropping at the open. Ch. 76 monitors application latency and Ch. 74 monitors process liveness, but nothing watches the OS and network counters that explain why the tail moved — and those counters are the cheapest early warning available because, on a properly isolated core, every one of them should read zero.
  SCOPE: Per-hot-thread counters that must stay at zero, sampled from a housekeeping core: /proc/<pid>/task/<tid>/status voluntary_ctxt_switches / nonvoluntary_ctxt_switches, /proc/<pid>/task/<tid>/sched (nr_migrations, nr_involuntary_switches, se.sum_exec_runtime), getrusage(RUSAGE_THREAD) ru_minflt/majflt/nivcsw, VmLck/VmSwap and Cpus_allowed drift — with 'any nonzero delta is an incident' semantics. // Per-core kernel health on isolated cores: /proc/interrupts and /proc/softirqs deltas, /proc/schedstat, cpuidle stateN/usage|time (deep C-state residency creeping back — Ch. 6), scaling_cur_freq and turbostat-style MSR residency, thermal_throttle counters, current_clocksource plus a kmsg watcher for 'TSC unstable', sched_rt_runtime_us / isolated / nohz_full config-drift checks, MCE and EDAC correctable-error counts (rasdaemon). // Network and NIC health as time series: ethtool -S deltas (rx_missed_errors, rx_no_buffer_count, per-queue drops, pause/PFC counters — Ch. 64), /proc/net/softnet_stat (time_squeeze, dropped), nstat / /proc/net/snmp (UdpInErrors, RcvbufErrors, TcpRetransSegs), ss -tmi per-order-session queue and retransmit state (Ch. 52), link flaps, multicast/IGMP membership (Ch. 51), PHC/PTP offset and path delay from pmc/phc2sys (Ch. 58), switch telemetry (Ch. 60). // Memory and storage: /proc/meminfo HugePages_Free/Unevictable, /proc/vmstat (thp_*, compact_*, numa_*, pgmajfault), /proc/pressure/* (PSI) on housekeeping cores, capture-device queue depth and latency (/sys/block/*/stat) and dirty-page pressure (Ch. 75). // Collection architecture: a sampler pinned to a housekeeping core; the cost of procfs/sysfs reads (which take mmap_lock or IPI); perf stat -C <core> -I streaming; rtla osnoise -q as a daemon; eBPF counters (Ch. 61); export in Prometheus/OpenMetrics style; alert rules written as SLOs (zero involuntary switches, zero IRQs on hot cores, PTP offset bound, zero rx_missed) rather than thresholds on averages. // The pre-open readiness gate: a script that asserts every Appendix C setting plus the counters above and fails closed before the session opens; re-running it after any kernel/BIOS/tuned/package change (Ch. 45 §45.5 drift). // Measure it: proving the monitor itself adds no noise to the hot core (cyclictest/rtla with and without the sampler running; sampler cost per poll on the reference box). // Pitfalls: top/htop/sar/collectd agents on hot cores, polling too often, averaging away the single spike, irqbalance or tuned restarting and reverting state, trusting process-alive instead of thread-quiet (ties Ch. 74 heartbeats).
  EXISTING: 76-production-profiling-end-to-end-case-study.md §76.2: Continuous application-latency monitoring (TSC/PTP stage stamps, HdrHistogram, bpftrace); diagram lists 'counters: drops, gaps, queue depths, restarts' with no mechanisms.; 42-thread-interrupt-pinning.md §42.3, §42.4.2: One-off checks: /proc/interrupts, perf stat -e cpu-migrations,context-switches, Cpus_allowed.; 44-cache-allocation-intel-rdt.md §44.4.2: 'Monitor continuously' via resctrl CMT/MBM occupancy and bandwidth; alerting idea stated for cache only.; 53-zero-copy-wire-market-data-decoding.md §53.4.4: ethtool -S rx_missed/rx_no_buffer and /proc/net/snmp RcvbufErrors as diagnostics for market-open drops.; 55-nic-features-offloads.md §55.3–§55.4: ethtool -S counters as one-off offload checks.; appendix-C-system-tuning-checklist.md C.5: Verification pass at tuning time; 'bake into a periodic check / pre-trading-day readiness gate' stated as an idea.; 74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.4.3: Heartbeats for process liveness; not OS/network health.

--- [os-kernel-depth #5] Inside the Scheduler: EEVDF, SCHED_DEADLINE, and PREEMPT_RT in Mainline (6.12+)
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part VII, immediately after Ch. 45 (Real-Time Scheduling & Kernel Tuning) — Ch. 45 keeps the recipe, this chapter supplies the internals and the 'should we run the RT kernel?' decision; or fold as expand-existing-chapter into Ch. 45 if page budget is tight (at minimum fix the CFS→EEVDF wording). | gap=partial | priority=medium | area=other
  HFT: Two questions every HFT infrastructure team gets asked — 'should our trading hosts run the real-time kernel now that it is mainline?' and 'why does our housekeeping thread get preempted differently since the kernel upgrade?' — are not answerable from Ch. 45, which lists the scheduling classes in one line each, still calls the default scheduler CFS, and relegates PREEMPT_RT to the reading list. The answer (busy-poll + isolation makes RT mostly irrelevant on hot cores, valuable for the blocking control tier) needs the internals to be convincing.
  SCOPE: EEVDF (6.6+) replaced CFS: virtual deadlines and lag, the per-task slice request via sched_setattr (sched_runtime as a latency hint), what changed in wake-up preemption, the removal of sched_min_granularity/wakeup_granularity sysctls and the new /sys/kernel/debug/sched/* knobs — and what it means for housekeeping threads that share a core. // SCHED_DEADLINE in depth: runtime/deadline/period via sched_setattr, admission control and its interaction with sched_rt_runtime_us, CBS throttling, SCHED_FLAG_RECLAIM (GRUB) and SCHED_FLAG_DL_OVERRUN (SIGXCPU), DL as the right class for periodic housekeeping (heartbeats, re-warm loops — Ch. 46) with guaranteed bandwidth; why a 100%-busy-polling hot thread does not fit DL. // RT-class internals Ch. 45 skips: SCHED_FIFO push/pull migration and migration/N, RT_RUNTIME_SHARE, the stalld starvation daemon as the mitigation for 'RT thread starves kthreads', rt_mutex and PI-futex internals, PTHREAD_PRIO_INHERIT across processes in shared memory (PTHREAD_PROCESS_SHARED). // PREEMPT_RT, now in mainline (6.12): sleeping spinlocks, forced-threaded IRQs (threadirqs) and what they do to NIC softirq/NAPI latency, PREEMPT_DYNAMIC and preempt=none|voluntary|full|lazy, local_lock conversions; what RT buys (bounded wake latency for threads that block) and costs (throughput, driver overhead) on a box whose hot threads never block. // The decision matrix: stock kernel + isolation + busy-poll (the HFT default) vs PREEMPT_RT for the control/order-gateway tier vs vendor RT kernels (RHEL kernel-rt); measured with cyclictest and rtla timerlat on both configurations on the reference box. // Kernel-config knobs that gate this book: CONFIG_HZ, CONFIG_NO_HZ_FULL, CONFIG_RCU_NOCB_CPU, CONFIG_PREEMPT_*, CONFIG_IRQ_TIME_ACCOUNTING, CONFIG_SCHED_DEBUG; reading /proc/config.gz and /boot/config-* before trusting a cmdline. // Measure it: wake-up latency per class (OTHER/EEVDF with slice request, FIFO, DEADLINE) under a noisy neighbor; RT-throttle stall reproduction; PREEMPT_RT vs stock on an isolated busy-poll core (expect no difference) and on a blocking control-plane thread (expect a large one). // Pitfalls: SCHED_FIFO priority above migration/ and ksoftirqd; RT throttling vs DL admission failures (EBUSY); cgroup v1 cpu.rt_runtime_us vs its absence in v2; assuming the RT kernel helps a busy-polling thread.

--- [os-kernel-depth #6] Signals on a Low-Latency Box: Delivery Cost, Masking and Funneling, and Async-Signal-Safe Crash Handling
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 41 (Context Switching & Its Mitigation) as new §41.4.3 'Signals: cost, masking and funneling to a housekeeping thread'; the crash-handler half as new §74.4.6 in Ch. 74 (Process Topology), cross-referenced from Ch. 5 §5.4.2 (core-dump analysis). | gap=absent | priority=medium | area=debugging-diagnostics
  HFT: A single SIGUSR1 or SIGCHLD delivered to the wrong thread costs the hot path a kernel entry, a kilobyte of vector-state save and a cache-cold restart, and the crash path is where a strategy's working orders either get pulled in microseconds or sit exposed until a supervisor times out. The book covers core-dump analysis and supervision but never says how signals are delivered, what they cost, or how to write the handler that is the first line of the kill-switch.
  SCOPE: What a signal costs the target thread: kernel entry, sigframe setup including the full XSAVE area (AVX/AVX-512 state — Ch. 29), handler, rt_sigreturn; measured round-trip and cache pollution on the reference box; which thread a process-directed signal lands on and why that is sometimes the hot thread. // Hot-thread discipline: pthread_sigmask(SIG_BLOCK) everything but synchronous faults at thread start; funnel asynchronous signals to a housekeeping thread via signalfd in epoll/io_uring or sigwaitinfo; SA_RESTART and interrupted syscalls on the I/O threads. // Async-signal-safe crash handling: sigaltstack + SA_ONSTACK, no malloc/printf/locks, pre-formatted binary crash records written with write(2), backtrace(3)/libunwind/std::stacktrace (C++23) safety caveats, writing a minidump then re-raising for the core_pattern pipeline (Ch. 74 §74.4.5). // Kill-switch from the handler: flipping a shared-memory halt flag the order gateway polls (Ch. 74 §74.4.4) before the process dies, so working orders are pulled within the handler's microseconds rather than after the supervisor notices. // Signals this book's techniques generate: SIGBUS from hugetlb exhaustion or truncated file maps (Ch. 15/26), SIGSEGV on stack guard pages (Ch. 23), SIGXCPU from SCHED_DEADLINE overrun and RT limits, SIGPROF-based profilers vs perf (Ch. 2), SIGPIPE on order sessions (Ch. 51). // Supervision plumbing: SIGCHLD/waitpid vs pidfd_open + poll, prctl(PR_SET_PDEATHSIG) for orphan cleanup, SIGSTOP/SIGCONT hazards for a thread holding a NIC ring (Ch. 62). // Measure it: a signal-to-hot-thread microbenchmark showing the stall and the subsequent cache-miss burst; perf trace -e 'signal:*' to find who is signaling whom. // Pitfalls: std::signal instead of sigaction, handlers that take locks or allocate, a core dump of a 10 GB mlocked process stalling survivors (Ch. 74), masking SIGSEGV.
  EXISTING: 74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.3 table, §74.4.3, §74.4.5: SIGCHLD/waitpid for exit detection; core_pattern piping to an async handler; no handler-side code or signal cost.; 05-debugging-low-latency-optimized-code.md §5.4.2: Core-dump post-mortem analysis in gdb; minidumps mentioned; no crash-handler mechanics.; 09-object-layout-alignment-padding.md §9.6: SIGBUS from misaligned SSE access.; 23-memory-management-fundamentals.md §23.4.1: 'the guard-page fault' named for unbounded stack growth.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il: 'signal handler' 0; 'sigaction' 0; 'SIGSEGV' 0; 'SIGALRM' 0; 'sigprocmask'/'pthread_sigmask' 0; 'signalfd' 0; 'async-signal' 0; 'SA_SIGINFO' 0; 'SIGUSR' 0; 'siginfo' 0; 'sigwait' 0; 'signal delivery' 0; 'SIGPROF' 0; 'SIGABRT' 0; 'crash handler' 0; 'libunwind'/'std::stacktrace'/'stacktrace' 0; 'sigaltstack' 0; 'SIGBUS' only 09; 'backtrace' only 05 (gdb bt).

--- [os-kernel-depth #7] Running Trading Workloads in VMs and Containers: KVM with Pinned vCPUs, SR-IOV/VFIO Passthrough, cgroup v2 Cpusets, and the Kubernetes Question
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part VII, after Ch. 45 (and after the proposed Noise Catalog / Scheduler chapters), before Ch. 46; alternative: a new Appendix M next to Appendix A (which already decides cloud vs bare-metal placement). | gap=absent | priority=medium | area=other
  HFT: Most trading firms run something virtualized or containerized on the same box as, or adjacent to, the hot path — risk, monitoring, backtest, sometimes an entire gateway in a VM with a passed-through VF — and the latency cost of a VM exit, a cpu.max throttle, or kvm-clock is as real and as invisible as any microarchitectural effect in Part II. The book currently says only 'the cloud is not colocated' (Appendix A) and 'mitigations matter on multi-tenant boxes' (Ch. 6), leaving the engineer who must decide VM vs container vs bare metal for each role without measurements.
  SCOPE: Where virtualization costs latency: VM exits (EPT violations, MSR/APIC access, HLT, PAUSE-loop exiting), two-dimensional page walks and EPT/NPT TLB pressure (huge pages at both levels — Ch. 15), interrupt delivery (posted interrupts/APICv, kvm.halt_poll_ns), kvm-clock vs invariant-TSC passthrough (-cpu host,invtsc, tsc-deadline) and its effect on clock_gettime (Ch. 17); measured guest vs host cyclictest and kernel-stack RX latency. // Building a low-latency guest: pinned vCPUs/emulator/iothreads (vcpupin, emulatorpin), 1 GiB hugepage backing, isolcpus inside and outside, vNUMA aligned to host nodes (Ch. 16), tuned realtime-virtual-host/-guest, disabling guest watchdogs and kvmclock stable-bit assumptions, mitigations in a nested setting (Ch. 6). // Device access from a guest: SR-IOV virtual functions and vfio-pci passthrough of a NIC/VF or FPGA (Ch. 66), IOMMU groups and ACS, DPDK/ef_vi inside a guest (Ch. 62), hardware timestamps and PTP via a VF (Ch. 58); what is lost (VF rate limiting, no DDIO/CAT control — Ch. 44/56). // Containers: cgroup v2 cpuset.cpus.partition=isolated vs isolcpus, cpu.max bandwidth throttling as a multi-millisecond stall source, systemd slices (AllowedCPUs, CPUWeight), memory.max vs mlockall, /dev/hugepages and RLIMIT_MEMLOCK inside a container, CAP_SYS_NICE/CAP_IPC_LOCK for SCHED_FIFO and mlock, default seccomp/AppArmor profiles (Ch. 72). // Kubernetes for trading: static CPU-manager policy and full-pcpus-only, topology manager, SR-IOV device plugin, hugepage resources, Guaranteed QoS — and why kubelet/kube-proxy/CNI still generate noise; where it is acceptable (research, backtest, non-hot services — Appendix A §A.7) and where it is not (the order path). // Cloud 'metal' instances (AWS .metal/Nitro) vs colocated bare metal: what a hypervisor-less tenant still cannot control (SMIs, firmware, NIC choice, PTP source — Appendix K). // Measure it: the same cyclictest/rtla timerlat and RX-latency harness on host, pinned VM with VF passthrough, and container with/without cpu.max; steal time (/proc/stat) as the giveaway. // Pitfalls: vCPU steal, cpu.max throttling, nested nohz_full without host isolation, irqbalance in the guest, kvm-clock turning clock_gettime into a syscall, VF flow steering limits (Ch. 57).
  EXISTING: appendix-A-arm-graviton.md A.7: Cloud vs bare-metal placement by tier; no virtualization mechanics.; 06-system-setup-for-low-latency.md §6.2.3: Multi-tenant/cloud as the case for keeping mitigations; 'VM exit' mentioned only under MDS/TAA flushes.; 45-realtime-scheduling-kernel-tuning.md §45.2, §45.4.1, §45.5: cgroup v2 cpuset.cpus.partition=isolated in two lines; cgroups to confine non-hot tasks.; appendix-C-system-tuning-checklist.md C.3, C.4: systemctl set-property AllowedCPUs; 'cgroup isolation' bullet.; 66-pcie-host-device-boundary.md §66.2.2: VFIO 'used by DPDK and device passthrough' in one clause.; 44-cache-allocation-intel-rdt.md §44.4.3: CAT for multi-tenant/multi-strategy boxes (processes, not VMs).

--- [os-kernel-depth #8] Userspace Drivers & Kernel Modules: UIO/VFIO Mechanics, a Minimal PCIe Doorbell/Completion Driver, and When to Go Kernel-Side
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part XI — Heterogeneous Computing, immediately after Ch. 66 (PCIe & the Host–Device Boundary) and before Ch. 67 (GPU); it is the hands-on complement to Ch. 66's mental model and the substrate Ch. 62 (DPDK/ef_vi) and Ch. 69 (FPGA) assume. | gap=partial | priority=medium | area=other
  HFT: Every kernel-bypass and FPGA path in Part X–XI rests on a user-space driver that maps a BAR and a DMA ring, and most shops end up writing or patching one — for a vendor FPGA card, a custom NIC firmware feature, or to shave the vendor library's abstraction cost. The book names UIO/VFIO as the thing DPDK uses but never shows how a BAR becomes a pointer or how a hugepage becomes an IOVA, so Ch. 66's measured 'device-register read costs a round-trip' lesson cannot be reproduced by the reader without DPDK.
  SCOPE: Three ways user space reaches a device: /sys/bus/pci/devices/*/resourceN mmap (quick hacks), UIO (uio_pci_generic: BAR mmap plus one interrupt eventfd, no IOMMU), VFIO (vfio-pci: IOMMU groups, VFIO_GROUP_GET_DEVICE_FD, VFIO_DEVICE_GET_REGION_INFO, VFIO_IOMMU_MAP_DMA, per-vector MSI-X eventfds, noiommu mode); binding with driverctl / dpdk-devbind.py; ACS and IOMMU-group splitting; iommu=pt and IOTLB cost (Ch. 66). // A minimal C++ VFIO driver for an FPGA/NIC-style device: map the BAR, allocate a hugepage-backed DMA ring, program its IOVA, post descriptors, ring the doorbell (posted write; write-combining and sfence — Ch. 66), poll a completion word in host memory, use the MSI-X eventfd only on the slow path. // DMA memory from user space: hugetlbfs pinning vs IOMMU remapping, VFIO_DMA_MAP_FLAG_READ/WRITE, why /proc/self/pagemap tricks are unsafe, cache-coherent vs non-coherent BARs, MAP_SHARED + O_SYNC for uncached MMIO, and the IOMMU as the DMA security boundary (Ch. 72). // When a kernel module is unavoidable: real interrupt handlers, dma_alloc_coherent, exposing rings to user space through a char device's mmap (the shim-driver pattern of Solarflare/Exablaze/Xilinx XDMA), ioremap_wc, pci_alloc_irq_vectors; the maintenance tax — out-of-tree builds, DKMS, kABI breakage on kernel updates, module signing under Secure Boot and lockdown. // Failure handling: a dead user-space driver leaves the NIC in an unknown state — function-level reset (FLR via sysfs reset), re-binding, and how this feeds Ch. 74's fast-restart story. // Measure it: MMIO register read vs write vs host-memory completion poll through VFIO; eventfd interrupt wake vs polling latency; a kernel char-device ioctl round-trip for comparison (extends Ch. 66 §66.3). // Pitfalls: forgetting bus-master enable, IOVA vs physical-address confusion, unbinding the management NIC, enable_unsafe_noiommu_mode in production, out-of-tree modules tainting the kernel and disabling lockdown-sensitive tooling.
  EXISTING: 62-kernel-bypass-userspace-networking.md §62.2, §62.4.1: 'rings mapped into the application's address space via DPDK's UIO/VFIO'; PMD 'bound via VFIO/UIO, unbound from the kernel driver' — passing mentions.; 66-pcie-host-device-boundary.md §66.2.2, §66.3, §66.5: IOMMU/VFIO named; measurement 'requires a mapped device BAR — e.g. via a driver or DPDK/VFIO'; IOMMU passthrough caveat.; 69-fpga-acceleration.md §69.2: Host–FPGA boundary at the architectural level; no driver mechanics.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il: 'uio_pci_generic'/'igb_uio' 0; 'driverctl'/'devbind' 0; 'VFIO_'/'IOMMU group'/'iommu group' 0; 'eventfd' 0; 'kernel module' 0 (grep -n -i 'kernel module|driver' in 62 and 66 returns only the passing mentions quoted above); 'ioremap'/'dma_alloc_coherent'/'char device' 0; 'module signing'/'Secure Boot'/'lockdown' 0; 'FLR'/'function level reset' 0; 'VFIO'/'vfio' only 62 and 66.

--- [os-kernel-depth #9] Host Lifecycle: Boot Time, Kernel & Distro Choice, systemd Service Hardening, Privileges and the Pre-Open Readiness Gate
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part XII, immediately after Ch. 74 (Process Topology) — Ch. 74 owns the process lifecycle, this chapter owns the host lifecycle; Appendix C.3/C.5 items ('put it in a systemd unit', 're-verify after changes') link to it. | gap=scattered | priority=medium | area=other
  HFT: When a trading host must be rebooted at 09:25, the number that matters is seconds from power-on to the first warm tick, and it is decided by boot ordering, kernel choice and unit configuration — not by anything in the application. Likewise the mundane question 'how does the hot process get SCHED_FIFO and mlockall without running as root?' has no answer in the book, although Ch. 72 tells the reader to drop privileges.
  SCOPE: Kernel choice: distro kernel vs LTS mainline vs vendor RT (kernel-rt) vs custom build; a table of which techniques in this book gate on which versions (io_uring opcodes, AF_XDP, MADV_COLLAPSE 6.1, EEVDF 6.6, mainline PREEMPT_RT 6.12, devmem TCP); pinning kernel + firmware + NIC driver/firmware versions as part of the deployable artifact; kernel live patching (kpatch/livepatch) — stop_machine-style transitions and why a trading host is never live-patched during the session. // Boot-to-ready: UEFI fast boot, kexec to skip firmware on a planned reboot, minimal initrd, systemd-analyze blame/critical-chain, ordering the tuning unit (Appendix C runtime knobs, hugepage reservation, irqbalance masked) Before= the trading services, PTP convergence (Ch. 58) and warm-up (Ch. 46) inside the time-to-ready budget; measuring a full host restart against a market-hours outage. // systemd directives for a hot process: CPUAffinity=, CPUSchedulingPolicy=fifo / CPUSchedulingPriority=, LimitMEMLOCK=infinity, LimitRTPRIO=, LimitCORE=, IOSchedulingClass=, OOMScoreAdjust=-1000, Restart=/RestartSec=/StartLimitBurst= (crash-loop policy — Ch. 74), WatchdogSec= with sd_notify(WATCHDOG=1) from a housekeeping thread, Slice= with AllowedCPUs= for everything else, ExecStartPre= readiness checks. // Running unprivileged: CAP_SYS_NICE, CAP_IPC_LOCK, CAP_NET_RAW/CAP_NET_ADMIN, CAP_SYS_RAWIO via AmbientCapabilities= or setcap; limits.conf/pam_limits vs systemd limits; RLIMIT_RTPRIO/MEMLOCK/NOFILE; /dev/hugepages and /dev/vfio permissions; acquire-then-drop at the steady-state boundary (Ch. 72 §72.4.6). // Configuration-drift control: /proc/cmdline and sysfs state asserted by a readiness script that diffs against Appendix C, tuned profiles vs explicit units, unattended-upgrade/needrestart/irqbalance re-enablement hazards (Ch. 45 §45.5), journald/rsyslog placement off hot cores. // Measure it: host-restart-to-first-warm-tick timeline on the reference box (firmware, kernel, units, tuning, PTP lock, warm-up); unit-ordering mistakes shown as jitter in cyclictest. // Pitfalls: Restart=always hammering a venue session, systemd-coredump stalling survivors (Ch. 74 §74.4.5), LimitMEMLOCK too low making mlockall fail silently (Ch. 26), DefaultTimeoutStopSec killing a draining gateway, kernel update changing CONFIG_HZ or re-enabling THP=always.
  EXISTING: 74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.3, §74.4.3, §74.4.4: Process detect/restart/recover/warm timing; supervisor with backoff; 'often supervised in turn (systemd, a watchdog, or a peer)'.; appendix-C-system-tuning-checklist.md C.3, C.5: 'put in a systemd unit / startup script'; 'bake verification into a periodic check and a pre-trading-day readiness gate'; re-verify after kernel/BIOS updates.; 45-realtime-scheduling-kernel-tuning.md §45.5: Config drift after kernel/distro updates; tuned profile caution.; 26-memory-mapping.md §26.4.2, §26.5: RLIMIT_MEMLOCK must be raised and mlock success verified; no mechanism for raising it.; 72-secure-programming-low-latency.md §72.4.6: 'Acquire what setup needs (mlockall, RT priority, raw sockets), then drop to an unprivileged user and drop capabilities' — no named capabilities or mechanism.; 48-io-uring-deep-dive.md §48.5: Kernel-version/feature-availability caveat for io_uring (also Ch. 61 §61.5 for eBPF).

--- [os-kernel-depth #10] Advanced Mapping & Locking Tricks: the Double-Mapped Ring, mremap, MCL_ONFAULT, MADV_POPULATE/COLLAPSE, userfaultfd, Stack Pre-Faulting and Self-Inflicted TLB Shootdowns
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 26 (Memory Mapping) as new §26.4.3 'Beyond MAP_POPULATE: advanced mapping tricks', with the stack pre-fault item also added to Ch. 23 §23.4.2 and the TLB-shootdown item cross-referenced from Ch. 41 §41.5 and Ch. 15 §15.5. | gap=partial | priority=medium | area=other
  HFT: Two of the most common self-inflicted hot-path stalls in production trading systems — a housekeeping thread unmapping a buffer and shooting down the hot core's TLB, and a decoder copying a wrapped message out of a ring — have cheap, well-known kernel-level fixes that the book does not show, and the newer pre-fault/collapse syscalls give the deterministic huge-page behaviour Ch. 15 wants without khugepaged. Ch. 26 covers the 2010-era toolkit (MAP_POPULATE, mlock, madvise WILLNEED) and stops.
  SCOPE: The double-mapped ('magic') ring buffer: memfd_create plus two adjacent MAP_FIXED mappings of the same pages so wrap-around reads and writes are contiguous — no split-message copies in decoders (Ch. 34/53/54); the MAP_HUGETLB variant and its alignment rules. // mremap(MREMAP_MAYMOVE|MREMAP_FIXED|MREMAP_DONTUNMAP) to grow an arena or relocate a mapping without copying — and why growth is a cold-path-only event. // Pre-fault without touching: MADV_POPULATE_READ/WRITE (5.14+) vs MAP_POPULATE vs a write loop, why a read-only pre-fault maps the shared zero page (and faults again on first write); MCL_ONFAULT (4.4+) for lock-on-first-touch of large sparse regions; mlockall(MCL_FUTURE) side effects on thread stacks and RLIMIT_MEMLOCK. // Deterministic huge pages without khugepaged: MADV_COLLAPSE (6.1+), transparent_hugepage/defrag semantics (always/defer/defer+madvise/madvise/never), verifying with /proc/<pid>/smaps AnonHugePages. // Stack pre-faulting for hot threads: pthread_attr_setstacksize, touching the stack to its planned depth at thread start (the RT-wiki pattern), ulimit -s and RLIMIT_STACK, guard pages and MAP_GROWSDOWN, alloca/VLA hazards (ties Ch. 23 §23.4.1). // userfaultfd as a test-time fault detector (any fault in a hot region fails the test) and as the mechanism for snapshotting/migrating shared-memory segments without stopping writers (ties Ch. 73/74). // Self-inflicted IPIs: munmap/mprotect/madvise(DONTNEED)/mmap(MAP_FIXED) by any thread of the process send TLB-shootdown IPIs to every core running that mm — including the isolated hot core; measuring with tlb:tlb_flush and irq_vectors:call_function tracepoints; designing loggers, capture writers and hot-reload (Ch. 71/73/75) to never unmap during the session. // Knobs: vm.overcommit_memory, vm.max_map_count, vm.unprivileged_userfaultfd, MAP_SYNC/DAX note (ties the CXL/PMEM proposal). // Measure it: contiguous-wrap decode vs split-copy decode; MADV_POPULATE vs touch loop pre-fault time; a 10 ms burst of hot-core spikes correlated with a housekeeping thread's munmap.
  EXISTING: 26-memory-mapping.md §26.2, §26.4.2, §26.5: MAP_HUGETLB/MAP_POPULATE/MAP_LOCKED; pre-fault + mlock + madvise(WILLNEED/HUGEPAGE/DONTNEED/RANDOM/SEQUENTIAL); one clause on munmap TLB shootdowns; RLIMIT_MEMLOCK.; 23-memory-management-fundamentals.md §23.4.1, §23.4.2: Stack is 'one instruction, no tail' with a warning about unbounded growth and the guard-page fault; pre-faulting pages by touching; first-touch NUMA placement.; 15-virtual-memory-tlb-huge-pages.md §15.4.1: THP enabled modes (always/madvise/never) and khugepaged jitter; no defrag modes or MADV_COLLAPSE.; 50-inter-process-communication.md §50.4.2: memfd/shm_open for shared segments; no double mapping.

--- [os-kernel-depth #11] Runtime Idle-State Control: PM QoS (/dev/cpu_dma_latency), Per-CPU cpuidle Limits and Governors
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 6 (System Setup for Low Latency) as new §6.4.4 'Controlling C-states at runtime: PM QoS and cpuidle', with a matching Appendix C.3 line and a monitoring hook for the health-monitoring proposal. | gap=partial | priority=medium | area=other
  HFT: The runtime PM QoS interface is what most production trading hosts actually use to hold C-states shallow (via tuned or a tiny daemon), and its failure mode — the holder dies and 50 µs wake-ups quietly return — is a classic first-message-after-the-quiet-spell tail event. Ch. 6 covers the BIOS and cmdline methods only, so the reader cannot explain why tuned works or why the box got slow after a daemon restart.
  SCOPE: PM QoS: open /dev/cpu_dma_latency, write the bound in µs (0 = C0/C1 only), keep the fd open for the lifetime of the constraint — the mechanism behind tuned latency-performance; process-scoped, revocable, no reboot, vs the cmdline intel_idle.max_cstate/processor.max_cstate/idle=poll Ch. 6 already gives. // Per-CPU control: /sys/devices/system/cpu/cpuN/cpuidle/stateN/disable and power/pm_qos_resume_latency_us (cap C-states only on hot cores and their SMT siblings — keep deep states on housekeeping cores to preserve turbo and thermal headroom, Ch. 43); cpuidle governors (menu, teo, ladder, haltpoll) and cpuidle.governor=; intel_idle.states_off, cpuidle.off=1; AMD amd_pstate/C-state analogs. // Reading the costs: stateN/latency (exit latency), residency, usage/time/above/below counters — as tuning input and as a drift signal (deep-state residency reappearing on a hot core means the constraint was lost). // idle=poll trade-offs: no MWAIT hints, lost turbo and thermal headroom, SMT-sibling interference, power; vs C1 with MWAIT; HWP/EPP (energy_performance_preference=performance) as the frequency-side counterpart. // Why it matters even on a busy-poll design: the hot core never idles, so PM QoS governs the semi-hot threads (control plane, timers — Ch. 41/timers chapter) and the cores whose wake-up IPIs and shared-package C-states (package C6 exit) still touch the hot core's latency. // Measure it: first-message-after-idle latency with (a) BIOS cap only, (b) cpu_dma_latency=0, (c) per-core resume-latency cap, (d) idle=poll — extending Ch. 6 exercise 5; turbostat C-state residency confirming each. // Pitfalls: the process holding cpu_dma_latency dies and deep C-states silently return; tuned restarting and overriding; EPP left at balance_power; package C-states still entered when all cores idle.
  EXISTING: 06-system-setup-for-low-latency.md §6.2.1, §6.4.3, §6.6 ex. 5: C-state exit-latency model; 'cap C-states via cmdline intel_idle.max_cstate=1 processor.max_cstate=1 idle=poll (or per-core via the cpuidle sysfs)' in one line; wake-up-tail exercise.; appendix-C-system-tuning-checklist.md C.1, C.2, C.3, C.5: BIOS C-state disable; cmdline caps; tuned latency-performance named; cpupower idle-info verification.; 46-keeping-the-hot-path-warm.md §46.2: Cold-start after idle gaps framed as cache/TLB/predictor decay; C-state exit referenced to Ch. 6.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il: 'cpu_dma_latency'/'dev/cpu_dma' 0; 'PM QoS'/'pm_qos' 0; 'resume_latency' 0; 'cpuidle' only 06 (one parenthetical); 'HWP'/'energy_perf' 0 ('EPP' hits are substrings of other words); 'haltpoll'/'teo governor' 0; 'turbostat' only 29 and 43 (frequency context); 'idle=poll' only 06.

--- [os-kernel-depth #12] Security Subsystems' Latency Cost: LSMs, audit, seccomp (and the SSBD Trap), Yama/ptrace and Kernel Lockdown
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 72 (Secure Programming) as new §72.4.9 'The cost of the kernel's own security subsystems', immediately after §72.4.6 (privilege reduction); Appendix C.2's 'audit=0 selinux=0' line then links to it instead of standing alone. | gap=partial | priority=medium | area=other
  HFT: Appendix C tells the reader to pass audit=0 selinux=0 'if your security policy permits' — which it usually does not — without saying what either actually costs, and the one security mechanism the book does recommend (seccomp at the steady-state boundary) can silently switch on Speculative Store Bypass Disable for the hot thread, undoing the mitigation tuning of Ch. 6. These are measurable, kernel-level latency effects with surgical alternatives, and a regulated trading firm needs the numbers to argue with its security team.
  SCOPE: LSM hooks on the syscalls housekeeping threads do make (SELinux/AppArmor AVC checks on write/sendmsg/openat): measured per-syscall overhead enforcing vs permissive vs disabled on the reference box; the compliance argument against selinux=0 and the targeted alternatives (permissive domains, unconfined_t for the trading unit). // audit: per-syscall cost with and without rules, auditctl -a task,never / -e 0 vs audit=0, why kernel.audit on the housekeeping tier is usually acceptable and on a syscall-free hot path is free. // seccomp mechanics and cost: BPF filter length vs per-syscall overhead, SECCOMP_RET_ALLOW fast path, seccomp_unotify; the SSBD trap — installing a filter without SECCOMP_FILTER_FLAG_SPEC_ALLOW enables Speculative Store Bypass Disable for the whole process on kernels booted with spec_store_bypass_disable=seccomp (the default before 5.16) — a hidden store-forwarding penalty on the hot path; verifying via /proc/<pid>/status Speculation_Store_Bypass (ties Ch. 6 mitigations, Ch. 72 §72.4.6). // prctl(PR_SET_SPECULATION_CTRL): PR_SPEC_STORE_BYPASS and PR_SPEC_INDIRECT_BRANCH per task, STIBP/IBPB on context switch and what they cost the housekeeping cores (Ch. 41). // Yama ptrace_scope, kernel.perf_event_paranoid, kptr_restrict, dmesg_restrict and kernel lockdown (integrity/confidentiality): what each blocks that this book relies on — perf, eBPF (Ch. 61), rr/gdb attach (Ch. 5), /dev/cpu/*/msr for RDT and turbo control (Ch. 44), /dev/mem, unsigned out-of-tree NIC/FPGA drivers — and how to grant CAP_PERFMON/CAP_BPF/CAP_SYS_PTRACE instead of disabling the control. // ptrace-based tools (strace, ltrace, gdb attach) vs perf trace/eBPF: the stop-and-copy cost that makes them unusable near the hot path. // IMA/EVM and dm-verity measured boot as the runtime half of §72.4.7's signed-build story; first-exec hashing cost only. // Measure it: syscall micro-loop on a housekeeping thread under each LSM/audit/seccomp setting; a store-forwarding microbenchmark with and without SSBD engaged. // Pitfalls: audit=0 removing compliance evidence; container runtimes installing default seccomp/AppArmor profiles; lockdown silently breaking perf/BOLT (Ch. 22) and MSR-based tuning; filters that force SSBD.
  EXISTING: 72-secure-programming-low-latency.md §72.4.4, §72.4.6, §72.5: Hardening cost measured for stack protector/FORTIFY/PIE/CFI/CET; seccomp 'near-zero hot-path cost' installed at the steady-state boundary; drop capabilities; namespaces — one bullet each, no LSM/audit/SSBD.; appendix-C-system-tuning-checklist.md C.2: 'audit=0 selinux=0 (if your security policy permits) — remove audit-subsystem overhead; weigh against Ch. 72'.; 06-system-setup-for-low-latency.md §6.2.3: Spectre/Meltdown/MDS mitigations and mitigations=; SSBD and its seccomp linkage not mentioned.; 02-measure-first-profiling-pmu.md §2.5: kernel.perf_event_paranoid as a perf prerequisite.
