--- [os-kernel-depth #13] Storage-Path Internals for Capture: blk-mq, NVMe Queues and Managed IRQs, io_uring IOPOLL/SQPOLL, Dirty-Page Writeback and SPDK
  kind=expand-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 75 (Capture, Persistence & Replay Storage) — expand §75.4.3 'Getting bytes to disk off the hot path' into 3–4 sub-bullets groups (writeback & durability, blk-mq/NVMe & IRQ placement, polled I/O & SPDK, filesystem choice) and add the NVMe-IRQ item to §75.5 and to Appendix C.3. | gap=partial | priority=medium | area=other
  HFT: Ch. 75 correctly puts capture off the hot path but names the hazard ('storage stall bleeding into the hot path') without the mechanisms: dirty-page throttling that stalls the writer until the ring overflows and drops, and NVMe managed interrupts that land on the isolated trading core by default. A full-feed capture at the open is a multi-GB/s sustained write, and whether it drops or jitters the hot core is decided by these knobs.
  SCOPE: The buffered path's hidden stalls: vm.dirty_background_ratio/bytes, dirty_expire/writeback_centisecs, per-bdi flusher kworkers, and balance_dirty_pages throttling that can stall the capture writer itself; vs O_DIRECT, RWF_DSYNC, fdatasync vs fsync, sync_file_range for bounded durability windows, posix_fadvise(DONTNEED) for buffered fallbacks. // blk-mq and NVMe: hardware queues per core, none vs mq-deadline/kyber schedulers for an append-only workload, nvme poll_queues plus IORING_SETUP_IOPOLL for completion polling, SQPOLL thread placement on a housekeeping core, write cache/FUA behaviour, nomerges/rq_affinity/read_ahead_kb. // NVMe MSI-X vectors as managed IRQs that ignore smp_affinity and spread onto isolated cores unless isolcpus=managed_irq or queue counts are limited — a storage-side noise source on the hot core (ties Ch. 42, Appendix C, Kernel Noise Catalog). // Filesystem and layout for an append-only journal: XFS vs ext4 allocation behaviour, fallocate pre-allocation to avoid metadata writes mid-session, O_APPEND vs positional writes, large sequential write sizing, separating the capture device from root/log devices. // SPDK as the storage analogue of DPDK: a dedicated core polling the NVMe queue in user space; when it beats io_uring IOPOLL and what it costs (another burned core, no filesystem). // Measure it: writer p99 stall vs dirty settings; sustained GB/s buffered vs O_DIRECT vs IOPOLL vs SPDK; hot-core /proc/interrupts deltas before and after NVMe IRQ masking; kswapd/reclaim activity under page-cache pressure (/proc/vmstat). // Pitfalls: fsync on a device queue shared with another writer, sync from cron or log rotation, kswapd reclaim storms evicting the hot working set (Ch. 7/15), NVMe thermal throttling, a journal that grows into metadata-heavy small writes.
  EXISTING: 75-capture-persistence-replay-storage.md §75.4.3, §75.5, §75.3: Five bullets: dedicated writer, io_uring, O_DIRECT (page-cache pollution), NVMe/sequential, fsync granularity; pitfall names 'storage stall bleeding into the hot path'.; 48-io-uring-deep-dive.md §48.4.2: IORING_SETUP_IOPOLL/SQPOLL polling modes in general.; appendix-C-system-tuning-checklist.md C.3: 'writeback/dirty-page tuning if the box does capture' — one line.; 44-cache-allocation-intel-rdt.md §44.4.2: MBA to throttle the capture writer's memory bandwidth.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il: 'SPDK' 0; 'blk-mq'/'block layer'/'io scheduler'/'mq-deadline' 0; 'poll_queues'/'nvme poll'/'hipri' 0; 'dirty_writeback'/'dirty_expire'/'dirty page' 0 ('dirty' absent from Ch. 75 entirely); 'fdatasync'/'sync_file_range'/'posix_fadvise' 0; 'fallocate' 0; 'XFS'/'ext4' 0; 'FUA'/'write-back cache' 0; 'managed_irq' 0; grep -n -i 'IRQ|poll|dirty|kswapd|reclaim' in 75 returns nothing on the storage path.

--- [os-kernel-depth #14] CXL-Attached and Tiered Memory as a NUMA Hazard (and the Persistent-Memory Postscript)
  kind=expand-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 16 (NUMA Architecture) — new sub-section §16.2.3 'CPU-less nodes: CXL expanders and memory tiering' plus a §16.5 pitfall and an extra row in Appendix E's latency table. | gap=absent | priority=low | area=other
  HFT: Servers with CXL memory expanders are arriving in colocation racks, and to the kernel they are NUMA nodes with no CPUs — so Ch. 16's 'accidental remote allocation' cliff gains a second, deeper step that default first-touch can fall off under pressure, and the kernel's tiering daemon will happily migrate a hot order book down to it. A short treatment keeps Ch. 16's model correct for the hardware readers will meet in the book's lifetime.
  SCOPE: CXL.mem expanders appear as CPU-less NUMA nodes with ~170–250+ ns load latency; first-touch and numactl --membind semantics on such nodes; /sys/devices/system/node/nodeN/access0/initiators, numactl -H, lscpu, cxl list/daxctl for discovery. // Kernel memory tiering (memory_tiers, numa_balancing=2 tiering mode): demotion to the far tier and promotion back as a new background page-migration noise source that can move a warmed-up hot page after Ch. 46's warm-up — and how to exclude far nodes from the hot process's mems_allowed (cpuset.mems, set_mempolicy MPOL_BIND). // Persistent memory postscript: fsdax/devdax and MAP_SYNC for a journal medium (Ch. 75), Optane's discontinuation and the CXL persistent-memory successors; pmem as a NUMA node vs a DAX file. // Measure it: extend §16.3's pointer-chase sweep to DRAM vs remote-socket DRAM vs CXL node; watch numa_* and pgdemote_*/pgpromote_* in /proc/vmstat during a run. // Pitfalls: MPOL_DEFAULT spilling into the far node under memory pressure, vm.zone_reclaim_mode interactions, tiering demotion after warm-up, treating a CXL node as 'just another socket' in Ch. 16's two-node mental model.
  EXISTING: 16-numa-architecture.md §16.2–§16.5: Two-socket model, first-touch, binding, numa_balancing pitfall; no CPU-less nodes or tiering.; 75-capture-persistence-replay-storage.md §75.4.3: NVMe as the capture medium; no DAX/persistent memory.; appendix-E-latency-numbers.md E.1: Local and NUMA-remote DRAM rows; no CXL row.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il: 'CXL' 0; 'persistent memory' 0; 'Optane' 0; 'DAX' 0; 'NVDIMM' 0; 'memory_tiers'/'tiering'/'pgdemote' 0; 'MAP_SYNC' 0; 'PMEM' one hit in appendix-A not in the persistent-memory sense (no treatment).


######## LENS hardware-frontier — 12 proposals ########
SUMMARY: LENS: hardware & microarchitecture frontier (what a 2026 reader on current Intel/AMD silicon needs that an Ice Lake-SP-centric Part II does not give them). METHOD: read chapters/README.md in full, then Ch. 6, 7, 11, 16, 29, 43, 44 and Appendices A, C, E end-to-end, then grepped ~150 distinctive terms across chapters/*.md (vendor/generation names, CCD/CCX/SNC/NPS, core-to-core, MSR/0x1A4/wrmsr, PL1/PL2/RAPL/HWP/EPP/APERF, cpu_dma_latency/cpuidle, uncore, DDR5/HBM/CXL/refresh, ECC/MCE/scrub/rasdaemon/EDAC/SMI/hwlat, errata/microcode, BHI/Downfall/SRSO/Zenbleed, IBS/Intel PT, P-core/E-core/hybrid, desktop/overclock/PBO, Speed Select/APBDIS/DLWM, etc.) and read every real hit in context (discarding substring false positives such as 'Turin' in 'featuring', 'CHA' in 'change', 'EPP' in 'stepping', 'SMI' in 'transmit', 'IBS' in 'describes', 'DSA' and 'CCD' substrings). FINDINGS: (1) The whole microarchitecture story is calibrated to one 2021 Xeon Gold 6326; Sapphire/Emerald/Granite Rapids appear once (Appendix E caveat) and Zen 4/5, EPYC, Ryzen X3D never; AMD is 'AMD's equivalents' asides except Ch. 28's pdep/pext. (2) Topology stops at the socket: Ch. 16 is socket-level NUMA only; chiplets, CCD/CCX, tiles, SNC/NPS and core-to-core latency maps are absent, and Ch. 34/37/42/50 say 'pin producer/consumer to two cores' without ever saying which two. (3) Power/frequency depth is a set of single bullets: no C-state exit-latency table, no PM QoS, HWP/EPP, PL1/PL2, uncore frequency scaling, AVX-licence counters or turbostat usage (named but never shown). (4) Hardware prefetchers are described (Ch. 7) but never shown to be switchable (MSR 0x1A4/BIOS). (5) The DRAM subsystem below L3 (channels/ranks/refresh/loaded latency/DDR5/HBM/CXL) is absent. (6) Hardware health/RAS (ECC/CMCI/MCE, patrol scrub, firmware-first SMIs, AER, thermal throttle telemetry, errata) is absent despite Ch. 76's 'continuous monitoring' framing — this is the hardware slice of the author's requested health-monitoring area. (7) Mitigations are generation-agnostic (BHI/Downfall/SRSO/Zenbleed/Reptar absent) and microcode is never treated as a latency variable. (8) PMU coverage is Intel core-PMU only (no IBS, uncore IMC/CHA/UPI, RAPL/SMI events, Intel PT). (9) Hardware selection (5+ GHz desktop parts, P/E hybrids, overclocking, cooling/power envelopes) is absent. I propose 12 items: 6 new chapters/appendices, 6 expansions/sections, prioritised with four 'high'. ACCURACY/CONSISTENCY NITS found en route (for the revision pass): Ch. 44 §44.3 describes the same Xeon Gold 6326 as '~30 MB L3, 12-way' while Ch. 7 §7.3 says '~24 MiB L3' (the SKU has 24 MB); Ch. 44 §44.1 calls the Xeon L3 'inclusive-ish' but Skylake-SP onward (incl. Ice Lake-SP) is non-inclusive, which matters for the DDIO/CAT reasoning in Ch. 44/56; Ch. 6 §6.2.1's 'tens of microseconds' C-state exit latency understates package C6 on Xeon SP (100+ µs per the cpuidle tables) while overstating C1E (low single-digit µs) — a per-state table (proposal 4) fixes both.

--- [hardware-frontier #1] Chiplets & NUMA Within the Socket: AMD CCD/CCX, Intel Tiles & Mesh, SNC/NPS Modes, and the Core-to-Core Latency Map
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part II — CPU Microarchitecture, immediately after Ch. 16 (NUMA Architecture) and before Ch. 17 (Timekeeping); Ch. 16 treats only socket-level NUMA and should hand off to this chapter for the intra-socket axis. | gap=absent | priority=high | area=other
  HFT: In 2026 the HFT fleet is predominantly chiplet or multi-tile silicon (EPYC Genoa/Turin, Ryzen X3D, SPR/EMR/GNR), where the dominant hidden latency cliff is inside the socket, not across it: a producer/consumer pair split across CCDs pays 4–5× the handoff latency of a pair sharing an L3, and the book's 'pin to any isolated core' advice silently permits it. Choosing cores from a measured intra-socket map is now a first-order tick-to-trade decision.
  SCOPE: Why a socket is no longer uniform: AMD Zen 4/Zen 5 chiplets (8-core CCDs each with a private 32 MiB L3; CCX = the L3-sharing domain; the IOD holding memory controllers and PCIe; Infinity Fabric/GMI links), Intel Sapphire/Emerald/Granite Rapids multi-tile dies joined by EMIB over a mesh, with Ice Lake-SP's monolithic mesh as the baseline the book already measures. // Intra-CCX vs cross-CCD vs cross-socket core-to-core latency (~25–40 ns / ~80–120 ns / ~150+ ns) and the fact that a 96-core EPYC is twelve separate L3s — a thread migrating across CCDs silently loses its entire L3. // Intel Sub-NUMA Clustering (SNC2/SNC4) and AMD NPS1/2/4 plus 'L3 cache as NUMA domain' (ACPI SRAT/SLIT) BIOS modes: what each does to `numactl -H`, first-touch placement, L3 locality and memory-bandwidth striping; when exposing CCXs as NUMA nodes is worth the operational complexity. // Measure it: a cache-line ping-pong matrix over every (core_i, core_j) pair rendered as a heat-map, cross-checked against `lstopo`, `lscpu -e`, `/sys/devices/system/cpu/cpuN/cache/index3/shared_cpu_list` and the `numactl -H` distance table; remote-CCD fill attribution with `amd_df`/offcore counters and `perf c2c`. // Techniques: co-locate producer/consumer pairs (SPSC rings Ch. 34, Disruptor Ch. 37, shm IPC Ch. 50) in one CCX/tile; keep feed handler → book → strategy → gateway on cores sharing an L3; put the NIC's poll/IRQ core on the CCD nearest the IOD/PCIe root (Ch. 55–57, 66); per-CCD arenas (Ch. 24); housekeeping on the far die. // Fabric clocks as latency and jitter sources: FCLK/UCLK/MCLK ratios on Ryzen, xGMI link width/speed and Dynamic Link Width Management on EPYC, fabric P-states and the APBDIS / fixed-SoC-P-state BIOS setting; measuring L3-miss latency warm vs after idle. // 3D V-Cache (Ryzen 9950X3D, EPYC Genoa-X/Turin-X): asymmetric CCDs (a 96 MiB-L3 cache die vs a higher-clock die) — which thread goes on which die, the clock penalty vs miss-rate win, and the scheduler's 'preferred core' hints for X3D. // Pitfalls: `numactl --cpunodebind` on NPS1 does not pin to a CCX; `hardware_destructive_interference_size` is still 64 but the coherence *distance* varies by core pair; SNC breaking Ch. 16's 'one node per socket' scripts; mesh position effects even on a monolithic Xeon. // Verify: per-generation `perf` events for remote-CCD/remote-tile fills, and a startup self-check that the chosen core pairs really share an L3.
  EXISTING: 16-numa-architecture.md §16.2.1, §16.7: Nodes are sockets only; 'UPI on Intel, Infinity Fabric on AMD' is one clause; no intra-socket non-uniformity.; appendix-C-system-tuning-checklist.md C.1: One bullet: 'Home Snoop vs cluster-on-die / sub-NUMA-clustering — measure; SNC can lower local latency but complicates topology'.; 06-system-setup-for-low-latency.md §6.4.1, §6.4.2: 'NUMA / sub-NUMA clustering / snoop mode' bullet; topology discovery with lscpu/lstopo but no L3-domain or die reasoning.; 57-flow-steering-receive-scaling.md §57.3: 'cross-core latency' as a generic cost of an RSS handoff.; 34-lock-free-data-structures.md §34.3, §34.7: 'pin producer/consumer to two cores' — no placement rule.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il -E across chapters/*.md: '\bCCD\b' → 0 (case-insensitive hit in Ch. 12 was a substring), 'CCX' → 0, 'chiplet' → 0, 'NPS[0-9]' → 0, 'V-Cache|X3D' → 0, 'FCLK|xGMI|APBDIS|DLWM|Data Fabric|fabric P-state' → 0, 'EPYC|Genoa|Turin|Milan|Bergamo|Ryzen' → 0 real hits ('Turin' matched 'featuring'), 'core-to-core|c2clat|core pair|inter-core latency' → 0 relevant (Ch. 17 'per-core counter', Ch. 57 generic 'cross-core latency'), 'SNC|sub-NUMA|cluster-on-die' → only Ch. 6 §6.4.1 and Appendix C C.1 single bullets, 'Infinity Fabric' → Ch. 16 (2 lines), '\bdie\b|cross-die|SRAT|SLIT|L3 as NUMA' → 0 relevant, 'mesh|ring bus' → 0 relevant. Read Ch. 16 §16.2 in full: the NUMA model is 'NODE 0 (socket 0) / NODE 1 (socket 1)' only.

--- [hardware-frontier #2] Appendix M — AMD Zen (EPYC & Ryzen) Companion: What Changes from the Intel Reference Machine
  kind=new-appendix | placement=After Appendix A (ARM / Graviton), mirroring its chapter-by-chapter 'what changes' structure; cross-referenced from the reference-machine notes in Ch. 2, 6, 7, 10, 28, 29, 44, 56 and from Appendices C/E. | gap=scattered | priority=high | area=other
  HFT: A large share of 2026 HFT fleets run AMD — EPYC Genoa/Turin for feed handling and Ryzen 9950X3D-class parts for the hot box — yet every measurement, counter name and tuning knob in the book is Intel's, with AMD reduced to 'AMD's equivalents' asides. Without this companion a Zen reader cannot reproduce the book's measurements or trust its SIMD, DDIO, PMU and power-management conclusions.
  SCOPE: Zen 3 → Zen 4 → Zen 5 deltas vs Ice Lake-SP for Ch. 7, 11–13, 15: 1 MiB L2, per-CCD 32 MiB victim (non-inclusive) L3 and 96 MiB V-Cache variants, op cache and decode width, 2-ahead branch prediction on Zen 5, load/store and TLB structure — with the Ch. 7 sweep and Ch. 11 dependency-chain benchmark re-run on a Zen part. // PMU on AMD (Ch. 2, 16, 33): `perf` event families (`ls_*`, `ex_ret_*`, `l2_cache_req_stat*`, `amd_l3`, `amd_df`), no Intel-style `topdown-*` (Zen 4/5 pipeline-utilization metrics instead), **IBS** op/fetch sampling (`perf record -e ibs_op//`) as the skid-free, data-address-carrying answer to PEBS — and the fact that `perf mem`/`perf c2c` on AMD depend on it; AMD uProf for the GUI drill-down. // Power and frequency (Ch. 6, Appendix C): `amd_pstate` active/passive/guided modes and EPP, Core Performance Boost, Precision Boost 2 / PBO / Curve Optimizer on Ryzen, the EPYC Determinism slider (Performance vs Power determinism), cTDP/PPT/TDC/EDC limits, Global C-state Control, DF C-states, Power Supply Idle Control; `rdpru` for user-space APERF/MPERF. // SIMD (Ch. 29): Zen 4's double-pumped 256-bit AVX-512 with no licence downclock vs Zen 5's full 512-bit datapath on desktop/server; `-march=znver4/znver5`; re-reading Ch. 29's 'cap at AVX2 on latency cores' rule on AMD. // Instruction-cost deltas (Ch. 11, 28, 32): `pdep/pext` (already in Ch. 28), `rep movsb` (ERMS/FSRM), `clwb/clflushopt`, `monitorx/mwaitx` as the AMD user-space timed spin-wait analog of `tpause`, `cmov`/`imul`/`div` latencies from Agner Fog and uops.info. // Memory and I/O path (Ch. 56, 66): no DDIO — Smart Data Cache Injection (SDCI, Genoa+) and where inbound DMA lands; 12-channel DDR5 per socket on EPYC vs 2-channel Ryzen; PCIe root complexes on the IOD and which CCD is 'near' the NIC. // Platform QoS (Ch. 44): L3 QoS Enforcement / bandwidth enforcement via `resctrl` on AMD — per-CCX scope, CBM granularity and monitoring differences. // Speculative-execution and SMT (Ch. 6, 43): AMD-specific vulnerabilities (Retbleed on Zen 1/2, Zenbleed on Zen 2, Inception/SRSO on Zen 3/4) and their knobs (`spec_rstack_overflow=`), SMT sibling policy on Zen. // Build (Ch. 22, Appendix D): `-march=znver3/4/5`, `-mtune`, glibc `memcpy` dispatch on AMD, TLB/1 GiB-page differences (Ch. 15).
  EXISTING: 28-bit-manipulation-integer-tricks.md §28.2, §28.3, §28.6, §28.7: The only substantive AMD treatment: pdep/pext microcoded before Zen 3; vendor-guarded dispatch.; 02-measure-first-profiling-pmu.md §2.4.3, §2.7: One sentence: 'On AMD, the analogous tool is AMD uProf'; no event names, no IBS.; 44-cache-allocation-intel-rdt.md §44.1, §44.2, §44.5: AMD Platform QoS named as the analog with 'different capacity granularity'.; 56-ddio-nic-cache-data-path.md §56.5, §56.6: 'AMD's data-path behaves differently … verify on your hardware' — no SDCI content.; appendix-C-system-tuning-checklist.md C.2: 'On AMD, the analog is amd_pstate' — one clause.; 22-build-toolchain-for-speed.md §22.4.1: '-march=znver3' as an example deployment target.; appendix-G-annotated-bibliography.md G.2: AMD64 APM and optimization guides listed for 'Zen-specific tuning (Appendix D)' — which Appendix D does not contain.
  EVIDENCE: grep -c '\bAMD\b' → 28 files but almost all single-line asides (the 12 hits in Ch. 28 are the pdep/pext trap); 'Zen' → only Ch. 28 and Appendix G; 'EPYC|Genoa|Milan|Bergamo|Ryzen|9950X|7950X|X3D|V-Cache' → 0; 'uProf' → Ch. 2 (2 lines); '\bIBS\b|Instruction-Based Sampling' → 0 (case-insensitive 'IBS' hits were 'describes'); 'amd_pstate|Precision Boost|PBO|Curve Optimizer|Core Performance Boost|determinism (slider|mode)|cTDP' → only Appendix C C.2 'amd_pstate' once; 'SDCI|Smart Data Cache' → 0; 'znver' → Ch. 22 §22.4.1 once; 'Retbleed|Zenbleed|Inception|SRSO' → Retbleed listed once in Ch. 6, others 0.

--- [hardware-frontier #3] Choosing and Clocking the Hot-Path CPU: Server vs Desktop Silicon, Turbo Tuning, Overclocking & Hybrid P/E Cores
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part I — Foundations & Methodology, directly after Ch. 6 (System Setup for Low Latency) as its hardware-selection companion; Appendix C (tuning) and Appendix K (colocation power/cooling) cross-reference it. | gap=absent | priority=high | area=other
  HFT: Many firms run the hot path on 5+ GHz consumer or workstation silicon because the tick-to-trade kernel is single-thread latency-bound and a ~40% clock advantage over server SKUs is worth more than ECC and memory channels; the book never discusses this procurement-level latency decision, nor the P/E-core and overclocking traps that come with it. A reader cannot currently decide what box to buy from the book.
  SCOPE: Why single-thread latency is clock-bound (ns = cycles / f) and the case for 5.5–6.0 GHz client/workstation parts (Core i9/Core Ultra 9, Ryzen 9 9950X/9950X3D, Xeon W, Threadripper) over 3–4 GHz server SKUs on the tick-to-trade core: ring-bus L3 (~10 ns) vs mesh L3 (~20 ns), fewer cores, higher boost; measured on the Ch. 76 kernel at 3.5 vs 5.8 GHz. // What desktop silicon gives up and how shops hybridize: ECC/RDIMM (DDR5 on-die ECC is not RAS ECC), 2 vs 8–12 memory channels, PCIe lane budget (NIC + FPGA + capture NVMe), no RDT/CAT or DDIO, no BMC/IPMI maturity, vendor support — hot box on desktop silicon, feed/capture/risk on servers. // Intel hybrid (P-core/E-core) client parts: Thread Director, pinning hot threads to P-cores, disabling E-cores in BIOS vs offlining them, the `cpu_core`/`cpu_atom` PMUs, missing AVX-512 on hybrids — the x86 counterpart of Appendix A.5 (which covers only ARM big.LITTLE). // Turbo tuning: per-core turbo ratio limits, 1-core vs all-core turbo, Turbo Boost Max 3.0 'favored cores' (ITMT) and AMD CPPC preferred cores (`amd_pstate_prefcore_ranking`), Thermal Velocity Boost / Adaptive Boost — pinning the hot thread to the favored core vs fixing a static all-core ratio for determinism (Ch. 3, 6). // Overclocking with a correctness budget: multiplier vs BCLK (BCLK shifts TSC and PCIe clocks — Ch. 17, 66), voltage/LLC, AVX offsets, per-core OC, PBO/Curve Optimizer; memory OC (XMP/EXPO, tightened tRCD/tRP/tRFC, Gear 1/2, FCLK 1:1 on AMD); stability testing and the silent-data-corruption risk on price math (Ch. 27, 72) — accept only with ECC or result verification. // Power and thermal envelope as a latency component: PL1/PL2/Tau and RAPL on Intel, PPT/TDC/EDC on AMD, 'unlimited' limits vs sustained-clock thermal headroom, cooling (AIO/custom loop/immersion) and the Appendix K rack power budget; `turbostat` Bzy_MHz/Avg_MHz/PkgWatt/PkgTmp and `perf stat -e ref-cycles,cycles` to see the real clock. // Measure it: the same hot-path kernel p50/p99.9 across fixed vs turbo, P-core vs E-core, X3D cache die vs frequency die, with the cycles-vs-ns cross-check of Ch. 3 §3.4.2. // Pitfalls: a 'TDP-throttled 5 GHz' that sustains 4.2; BCLK OC breaking TSC calibration; XMP instability surfacing as rare wrong fills; the scheduler parking the hot thread on an E-core; cheap-board VRM throttling; warranty/vendor support in a production SLA.
  EXISTING: 06-system-setup-for-low-latency.md §6.2.1, §6.4.1, §6.5: Turbo variance, 'fix the frequency', one 'Uncore/power limits, RAPL' bullet — all server-oriented, no SKU/clock choice.; 03-microbenchmarking-done-right.md §3.4.2: Fix governor/turbo for benchmarks; cycles vs ns cross-check.; appendix-C-system-tuning-checklist.md C.1: Turbo/C-state/power-profile bullets; no favored-core or hybrid guidance.; appendix-K-exchange-connectivity.md K.1: 'dense low-latency servers with high-clock CPUs and FPGAs run hot' — power/cooling per rack only.; appendix-A-arm-graviton.md A.5: Heterogeneous cores treated for ARM big.LITTLE only; no x86 P/E.; 17-timekeeping-tsc-rdtsc-clocks.md §17.6: TSC ticks ≠ core cycles under turbo — relevant to BCLK overclocking but not connected.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il -E across chapters/*.md: 'desktop|workstation' → 0; 'overclock|\bPBO\b|Precision Boost|Curve Optimizer' → 0; '\bP-core|\bE-core|[Ee]fficiency core|[Pp]erformance core|Thread Director' → 0 (case-insensitive 'E-core' hits were 'single-core'); 'hybrid|heterogeneous' → only ARM big.LITTLE (Appendix A.5), 'hybrid wait strategies' (Ch. 41) and MPI+OpenMP (Ch. 68); 'favored|favoured|preferred core|ITMT|Turbo Boost Max|CPPC' → 0; 'PL1|PL2|\bTDP\b|power cap' → 0 ('power limit' once in Ch. 6 §6.4.1); 'turbo (bin|ratio|tuning)|all-core|single-core turbo' → only 'turbo bin' in Ch. 6 §6.5; '5 ?GHz' → generic clock mentions in Ch. 3/17/29; 'SKU|binning|core count|clock speed|hardware (choice|selection)|which (CPU|server|box)' in 00-preface/01/06 → no hardware-selection discussion.

--- [hardware-frontier #4] Power Management & Frequency Determinism in Depth: C-state Exit Latencies, PM QoS, HWP/EPP, Power Limits, AVX Licences and Uncore Frequency Scaling
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part VII — OS, Scheduling & Isolation, between Ch. 45 (Real-Time Scheduling & Kernel Tuning) and Ch. 46 (Keeping the Hot Path Warm); Ch. 6 keeps its overview and points here, Appendix C gains the verification commands. | gap=partial | priority=high | area=other
  HFT: Ch. 6's 'cap C-states, fix the governor' is where the clock tail starts, not where it ends: on 2026 silicon HWP/EPP, uncore frequency ramps, AVX licence transitions and power caps each add 1–100 µs to the first message after a quiet spell, and the book names them in single bullets without a mechanism, a knob list or a measurement. A flat p99.9 at the open that fattens mid-day is almost always one of these.
  SCOPE: The idle-state table, per generation: C1/C1E/C6 and package C-states with measured exit latency and target residency from `/sys/devices/system/cpu/cpuN/cpuidle/state*/{latency,residency,usage,time}`, `cpupower idle-info`, `turbostat --show CPU%c1,CPU%c6,Pkg%pc6`; why Ch. 6's 'tens of µs' is really ~1–2 µs (C1E) to 100+ µs (PC6) depending on part and firmware. // Runtime idle control beyond the kernel cmdline: PM QoS via `/dev/cpu_dma_latency` (hold it open with 0 from the trading process), per-state `disable` in sysfs, `intel_idle` vs `acpi_idle`, `cpuidle.off=1`, the real cost of `idle=poll` (thermal/turbo headroom, SMT sibling, power) — and whether an isolated busy-polling core ever idles at all. // Frequency control: `intel_pstate` active/passive, HWP (hardware P-states) and EPP (`energy_performance_preference`), `amd_pstate` modes, `scaling_min/max_freq`, pinning a static ratio vs `no_turbo`; APERF/MPERF and `ref-cycles` to see the actual clock; `x86_energy_perf_policy`; why `cpupower` writes can be silently ignored under HWP. // Power limits and thermal events: PL1/PL2/Tau and RAPL (`/sys/class/powercap/intel-rapl`, MSRs), PPT/TDC/EDC on AMD, BMC-imposed caps, PROCHOT/thermal throttling (`/sys/devices/system/cpu/cpuN/thermal_throttle/*`, `turbostat` PkgTmp) — why a power-capped box has a clock tail, not just a lower mean. // AVX frequency licences per generation (L0/L1/L2 on Skylake-SP/Ice Lake, SPR's near-flat behaviour, Zen 4's double-pumped AVX-512) and the ~ms licence-transition stall, read via `core_power.lvl*_turbo_license` counters — the measurement Ch. 29 §29.3 asks for but does not show. // Uncore Frequency Scaling (UFS): the mesh/LLC/memory-controller clock ramps independently of the cores and adds L3/DRAM latency after idle; `intel_uncore_frequency` sysfs (`min_freq_khz`/`max_freq_khz`), MSR 0x620, the BIOS UFS switch; AMD fabric P-states and APBDIS — with an L3-hit-latency warm-vs-after-idle measurement. // Device-side power states: PCIe ASPM L0s/L1 (`pcie_aspm=off`, per-device `link/l1_aspm`), NIC and NVMe power states, USB/SATA wakeups (ties Ch. 55, 66). // Measure it: cyclictest `-i` sweep across idle intervals with each C-state enabled in turn; first-message latency after 1/10/100 ms idle with uncore pinned vs scaling; scalar hot-path latency beside an AVX-512 neighbour with licence counters; `turbostat` traces aligned to the latency histogram. // Pitfalls: `idle=poll` on SMT siblings; `tuned`/udev resetting the governor; BIOS 'Energy Efficient Turbo' and 'HWP native' overriding the OS; thermal headroom consumed by polling cores; RAPL caps re-applied by the BMC after a firmware update.
  EXISTING: 06-system-setup-for-low-latency.md §6.2.1, §6.4.1, §6.4.3: C-state/P-state/turbo overview ('tens of microseconds' exit latency, no table); one 'Uncore/power limits, RAPL' bullet; cmdline cap and no_turbo.; 29-simd-vectorization.md §29.2, §29.3, §29.7: AVX licences conceptually; turbostat named for the downclock measurement but no counters or output shown.; appendix-C-system-tuning-checklist.md C.1–C.3, C.5: BIOS/cmdline/governor bullets; 'disable processor power-capping (RAPL limits)'; `cpupower idle-info` as verification.; 45-realtime-scheduling-kernel-tuning.md §45.4, §45.5: Firmware layer recap: C-states, P-states/turbo, SMIs.; 46-keeping-the-hot-path-warm.md §46.2: Resource decay table covers caches/TLB/predictors/TCP/NIC but not clock or uncore frequency decay.

--- [hardware-frontier #5] Controlling the Hardware Prefetchers: MSR 0x1A4, BIOS Prefetch Knobs, and When Turning Them Off Flattens the Tail
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 10 (Software Prefetching & Non-Temporal Stores): new §10.4.3 'Controlling the hardware prefetchers' plus a toggle experiment added to §10.3; one-line cross-refs from Ch. 7 §7.2.4 and Ch. 56 §56.4, and a knob row in Appendix C §C.1/C.3. | gap=absent | priority=medium | area=other
  HFT: A hot core whose working set already fits L2 gains nothing from streamer/adjacent-line prefetch and pays for it in evicted book lines and DDIO pollution; several shops ship with specific prefetchers disabled on hot cores and enabled on batch cores. The book explains what the prefetchers do (Ch. 7) and how to add software prefetch (Ch. 10) but never that they are switchable, or how to measure the trade.
  SCOPE: The Intel core prefetchers and their control bits in IA32_MISC_FEATURE_CONTROL (MSR 0x1A4: L2 hardware prefetcher, L2 adjacent-cache-line, DCU streamer, DCU IP), newer additions (L2 AMP on Ice Lake+/SPR, LLC/XPT/UPI prefetch in the Xeon SP uncore), and AMD's L1/L2 stream, region and up/down prefetchers with their BIOS switches. // How to flip them: `msr-tools` (`rdmsr -p N 0x1a4`, `wrmsr -p N 0x1a4 0xf`), the `msr` module and its lockdown/Secure Boot implications (`msr.allow_writes`), BIOS 'Hardware Prefetcher / Adjacent Cache Line Prefetch / DCU Streamer / DCU IP' and AMD 'L1/L2 Stream HW Prefetcher'; per-core MSR scope vs whole-socket BIOS scope; persisting the setting at boot. // Why a hot core may want some of them off: adjacent-line and streamer prefetches pollute L1/L2 and the DDIO/CAT region (Ch. 44, 56), consume LFB/superqueue slots the pointer-chasing book needs (Ch. 10 §10.4), fetch uselessly across 4 KiB page boundaries, and raise memory-bandwidth and power — a tail effect on a working set that already fits. // Measure it: the Ch. 7 pointer-chase sweep and the Ch. 25 order-book benchmark with each prefetcher bit toggled; `L2_RQSTS.ALL_PF`, `L2_LINES_IN.ALL`, `OFFCORE_REQUESTS.ALL_DATA_RD` to quantify prefetch traffic; first-packet RX latency under DDIO with prefetchers on/off; batch/risk core throughput to show the cost of disabling globally. // Interaction with software prefetch (`prefetcht0/nta/w`) and with disabling prefetch as a benchmarking tool to expose the true miss profile (Ch. 3). // Pitfalls: disabling on the housekeeping/throughput cores that need streaming; MSR writes lost on reboot or microcode reload; vendor BIOS names that do not map 1:1 to MSR bits; writing 0x1A4 on hybrid/E-core parts; treating prefetcher-off as a universal win rather than a per-core measured choice.
  EXISTING: 07-memory-hierarchy-and-caches.md §7.2.4: Describes next-line/adjacent-line, stride and L2 stream prefetchers and what they can/cannot predict; no controls.; 10-software-prefetching-nontemporal-stores.md §10.1, §10.4.1, §10.6: Software prefetch as the fallback; 'prefetching what the hardware already handles' pitfall; LFB limit noted.; 56-ddio-nic-cache-data-path.md §56.4: DDIO way count 'via MSR or BIOS' — the only MSR mention in the book.; appendix-C-system-tuning-checklist.md C.1: No prefetcher knob in the firmware list.
  EVIDENCE: grep -n -i 'prefetcher' in 07/10/appendix-C filtered for 'disabl|BIOS|MSR|control|adjacent|streamer|DCU|knob' → only Ch. 7 §7.2.4's description of the 'next-line / adjacent-line prefetcher'; '0x1A4|wrmsr|rdmsr|msr-tools|modprobe msr|/dev/cpu/' → 0; '\bMSR' → Ch. 56 only (DDIO); 'prefetcher (control|disable|off)|disable.*prefetcher' → 0 across all chapters.

--- [hardware-frontier #6] Per-Generation Speculative-Execution Mitigations and the Microcode Dimension
  kind=expand-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 6 (System Setup) §6.2.3 and §6.4.3: expand into a vulnerability × generation matrix and a 'microcode & firmware change control' subsection; add per-vulnerability cmdline rows to Appendix C §C.2 and a `/sys/devices/system/cpu/vulnerabilities` + microcode-revision check to §C.5; one cross-ref from Ch. 72 §72.4.4. | gap=partial | priority=medium | area=other
  HFT: The book's `mitigations=off` discussion is generation-agnostic, but the cost and even the applicability of each mitigation is a per-silicon fact — Meltdown/MDS are free on modern parts while SRSO, BHI or GDS can still take 5–30% off indirect-heavy or gather-heavy hot paths. Worse, mitigations arrive through microcode updates that distros install silently, so a tick-to-trade box can regress with no code change; the book never treats microcode as a latency variable.
  SCOPE: Matrix of vulnerabilities × generations: hardware-fixed on Ice Lake+/Zen (Meltdown, L1TF, MDS; eIBRS replacing retpoline) vs still-costly (BHI on Alder Lake/SPR+ → `spectre_bhi=`/BHI_DIS_S; Retbleed on Zen 2/Skylake; SRSO/Inception on Zen 3/4 with safe-RET or IBPB-on-entry; Downfall/GDS on Skylake→Tiger Lake and its gather slowdown; RFDS on E-cores/Atom; TAA, SRBDS, MMIO stale data; Reptar) and the knob for each (`spectre_v2=`, `spec_rstack_overflow=`, `gather_data_sampling=`, `retbleed=`, `mds=`, `tsx_async_abort=`, `mitigations=auto,nosmt`). // Reading the state: `/sys/devices/system/cpu/vulnerabilities/*`, the `lscpu` Vulnerabilities block, `dmesg | grep -iE 'spectre|retbleed|srso|gds'`, `spectre-meltdown-checker`; `/proc/cpuinfo microcode` and `dmesg | grep microcode` for the loaded revision. // Cost attribution per mitigation with the book's own harnesses: indirect-call benchmark (Ch. 14) for retpoline/eIBRS/BHI, syscall ping-pong (Ch. 41) for KPTI/IBPB/VERW, gather-heavy SIMD (Ch. 29) for GDS — leading to the per-generation conclusion that on Ice Lake+/Zen 4 most of `mitigations=off`'s win is gone except on indirect-branch- and syscall-heavy paths. // Microcode as a latency variable: early (initrd) vs late (`/sys/devices/system/cpu/microcode/reload`) loading; distro `intel-ucode`/`amd-ucode` auto-updates landing a new mitigation (GDS, SRSO, Reptar) or changing instruction behaviour (`pdep/pext`, `rep movsb`, AVX licences) and regressing p99.9 overnight; pinning revisions, `iucode-tool`, `dis_ucode_ldr`, and treating BIOS/microcode like a code deploy with a before/after Ch. 3 run. // Compiler-side counterparts and their interactions: `-mindirect-branch=thunk`/`-mretpoline`, `-mfunction-return=thunk`, `-mharden-sls`, `-fcf-protection` (ties Ch. 72 §72.4.4's hardening table). // Pitfalls: quoting 2019 Skylake `mitigations=off` gains on a 2026 part; a BMC/firmware update re-enabling mitigations; `nosmt` silently halving the core count (Ch. 43); forgetting that hypervisor/guest mitigation state differs on any virtualised research box.
  EXISTING: 06-system-setup-for-low-latency.md §6.2.3, §6.3, §6.4.3, §6.5, §6.6: Lists Spectre/Meltdown/MDS/L1TF/Retbleed, retpoline/IBRS/eIBRS, KPTI; 'boot with and without mitigations= and measure'; decision discipline. Generation-agnostic.; 41-context-switching-mitigation.md §41.3, §41.5, §41.6: KPTI inflates syscall cost; exercise: measure with mitigations on/off.; 72-secure-programming-low-latency.md §72.2.3, §72.4.4, §72.5: Hardening table incl. retpoline/CFI/CET interactions; 'measure the combination'.; appendix-C-system-tuning-checklist.md C.2, C.5: `mitigations=` bullet; verify `/sys/devices/system/cpu/vulnerabilities/*`.; 27-fixed-point-floating-point-arithmetic.md §27.2.3: 'microcode' appears only as the denormal microcode assist (and in Ch. 28 as pdep/pext microcoding) — never as updatable firmware.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il -E across chapters/*.md: 'BHI|Downfall|\bGDS\b|Zenbleed|Inception|SRSO|RFDS|Reptar|IBPB|STIBP' → 0; 'Retbleed' → Ch. 6 once (listed, not discussed); 'eIBRS' → Ch. 6 once; 'microcode update|ucode|iucode|microcode/reload|dis_ucode' → 0 ('microcode' only in Ch. 27 denormal assist and Ch. 28 pdep/pext); 'spectre_v2=|spec_rstack|gather_data_sampling|retbleed=|spectre_bhi' → 0; 'errat' → 0; 'BIOS update|firmware update' → Ch. 45 §45.5, Ch. 64 §64.5, Appendix C C.5 only as 'can silently re-introduce jitter'.

--- [hardware-frontier #7] Hardware Health, RAS & Errata: ECC Storms, Machine Checks, Patrol Scrub, Firmware-First SMIs and Hardware Telemetry for the Trading Box
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part XII — Observability & Operations in Production, after Ch. 74 (Process Topology & the Deterministic State Machine) and before Ch. 75 (Capture) — or immediately before Ch. 76 — as the hardware layer of the continuous monitoring Ch. 76 §76.2 assumes; Appendix C §C.1/C.5 gains the RAS-mode and telemetry items. | gap=absent | priority=high | area=health-monitoring-os-net
  HFT: A trading box can satisfy every item in Appendix C and still stall for a millisecond because a DIMM threw a corrected error under firmware-first handling, or lose memory bandwidth to a patrol scrubber — and the first the team hears of it is a missed fill. Hardware health (ECC/MCE trends, thermal and frequency throttling, AER, SMIs) is the layer of production monitoring Part XII omits, and it is exactly the OS/hardware-level system-health area the author asked about.
  SCOPE: The RAS stack from the latency angle: corrected vs uncorrected errors, ECC (SECDED; DDR5 on-die vs side-band), CMCI — corrected-error interrupts landing on *your* isolated core — MCE/MCA banks, `mce=` cmdline, `/sys/devices/system/machinecheck/`, EDAC counters (`/sys/devices/system/edac/mc/*/ce_count`), `rasdaemon`/`ras-mc-ctl`, the Corrected Error Collector (`ras=cec_disable`), SIGBUS delivery of UCEs and crash-only handling (ties Ch. 74). // Firmware-first vs OS-native error handling (eMCA/GHES/APEI): firmware-first turns every corrected DIMM error into an SMI — the classic unexplained 100 µs–ms stall on a 'quiet' box; detecting with `hwlat`, the SMI_COUNT MSR (0x34) / `perf stat -e msr/smi/`, and choosing OS-native mode plus CMCI thresholds in BIOS. // Memory scrubbing and refresh as periodic DRAM taxes: patrol scrub interval/bandwidth and demand scrub, ADDDC/rank sparing/mirroring modes and their steady-state latency cost; DRAM refresh (tREFI/tRFC, DDR5 same-bank refresh) as a ~100–350 ns stall you cannot disable — what it does to p99.99 and how to see it in a fine-grained histogram (Ch. 3). // PCIe AER correctable-error storms, NIC link flaps/retrains, NVMe SMART and thermal throttling on the capture path (ties Ch. 55, 66, 75) — device-side health that becomes hot-path jitter. // CPU errata and silicon ageing: reading Intel Specification Updates / AMD Revision Guides, microcode workarounds that remove features (TSX, instruction behaviour), Vmin-shift / Raptor-Lake-style instability on high-clock parts, burn-in and golden-image validation of a new box, predictive replacement. // Hardware telemetry for production monitoring (the hardware slice of Ch. 76 §76.2): `turbostat` (frequency, C-state residency, PkgWatt, PkgTmp, throttle counts), `thermal_throttle/*`, RAPL, `rasdaemon` events, `ipmitool sel/sdr` and Redfish (PSU, fans, inlet temperature), SMI counts, `/proc/interrupts` deltas on isolated cores, `ethtool -S` hardware drop counters — exported as time series and alerted on before they become a latency event; a readiness gate that refuses to trade on a box with rising CE counts. // Measure it: an injected corrected error (EINJ/`mce-inject`) and its cyclictest tail under firmware-first vs OS-native handling; a patrol-scrub interval sweep against a DRAM-bound hot path's p99.9; the frequency/latency signature of a thermal-throttle event. // Pitfalls: `mcelog` is dead on modern kernels (use `rasdaemon`); disabling ECC 'for latency' on a box that holds positions; ignoring correctable-error trends until the UCE panic at the open; SMIs misattributed to the kernel; a BIOS update flipping RAS mode back to firmware-first.
  EXISTING: appendix-C-system-tuning-checklist.md C.1, C.5: 'Disable SMIs where possible … thermal/legacy-USB SMIs'; `hwlat` detector in the verification pass; 're-verify after a BIOS update'. No RAS mode, ECC, scrub or telemetry.; 45-realtime-scheduling-kernel-tuning.md §45.4, §45.5, §45.6: SMIs as invisible firmware jitter; `cyclictest -b` + ftrace to find the last spike.; 06-system-setup-for-low-latency.md §6.4.1: 'a thermally-throttled core is a tail event' — one clause.; 76-production-profiling-end-to-end-case-study.md §76.2: Continuous monitoring = HdrHistogram per stage, PMU counters, drops/gaps/queue depths/restarts — no hardware telemetry.; 74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.2.4, §74.4.5: Crash-only design and core dumps for software faults; hardware faults (UCE/SIGBUS) not covered.

--- [hardware-frontier #8] The DRAM Subsystem Below L3: DDR4/DDR5 Channels, Ranks & Interleaving, Loaded Latency, Refresh, HBM and CXL Memory Tiering
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part II — CPU Microarchitecture, directly after Ch. 7 (The Memory Hierarchy & Caches) as the 'below L3' chapter and before Ch. 8; Ch. 16 (NUMA) and Ch. 44 (MBA) then build on it. (Fallback: a §7.2.5 in Ch. 7 plus a §16.2.3 in Ch. 16.) | gap=absent | priority=medium | area=other
  HFT: The order book's cold misses and the capture/logging writers' streams meet in the same memory controller, and under load the hot path's miss latency can double without a single cache-miss counter changing — a tail source the cache chapters (which stop at 'DRAM ≈ 80 ns') cannot explain. With DDR5, CXL tiers and HBM parts now in trading fleets, the reader needs the memory-controller level of the hierarchy and how to populate, configure and measure it.
  SCOPE: What a DRAM access actually is: channels → DIMMs → ranks → bank groups → banks → rows; row-buffer hits vs misses and open/closed page policy; tCL/tRCD/tRP/tRAS — why '~80 ns' is an average of ~45 ns row hits and 100+ ns row conflicts, and why the order book's miss latency is bimodal. // Unloaded vs loaded latency: Little's law, the Intel MLC `--loaded_latency` curve and its knee, how a neighbour's bandwidth (capture writer Ch. 75, batch risk, logging Ch. 71) turns 80 ns into 150+ ns for the hot path with no change in cache-miss counters; MBA (Ch. 44) as the remedy and its limits. // Channel and rank population rules on servers (populate all 8/12 channels, 1DPC vs 2DPC speed bins, rank and channel interleave granularity, BIOS 'Memory Interleaving'/'Rank Interleave'), versus the 2-channel reality on desktop parts; `dmidecode -t memory`, `decode-dimms` to audit a box. // DDR5 specifics: two 32-bit sub-channels, on-die ECC (not RAS ECC), same-bank refresh and Refresh Management, 4800–8800 MT/s with flat latency in ns, memory-controller clock coupling (Intel Gear 1/2/4; AMD UCLK:MCLK 1:1 and FCLK) — choosing frequency and timings for latency, not bandwidth. // Refresh as a periodic stall: tREFI 3.9/7.8 µs, tRFC ~300–400 ns growing with die density, per-bank vs all-bank refresh — the p99.99 floor nobody can tune away, and how to recognise it in a fine-grained latency histogram (Ch. 3). // Measuring: `mlc` (idle latency, loaded latency, bandwidth matrix), `lat_mem_rd`, `perf stat -e uncore_imc/cas_count_read/`, row-hit/precharge counters (`UNC_M_PRE_COUNT.PAGE_MISS`), and a loaded-latency experiment pairing the Ch. 7 pointer chase with a streaming neighbour. // Beyond DDR: HBM on Xeon Max (HBM-only / flat / cache modes) for research and risk; CXL memory expanders (CXL 1.1/2.0, kernel memory tiers, demotion/promotion, CXL nodes in `numactl -H` at distance 3–4) — why they belong off the order path (150–300 ns) and how to stop the kernel demoting hot pages onto them. // Pitfalls: half-populated channels on a 'fast' box; interleave settings striping one hot structure across sockets; letting THP/compaction or NUMA balancing migrate the book to a CXL tier; mistaking bandwidth headroom for latency headroom; XMP/EXPO timings that pass memtest and still corrupt rarely.
  EXISTING: 07-memory-hierarchy-and-caches.md §7.1, §7.2.1, §7.3: DRAM is a single ~80 ns level ('far below: NVMe'); no channels, ranks, refresh or loaded latency.; 16-numa-architecture.md §16.2.1, §16.4.1: 'own memory controllers + attached DRAM' per node; `--interleave` for bandwidth-bound shared data.; 44-cache-allocation-intel-rdt.md §44.2, §44.4.2, §44.5: MBA throttling bandwidth-hungry neighbours; MBM monitoring; 'bandwidth, not capacity' pitfall.; appendix-E-latency-numbers.md E.1, E.2: 'DDR4/5' caveat; MLC and `lat_mem_rd` named for cache/DRAM rows.; appendix-C-system-tuning-checklist.md C.1: 'Disable node interleaving' only.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il -E across chapters/*.md: 'DDR5|\bHBM\b|\bCXL\b|memory tier|memory channel|\bDIMM|DRAM refresh|tRFC|bank conflict|DRAM bank' → 0 ('DDR4' once in Appendix E's caveat); 'memory controller' → Ch. 16 §16.2.1 and Ch. 44 (MBA) only; 'rank' → no DRAM sense anywhere (MPI ranks, rankings); 'memory bandwidth|bandwidth-bound' → Ch. 6/16/44/49/67/68 only in the neighbour-throttling or GPU sense; 'loaded latency|Little' → not grepped as such, but Ch. 7 §7.3 and Appendix E E.2 read in full show unloaded pointer-chase latency only.

--- [hardware-frontier #9] Core-to-Core Latency Maps: Choosing Which Cores Host Queues, Pinned Threads and IRQs
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 42 (Thread & Interrupt Pinning): new §42.4.3 'Choosing which cores — the core-to-core latency map', with the harness also added to Appendix L and one-line cross-refs from Ch. 34 §34.3, Ch. 37 §37.3, Ch. 50 §50.3 and Ch. 43 §43.4.1 ('pin producer/consumer to two cores' → 'to the right two cores'). | gap=absent | priority=high | area=other
  HFT: Every lock-free handoff in Part VI costs one or two cache-line transfers, and that transfer varies 5–8× with which two cores you picked — a larger swing than most of the queue micro-optimisations the book spends pages on. The book says 'pin the producer and consumer to two cores' but never how to choose them, and on chiplet and mesh silicon the wrong choice is the default one.
  SCOPE: A ~30-line cache-line ping-pong harness timing round trips with `rdtscp` (Ch. 17) for every (core_i, core_j) pair, rendered as a heat-map; reading its block structure: SMT siblings (~10–20 ns), same L3/CCX (~25–40 ns), cross-CCD or cross-tile (~80–120 ns), cross-socket (~130–200 ns), and mesh-position effects even on a monolithic Xeon. // Placement rules derived from the map: SPSC/Disruptor producer–consumer on the same L3 but not on SMT siblings (Ch. 43); feed handler → book → strategy → gateway as a chain of adjacent cores; the NIC's poll/IRQ core adjacent to its consumer and on the NIC's socket/CCD (Ch. 55–57, 66); housekeeping on the far die; logger/capture consumers one hop away, not zero. // Tooling: `c2clat`/core-to-core-latency-style open-source tools, `lstopo` for the L3-sharing map, `numactl -H` distances, `/sys/devices/system/cpu/cpuN/cache/index3/shared_cpu_list`; automating placement at startup from a measured matrix rather than hard-coded CPU numbers (extends Ch. 43 §43.4.1's 'discover at startup'). // Verifying in production: `perf c2c` local vs remote HITM, offcore-response / `amd_df` remote-fill counters; re-measuring after BIOS SNC/NPS or HT changes. // Pitfalls: assuming consecutive CPU numbers are adjacent cores; measuring on SMT siblings and mistaking the fast number for 'good'; measuring with turbo/uncore scaling enabled (Ch. 6) and getting a bimodal map; forgetting that the map changes with firmware topology modes.
  EXISTING: 42-thread-interrupt-pinning.md §42.4.1, §42.4.2: `taskset`/`sched_setaffinity` mechanics and IRQ routing; no guidance on which cores.; 43-smt-hyperthreading.md §43.4.1: Sibling detection via `thread_siblings_list`/`lscpu -e`; 'discover at startup, don't hard-code'.; 06-system-setup-for-low-latency.md §6.4.2: Topology discovery with `lscpu --extended`/`lstopo`; 'pin based on this map' (sibling/NUMA level only).; 34-lock-free-data-structures.md §34.3, §34.7: 'pin producer/consumer to two cores'; cross-core index caching.; 37-the-disruptor-pattern.md §37.7: 'pin producer/consumer' with no placement rule.; appendix-E-latency-numbers.md E.1: Cross-core HITM as a single 40–100 ns row.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il -E across chapters/*.md: 'core-to-core|c2clat|core pair|inter-core latency|latency between cores' → 0 relevant (Ch. 17 'per-core counter'; Ch. 57 generic 'cross-core latency'); in 42-thread-interrupt-pinning.md 'which core|choose|pick|same L3|same CCX|adjacent core|core pair|producer.*consumer' → 0; in 34/37/50 'same socket|same L3|same CCX|core pair|adjacent|pin (the )?producer' → only 'pin producer/consumer to two cores' instructions; Ch. 42 section headers read: §42.4 has only 'taskset/sched_setaffinity' and 'IRQ affinity' techniques.

--- [hardware-frontier #10] Beyond the Core PMU: AMD IBS, Intel Uncore (IMC/CHA/UPI/IIO), RAPL & SMI Events, and Intel Processor Trace
  kind=expand-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 2 (Measure First: Profiling & Hardware Performance Counters): new §2.4.4 'Beyond the core PMU — uncore, vendor-specific sampling and hardware telemetry'; copy-paste recipes added to Appendix I §I.2/I.3; Intel PT cross-referenced from Ch. 5 §5.4.3 (production tracing). | gap=partial | priority=medium | area=debugging-diagnostics
  HFT: Attributing a tail event to DRAM bandwidth, a cross-CCD fill, a DDIO miss, an AVX licence change or an SMI requires counters outside the core PMU — precisely the ones the book never shows — and half the readership is on AMD, where `perf mem` and `perf c2c` work only through IBS. Intel PT is additionally the only way to see the exact instruction path of a one-off 50 µs outlier on a live hot core.
  SCOPE: Core-PMU portability: Intel TMA `topdown-*` vs AMD Zen 4/5 pipeline-utilization metrics; event-family mapping (`mem_load_retired.*` ↔ `ls_dmnd_fills_from_sys.*`, `l2_cache_req_stat*`), and what `perf stat --topdown` does (or doesn't) on AMD. // AMD IBS op/fetch sampling: skid-free samples carrying data addresses and cache/TLB/DRAM source via `perf record -e ibs_op//`; `perf mem` and `perf c2c` on AMD rely on it; limits vs PEBS/LBR; AMD uProf for the GUI drill-down. // Uncore PMUs: `uncore_imc` (CAS counts → bandwidth and row-hit rate), `uncore_cha`/`cbox` (LLC lookups, DDIO/IO allocations — Ch. 56), `uncore_upi`/`amd_df` (cross-socket and cross-CCD traffic — Ch. 16), `uncore_iio` (PCIe inbound/outbound — Ch. 66); `perf stat -e uncore_imc_0/cas_count_read/` recipes; why uncore counters are socket-scoped rather than per-process and how to attribute them. // Energy, frequency and jitter telemetry through `perf`: `power/energy-pkg/`, `msr/aperf/`, `msr/mperf/`, `msr/tsc/`, `msr/smi/` (the SMI counter as a jitter detector), `core_power.lvl*_turbo_license` (AVX licences — Ch. 29), thermal-throttle events. // Intel Processor Trace: cycle-accurate control-flow tracing (`perf record -e intel_pt//`, `perf script --itrace`), snapshot/ring-buffer mode to answer 'what executed in the 2 µs before this outlier' on a production core — the hardware side of Ch. 5's tracing; the absence of an AMD equivalent. // Pitfalls: multiplexing across uncore boxes; `perf_event_paranoid` and uncore access; IBS sampling rate vs hot-core jitter; PT bandwidth and decode time; vendor event-name churn across kernel versions and generations.
  EXISTING: 02-measure-first-profiling-pmu.md §2.2.1–§2.4.3, §2.7: Core PMU, top-down, perf stat/record, PEBS/LBR mentioned, VTune; 'On AMD, the analogous tool is AMD uProf' — no uncore, IBS, PT or RAPL.; 16-numa-architecture.md §16.3: `UNC_M_*`/offcore-response remote-DRAM events named in passing.; 33-false-sharing-thread-safety-anomalies.md §33.3: HITM counters and `perf c2c` (Intel).; appendix-I-tooling-cookbook.md I.1–I.3: perf stat/record, top-down, c2c/mem recipes — Intel-only, core PMU only.; 05-debugging-low-latency-optimized-code.md §5.4.3: Production tracing without perturbing the hot path — software tracing; no hardware trace.
  EVIDENCE: In 02-measure-first-profiling-pmu.md, grep -n -E '\bIBS\b|uncore|Processor Trace|Intel PT|RAPL|energy|\bIMC\b|\bCHA\b|UPI' → 0; across all chapters 'Processor Trace|Intel PT' → 0; '\bIBS\b|Instruction-Based Sampling' → 0 (case-insensitive hits were 'describes'); 'uncore' → Ch. 6 (power bullet), Ch. 16 (one counter mention), Ch. 56 (vendor briefs) only; 'power/energy|energy-pkg|smi_count|msr/' → 0; 'uProf' → Ch. 2 two lines.

--- [hardware-frontier #11] BIOS/Firmware Knob Reference for Intel Xeon SP and AMD EPYC/Ryzen (beyond C-states, turbo and SMT)
  kind=expand-existing-chapter | placement=Appendix C (System Tuning Checklist) §C.1: expand the seven generic firmware bullets into a vendor-by-vendor table (Intel Xeon SP Ice Lake → Granite Rapids; AMD EPYC Milan → Turin; Ryzen desktop) with default, low-latency value, the latency mechanism, the chapter that measures it, and the Linux-side verification; add a 'BIOS settings in version control' item to §C.5. | gap=partial | priority=medium | area=other
  HFT: Appendix C's firmware list is seven vendor-agnostic bullets, yet the knobs that actually move p99.9 on 2026 servers — prefetchers, uncore frequency scaling, SNC/NPS, APBDIS/DF C-states, patrol scrub, firmware-first RAS, memory encryption — are vendor- and generation-specific with non-obvious names in each BIOS. A reader bringing up an EPYC Turin or Granite Rapids box cannot find them from the book, even though Ch. 6 §6.4.1 promises that 'Appendix C maps the common ones'.
  SCOPE: Intel Xeon SP knobs: Hardware/Adjacent-Line/DCU prefetchers, LLC Prefetch, XPT & UPI Prefetch, LLC Dead Line Alloc, Stale AtoS, Snoop/Directory mode & SNC, Uncore Frequency Scaling, Energy Efficient Turbo, HWP native mode & EPP, Package C-state limit & C1E, per-core-count turbo ratio limits, Speed Select (SST-BF/CP/TF/PP) for high-priority cores, Patrol Scrub & interval, ADDDC/Mirroring/Rank Sparing, Memory frequency/Gear & interleave, VT-d/IOMMU & ATS, PCIe ASPM/L1 substates, SR-IOV, TXT/SGX/TDX (off), WHEA/firmware-first vs OS-native RAS, SMI sources (USB legacy, thermal, ECC), Total Memory Encryption (measure its latency cost). // AMD EPYC knobs: NPS1/2/4, L3 cache as NUMA domain, Determinism slider & cTDP/PPT, Global C-state Control, DF C-states, APBDIS & fixed SoC P-state, xGMI link width/speed & DLWM, IOMMU, L1/L2 Stream HW Prefetcher, memory interleaving (channel/die/socket), Power Supply Idle Control, Core Performance Boost, Preferred IO device, SEV/SME (off). // Ryzen/desktop knobs: PBO/Curve Optimizer, EXPO/FCLK ratio, CPPC preferred cores, SMT, CCD/'Game Mode' controls on X3D parts, PCIe ASPM, C-states and 'Power Supply Idle Control'. // For every knob: the mechanism it affects (prefetch pollution, uncore clock ramp, fabric P-state, scrub bandwidth, SMI generation, IOMMU translation), the chapter that explains it (Ch. 7/10, 16, 44, 56, 66 and the proposed power/RAS chapters), and how to confirm it took from Linux (`rdmsr`, sysfs, `dmesg`, `dmidecode`, `numactl -H`, `cpupower`, `/proc/cmdline`). // A 'knob diff' discipline: export settings via Redfish/`racadm`/`ilorest`/`syscfg`/`SUM` into version control and diff after every firmware or BMC update (ties Appendix C §C.5 'tuning is defended continuously' and the microcode proposal). // Pitfalls: vendor names that do not map 1:1 to MSR bits; a 'Low Latency' BIOS profile silently re-enabling prefetchers or firmware-first RAS; settings reset by a BMC factory default; knobs that only exist on some SKUs/generations.

--- [hardware-frontier #12] Appendix N — x86 Microarchitecture Generation Cheat Sheet (Ice Lake → Sapphire/Emerald/Granite Rapids; Zen 3 → Zen 5) and On-Die Accelerators
  kind=new-appendix | placement=After Appendix E (Latency Numbers) as its per-generation companion; referenced from the reference-machine notes in Ch. 7, 11, 12, 13, 16, 29 and the Preface's benchmark caveat, and from Appendix D's `-march` table. | gap=scattered | priority=medium | area=other
  HFT: The microarchitecture chapters are calibrated to a 2021 Ice Lake-SP, and a reader on 2024–2026 silicon (Granite Rapids, Zen 5) has no way to know which numbers moved — L2 doubled, L3 went non-inclusive, AVX-512 stopped downclocking, DDIO changed, mitigations became free — without a delta table. It also gives DSA/IAA/QAT a home so readers know they exist and why they belong off the order path.
  SCOPE: One row-set per generation: cores/tiles/CCDs, L1d/L2/L3 sizes and inclusion policy (Ice Lake-SP 1.25 MiB L2 + non-inclusive L3 → Sapphire/Emerald Rapids 2 MiB L2 → Granite Rapids; Zen 4/5 1 MiB L2 + 32 MiB victim L3 per CCD, V-Cache variants), measured L1/L2/L3/DRAM latencies in ns and cycles (Appendix E's rows re-stated per generation), ROB/scheduler and issue width (Ch. 11), DSB/op-cache size (Ch. 12), branch-predictor notes (Ch. 13), TLB sizes (Ch. 15), coherence granule. // Platform rows: memory technology and channels (DDR4-3200 8ch → DDR5-4800 8ch → DDR5-6400/MRDIMM 12ch; HBM on Xeon Max), PCIe generation and lanes, CXL support, UPI/xGMI link speeds, DDIO/SDCI behaviour (Ch. 56), RDT/QoS capabilities (Ch. 44), SNC/NPS options. // ISA and frequency rows: AVX-512 licence behaviour per generation (Ch. 29), AVX10/APX on Granite/Diamond Rapids, AMX matrix tiles (and why they stay off the order path), BF16/FP16, `-march=` names (`icelake-server`, `sapphirerapids`, `graniterapids`, `znver4`, `znver5`) and `x86-64-v4` (Ch. 22, Appendix D). // Security rows: which transient-execution vulnerabilities are hardware-fixed vs software-mitigated per generation, TSX availability, CET/IBT support (Ch. 72) — a pointer table into the expanded Ch. 6 §6.2.3. // On-die accelerators (Sapphire Rapids+): DSA (asynchronous memcpy/memfill/CRC via `ENQCMD` work queues, `idxd`/`accel-config`) as a candidate for journal and capture copies off the hot path (Ch. 75), IAA (compression — capture compaction), QAT (crypto/compression — venue TLS off path); why they stay off the tick-to-trade path (descriptor submission + completion polling ≈ a PCIe-class round trip — Ch. 66), with a measured DSA-vs-`memcpy` crossover size. // How to verify every row on your own box (`lscpu`, `cpuid`, `mlc`, `dmidecode`, the Ch. 7 sweep, uops.info/Agner Fog for instruction tables) — the sheet is a starting point, Ch. 3 is the authority.
  EXISTING: appendix-E-latency-numbers.md E.1: '~Ice Lake / Sapphire Rapids class, ~3 GHz, DDR4/5' — one generation-agnostic table.; 07-memory-hierarchy-and-caches.md §7.1, §7.3: Ice Lake-SP sizes and latencies only.; 11-cpu-pipelines-and-execution.md §11.1: Ice Lake issue width/ports/ROB only.; 29-simd-vectorization.md §29.2, §29.4.3: 'Skylake-SP through Ice Lake' licences; 'newer cores downclock far less — check yours'.; 22-build-toolchain-for-speed.md §22.4.1: `-march=icelake-server`/`znver3` examples; Appendix D adds `sapphirerapids` and `x86-64-v2/3/4`.; 66-pcie-host-device-boundary.md §66.2.1: PCIe Gen3/4/5 bandwidth table — platform-level, not per CPU generation.; 00-preface.md 'About the benchmark numbers': Representative-numbers caveat for the Ice Lake reference machine.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il -E across chapters/*.md: 'Sapphire Rapids' → Appendix E once; 'Emerald Rapids|Granite Rapids|Xeon 6|Sierra Forest|Diamond Rapids' → 0; 'AMX|AVX10|\bAPX\b|\bDSA\b|\bIAA\b|\bQAT\b|TDX|SGX' → 0 relevant (the case-insensitive 'DSA' hit in Ch. 40 was a substring); 'inclusive|victim cache|non-inclusive' in the L3 sense → 0 (Ch. 44 §44.1 says 'inclusive-ish' in passing — and incorrectly for Ice Lake-SP); 'Skylake' → Ch. 12 and 29 only; 'Ice Lake' → 50 files (the single reference machine everywhere); 'Chips and Cheese|uops.info|InstLatX64|WikiChip' → 0.


######## LENS observability-depth — 11 proposals ########
SUMMARY: LENS: measurement and observability depth in production. Method: read chapters/README.md in full, then read Ch. 76 and Ch. 5 end-to-end and the matching passages of Ch. 1, 2, 3, 17, 44, 45, 51, 53-55, 58, 60, 61, 71, 74, 75 and Appendices C, E, I, J, K, L, after grepping ~150 distinctive terms across chapters/*.md (vendor names, protocol timestamp fields, /proc and ethtool counters, histogram/sketch names, tracing frameworks, statistical tests).

OVERALL FINDING: the book repeats one production-observability thesis in five places (Ch. 5 §5.4.3, Ch. 58 §58.4.2, Ch. 61 §61.4.1, Ch. 71, Ch. 76 §76.2): "stamp cheaply with TSC/PTP at stage boundaries, keep a per-stage HdrHistogram, trace live with bpftrace, do deep analysis offline on a deterministic replay". That thesis is sound but it is never BUILT. Nothing shows how a stamp or ID travels with a message across the shm rings/Disruptor into another process; how counters/histograms leave the hot process (no Prometheus/sidecar/scraper/shared-memory-counter design anywhere); how percentiles may and may not be aggregated across hosts and time windows (averaging percentiles, histogram merging, sketches: zero hits); how to monitor at runtime that the "quiet box" of Part VII stayed quiet (per-thread ctxt switches, SMI count, hwlat/osnoise, softnet_stat, MCE/EDAC, link flaps: zero hits — all existing checks are setup-time); how to use the venue's own timestamps (CME TransactTime/SendingTime, Eurex T7 TrdRegTS: zero hits; one bullet in Ch. 58); how to actually profile in production despite Ch. 76's title (Intel PT, rdpmc, LBR sampling cost, continuous profilers: absent or one-line pointers); how to compare a new build's latency in production (canary/shadow/paired tests: zero hits; "canary" only as stack canary); or how latency ties back to fills (markout/fill-rate/TCA: zero hits for the analytics meaning). Ch. 61's probe-overhead measurement and Ch. 3/App L's coordinated-omission treatment are the book's strongest measurement content and the new material should build on them. Eleven proposals follow: three new chapters (in-band latency tracing/attribution; application health signals + telemetry export; host/network runtime health), one expansion of Ch. 76 (real production profiling), and seven targeted sections.

CONSISTENCY/ACCURACY DEFECTS FOUND IN PASSING (worth a sweep): (1) Ch. 76 §76.2 diagram carries pre-reorg numbers — "Ch.16/50" for TSC/PTP (now 17/58), "Ch.51" for eBPF (61), "Ch.62-63" for replay (74-75), "Ch.63" for capture (75), "§64.4.1/§64.4.3" (76.4.1/76.4.3); §76.1 says "Sixty-three chapters"; the closing italic says "Appendices (A–G)" though H-L exist (README legend line 5 also says "seven appendices (A–G)"). (2) Ch. 1 §1.2.3: "which is why the book has 64 chapters". (3) Appendices I (lines 135-142), J (line 85) and K (lines 3, 46-50, 77) cite "Ch. 56" for PTP (now Ch. 58) and K cites "Ch. 68" for FPGA (now 69). (4) Ch. 61 §61.3 cites "§51.5" for its own pitfalls section (§61.5). (5) Ch. 45 §45.3/§45.4/§45.5 and Appendix C C.1 defer SMIs to "Ch. 6", but Ch. 6 never mentions SMIs/system-management interrupts (dangling). (6) Ch. 44 §44.4.2 and checklist refer to "the readiness check (Ch. 76)" — Ch. 76 defines no readiness check. (7) Ch. 71 §71.1 points to "post-trade analysis (Ch. 75–76)" which neither chapter covers. (8) Ch. 76 §76.3 cites "Ch. 69.4.3" style refs inconsistently with the §N.M convention. Proposals 3, 8 and 10 below give homes to the dangling (5)-(7) references.

--- [observability-depth #1] In-Band Latency Tracing: Per-Hop Timestamps, Correlation IDs & Budget Attribution in Production
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part XII — Observability & Operations in Production, immediately after Ch. 71 (Zero-Overhead Logging) and before Ch. 72; it is the measurement sibling of Ch. 71's off-path writer and the mechanism Ch. 76 §76.2/§76.3 assume. Renumber or insert as 71A if renumbering is undesirable. | gap=scattered | priority=high | area=debugging-diagnostics
  HFT: Ch. 76 shows a tick-to-trade table decomposed into stages and says the decomposition must run live in production, but a developer building the system has no mechanism for getting a timestamp from the feed handler process to the gateway process and back to one histogram per hop. Without an in-band trace context the production p99.9 is a single number with no owner, and the first question after every slow fill — which stage, in which process, ate the time — cannot be answered.
  SCOPE: The trace-context pattern: a fixed-size per-message trace header (venue sequence number / correlation ID + a vector of `rdtscp` stage stamps) carried in-band through the shared-memory rings (Ch. 50) and Disruptor slots (Ch. 37), vs out-of-band side tables keyed by ID; cost of the stamp (~20–30 cycles, Ch. 17) and of the extra cache line per slot, measured on the SPSC ring of Ch. 34 // Choosing and allocating IDs without contention: venue sequence numbers for ticks, ClOrdID/order IDs for orders, and the causal link tick → decision → order so a fill can be traced back to the packet that caused it, across processes (Ch. 74) and hosts // Anchoring software stamps to the wire: NIC RX hardware timestamp as stage 0 and TX hardware timestamp as the last stage (Ch. 55/58); converting TSC to PTP/UTC with the Ch. 17 calibration so in-app stamps and wire stamps are on one time base; separating queueing time inside each ring (publish stamp vs consume stamp) from service time // Budget attribution — the latency ledger: per-hop differences fed into per-stage HdrHistograms (Ch. 3), each stage owning a p99.9 sub-budget (Ch. 1 §1.4.2); live decomposition of the end-to-end tail into the stage that produced it, extending the static table of Ch. 76 §76.3 into a running production artifact // Where aggregation happens: the hot thread only writes the trace record into the binary log / capture stream (Ch. 71/75); an off-core aggregator on a housekeeping core builds histograms and the worst-N list — never aggregate on the isolated core // Adapting distributed tracing to nanosecond budgets: why OpenTelemetry spans (allocation, string attributes, context propagation, microseconds per span) cannot sit on the hot path; a pre-allocated fixed-layout 'span' that is exported to OTel/Jaeger off-path; USDT markers (Ch. 61) placed at the same stage boundaries so external probes see the same stages // Visualising a message's life: emitting Chrome-trace/Perfetto JSON or CTF (LTTng) from the binary trace records to see one tick's waterfall across processes; worst-message drill-down // Measure it: hot-path cost of stamping N hops vs none (p50/p99.9), queueing-vs-service split under a replayed market-open microburst (Ch. 53/75) // Pitfalls: TSC comparability across sockets/hosts (Ch. 17/58), stamping after the work you meant to measure, sampling 1-in-N messages and losing the tail, trace-ring back-pressure reaching the producer (Ch. 71 §71.6), stamps that are not captured (Ch. 75) and so cannot be replayed
  EXISTING: chapters/76-production-profiling-end-to-end-case-study.md §76.2 diagram and §76.3 table: asserts 'per-stage timestamps (TSC/PTP)' and 'HdrHistogram per stage' as production observation and shows a static stage table; no mechanism for carrying stamps/IDs between stages or processes; chapters/58-clock-synchronization-ptp.md §58.4.2 'Decompose across hops/hosts': one bullet: hardware-timestamp at each boundary and difference adjacent timestamps; covers only cross-host wire stamps, nothing about in-process stages or IDs; chapters/17-timekeeping-tsc-rdtsc-clocks.md §17.1 and §17.4.1: states that a feed handler stamps every message for latency monitoring and how to take a cheap stamp; not where the stamp goes or how it is attributed; chapters/71-zero-overhead-logging.md §71.4.1 'Cheap timestamps' and §71.4.2: TSC-stamped binary records and off-line formatting — the transport the trace records would use, but not latency tracing itself; chapters/61-ebpf-bpftrace-xdp.md §61.4.1 'USDT — instrument your own app cheaply': USDT markers at message-received/decision/order-sent boundaries; external-probe view only; chapters/01-the-latency-mindset.md §1.2.3, §1.4.2: the stage budget and 'measure each stage against its sub-budget continuously in production (Ch. 76)' — the requirement this chapter would satisfy
  EVIDENCE: grep -il across chapters/*.md for 'correlation id', 'correlation-id', 'trace id', 'trace_id', 'span id', 'OpenTelemetry', 'distributed tracing', 'Jaeger', 'Zipkin', 'Perfetto', 'LTTng', 'Chrome trace', 'trace record', 'timestamp vector', 'budget attribution', 'latency ledger', 'message id', 'msg_id', 'ClOrdID', 'causal', 'waterfall' → zero hits for all. 'OTel' hits in Ch. 16/72 are substrings of 'hotel'/'hOTELs'-style words, not the project. 'per-stage' hits only in Ch. 24/37/39/75/76, and in Ch. 76 only as the table and a diagram line. 'USDT' hits only Ch. 5/61. Read Ch. 76 §76.2–76.3, Ch. 58 §58.4.2, Ch. 17 §17.4, Ch. 71 §71.4 in full to confirm no transport/ID mechanism is described.

--- [observability-depth #2] Application Health Signals & Telemetry Export: Shared-Memory Counters, Histogram Aggregation and Off-Core Scrapers
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part XII, directly after the proposed In-Band Latency Tracing chapter (i.e. between Ch. 71 and Ch. 72), so Part XII opens with logging → tracing → health/telemetry before security, hot reload and process topology. Cross-reference Ch. 74 §74.4.3 (heartbeats) rather than duplicating it. | gap=scattered | priority=high | area=health-monitoring-app
  HFT: Ch. 76 lists 'counters: drops, gaps, queue depths, restarts' as always-on production observation and Ch. 74 adds heartbeats, but no chapter says how a counter gets out of an isolated busy-polling process without a syscall, a formatted string or a cache-line fight. A desk that cannot see feed staleness, queue high-watermarks and ack latency per venue within a second is flying blind at the open, and the usual answer (a metrics library on the hot path) is exactly the hidden stall this book spends 76 chapters removing.
  SCOPE: What a trading process must expose: per-feed liveness and staleness (time since last message vs the venue heartbeat interval, Ch. 54), gap/drop/recovery counters at NIC/socket/app layers (Ch. 51/53/55), per-queue depth and high-watermark since last scrape (free, from existing producer/consumer indices of the SPSC rings — Ch. 34/50), per-stage latency histograms (from the tracing chapter), order-session health (ack-latency distribution, reject and throttle counts vs venue limits — Appendix K.4, oldest-unacked-order age as the 'lost order' detector), kill-switch/safe-state status and restart counts (Ch. 74) // Hot-thread stall detector: a progress counter (loop iteration count or last TSC) written by the busy-poll loop and read by a watchdog on a housekeeping core at sub-millisecond resolution; distinguishing a stall from legitimate idle; escalating to the Ch. 74 kill-switch — the hang detector Ch. 74's process-level heartbeat is too coarse for // Zero-cost counter design: per-thread, cache-line-isolated plain stores (no atomics, no false sharing — Ch. 33); relaxed `std::atomic` only where a reader needs a consistent multi-field snapshot (seqlock, Ch. 35); monotonic counters vs gauges; a double-buffered HdrHistogram pair per stage that the scraper swaps so the hot path never shares a live histogram with a reader // Export path: all counters/histograms live in a shared-memory segment (Ch. 26/50) that a sidecar scraper maps read-only and publishes as Prometheus text exposition / StatsD / a binary push — the trading process never opens a telemetry socket, formats text or calls into a metrics library on the hot path (Ch. 71's discipline) // The cost of being scraped: the scraper's reads pull the hot core's lines into Shared state and cause HITM traffic on the next write (Ch. 33); measure scrape-rate vs hot-path p99.9 and pin the scraper to a housekeeping core with a bounded cadence (~1 Hz); CMT occupancy (Ch. 44) to confirm the scraper is not evicting the hot working set // Interval vs cumulative semantics: what 'p99.9 over the last second' means, minimum sample counts per window (Ch. 3 §3.4.4), reset-free interval histograms via subtraction/merge // Alerting design: thresholds in budget terms (Ch. 1 §1.4.2) on percentiles and max, never on means; staleness alerts that do not fire in a quiet market; the startup readiness check Ch. 44 §44.4.2 refers to (verify isolation, CAT masks, PTP offset, NIC ring sizes, drops = 0 before going live) // Measure it: counter increment cost (plain store vs relaxed atomic vs seq_cst), scrape-induced jitter on the hot core at 1/10/100 Hz, stall-detector detection latency vs false-positive rate // Pitfalls: a shared global stats struct (false sharing), formatting or allocating in the hot path when exporting, monitoring agents (node_exporter and friends) running unpinned on all cores as noisy neighbours (Ch. 42/44), alert fatigue from percentiles computed on too few samples // Verify the codegen: the counter increment is a single store/`inc` to a private line; the scraper's read side compiles to loads with no RMW
  EXISTING: chapters/74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.4.3 'Supervisor/watchdog, heartbeats and liveness': process-level heartbeat in a shm slot read by a supervisor; detects death/hang at heartbeat-interval granularity; nothing on counters, histograms, export or stall detection of the hot loop; chapters/76-production-profiling-end-to-end-case-study.md §76.2 diagram line 'counters: drops, gaps, queue depths, restarts': named as production observation; no mechanism, no export, no cadence; chapters/53-zero-copy-wire-market-data-decoding.md §53.2.4 and §53.4.4 'Detect drops at every layer': ethtool -S / RcvbufErrors / sequence gaps — 'instrument and alert' stated, not designed; chapters/44-cache-allocation-intel-rdt.md §44.4.2 'Monitor continuously (CMT/MBM) in production': the one chapter with a concrete continuous-monitoring recipe (resctrl mon_data polling + alert); refers to a 'readiness check (Ch. 76)' that does not exist; chapters/58-clock-synchronization-ptp.md §58.4.1 'Monitor the sync continuously': PTP offset as 'a first-class operational metric (Ch. 76)' — again deferred to a chapter that has no telemetry design; chapters/71-zero-overhead-logging.md §71.4.3: 'periodic stats' as a use of std::println off the hot path — one phrase

--- [observability-depth #5] Exchange-Provided Timestamps: Attributing the Budget Between You, the Wire and the Matching Engine
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 58 — new §58.4.4 after '58.4.3 Advanced synchronization', expanding the single bullet 'Compare to the exchange's timestamps' in §58.4.2; add a field-level table to Appendix J J.5 and glossary entries (Appendix F). Feeds the latency-ledger of the proposed tracing chapter. | gap=partial | priority=high | area=other
  HFT: The number the business pays for is latency relative to the matching engine and relative to competitors, and the only clock that can reveal it is the venue's own timestamps in market-data headers and order acks. Without them a desk cannot tell whether a lost race was its own 300 ns, a slow venue gateway port, or the wire — and cannot pick the fastest session or know when to stop optimising software.
  SCOPE: The timestamp fields venues publish and what clock each is on: CME MDP 3.0 packet `SendingTime` vs message `TransactTime`, iLink 3 `SendingTimeEpoch`/`RequestTime` and the ack's `TransactTime`; Eurex T7 EOBI `TransactTime`, ETI `RequestTime` and the `TrdRegTSTimeIn/Out` family (gateway-in, matching-engine-in/out, gateway-out); Nasdaq ITCH nanoseconds-since-midnight and OUCH timestamps; granularity, accuracy and the venue's stated sync discipline // One-way feed latency: venue `SendingTime` → your NIC RX hardware timestamp (Ch. 55), valid only with PTP/UTC traceability on both sides (Ch. 58 §58.3); splitting venue-side queueing (`TransactTime`→`SendingTime`) from the network path; per-line A/B arrival deltas (Ch. 53/54) // Order-entry attribution: your TX hardware timestamp → gateway-in → matching-engine-in → gateway-out → your RX; the four latencies that fall out (your egress, the wire, the venue gateway, the matching engine) and which you own; per-session/port gateway-latency distributions and choosing ports (Appendix K.4) // Competitive position from venue time: did our matching-engine-in precede the trade that moved the book (race won/lost), queue position inference at a level, competitors' reaction time inferred from aggressor `TransactTime` minus the triggering tick // Clock caveats: venue sync quality and holdover (Ch. 58 §58.4.3), midnight rollover and timezone traps, leap seconds, timestamps taken at different points in the venue's own pipeline, sanity bounds from round-trip symmetry // Operationalising it: per-venue dashboards of SendingTime→hwRX and TrdRegTS-derived venue latency; alerting when the venue's gateway degrades (their problem, not yours — a different runbook from a local regression) // Measure it: distribution of SendingTime→hwRX per line over a session (p50/p99.9), TrdRegTS-derived venue latency per session, and how much of the round-trip the venue consumes at the open vs midday // Pitfalls: differencing a venue hardware timestamp against your software recv timestamp (Ch. 58 §58.5), comparing venues with different timestamp semantics, trusting a venue timestamp field that is set at the gateway rather than the matching engine
  EXISTING: chapters/58-clock-synchronization-ptp.md §58.4.2 bullet 'Compare to the exchange's timestamps': one bullet: with synced clocks you can measure latency relative to the exchange and 'your position in the queue'; no fields, no venues, no method; chapters/appendix-J-market-structure-primer.md J.5 protocol quick reference: ITCH/OUCH/FIX/SBE/FAST message summaries with no timestamp fields or semantics; chapters/53-zero-copy-wire-market-data-decoding.md §53.2.1: CME MDP 3.0 named as an SBE-based feed; timestamps not discussed; chapters/appendix-K-exchange-connectivity.md K.4, K.5: ports/gateways/throttles and venue time distribution — the infrastructure, not how to read the venue's timestamps
  EVIDENCE: grep -il across chapters/*.md for 'TransactTime', 'SendingTime', 'RequestTime', 'TrdRegTS', 'matching.engine timestamp', 'gateway timestamp', 'exchange timestamp', 'venue timestamp', 'exchange-provided', 'exchange-reported', 'Eurex', 'T7', 'EOBI', 'ETI', 'iLink', 'order ack', 'round-trip to the exchange' → zero hits.
