--- [observability-depth #6] Latency Dashboards & Percentile Math Pitfalls: Aggregating Tails Across Hosts and Time Without Lying
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Inside the proposed Application Health & Telemetry chapter as its 'Mental model' core; if that chapter is not adopted, add as §3.4.5 in Ch. 3 (Micro-benchmarking) titled 'Percentiles in production: aggregation, windows and coordinated omission live', with a pointer from Ch. 76 §76.4.1 ('a dashboard'). | gap=partial | priority=high | area=health-monitoring-app
  HFT: Ch. 1 says the mean lies and Ch. 3 teaches HdrHistogram and coordinated omission for a benchmark, but every production dashboard is an aggregation across cores, hosts, symbols and minutes, and the default aggregation (average the per-host p99s) silently reintroduces the mean the book banned. A desk whose fleet-wide p99.9 panel is an average of percentiles will miss the one host with the fat tail — the host losing the races.
  SCOPE: Why percentiles cannot be averaged — across hosts, symbols or time windows — and why p99 of per-host means ≠ mean of per-host p99s; the correct operation is merging histograms (`hdr_add` on HdrHistograms, log-encoded histogram interchange) and recomputing; a worked example where the averaged p99.9 is off by 10× from the merged one // Interval vs cumulative histograms and what 'p99.9 over the last second' means; minimum sample counts per window (Ch. 3 §3.4.4: ≫10⁴ for p99.9) and why quiet-market windows produce noise percentiles and false alerts // Mergeable sketches when full histograms are too large to ship: t-digest, DDSketch (relative-error bound), Prometheus native/exponential histograms — what they guarantee and what they lose at the far tail vs HdrHistogram's fixed significant digits // Coordinated omission in production: a busy-poll loop that times only its own service time never sees the queue built during a microburst; measure each message's age from its NIC RX hardware timestamp (Ch. 55) instead — the live equivalent of Appendix L's `hdr_record_corrected_value` // Max and breach counts are metrics: per-window max, p99.99 and count-over-budget alongside p99; latency × time heatmaps to expose the bimodality a percentile line hides (Ch. 3 §3.4.4 'Watch for multimodality') // Attribution next to the aggregate: keep the worst-N messages' trace records (proposed tracing chapter) with each window so a p99.9 spike is immediately explainable // Alerting on tails: thresholds in budget terms (Ch. 1 §1.4.2), breach-rate windows instead of single-sample triggers, suppressing alerts when sample count is below the percentile's support // Measure it: the same captured session (Ch. 75) aggregated three ways — averaged percentiles, merged histograms, sketches — with the resulting error at p99/p99.9/p99.99 // Pitfalls: Grafana-style avg() over percentile series, resetting a histogram mid-scrape, time windows not aligned across hosts without PTP (Ch. 58), reporting p99 from 200 samples
  EXISTING: chapters/01-the-latency-mindset.md §1.3 point 1, §1.5 'Reporting (or optimizing) the average': mean vs percentile on one sample; 'if a dashboard… reports average latency, ask at what percentile' — the only dashboard mention; nothing on aggregating percentiles; chapters/03-microbenchmarking-done-right.md §3.2.2, §3.4.3, §3.4.4: coordinated omission, HdrHistogram recording, sample counts per percentile, multimodality, Mann–Whitney for A vs B — all in the benchmark setting; no merging/windowing/production aggregation; chapters/appendix-L-benchmark-harness.md L.2: `hdr_record_corrected_value` for open-loop arrival in a harness; chapters/76-production-profiling-end-to-end-case-study.md §76.4.1 'Track the trend… a dashboard': one sentence; no aggregation semantics
  EVIDENCE: grep -il across chapters/*.md for 'average of percentiles', 'percentile of percentiles', 'histogram merg', 'merg.* histogram', 'hdr_add', 't-digest', 'tdigest', 'DDSketch', 'percentile math', 'log-linear', 'windowed', 'interval histogram', 'per-minute', 'rolling percentile', 'heatmap' → zero hits. --- [observability-depth #7] Measuring Latency Changes in Production: Canary Hosts, Shadow Runs and Paired Comparison on the Same Ticks
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 76 — new §76.4.4 'From CI gate to production: canary and shadow comparison', after §76.4.3; cross-reference Ch. 73 §73.4.4 (cutover), Ch. 74 §74.4.5 (sharding), Ch. 46 §46.4.1/§46.5 (side-effect suppression) and Ch. 60 §60.4.1 (layer-1 replication of the feed). | gap=absent | priority=medium | area=other
  HFT: Ch. 76 defends latency with a CI replay gate on a lab box and then warns that lab and production drift; the missing step is measuring the new build against the old one in production itself, on the same ticks, before it is allowed to send orders. A desk that only has the lab gate ships regressions that appear solely under real contention and discovers them in P&L.
  SCOPE: Why the CI gate (§76.4.1) is necessary but insufficient: effects that exist only in production — DDIO and neighbour contention (Ch. 44/56), real microbursts, venue behaviour — and the regime dependence of latency // The shadow host: a second box fed the identical multicast stream by layer-1 replication (Ch. 60), running the candidate build with order transmission suppressed at the final gate (Ch. 46's dry-run path, with its 'never send a synthetic order' pitfall) — a latency A/B on bit-identical input // Paired comparison: because both builds see the same packet with hardware RX timestamps, compare per-message decision latency pairwise and analyse the difference distribution, eliminating market-regime covariates; vs comparing two independent p99.9s // Statistics for tails: Mann–Whitney U (Ch. 3 §3.4.4 for benchmarks), bootstrap confidence intervals on the p99.9 difference, KS test on the full distribution; sample sizes needed to see a 50 ns shift at p99.9; multiple-comparison traps when watching hundreds of symbols // Confounders to equalise: BIOS/kernel/flags (§76.5 drift), host fingerprint equality (proposed baselining section), NUMA node and NIC port, time-of-day stratification (open vs midday) // Rollout mechanics with Ch. 73/74: per-shard (symbol/venue) cutover, draining vs instantaneous, automatic rollback when the canary's p99.9 breaches budget, keeping the old build warm (Ch. 46) // Correctness alongside latency: the deterministic core (Ch. 74) means shadow and production outputs must match bit-for-bit while latency differs — divergence is a behaviour change, caught before it trades // Measure it: plant a 50 ns regression (an added virtual call, Ch. 14) and count the ticks until the paired test detects it vs an unpaired comparison of per-host percentiles // Pitfalls: canary on different hardware/NUMA/port, shadow traffic leaking real orders (Ch. 46 §46.5), comparing across sessions with different volatility, declaring victory on p50
  EXISTING: chapters/76-production-profiling-end-to-end-case-study.md §76.4.1 CI gates; §76.5 'Drift between test and production': lab-only regression gate; drift acknowledged as a pitfall, not addressed with a production comparison technique; chapters/46-keeping-the-hot-path-warm.md §46.4.1 'Suppress the side effects', §46.5: dry-run/shadow traffic for warming with bulletproof order suppression — the mechanism a shadow run reuses, but for a different purpose; chapters/73-hot-reload-live-reconfiguration.md §73.4.4 'Draining vs instantaneous cutover': cutover mechanics, not measurement of the change; chapters/03-microbenchmarking-done-right.md §3.4.4 'Compare, don't just measure': Mann–Whitney via Google Benchmark compare.py — benchmark context only

'canary' hits only Ch. 72 §72.3 (stack-protector canary). 'shadow' hits Ch. 46 (warming traffic) only. 'Mann-Whitney' hits only Ch. 3.

--- [observability-depth #8] Baselining & Drift Detection: Per-Host Latency Fingerprints, Readiness Checks and Change-Point Alarms
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 76 — new §76.4.5 'Baselines, fingerprints and drift' after the proposed canary section; define the 'readiness check' that Ch. 44 §44.4.2 and its checklist already cite as living in Ch. 76. Reuse Appendix L skeletons and Appendix E expected values. | gap=partial | priority=medium | area=health-monitoring-os-net
  HFT: Ch. 45 says to re-run cyclictest after any change and Ch. 76 says to watch the trend for slow drift, but neither says what the baseline is, how a host proves it still matches it before trading, or how a few-nanoseconds-per-release drift is detected statistically. A box that silently lost its C-state cap after a firmware update looks fine on average and is 30 µs worse at p99.99 — exactly the failure a per-host fingerprint catches at boot.
  SCOPE: The host fingerprint: a fixed micro-suite run at boot and before go-live — L1/L2/L3/DRAM latency sweep (Ch. 7), cross-core HITM (Ch. 33), null syscall (Ch. 41), `rdtsc` cost (Ch. 17), `cyclictest` max (Ch. 45), NIC RX→app (Ch. 55), PTP offset (Ch. 58) — producing a vector compared against Appendix E, the fleet and the host's own history; readiness fails on deviation (a BIOS reset re-enabled C6, turbo came back, THP switched to always) // Recording provenance with each fingerprint: kernel, microcode, BIOS, NIC firmware, driver, build flags — so a shift can be bisected to the change that caused it (microcode updates alter mitigation cost, Ch. 6) // Live drift on per-stage p99.9 time series: change-point detection (CUSUM, PELT), seasonality-aware baselines (open/close, expiries), alerting on level shifts rather than single spikes; distinguishing drift from regime // Fleet comparison: the same build on N hosts — an outlier host indicates hardware or configuration, not code; pairing with the host-health chapter's counters to name the cause // Correlating a detected shift with events: deploys, kernel/BIOS updates, a new neighbour (Ch. 44 CMT occupancy), NIC firmware, grandmaster holdover (Ch. 58) // Measure it: detect a governor flip, THP re-enable or disabled CAT mask from the fingerprint alone, with the tolerance band that avoids flapping // Pitfalls: fingerprinting a cold box and comparing to a warm baseline (Ch. 46), bands too tight or too loose, using the mean fingerprint as the baseline, trusting Appendix E's numbers without re-deriving them on your hardware
  EXISTING: chapters/76-production-profiling-end-to-end-case-study.md §76.4.1 'Track the trend'; §76.5 'Drift between test and production', 'Tuning once and walking away': drift named; no baseline definition, fingerprint or detection method; chapters/44-cache-allocation-intel-rdt.md §44.4.2 and checklist: 'verify with CMT/MBM in the readiness check (Ch. 76)' — the referenced readiness check does not exist in Ch. 76; chapters/45-realtime-scheduling-kernel-tuning.md §45.5 'Assuming tuned once = tuned forever': re-run cyclictest after kernel/BIOS/config changes; manual; chapters/appendix-C-system-tuning-checklist.md C.5 Verification pass: one-time cyclictest/jitter verification after tuning; chapters/appendix-L-benchmark-harness.md L.1–L.4: the reusable harness a fingerprint suite would be built from
  EVIDENCE: grep -il across chapters/*.md for 'change point', 'change-point', 'CUSUM', 'anomaly', 'control chart', 'seasonal' → zero hits. 'fingerprint' hits Ch. 11/12/13/14/16/21/23/24/26 — read: all are 'the signature of X in the counters', not host fingerprints. 'readiness' hits Ch. 44 (pointing to Ch. 76) and otherwise only epoll readiness (Ch. 38/47/48/52); grep -n -i readiness in 76-*.md returns nothing. 'baseline' appears in 28 files, every hit a benchmark baseline, never a host/fleet baseline.

--- [observability-depth #9] Passive Wire-Level Latency Measurement: Optical Taps vs SPAN, Timestamped Mirrors and Capture-Appliance Analytics
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 60 — new §60.4.4 'Passive measurement: taps, SPAN and latency appliances' (or expand §60.4.3), with a pointer from Ch. 75 §75.4.1 and Ch. 58 §58.3; add vendors/terms to Appendix F.6 and Appendix G. | gap=partial | priority=medium | area=health-monitoring-os-net
  HFT: Every serious desk runs an independent wire-level latency monitor because the application's own numbers can be wrong in exactly the way that matters (software timestamps, a stamp after the real work, a dropped packet the app never saw). Knowing that a SPAN port adds microseconds of jitter at the open, and how a tap-fed capture host reconstructs per-order tick-to-trade from the wire, is what makes the in-app latency ledger trustworthy.
  SCOPE: Passive optical taps vs switch SPAN/mirror ports: SPAN shares the switch's egress queue, adds timestamp jitter, drops and may reorder under the market-open burst; a passive tap feeding a hardware-timestamping capture NIC (Ch. 55) is the ground-truth wire time, independent of the host under test — measure the SPAN-vs-tap timestamp error on the same stream // The appliance model (Corvil/Pico, Instrumentix, Beeks-style, Arista 7130/Metamako timestamped mirrors): decode market data and order entry at the tap, correlate tick → order by content (order IDs, symbol/price/side), and produce wire-to-wire tick-to-trade per order without touching the trading host — the independent auditor of the in-app numbers from the proposed tracing chapter // Building the open equivalent: layer-1 replication (Ch. 60 §60.4.1) → capture host with PTP-synced NIC (Ch. 58) → lossless capture (Ch. 75) → decoder (Ch. 53) → join by ID → histograms; what it costs and where it falls short of an appliance // What passive measurement can and cannot attribute: wire-in to wire-out only; pairing with in-band stage stamps for the inside breakdown; a disagreement between appliance and application numbers as a diagnostic (software-timestamp bias, Ch. 58 §58.5; a stage stamped in the wrong place) // Switch-appended timestamps: Arista 7150/7130 timestamp trailers and Metamako/Exablaze formats, how to strip and parse them off the frame, PTP discipline of the switch clock, and using them for per-hop fabric latency (Ch. 60 §60.3) // Operational uses beyond your own latency: line A vs B arrival deltas and which line is faster per venue (Ch. 53/54), venue gateway latency from venue timestamps (proposed §58.4.4), detecting a slow switch hop or a flapping link (host-health chapter) // Measure it: SPAN vs tap timestamp error distribution during a replayed open; appliance-measured vs in-app tick-to-trade discrepancy and its explanation // Pitfalls: SPAN oversubscription dropping exactly the burst you care about, capture NIC without hardware timestamps, RTT/2 as one-way (Ch. 60 §60.5), appliance clock not on the same grandmaster
  EXISTING: chapters/60-network-fabric-switching.md §60.3 (passive tap for per-hop measurement), §60.4.3 'Switch-based replication, timestamping, and taps': three bullets: switch hardware timestamping, layer-1/SPAN replication for capture, aggregation taps as 'the observability backbone (Ch. 76)'; no SPAN-vs-tap fidelity, no appliance/analytics model, no vendors; chapters/75-capture-persistence-replay-storage.md §75.3 table row 'Off-NIC / off-host capture'; §75.4.1: tap/mirror/SPAN/SmartNIC and 'a dedicated capture appliance' for lossless compliance capture — capture, not latency analytics; SPAN and tap treated as equivalent; chapters/58-clock-synchronization-ptp.md §58.3: wire-to-wire with hardware timestamps on both hosts; nothing about measuring from a third, passive vantage point
  EVIDENCE: grep -il across chapters/*.md for 'Corvil', 'Instrumentix', 'optical tap', 'fiber tap', 'tap aggregat', 'ExaLINK', 'timestamp trailer' → zero hits. 'Pico' matches only 'picosecond' in Ch. 58. 'SPAN port', 'mirror port', 'capture appliance' hit only Ch. 75 (§75.3/§75.4.1, capture context). 'passive tap' hits only Ch. 60 §60.3. 'Metamako'/'Arista' hit Ch. 60/64 as switch vendors, not as measurement devices.

--- [observability-depth #10] Post-Trade Latency Analytics: Fill Rates, Markouts and Race Outcomes as a Function of Latency
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 76 — new §76.4.6 'Closing the loop: latency to fills' (or as an appendix 'Appendix M — Latency Analytics for the Desk' if Ch. 76 grows too large); gives a landing to Ch. 71 §71.1's forward reference 'post-trade analysis (Ch. 75–76)' and extends Appendix K.7's 'marginal nanosecond changes fill rates'. | gap=absent | priority=medium | area=other
  HFT: The book's north-star is tail latency because latency wins fills, yet nothing shows how to verify that claim on your own orders — which stage's nanoseconds actually changed fill rate or markout. Post-trade analytics turns the capture and the latency ledger into the business feedback loop that decides whether the next quarter goes into decode, the gateway or an FPGA.
  SCOPE: Joining the capture (Ch. 75) with the order lifecycle (Appendix J.3) and venue timestamps (proposed §58.4.4) to compute per order: reaction latency from triggering tick, time-to-ack, filled/not filled, inferred queue position at entry, markout at +1/+10/+100 ms // Fill-rate-vs-latency curves per symbol and venue: the marginal value of a nanosecond, the knee beyond which faster buys nothing, and using the curve to direct engineering effort (which stage of the ledger to attack — Ch. 76 §76.3) // Race analysis: ordering our matching-engine-in against the competing event (venue `TransactTime`), classifying lost races as network / decision / gateway using the stage attribution of the tracing chapter, and the distribution of margins by which races are lost // Cancel latency as its own metric: adverse-selection losses from slow cancels vs gains from fast entries; why cancel-path latency deserves a separate budget (Ch. 1 §1.4.2) // Storage and joins at nanosecond alignment: time-indexed columnar layout (Ch. 75 §75.4.4), joining feeds and order logs on PTP time without look-ahead bias, handling clock error that can invert event order (Ch. 58) // Measure it: a fill-rate curve and markout difference between the fastest and slowest latency deciles from one captured session // Pitfalls: survivorship in fills, mistaking regime correlation for latency effect, trusting software timestamps in the join, comparing days with different volatility
  EXISTING: chapters/76-production-profiling-end-to-end-case-study.md §76.4.3 Simulation harnesses: models queue position, fills and venue responses for closed-loop strategy testing — forward simulation, not analysis of realised fills vs latency; chapters/71-zero-overhead-logging.md §71.1: 'for post-trade analysis (Ch. 75–76)' — forward reference with no landing section; chapters/75-capture-persistence-replay-storage.md §75.1, §75.4.5: capture 'for research and compliance' and feeding backtests; no latency analytics; chapters/appendix-K-exchange-connectivity.md K.7: 'firms spend where the marginal nanosecond still changes fill rates' — the economic claim without a method
  EVIDENCE: grep -il across chapters/*.md for 'fill-rate', 'hit rate', 'hit ratio', 'TCA', 'transaction cost', 'slippage', 'adverse selection', 'markout', 'mark-out', 'fill probability', 'who got there first' → zero hits. 'fill rate' hits only Ch. 54 (A/B gap-fill rate) and Appendix K.7 (one phrase). 'queue position' hits only Ch. 75 §75.4.5 and Ch. 76 §76.4.3 in the simulation sense. 'post-trade' hits only Ch. 71 §71.1 as a forward reference. 'P&L' hits Ch. 75/76 only in backtest/simulation context.

--- [observability-depth #11] Anomaly-Triggered Diagnostics: Flight-Recorder Rings and the Incident Bundle
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 5 — new §5.4.4 'Capturing the moment: flight-recorder rings and the incident bundle' after §5.4.3, since Ch. 5 is the debugging methodology chapter; cross-reference Ch. 71 §71.6 (overwrite-oldest policy), Ch. 74 §74.4.5 (core dumps without stalling), Ch. 61 (eBPF ring of sched events) and the proposed tracing and host-health chapters. | gap=absent | priority=medium | area=debugging-diagnostics
  HFT: After a slow fill the desk asks why that one message took 40 µs, and Ch. 5's answer — gather in production with eBPF, reproduce offline — assumes someone was already tracing the right thing at 09:31:42. An always-on flight recorder frozen at the moment of the breach, bundled with the host's interrupt, scheduler and NIC state over the same microseconds, is the only way to answer the question for an event that will not recur on demand.
  SCOPE: The always-on in-memory flight recorder: a per-thread overwrite-oldest ring of binary trace/log records (Ch. 71's policy) sized for the last N milliseconds, frozen — not stopped — on a trigger by swapping the ring pointer so the hot path keeps writing to a fresh ring while a housekeeping core dumps the frozen one // Triggers: a message's end-to-end latency over budget (tracing chapter), the stall detector, a sequence gap or venue reject burst (Ch. 53/54), a PTP desync (Ch. 58), a neighbour's CMT occupancy spike (Ch. 44); arming and rate-limiting triggers so a bad minute does not produce a thousand dumps // What the incident bundle contains and how each piece is gathered without stalling: the frozen trace rings of every process, per-stage histograms and counters snapshot (telemetry chapter), `/proc/interrupts`, `/proc/softirqs`, `softnet_stat` and `ethtool -S` deltas, PTP state, the eBPF ring of `sched_switch`/IRQ events on the hot cores (Ch. 61), an Intel PT snapshot of the hot thread (production-profiling expansion), and a minidump only if a process died (Ch. 74 §74.4.5) // Post-hoc correlation — answering 'why was this one message slow': aligning the message's stage stamps with the host-event timeline (an IRQ on the hot core at T+40 µs, an SMI, a page fault, a neighbour eviction) on one PTP/TSC time base (Ch. 17/58) // Replay as the second step: using the frozen window's captured input (Ch. 75) to reproduce the slow message deterministically (Ch. 74) and attach heavy tools (Ch. 5 §5.4.2) // Retention, size and secrets: bounding bundle size, shipping it off the box asynchronously, redaction of credentials (Ch. 72 §72.4.5) // Measure it: hot-path cost of the always-on ring (writes per message) and the dump's effect on the surviving threads' tail; time from trigger to complete bundle // Pitfalls: a trigger that itself does I/O on the hot thread, dumping synchronously on the hot core, rings too small to reach back before the cause, bundles without the host-event side (app-only forensics cannot see the IRQ)
  EXISTING: chapters/05-debugging-low-latency-optimized-code.md §5.3 'Core dumps for the crash you can't reproduce'; §5.4.3 'Production tracing without perturbing the hot path': eBPF probes, lock-free log records and perf counters as production observation; capture-replay for reproduction — all reactive/manual, no trigger, freeze or bundle pattern; chapters/71-zero-overhead-logging.md §71.6 'Ignoring back-pressure / full-buffer policy': drop-newest vs overwrite-oldest ring policy — the ring the flight recorder would be, without the freeze-on-trigger use; chapters/74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.4.5 'Capture core dumps without stalling survivors': async/bounded/off-path core handling for a crash — the one diagnostic artifact the book does design; nothing for a non-crash latency incident; chapters/76-production-profiling-end-to-end-case-study.md §76.4.2 'Debug production incidents by replay': replay the captured window of a bad fill under a debugger — the second step, assuming you already know which window and which host events to look at
  EVIDENCE: grep -il across chapters/*.md for 'flight recorder', 'black-box', 'trace buffer', 'trace ring', 'ring of trace', 'snapshot of the ring', 'on anomaly', 'when the p99', 'trigger.* dump', 'incident bundle', 'diagnostic bundle' → zero hits. 'black box' hits only Appendix K.5 ('not black boxes'). 'last N' hits Preface/Appendix A in unrelated senses. 'core dump' material (Ch. 5/74) covers crashes only — confirmed by reading Ch. 5 §5.3–5.4 and Ch. 74 §74.4.5 in full.


######## LENS networking-depth — 12 proposals ########
SUMMARY: LENS: networking depth beyond Parts VIII–X and Appendix K. Method: read chapters/README.md in full, then for ~20 candidate topics grepped 4–8 distinctive terms each across all 89 chapter/appendix files (grep -n -i -E / grep -w) and read the matching passages in Ch. 47, 51, 52, 53, 54, 55, 57, 60, 61, 62, 74, 75, 76 and Appendices C, I, J, K, F. Headline: the book is deep on the host fast path (epoll/io_uring/zero-copy/bypass), the NIC (RSS, steering, DDIO, timestamps, LAUNCHTIME) and switching technology (cut-through vs L1), but the "plumbing" an engineer must get right before any of that matters is thin or absent: multicast group management (IGMP/snooping/querier, igmp_max_memberships, IP_MULTICAST_ALL — zero hits beyond one join-call bullet in §51.4.2), order-entry session liveness and TCP failure timing (SO_KEEPALIVE, TCP_USER_TIMEOUT, RTO floor, tcp_retries2, SoupBinTCP/FIX session — zero hits), a mechanism walkthrough of the kernel RX/TX datapath with per-stage tracing (NAPI/softirq/qdisc/BQL/conntrack/netns — zero hits), network health telemetry as a discipline (softnet_stat, dropwatch/kfree_skb, TCP_INFO, FEC counters, link flaps — zero hits; drop counters appear only as scattered bullets), line-rate capture and timing-faithful replay tooling (TPACKET_V3, pdump, tcpreplay, MoonGen — zero hits, while Ch. 53/55/60 exercises repeatedly say "replay a captured microburst"), encrypted/web-protocol venues (kTLS, TLS, WebSocket, JSON — zero hits), cloud networking (ENA/EFA/SR-IOV/placement groups — zero hits; one paragraph in App A.7), L2/L3 hygiene (ARP/ND/DNS/MTU/VLAN/DSCP/rp_filter — zero hits), redundancy design (bonding/LACP/failover timing — zero hits; three conceptual bullets in K.6), and NIC firmware/driver management (zero hits). Twelve proposals follow, five rated high. CONSISTENCY/ACCURACY FINDINGS (spot-checked, worth a sweep): (1) the renumber missed cross-refs inside diagram/code blocks and some appendices — e.g. appendix-K line 3 and K.5 cite PTP as "Ch. 56" (now 58) and FPGA as "Ch. 68" (now 69); 54-reliable-multicast line 27 "often TCP — Ch.69" (should be 52); 76-production-profiling §76.2 diagram cites "Ch.16/50", "Ch.51", "Ch.62-63", "Ch.63", "§64.4.1/§64.4.3" (should be 17/58, 61, 74–75, 75, §76.4.x); 55-nic-features line 59 "(Ch.39)" for interrupt latency (41); 46-keeping-warm line 30 "(Ch.47)" for TCP cwnd (51/52); 30-memory-model line 103 "HITM — Ch. 6/32" (7/33); 34-lock-free line 147 "(Ch. 32)" and 37-disruptor line 114 "(Ch. 33)" for false sharing/SPSC (33/34); 68-mpi line 71 "§56.5" (§68.5); 45-realtime line 72 "(Ch.5)" for mitigations (6); appendix-J cites bounds checks/untrusted input as "(Ch. 53, 60)"/"(Ch. 72, 60)" (60 is fabric; 72 intended) and seqlock publication as "(Ch. 34)" (35). (2) README line 5 and 00-preface line 46 still say "seven appendices (A–G)" though twelve (A–L) exist. (3) Accuracy: Ch. 60 §60.4.1/§60.5/checklist advise "uniform and high (25/100 G)" link speeds purely on serialization grounds, ignoring that 25G optics and all PAM4 (50G-per-lane 100G+) links require RS-FEC adding ~100–250 ns per hop, which is why many HFT paths deliberately stay at 10G NRZ — see proposal 10. (4) Ch. 60 §60.4.1 implies "layer-1" is the fan-out answer without distinguishing pure L1 replication (~ns) from FPGA mux/aggregation (tens of ns) when many servers share one exchange port.

--- [networking-depth #1] Network Health Telemetry & Drop Forensics: NIC, Driver, Stack, Socket and Fabric
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part XII — Observability & Operations, immediately after Ch. 71 (Zero-Overhead Logging) and before Ch. 72; the OS/network counterpart to the app-level monitoring in Ch. 76 §76.2. Alternatively Part IX after Ch. 60 as the closing 'operate it' chapter of the NIC/fabric part. | gap=scattered | priority=high | area=health-monitoring-os-net
  HFT: A silent drop at the open is a wrong book and a lost race, and today the book tells the reader to 'watch for drops at every layer' in three scattered bullets without a method, a cost model for collecting the counters, or the forensic tools that say where the packet died. HFT operations live on these counters — the reader needs one chapter that turns them into a measured, zero-overhead discipline.
  SCOPE: The counter hierarchy and what each layer's drop means: NIC MAC/PHY (`ethtool -S`: rx_missed_errors, rx_no_buffer_count, rx_out_of_buffer, per-queue drops, rx_crc_errors), driver/NAPI (`/proc/net/softnet_stat` dropped and time_squeeze), stack (`nstat`/`/proc/net/snmp`: IpInDiscards, UdpInErrors, UdpRcvbufErrors, TcpRetransSegs, TCPTimeouts, TCPSpuriousRTOs), socket (`ss -tim`, `TCP_INFO` fields rtt/retrans/notsent/busy_time, `SO_MEMINFO`), application (per-feed sequence gaps, A/B skew). // Drop forensics with drop reasons: `perf trace -e skb:kfree_skb`, `dropwatch`, bpftrace on `kfree_skb` with `SKB_DROP_REASON` (5.17+), eBPF drop-reason histograms per interface/socket — turning 'we lost packets at the open' into 'the ring overflowed on queue 5 for 340 µs'. // Link-layer health as leading indicators: link flaps and carrier changes via a netlink (RTM_NEWLINK) subscriber in the gateway, FEC corrected/uncorrected codewords (`ethtool --show-fec`, fec counters), PCS symbol errors, transceiver DOM (`ethtool -m`: RX power, temperature) trending toward failure. // Timing-plane health: `ptp4l`/`phc2sys` offset and path-delay series, PHC-vs-TSC drift, hardware-timestamped inter-arrival deltas as a feed-liveness signal, distinguishing 'stalled group' from 'quiet market' (ties Ch. 58, proposal 3). // Fabric telemetry read from the switch: egress drops and queue depth, PFC pause counts (Ch. 64), microburst detectors (Arista LANZ-style), sFlow/gNMI streaming telemetry, one-way latency from tap/SPAN monitors — correlated with host counters on a shared PTP timebase. // The zero-overhead collection pattern: a housekeeping-core collector sampling counters at 1–10 Hz (the `ethtool` ioctl and `/proc` parse cost measured, never on the hot core), recording into pre-allocated HdrHistograms/time series (Ch. 3, 71), exporting off-box; alert policy (any rx_missed > 0 during the open is an incident, retransmit on the order socket is an incident). // The bypass blind spot: under Onload/ef_vi/DPDK the kernel counters go dark — `onload_stackdump`, ef_vi/libexanic stats, DPDK `rte_eth_stats_get`/xstats, and what 'drop' means in each (ties Ch. 62 §62.5). // Measure it: induce drops with an undersized ring under a replayed burst (proposal 5) and show which counter lights up at each layer and how fast; measure the collector's own hot-path perturbation with and without core separation. // A symptom→counter→chapter runbook table: rx_missed→ring (Ch. 53/55); time_squeeze→netdev_budget/NAPI (proposal 2); UdpRcvbufErrors→SO_RCVBUF (Ch. 51); TcpRetransSegs→RTO/session (proposal 4); PFC pause→Ch. 64; PTP offset→Ch. 58.
  EXISTING: chapters/51-socket-optimization-tcp-tuning.md §51.4.2 'Detect drops' bullet (line ~109): One bullet: ethtool -S rx_dropped/rx_missed, netstat//proc/net/udp, sequence gaps.; chapters/53-zero-copy-wire-market-data-decoding.md §53.2.4 and §53.4.4 'Detect drops at every layer' (lines ~59, 180): Names ethtool -S, /proc/net/snmp RcvbufErrors, ss -u; no softnet_stat, drop reasons, link/FEC/PTP health, or collection cost.; chapters/55-nic-features-offloads.md §55.7 checklist (line ~125): 'drops are monitored (ethtool -S)'.; chapters/58-clock-synchronization-ptp.md §58.4.1 (lines ~94, 105, 122, 143): Monitor ptp4l/phc2sys offset and alert — timing health only.; chapters/64-lossless-ethernet-rdma-congestion.md §64.4.3 (line ~71): Monitor PFC pause counts, ECN marks, buffer occupancy — RDMA fabric only.; chapters/76-production-profiling-end-to-end-case-study.md §76.2 diagram (line ~28): One diagram line: 'counters: drops, gaps, queue depths, restarts'.; chapters/appendix-I-tooling-cookbook.md I.8 Network & NIC (lines 125–144): ethtool -S grep and ss -tim one-liners.
  EVIDENCE: grep -n -i -E 'softnet_stat|time_squeeze|netdev_budget|dropwatch|drop_monitor|kfree_skb|nstat' → 0 hits. 'ss -ti|tcp_info|TCP_INFO|tcpi_' → only 'ss -tim' in appendix-I line 132. '\bFEC\b|RS-FEC|link flap|carrier' → 0 hits. 'telemetry|gNMI|sFlow|IPFIX|SNMP|LANZ|Corvil' → 0 relevant hits. 'ethtool -S|rx_missed|rx_no_buffer|RcvbufErrors|/proc/net/snmp' → only Ch. 51 line 109, Ch. 53 lines 56/59/180/237/246, Ch. 55 lines 102/118/125, appendix-I lines 134/142 — all single bullets with no collection method or cost.

--- [networking-depth #2] Anatomy of the Linux Network Datapath: Where the Microseconds Go (RX/TX Walkthrough, NAPI/softirq, qdiscs, netfilter, namespaces)
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part VIII — Kernel I/O, Sockets & Zero-Copy, immediately after Ch. 47 (Linux Native I/O) and before Ch. 48 (io_uring): the mechanism chapter that Ch. 48–52, 55, 57, 61 and 62 currently assume. | gap=absent | priority=high | area=debugging-diagnostics
  HFT: The book quotes 5–20 µs for the kernel stack and then jumps to bypass, but every kernel-path reader (recovery channels, drop-copy, order replies on Onload-less boxes, AF_XDP middle grounds) needs to know which stage owns which microsecond to tune or trace it. Without the walkthrough, Ch. 51/52/55/57 knobs are incantations rather than mechanisms, and the diagnostic question 'where did the packet wait' cannot be answered.
  SCOPE: The RX path stage by stage with a per-stage cost column: NIC DMA → MSI-X hardirq → NAPI schedule → `net_rx_action` softirq → driver poll → GRO → `netif_receive_skb` (generic XDP hook) → IP → UDP/TCP → socket receive queue → `sk_data_ready` wakeup → `recvmsg` copy; measured with ftrace `function_graph` and bpftrace on `net:netif_receive_skb`, `net:napi_gro_receive_entry`, `skb:consume_skb`, `sock:*` tracepoints plus hardware RX timestamps (Ch. 55). // The softirq/ksoftirqd trap on isolated cores: `netdev_budget`/`netdev_budget_usecs`, time_squeeze, ksoftirqd fallback, threaded NAPI (`/sys/class/net/*/threaded`), `napi_defer_hard_irqs` and `gro_flush_timeout`, and how `SO_BUSY_POLL`/`SO_PREFER_BUSY_POLL`/epoll busy-poll (`EPIOCSPARAMS`, 6.9+) change the picture — the mechanics under Ch. 47 §47.4.1 and Ch. 55 §55.4.2. // The TX path: `sendmsg` → skb alloc → IP → qdisc enqueue (`noqueue`/`mq`/`pfifo_fast`/`fq`) → BQL → `ndo_start_xmit` → doorbell MMIO (Ch. 66) → DMA → TX completion interrupt; where queuing hides (qdisc, BQL, ring) and the `noqueue`/per-queue-qdisc choice for the order NIC; measuring sendmsg-return-to-wire with hardware TX timestamps. // The netfilter/conntrack tax: per-packet hook cost even with an empty ruleset, `nf_conntrack` lookups and table pressure, `raw` table `NOTRACK`/`notrack`, unloading the modules on a trading host, nftables flowtables; measured delta on a UDP RX path. // Network namespaces and containers: veth + bridge + NAT adds microseconds and extra softirq hops; host networking, macvlan/ipvlan, and SR-IOV VF passthrough as the container-era answers — measured against bare host sockets. // Routing and socket lookup costs: FIB lookup, `rp_filter` strict/loose, socket hash lookup with many sockets, `SO_RCVBUF` vs skb truesize accounting (why a 4 MB buffer holds fewer packets than you think). // Memory and cache effects of the stack: skb allocation/recycling, page-pool, where the copy happens (ties Ch. 49), DDIO interaction (Ch. 56), why bypass removes the whole column (Ch. 62). // Measure it: an end-to-end RX timeline histogram per stage on the reference box, kernel-stack vs busy-poll vs AF_XDP vs bypass; TX doorbell-to-wire distribution; conntrack on/off and netns on/off deltas. // Pitfalls: tuning sysctls without knowing which stage they touch; GRO off but NAPI budget still batching; isolated core still running ksoftirqd; container 'host networking' that is not; kernel-version drift in tracepoint names.

--- [networking-depth #3] Multicast Group Management & the Colo Multicast Fabric: IGMP/MLD, Snooping, Join Latency and the Linux Traps
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part IX — Market Data, NIC & Fabric, immediately after Ch. 54 (Reliable Multicast & Feed Recovery) and before Ch. 55: Ch. 54 recovers the stream once it arrives; this chapter makes sure the stream arrives, on the right NIC, on time, at the open. | gap=absent | priority=high | area=health-monitoring-os-net
  HFT: Every tick in the book arrives as UDP multicast, yet the only guidance is one bullet to call `IP_ADD_MEMBERSHIP`; in practice the first market-open incidents a new feed handler suffers are igmp_max_memberships rejections, IP_MULTICAST_ALL cross-talk, snooping tables aging out and joins that land seconds late. Group management is the part of the network a C++ developer actually controls from the host, and it decides whether the decoder has anything to decode.
  SCOPE: How the feed physically reaches the host: IGMPv2/v3 (and MLD for IPv6) membership reports, queries and leaves; IGMP snooping on the access switch and the querier-election problem (no querier → snooping entries age out → the feed silently stops after ~260 s); unknown-multicast flooding and its CPU cost on every host; PIM-SM/SSM and RPF in the exchange distribution tree; L1 replication (Ch. 60) vs snooping replication. // Source-specific multicast done right: `IP_ADD_SOURCE_MEMBERSHIP`/`MCAST_JOIN_SOURCE_GROUP`, why SSM matters when A and B share group ranges or multiple sources exist, `force_igmp_version`, unsolicited report count and re-joining after a link flap. // The Linux traps every feed handler hits: `net.ipv4.igmp_max_memberships` (default 20) when joining hundreds of channels; `IP_MULTICAST_ALL` (default on — a socket bound to a port receives every group any process on the host joined → cross-talk and phantom drops; set 0); `rp_filter` discarding multicast on the 'wrong' interface; binding to the group address vs INADDR_ANY; `IP_MULTICAST_IF`/`SO_BINDTODEVICE`/`IP_PKTINFO` for multi-NIC A/B; `IP_MULTICAST_LOOP`; `netdev_max_backlog`. // Join latency as a measured quantity: setsockopt → first packet (snooping table programming + query cycle) with hardware RX timestamps; pre-joining all channels before the open vs staggered joins; the cost of re-joining mid-session; leave latency and prune storms when a process exits. // Liveness: detecting a silently-stopped group (per-group stall detector against expected inter-arrival, heartbeat messages from the exchange) and automatic re-join/escalation (ties Ch. 54 §54.4.3 safe-state and proposal 1). // Diagnostics on host and switch: `ip maddr`, `/proc/net/igmp`, `ip -s mroute`, `tcpdump igmp`, switch-side snooping group tables and querier state, `ethtool -S` multicast counters; a 'feed stopped' runbook. // Fabric design for fan-out: VLAN placement of feed groups, TTL/scope, snooping on vs off per VLAN (and the flooding trade), sizing replication for market-open bursts (ties Ch. 60 §60.4.2), multicast across redundant switches (proposal 9). // Measure it: join-latency distribution on the reference switch; CPU cost of unknown-multicast flooding on an isolated core; `IP_MULTICAST_ALL` cross-talk demonstration; `igmp_max_memberships` failure mode. // Pitfalls: joining only on A; assuming a join is instant at 09:29:59; snooping disabled 'for latency' flooding every port; a querier that is the trading host; IGMP leave dropping a group another socket still wants.
  EXISTING: chapters/51-socket-optimization-tcp-tuning.md §51.2 (line ~41) and §51.4.2 first bullet (line ~105): Join via IP_ADD_MEMBERSHIP, IP_MULTICAST_IF, SSM via IP_ADD_SOURCE_MEMBERSHIP named in one sentence; no IGMP/snooping/querier/join-latency/trap discussion.; chapters/appendix-C-system-tuning-checklist.md C.3 network sysctl item (line 47): 'tune igmp/reverse-path as needed' — no specifics.; chapters/60-network-fabric-switching.md §60.4.1, §60.4.3 (lines ~65, 81): Layer-1 replication for fan-out; no snooping/IGMP behaviour of the L2 fabric.; chapters/54-reliable-multicast-feed-recovery.md whole chapter: Sequence gaps and recovery channels; assumes the group is already flowing.; chapters/appendix-J-market-structure-primer.md J.4 (line ~49): Multicast and A/B lines conceptually.
  EVIDENCE: grep -n -w -i 'igmp' → only appendix-C line 47 ('tune igmp/reverse-path as needed'). grep -n -i -E 'pim-sm|pim sparse|\bmld\b|snooping|querier|rendezvous point|join latency|multicast flood|unknown multicast' → 0 relevant hits (the only 'snooping' hit is MESI in Ch. 7). --- [networking-depth #4] Order-Entry Session Engineering: TCP Liveness, Failure-Detection Timing, Reconnect & Gateway Failover
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part VIII — Kernel I/O, Sockets & Zero-Copy, immediately after Ch. 52 (Advanced TCP Internals & Tuning) as the chapter that closes the Part: Ch. 51–52 make the order socket fast; this chapter makes it survivable, and it is what Ch. 74's 'order gateway owns the venue session' and Appendix K.6's 'sessions reconnect and resynchronize' point at. | gap=partial | priority=high | area=other
  HFT: The order path is the one leg of tick-to-trade where a lost or duplicated message costs real money, and Linux TCP defaults will keep a dead gateway session 'connected' for minutes while resting orders sit exposed. Ch. 51–52 make the socket fast; nothing in the book says how fast you learn it is broken, or how to fail over to a warm backup session without duplicating an order.
  SCOPE: The session layer above TCP as a deterministic state machine (Ch. 74): FIX session (Logon/Heartbeat/TestRequest/ResendRequest/SequenceReset/gap fill), SoupBinTCP under OUCH, binary sessions (CME iLink 3, Eurex ETI-style): sequence numbers in both directions, what must be persisted, and recovery after reconnect without duplicating an order. // How long TCP takes to tell you the gateway is gone, by default: `tcp_retries2`=15 (~15 minutes to RST), RTO floor 200 ms and RACK-TLP tail-loss probes, `tcp_thin_linear_timeouts`/`TCP_THIN_LINEAR_TIMEOUTS` for thin streams, per-route `rto_min` and the `TCP_RTO_MIN_US` socket option (6.11+, verify toolchain/kernel), `TCP_USER_TIMEOUT` as the bounded unacked-data timer, `SO_KEEPALIVE` + `TCP_KEEPIDLE/KEEPINTVL/KEEPCNT` (seconds) vs application heartbeats (hundreds of ms) and TestRequest. // Half-open and silently dead sessions: detecting them with heartbeat deadlines, `TCP_INFO` (`tcpi_retrans`, `tcpi_unacked`, `tcpi_rto`, `tcpi_lost`) polled off-path (proposal 1), and link-down notifications (netlink) that beat every timer. // Cancel-on-disconnect: venue semantics and timers vs your own detection latency — the seconds during which resting orders are live and invisible; mass-cancel on failover; drop-copy reconciliation of in-flight orders (ties App F/J). // Warm standby and failover: pre-established, logged-on, heartbeated sessions to backup gateways (ties Ch. 46), idempotent client order IDs, sequence resynchronization, reconnect storms and backoff, exchange logon throttles (K.4), failover across NICs/paths (proposal 9). // Order-burst shaping against venue throttles: token-bucket pacing of orders per session, `SO_TXTIME`/pacing (Ch. 59) to stay under messages-per-second limits without software stalls; `TCP_CORK`/`MSG_MORE` for multi-part messages; `SO_LINGER`/RST vs FIN on shutdown and TIME_WAIT on fast reconnect. // How userspace stacks change the knobs: Onload `EF_TCP_*` timeouts/retransmit controls, ef_vi/DPDK custom stacks and where their RTO/keepalive logic lives (Ch. 62 §62.4.2). // Measure it: inject blackhole loss with `tc netem`/`nft drop` and measure time-to-detect and time-to-failover distributions under default vs tuned timers; the cost of heartbeat traffic on the hot path (should be a few stores — Ch. 71 pattern). // Pitfalls: trusting TCP to tell you; keepalive at 7200 s; reconnecting with reset sequence numbers; failover that re-sends unacked orders; heartbeats run on the hot core; `TCP_USER_TIMEOUT` set shorter than the venue's processing latency causing self-inflicted disconnects.
  EXISTING: chapters/52-advanced-tcp-internals-tuning.md §52.4.1–§52.4.3 (lines 55–80): NODELAY/QUICKACK/NOTSENT_LOWAT/GRO/congestion control/initcwnd/busy-poll/userspace TCP — speed knobs only; no keepalive, RTO, retries, user-timeout or session layer.; chapters/46-keeping-the-hot-path-warm.md §46.4.2 (lines ~130, 164): Pre-establish and heartbeat connections for warmth — not for liveness/failover.; chapters/74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.2.1 'Order gateway' bullet (line ~37): Gateway owns the venue session, sequence numbers, cancel-on-disconnect — one sentence.; chapters/appendix-K-exchange-connectivity.md K.4, K.6 (lines ~37–45, 57): Gateways/ports/throttles described commercially; 'sessions are designed to reconnect and resynchronize' with no mechanism.; chapters/appendix-J-market-structure-primer.md J.3 (lines 28–40): Order state machine (New/Ack/Fill/Cancel); no session layer.; chapters/38-coroutines-async-models.md §38.4.1 (line ~123): Venue sessions as the coroutine use case.

--- [networking-depth #5] Line-Rate Packet Capture & Timing-Faithful Replay: Taps, SPAN, AF_PACKET/PF_RING/pdump, and Replaying the Open
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part XII — Observability & Operations, immediately after Ch. 75 (Capture, Persistence & Replay Storage) and before the Ch. 76 capstone; Ch. 75 owns the journal and the storage path, this chapter owns the capture point/stack and the test harness that pushes a capture back onto the wire (the 'replay a captured microburst' every Ch. 53/55/60 exercise assumes). | gap=partial | priority=high | area=debugging-diagnostics
  HFT: Market-open behaviour cannot be reproduced in a lab unless you can capture it losslessly and push it back at the original nanosecond cadence, and the book's own exercises (Ch. 53 ex. 4, Ch. 55 ex. 5, Ch. 60 ex. 3) say 'replay a captured microburst' without naming a tool or its timing accuracy. This is the diagnostics harness behind feed-handler tuning, switch evaluation and the CI latency gate.
  SCOPE: Capture points and their fidelity: in-app journal vs host `AF_PACKET` vs NIC mirror vs switch SPAN/mirror (oversubscription drops, timestamp inaccuracy, ERSPAN encapsulation jitter) vs passive optical tap + L1 replicator (Ch. 60) vs hardware capture cards and switch-side timestamping/aggregation (Napatech, Endace, ExaNIC, Arista MetaWatch) — a table of 'what you lose' per point. // Host capture stacks at line rate: `AF_PACKET` `TPACKET_V3` mmap rings with `PACKET_FANOUT`, `PF_RING`/ZC, DPDK `rte_pdump`/`dpdk-pdump` and `rte_pcapng`, AF_XDP capture (Ch. 61), ef_vi/Onload capture hooks, `tcpdump -B`/`--time-stamp-precision nano`/`-j adapter_unsynced`, capture-host CPU/NUMA/NVMe sizing (ties Ch. 75 §75.4.3). // Timestamps in the file: pcapng interface blocks, hardware vs software timestamp provenance, PTP-disciplined (Ch. 58) vs free-running PHC, and keeping both A and B lines with their original skew. // Replay tools and their accuracy ceilings: `tcpreplay`/`tcpreplay-edit` (`--timer`, `--pps`, `-K` preload; tens-of-µs timing error), `tcprewrite` for MAC/IP/group rewriting, `MoonGen`/`TRex`/DPDK `pktgen` for Mpps with controlled inter-packet gaps, measured replay-timing error distributions on the reference NIC. // Nanosecond-faithful replay with hardware launch time: using `SO_TXTIME`/ETF/LAUNCHTIME (Ch. 59) or a DPDK/ef_vi replayer to reproduce captured inter-arrival times, time-scaling a burst (1×, 2×, 5×) to find the receiver's breaking point, replaying both lines from two ports with original skew. // Lab topology that does not lie: replay host → cut-through switch → system under test (no loopback), validating replay fidelity against the SUT's hardware RX timestamps, isolating the replayer the same way as a trading host (Part VII). // Replay as the regression input: feeding the Ch. 53/55/60 exercises, the Ch. 76 §76.4.1 CI latency gate and the Ch. 74 deterministic core from the same capture; synthetic burst generation when no capture exists. // Measure it: per-stack capture drop rate vs pps and packet size on the reference box; replay timing error per tool; SPAN vs tap timestamp error distribution. // Pitfalls: capturing on the trading host's hot core; SPAN as 'lossless'; replaying with the kernel's `pfifo_fast` smoothing the burst you wanted to reproduce; MAC/TTL/checksum rewrites that change the receiver's path; trusting software timestamps in the capture.

--- [networking-depth #6] Encrypted & Web-Protocol Venues: TLS, kTLS, WebSocket, JSON and REST on a Low-Latency Path
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part X — Kernel Bypass, RDMA & Transport, immediately after Ch. 65 (Transport Beyond TCP) as the closing transport chapter: Ch. 65 covers purpose-built fast transports; this chapter covers the slow-by-mandate transports (TLS, WSS, HTTPS) that crypto and cloud-hosted venues impose, and how to make them as fast as they can be. | gap=absent | priority=medium | area=other

  SCOPE: Where encryption is mandatory on the order/market-data path (crypto venues, FIX-over-TLS gateways, cloud-hosted venues, some drop-copy) and the latency tiers it implies (ms-scale tick-to-trade); what transfers from Parts VIII–IX and what does not. // TLS cost model: handshake round-trips, TLS 1.3 resumption/PSK and the 0-RTT replay hazard for orders, record-layer overhead per 100–300-byte order, cipher choice (AES-128/256-GCM on AES-NI/VAES vs ChaCha20-Poly1305) measured in ns/record, record sizing vs the library's own coalescing (a Nagle in disguise), and libraries (OpenSSL/BoringSSL/wolfSSL/s2n) compared on the reference box. // kTLS: moving record encryption into the kernel (TX/RX), interaction with `sendfile`/`splice` and zero-copy (Ch. 49), what you lose (userspace visibility, some cipher suites), measured against userspace TLS; --- [networking-depth #7] Appendix M — Trading in the Cloud: Virtualized NICs, SR-IOV/ENA/EFA, Placement Groups and the Latency You Can and Cannot Buy
  kind=new-appendix | placement=Appendices, after Appendix K (Exchange Connectivity & the Physical Layer) and cross-referenced from Appendix A.7 (Graviton in the cloud) and Ch. 6; the cloud counterpart to Appendix K's colocation floor. | gap=absent | priority=medium | area=other

  SCOPE: Why colocation wins (K.1) and where the cloud is nonetheless the venue: exchanges and crypto venues hosted in public cloud regions (e.g. ap-northeast-1), Nasdaq/CME cloud programs, Local Zones/Outposts; matching the latency tier to the venue (ties Ch. 1 tiers). // The virtualized NIC path: SR-IOV VF passthrough vs paravirtual (virtio-net/vhost) vs hardware-offload NICs (AWS ENA on Nitro: LLQ, default interrupt moderation, `ethtool -C` on ENA, queue counts by instance size), DPDK on ENA/VFs, what busy-poll and bypass look like inside a VM (Ch. 47, 62). // EFA/SRD and libfabric OS-bypass for intra-cluster traffic (risk fan-out, Ch. 68 MPI) — and why it is not a venue path; no native multicast in a VPC (Transit Gateway multicast caveats) so feeds arrive as unicast/TCP (ties Ch. 51, proposal 3). // Placement and limits: cluster placement groups, same-AZ/same-rack effects, instance network baseline vs burst credits (a throttle that manufactures market-open drops), bare-metal (`.metal`) instances for PMU/TSC/hugepage/SMT control (Ch. 2, 15, 17, 43). // Hypervisor jitter: steal time, VM exits (Ch. 6 §6.2.3), noisy neighbours, vCPU-to-core pinning inside a guest vs host, `isolcpus`/`nohz_full` in a VM, measured with `cyclictest` (Ch. 45) on metal vs VM. // Time in the cloud: no GNSS/PTP grandmaster (Ch. 58) — provider time services (AWS Time Sync PTP hardware clock on Nitro via ENA, chrony fallbacks) and the achievable offset, with consequences for cross-host tick-to-trade measurement. // Observability limits: no switch counters, taps or SPAN; what remains (ENA stats, VPC flow logs, proposal 1 host counters) and how to measure one-way latency without fabric access. // Measure it: intra-placement-group vs cross-AZ RTT p50/p99.9, ENA interrupt-moderation on/off, VF vs virtio vs metal RX latency, burst-credit throttling under a replayed microburst (proposal 5). // Decision guide: a table of Part VII–X techniques marked works / degraded / unavailable in a VM, and the cost-latency trade against colocation (K.7).

--- [networking-depth #8] Layer-2/3 Hygiene for the Order and Feed Paths: ARP/ND, DNS, Routing, MTU, VLAN & QoS Marking
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part IX — Market Data, NIC & Fabric, immediately before Ch. 60 (Network Fabric & Switching), after Ch. 59: the host-side configuration of the path Ch. 60 then follows into the switch. (Alternatively a §51.4.3 'Network-layer hygiene' section if a chapter is too much.) | gap=absent | priority=medium | area=health-monitoring-os-net
  HFT: The first order after a 30-second quiet spell can wait for an ARP probe, a reconnect can block in getaddrinfo, and a 9000-byte snapshot frame can add microseconds at a cut-through egress — none of which any Part VIII/IX knob addresses because the book never discusses the layers between the socket and the switch. These are cheap, deterministic wins and classic post-mortem findings.
  SCOPE: ARP/neighbour behaviour that stalls a first packet: unresolved neighbour → packet queued until reply (ms if the gateway is slow), stale-entry re-probing (`base_reachable_time_ms` ~30 s randomized → a unicast probe mid-session), permanent entries (`ip neigh replace ... nud permanent`) for exchange gateways and recovery servers, `arp_ignore`/`arp_announce` on multi-NIC hosts, gratuitous ARP on failover (proposal 9); IPv6 ND/RA where a venue mandates IPv6 (and MLD for multicast — proposal 3). // DNS off the hot path: `getaddrinfo` blocking and 5 s timeouts, resolve-at-startup with pinned `/etc/hosts`, `nsswitch`/`nscd`/`systemd-resolved` surprises, TLS libraries that resolve or fetch OCSP at connect time (proposal 6). // Routing on a multi-homed host: policy routing (`ip rule`) per NIC, `SO_BINDTODEVICE`, `rp_filter` strict vs loose and multicast arriving on the 'wrong' interface, default-gateway failover timers (VRRP/HSRP 1–3 s) vs application-level path failover, FIB lookup cost (proposal 2). // MTU and frame size: 1500 vs jumbo on market-data vs order paths — serialization math (9000 B at 10G = 7.2 µs), store-and-forward penalty and cut-through egress head-of-line (Ch. 60), PMTUD black holes and the DF bit on UDP snapshot/retransmission channels (Ch. 54), fragment reassembly cost (`ipfrag_*`), MSS clamping on TCP order sessions. // VLANs and QoS marking: 802.1Q tag cost and `rxvlan`/`txvlan` offload, 802.1p/DSCP marking from the socket (`SO_PRIORITY`, `IP_TOS`, `mqprio`/`skbprio` queue mapping) so orders ride a strict-priority egress queue ahead of capture/bulk traffic, DCB/ETS classes (ties Ch. 64), verifying with switch queue counters (proposal 1). // Checksums and offload interplay with hygiene: `rx-checksum` off masking a bad transceiver; `tx-nocache-copy`; verifying with `ethtool -k` per interface. // Measure it: an ARP-stall timeline captured with hardware timestamps (first order after a neighbour expiry); per-hop latency vs MTU on the reference switch; DSCP strict-priority vs best-effort order latency under a background flood; DNS resolve latency distribution. // Pitfalls: resolving a hostname on reconnect in the hot path; letting `irqbalance`-era defaults manage neighbour timers; jumbo frames 'for throughput' on the feed VLAN; one VLAN for orders and capture; rp_filter strict on an asymmetric A/B host.
  EXISTING: chapters/62-kernel-bypass-userspace-networking.md §62.2 (line ~34): 'The kernel provided IP/UDP/TCP, ARP, routing, the firewall' — the only ARP mention, as something bypass loses.; chapters/52-advanced-tcp-internals-tuning.md §52.4.2 (line ~69): `ip route ... initcwnd` — the only routing command.; chapters/appendix-C-system-tuning-checklist.md C.3 (line 47): 'tune igmp/reverse-path as needed'.; chapters/57-flow-steering-receive-scaling.md §57.2 (line ~34): VLAN as an n-tuple match field only.; chapters/64-lossless-ethernet-rdma-congestion.md §64.2, §64.4.1: PFC/ETS traffic classes for RoCE only — no DSCP/802.1p marking from the application.; chapters/60-network-fabric-switching.md §60.2 (lines ~32, 38): Serialization delay for 1500-byte frames; no MTU decision.
  EVIDENCE: grep -n -w 'ARP' → only Ch. 62 line 34. grep -n -w 'MTU' → 0; 'jumbo|PMTU|fragment|DF bit|IP_MTU_DISCOVER' → 0 relevant (fragmentation hits are memory). '\bDNS\b|getaddrinfo|gethostbyname|nsswitch|/etc/hosts|nscd|systemd-resolved' → 0. 'IPv6|ipv6|neighbor discovery|SLAAC|gratuitous|GARP' → 0. --- [networking-depth #9] Redundancy Engineering on the Host and Fabric: Dual-NIC/Dual-Switch A/B Design, Bonding Modes and Failover Timing
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 60 Network Fabric & Switching, as a new §60.4.4 'Redundant paths: dual-NIC/dual-switch design, bonding and failover timing' (with a matching §60.5 pitfall and checklist items), cross-referenced from Appendix K.6 (which stays conceptual) and from proposal 4's gateway failover. | gap=partial | priority=medium | area=other
  HFT: Appendix K.6 says A/B lines ride diverse paths and gateways have failover paths, but never how the host is wired and configured to use them or how long failover actually takes — bonding defaults detect a dead link in 100 ms to 30 s while resting orders are exposed. Dual-NIC/dual-switch design and measured failover timing are standard HFT operational engineering with no home in the book.
  SCOPE: Do not bond market-data NICs: the feed handler's A/B arbitration (Ch. 53/54) is the redundancy — line A on NIC0 → switch A, line B on NIC1 → switch B, separate RX queues/IRQs/cores (Ch. 42/57), separate PTP paths (Ch. 58), and why a bond hides exactly the per-line gap statistics you need. // Bonding/teaming for order entry and management: active-backup vs 802.3ad LACP (hashing cannot split one flow; `xmit_hash_policy`), `fail_over_mac`, gratuitous ARP on switchover, where the bond driver sits in the TX path and what it costs (proposal 2). // Failover detection timing: `miimon` (100 ms default) vs `arp_interval` monitoring vs LACP fast/slow timeouts (1–30 s) vs an application subscribing to netlink `RTM_NEWLINK` and failing over in microseconds; VRRP/HSRP gateway timers (proposal 8). // Dual-homed order sessions: primary and warm-standby gateway sessions on different NICs/paths (proposal 4), the sequence-resync and duplicate-order hazards of path failover, and how cancel-on-disconnect timers compare with your failover time. // Switch-side implications: MLAG/stacking and spanning-tree behaviour vs cut-through latency and multicast snooping state on the backup path (proposal 3); keeping the backup path warm (joins present, ARP resolved, sessions logged on — Ch. 46). // Measure it: failover-time distribution with hardware timestamps on both paths for link-pull, switch-reboot and gateway-kill scenarios; the latency asymmetry between primary and backup paths (Ch. 60 §60.3 one-way measurement). // Pitfalls: LACP on the feed NICs; a backup path that is store-and-forward; shared fate (both lines through one switch or one NIC); failover that resets IGMP state; untested failover discovered at the open.
  EXISTING: chapters/appendix-K-exchange-connectivity.md K.6 (lines 52–58): Three conceptual bullets: diverse physical paths for A/B, redundant cross-connects/gateways, latency-vs-resilience.; chapters/60-network-fabric-switching.md §60.4.2 'Symmetric paths' (line ~74), §60.3: Symmetry for measurability; no redundancy design.; chapters/74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.3 (line ~75): Measures process failover/restart time, not network path failover.; chapters/53-zero-copy-wire-market-data-decoding.md §53.2.3: A/B arbitration logic; interface/NIC placement not discussed.

'redundan' → A/B feed and capture redundancy statements only (Ch. 51/53/60/75), no NIC/switch wiring.

--- [networking-depth #10] Expand Ch. 60: Link Speed, FEC/PAM4 Latency, and Layer-1/FPGA Switch Applications (Multiplexing, Timestamping, In-Switch Risk)
  kind=expand-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 60 Network Fabric & Switching — add a §60.2 sub-topic 'Link speed, FEC and PAM4: the hidden per-hop cost' and extend §60.4.1/§60.4.3 with 'Layer-1 replication vs FPGA multiplexing/aggregation' and 'in-switch timestamping and risk'; correct the 'uniform and high (25/100 G)' guidance in §60.4.1, §60.5 and the checklist. | gap=partial | priority=medium | area=other
  HFT: Ch. 60 tells readers to run uniform 25/100G links to minimize serialization, but on a 300-byte order the mandatory FEC on those links costs more than the serialization it saves, which is why many HFT cross-connects deliberately stay at 10G; the chapter also folds pure L1 replication and FPGA multiplexing into one 'layer-1' bucket although they differ by an order of magnitude and solve different problems (fan-out vs fan-in to scarce exchange ports).
  SCOPE: Link-speed latency is not just serialization: 25G optics/long DACs and all PAM4 lanes (50G-per-lane 100G/200G/400G) require RS-FEC (Clause 91/108) adding ~100–250 ns per link plus PAM4 DSP latency, so for 64–300-byte frames 10G NRZ (no FEC) can beat 25/100G one-way; the arithmetic for a 300-byte frame at 10G (240 ns serialization) vs 25G+FEC vs 100G PAM4+FEC; when FEC can be disabled (short CA-N DACs, `ethtool --set-fec off`) and when the exchange handoff dictates it. // Transceiver and cable latency: DAC vs AOC vs SR/LR optics (ns-scale), retimers/gearboxes in breakout cables, and verifying link mode with `ethtool`; a corrected recommendation replacing 'uniform and high (25/100 G)' with 'measure per-hop latency per speed/FEC mode; prefer the fastest *no-FEC* mode the handoff allows'. // Layer-1 replication (~1–5 ns) vs FPGA multiplexing/aggregation (tens of ns, e.g. 7130 MetaMux / ExaLINK Fusion mux apps): the fan-in problem of many trading servers sharing scarce exchange ports, mux latency vs port cost, and fairness/arbitration inside the mux. // Switch-side timestamping and capture aggregation (MetaWatch-style): sub-ns timestamps appended to frames, aggregation to a capture host (proposal 5), using the switch as the authoritative one-way latency monitor (ties §60.3, Ch. 58). // In-switch pre-trade risk and kill-switch (MetaProtect-style FPGA apps) and in-switch feed filtering: what moves off the host, what it does to Ch. 72/74 fault domains and to measurement. // Cut-through latency by frame size and speed mismatch on the named platforms (7130/7150/Nexus 3550) from vendor datasheets, and multicast replication behaviour (snooping vs L1 — proposal 3). // Measure it: per-hop one-way latency at 10G/25G/25G+FEC/100G for 64/300/1500-byte frames with hardware timestamps; mux vs L1 replicator delta.
  EXISTING: chapters/60-network-fabric-switching.md §60.2 (lines 32–41), table (lines 49–53), §60.4.1 (lines 64–67), §60.4.3 (lines 76–81), §60.5 (line 92), checklist (line 108): Store-and-forward/cut-through/layer-1 defined with vendor names and ns ranges; 'Keep link speeds uniform and high (25/100 G)'; switch timestamping and replication mentioned; no FEC/PAM4, no mux/fan-in, no in-switch risk.; chapters/appendix-K-exchange-connectivity.md K.2 (line 23): 'Fiber vs copper at short range is a wash on latency' — transceiver cost/density only.; chapters/appendix-F-glossary.md F.6 'Cut-through / store-and-forward / layer-1', 'Serialization delay': Glossary entries consistent with the chapter's framing.
  EVIDENCE: grep -n -E '\bFEC\b|RS-FEC|forward error' → 0. 'PAM4|NRZ' → 0. 'preamble|inter-frame|interframe|\bIFG\b|\bIPG\b' → 0. 'autoneg|SFP|QSFP|transceiver|\bDAC\b|direct attach' → appendix-K lines 21/23 only. 'metamux|metawatch|metaprotect|fusion|mux' → Ch. 60 lines 33/34/65/118 name Metamux/Metaconnect as layer-1 devices only; no mux-vs-replicate distinction, no MetaWatch/risk apps. Ch. 60 line 67 verbatim: 'Keep link speeds uniform and high (25/100 G) along the path to minimize serialization delay'.

--- [networking-depth #11] Expand Ch. 55: NIC Selection, Firmware/Driver Version Management and a Per-NIC Latency Baseline You Re-run After Every Change
  kind=expand-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 55 NIC Features & Offloads — add §55.4.4 'Choosing the NIC: measured latency classes and feature matrix' and §55.4.5 'Firmware, driver and configuration management', plus a §55.3 baseline recipe; cross-reference Ch. 76 §76.4.1 (regression gates) and Appendix C. | gap=absent | priority=medium | area=health-monitoring-os-net
  HFT: The NIC is the first and last hop of tick-to-trade, and its firmware, driver and module parameters change latency by hundreds of nanoseconds with no source-code diff to review; the book tunes NIC features thoroughly but never says how to pin, verify or regression-test the NIC stack itself, or how to choose a card on measured rather than datasheet latency.
  SCOPE: Identify what you are running: `ethtool -i` (driver, firmware, bus-info), in-tree vs vendor out-of-tree drivers (sfc vs Onload bundles, mlx5 upstream vs OFED/DOCA, Intel `ice`/`i40e` with DDP packages), DKMS and the kernel-upgrade regression risk, pinning kernel+driver+firmware as one tested triple. // Driver module parameters and runtime settings that move latency: mlx5 CQE compression/moderation and striding RQ, Intel ITR/adaptive moderation, sfc `rx_irq_mod_usec`/Onload `EF_*` spin settings, persisted via `/etc/modprobe.d` and `udev` so a reboot does not silently revert them. // Firmware release notes as a latency change log: timestamp accuracy, pacing/LAUNCHTIME behaviour, RSS/steering fixes; the discipline of re-validating after every NIC firmware update (and after switch firmware — Ch. 60). // A repeatable NIC latency baseline: two-port or loopback-cable test using hardware TX→RX timestamps (Ch. 55/58) and `sockperf`/`ef_vi`/DPDK ping-pong, pinned and isolated (Part VII), stored as a time series and gated like Ch. 76 §76.4.1 — the number that tells you a driver update cost 300 ns. // The vendor landscape with measured RX-to-user latency classes and feature support (hardware timestamps, PHC, LAUNCHTIME, RSS contexts, n-tuple, PCIe gen/width): Solarflare/AMD X2/X3 + ef_vi/Onload, Cisco ExaNIC, NVIDIA ConnectX-6/7 + VMA/XLIO, Intel E810 + DPDK — with an explicit 'numbers date quickly; here is how to measure your own' caveat. // Physical placement: PCIe slot generation/width and the NIC-local NUMA node (Ch. 16, 56, 66), `ethtool -T` capability check, verifying link mode/FEC (proposal 10). // Pitfalls: a NIC upgraded by a package manager mid-week; adaptive moderation re-enabled by a new driver default; comparing latency across firmware versions without a fixed baseline harness; buying a NIC on datasheet latency measured at 64 bytes.

--- [networking-depth #12] Expand Appendix K.3: RF Link Engineering and Heterogeneous-Path Arbitration (Microwave vs Fiber Copies of the Same Feed)
