######## LENS resilience-ha — 12 proposals ########
SUMMARY: LENS: fault tolerance, high availability and failover for a production tick-to-trade system. Method: read README.md in full plus Ch. 46, 51, 54, 58, 73, 74, 75, 76 and Appendices J/K end-to-end, then grepped every candidate's distinctive terms across all 89 files (greps recorded per proposal).

HEADLINE FINDING: the book's resilience story stops at the host boundary. Ch. 74 is excellent on in-host containment (process-per-role, supervisor/heartbeats, crash-only restart, kill-switch living in the surviving gateway), Ch. 73 covers live reconfiguration, Ch. 75 the journal, Ch. 54 market-data gap recovery. But nothing covers what happens when the host, the venue session or the primary dies: no cross-host replication or standby, no sequencer (despite Appendix J.6 naming the role), no leader election / fencing / split-brain avoidance on the order path, no order-entry session layer (logon, sequence numbers, resend, takeover), no order-state reconciliation (drop-copy is defined in the glossary and never developed), no checkpoint mechanics (both Ch. 74 and 75 say recovery is 'bounded by snapshot frequency' without showing how to snapshot without stalling), no shm reattach/torn-write protocol (Ch. 26 and 50 both say 'see Ch. 74', which has none), no overload/degraded-mode policy, no fault-injection methodology, no feed-liveness (silent feed) detection, no host-side PTP failover behavior, and no DR. Twelve proposals follow: five high (session resilience, order-state reconciliation, sequencer/replication, split-brain-free failover, checkpoint mechanics), four medium, three low. Suggested home: a new Part XIII 'Fault Tolerance, High Availability & Failover' between Ch. 75 and the capstone (Ch. 76 must stay last), ordered Session Resilience -> Order-State Recovery -> Sequencer & Replication -> Failover without Split-Brain, with the smaller items as sections in Ch. 54/58/74/76 and Appendices E/F/K.

CONSISTENCY/ACCURACY ISSUES FOUND (relevant to the author's 'review for consistency' request): (1) Dangling cross-references that promise FT content elsewhere: Ch. 65 §65.8 'Aeron Cluster / consensus material (Ch. 74)' - Ch. 74 has no consensus/replication text; Appendix F 'Drop-copy ... (Ch. 74)' - Ch. 74 never mentions drop-copy; Ch. 26 §26.4-26.6 and Ch. 50 §50.2/50.5/50.6 'design re-attach (header version/PID check - Ch. 74)' - absent from Ch. 74; Appendix K.6 'sessions ... reconnect and resynchronize (J.3)' - J.3 has no session-layer content; Appendix J.6 'Sequencer ... (Ch. 74, 75)' - neither chapter uses the term. (2) §74.3 is titled 'failover/restart time' but covers only single-host restart. (3) The Phase-3 renumber missed chapter references inside ASCII diagrams/tables and in Appendices J/K: Ch. 74 diagrams cite 'Ch.48' for decode (should be 53), 'Ch.44-52' for RX, 'Ch.16' for clocks (17); Ch. 75 diagram cites Ch.33/36/50/45 (should be 34/37/55-58/48) and '§75.2 ... Ch. 23's persist before you act' is unexplained; Ch. 76 diagram cites Ch.51 for eBPF (61), Ch.62-63 for replay (74-75), Ch.63 for capture (75), §64.4.1/§64.4.3 (§76.4.x); Ch. 54 diagram 'TCP - Ch.69' (52); Ch. 73 diagram '§61.4.3' (§73.4.3) and 'Ch.35' for reclamation (36); Ch. 46 table cites Ch.6/11/14/12/47 for caches/L1i/TLB/predictors/TCP (7/12/15/13/51) and '§43.2' (§46.2); Appendix J cites 'Ch. 56' for hardware timestamps (58), 'Ch. 34' for seqlock publication (35), 'Ch. 60' for untrusted input (72); Appendix K cites 'Ch. 56' for PTP five times (58) and 'Ch. 68' for FPGA (69). (4) Counts: README line 5 says 'seven appendices (A-G)' (there are twelve, A-L); Ch. 76 §76.1 says 'Sixty-three chapters built a toolkit' and its closing paragraph says 'The Appendices (A-G)'. Recommend a sweep for `Ch\.\d+` (no space, diagram style) and `§\d+\.\d+` inside code fences, plus Appendices J/K.

--- [resilience-ha #1] The Sequencer & Replicated Deterministic State Machines: Hot/Warm Standby Across Hosts
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part XII (or a new Part XIII 'Fault Tolerance, High Availability & Failover' inserted before the capstone). After Ch. 75 (Capture/Persistence) and after the proposed Session-Resilience and Order-State-Recovery chapters; before the proposed split-brain/failover chapter; Ch. 76 stays last. Prerequisites: Ch. 74 (deterministic core), Ch. 75 (journal), Ch. 35/50 (publication, shm rings), Ch. 51/54 (multicast), Ch. 63 (RDMA), Ch. 65 (Aeron), Ch. 46 (warming). | gap=absent | priority=high | area=design-patterns
  HFT: A crash-only restart on one host (Ch. 74) still leaves the firm blind for hundreds of milliseconds with open positions; a replica that already holds bit-identical state can take over in microseconds. Every replication design adds a hop, a hash or an ack to tick-to-trade, so the availability/latency trade must be measured rather than assumed - the book's own method applied to HA.
  SCOPE: Replication models for a trading system: active-passive hot standby (replica consumes the same input stream, holds identical state, outputs suppressed), active-active with a single emitter, warm standby + journal shipping, cold standby + snapshot restore; RPO/RTO of each and which failures each survives (process, host, rack, site). // The sequencer pattern: one process stamps a total order on every input (market data, fills/acks, timers, operator commands, config changes from Ch. 73) so N deterministic replicas (Ch. 74) fold the same sequenced stream; sequencer placement (in-process, shm, switch-side), its ns cost as an extra hop, and sequencer redundancy (primary/backup sequencer, sequence-number lease). // Replication transports and their latency: multicast of the sequenced stream (Ch. 51), one-sided RDMA WRITE of state/journal into the standby (Ch. 63 exercise-3 primitive), Aeron Cluster / Raft replicated log (Ch. 65); synchronous (ack-before-emit) vs asynchronous replication and the RPO bought with each microsecond. // Timers, randomness and 'now' as sequenced inputs so replicas fire identically (extends Ch. 74.2.2); output journaling and the single-emitter rule so a standby never sends. // Replica lag and divergence detection: sequence lag, PTP-timestamped apply latency (Ch. 58), periodic state checksums/hashes exchanged between primary and replicas (ties Ch. 76's golden diff), and the response to divergence (fence the replica, re-snapshot). // Keeping the standby warm: the replica runs the real hot path on live traffic with outputs suppressed (Ch. 46's side-effect-suppression discipline reused) so promotion is warm, not cold. // Measure it: tick-to-trade added by the sequencer hop and by synchronous replication (ns, HdrHistogram); apply-lag distribution under a replayed open microburst (Ch. 53/75); primary->standby promotion time (detect + promote + first correct order); warm vs cold standby first-tick latency. // Economics and placement: same host (other core), same rack, other rack, other colo (Appendix K) - availability gained vs ns spent; when asynchronous replication on the order path is the right call. // Pitfalls: a replica that reads its own clock; non-sequenced inputs (a config change applied to one replica); replication back-pressure stalling the primary (Ch. 75's lesson); the sequencer as the new single point of failure; output journal written after the send.
  EXISTING: 74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.2.2, §74.3, §74.4.4: Deterministic state machine and single-host restart; §74.3 is titled 'failover/restart time' but covers only in-host restart - no cross-host replica or promotion.; 65-transport-beyond-tcp.md §65.4.1 (line 64), §65.8 (line 132): One sentence on Aeron Cluster 'Raft-based fault-tolerant replicated state machines'; §65.8 points to 'Aeron Cluster / consensus material (Ch. 74)' - Ch. 74 contains no consensus or replication material (dangling cross-reference).; appendix-J-market-structure-primer.md J.6 'Sequencer': One-line role definition pointing at Ch. 74/75, which never use the word 'sequencer'.; 63-infiniband-verbs-rdma.md §63.6 exercise 3: One-sided RDMA publication of a snapshot + sequence into a remote reader's memory - the primitive, not a replication design.; 60-network-fabric-switching.md §60.4.1, §60.4.3: Layer-1 'replication' means physical fan-out of a feed to redundant systems; no state replication.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il -E 'standby|hot spare|replica|replication|active-passive|active/passive|active-active|journal shipping|state machine replication|lockstep' chapters/*.md -> only unrelated senses: 'lockstep' (Ch. 8/11/32/67 SIMD/convoy), Ch. 16 per-NUMA-node copies, Ch. 60 layer-1 fan-out, Ch. 65 Aeron Cluster one-liner. grep -n -i -E 'sequencer|total order|aeron (cluster|archive)|event.sourc' -> 'sequencer' only appendix-J:72; Aeron Cluster only 65:64/124/132. grep -i -E 'raft|consensus|replica|sequencer' 74-*.md -> no hits. grep -n -i -E 'diverge|state hash|hash of (the )?state|checksum .* state' -> only replay-vs-golden in 74-76; no replica divergence detection. grep -n -i -E '\bRTO\b|\bRPO\b|MTTR|recovery time objective|availability' -> no HA usage anywhere.

--- [resilience-ha #2] Failover Without Split-Brain on the Order Path: Leases, Fencing Tokens & Exchange-Side Safety Nets
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part XII / proposed Part XIII, immediately after the Sequencer & Replication chapter and before Ch. 76. Builds on Ch. 74.4.3-4 (supervisor, kill-switch), Ch. 17 (monotonic clocks for leases), Ch. 58 (PTP), Ch. 65 (Aeron Cluster), Appendix K.4 (venue ports/throttles). | gap=absent | priority=high | area=other
  HFT: A failover that lets two gateways send under the same session is worse than an outage: it doubles positions at the worst moment and is a regulatory event. The chapter makes 'exactly one sender' a measured, enforced property on the order path rather than an assumption.
  SCOPE: Why split-brain is a financial invariant, not a consistency nicety: two instances both believing they are primary -> duplicated orders and doubled position; the 'exactly one emitter per venue session' rule and where it must be enforced (gateway, session, exchange). // Cross-host failure detection: heartbeats over two independent paths (shm in-host - Ch. 74; multicast/unicast plus a side channel cross-host), fixed timeouts vs phi-accrual, and the detect-time vs false-positive trade now with network partitions and SMI/NUMA stalls (Ch. 6/16) that look like death. // Leases and fencing: a lease-holding primary that self-fences (stops emitting) when its lease expires on a monotonic clock (Ch. 17 - never CLOCK_REALTIME); a monotonically increasing epoch/fencing token stamped on every outbound order and checked at the last hop (gateway/session); STONITH-style forced kill of the old primary via the supervisor. // Consensus vs two-node simplicity: when a 3-node Raft / Aeron Cluster (Ch. 65) earns its quorum round-trip on the order path versus a two-node primary/standby that relies on exchange cancel-on-disconnect as the safety net; measuring the quorum RTT and where it sits in the tick-to-trade budget. // Exchange-side safety nets used deliberately: cancel-on-disconnect (CoD) semantics and timing per venue, port-level kill switches, mass-cancel on logout, self-match prevention across two instances of yourself, venue throttles (Appendix K.4) under a failover burst. // Promotion protocol: fence old primary -> confirm venue-side state (CoD fired or mass-cancel acked) -> standby takes session ownership (ties Session-Resilience chapter) -> reconcile working orders (ties Order-State-Recovery chapter) -> warm (Ch. 46) -> emit. // Measure it: end-to-end failover time distribution (detect -> fenced -> promoted -> first accepted order) under injected partitions (tc netem / link down - ties the fault-injection section); prove zero duplicate orders at a simulated exchange across 10^4 induced failovers. // Pitfalls: timeouts shorter than a legitimate stall; clock-based leases on unsynchronized clocks; a fencing token checked everywhere except at the socket; both nodes down after a false positive; failing over into a cold standby (Ch. 46).
  EXISTING: 74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.2.1 line 37, §74.4.3, §74.4.4 line 127: Single-host supervisor/heartbeats/kill-switch; 'cancel-on-disconnect' appears twice in passing and is never defined or designed around.; 65-transport-beyond-tcp.md §65.4.1 line 64: Aeron Cluster 'for consensus/failover' in one clause; no leader election, lease or fencing content.; appendix-K-exchange-connectivity.md K.4, K.6: Venue ports/throttles and one sentence that 'redundant cross-connects and gateways give order entry a failover path'.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il -E 'split.brain|leader election|fencing|fence token|\blease\b|quorum|raft|paxos|consensus|cancel.on.disconnect|mass cancel' chapters/*.md -> 'fencing' only as memory barriers (Ch. 10/17/30/66), 'lease' only Ch. 44's CLOS metaphor, raft/consensus only Ch. 65 (pointing at Ch. 74, which has none), cancel-on-disconnect only 74:37 and 74:127 (passing); zero hits for split-brain, leader election, quorum, paxos, mass cancel. grep -n -i -E 'IGMP|virtual IP|\bVIP\b|VRRP|keepalived|gratuitous ARP|TCP_USER_TIMEOUT|SO_KEEPALIVE' -> zero hits (no failover-mechanics plumbing anywhere).

--- [resilience-ha #3] Order-Entry Session Resilience: Sequence Numbers, Reconnect & Session Takeover
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part XII / proposed Part XIII, first of the new HA chapters (directly after Ch. 75, before Order-State Recovery) because session semantics are a prerequisite for reconciliation and failover. Alternative: Part IX after Ch. 54, as the order-entry counterpart to feed recovery. Prerequisites: Ch. 51/52 (TCP), Ch. 53 (OUCH/FIX/SBE framing), Ch. 46 (connection warming), Ch. 26/75 (persisting state), Appendix J.3/J.5. | gap=absent | priority=high | area=other
  HFT: The venue session is the only thing that can cancel or amend your working orders; losing it mid-session strands exposure. Resuming the session with correct sequence numbers from a restarted or standby gateway is the mechanism behind the 'keep the venue session up' promise Ch. 74 makes but never implements.
  SCOPE: The session layer under order entry: FIX session (Logon/Logout, Heartbeat/TestRequest, MsgSeqNum both directions, ResendRequest, SequenceReset-GapFill, PossDupFlag), SoupBinTCP for OUCH (login with requested sequence number, server-side sequencing), CME iLink 3 and other binary session layers - what state each requires the client to keep. // Which state must outlive the process: next inbound/outbound sequence numbers, in-flight (sent-not-acked) orders, last-received execution report - persisted in shared memory (Ch. 26, 74) and the journal (Ch. 75) so a restarted gateway or a standby resumes the same session without a sequence reset. // Write-before-send on the order path: journaling the outbound message before TX (WAL for orders), measuring its ns cost against the recovery it buys, and where the journal write sits relative to the NIC doorbell (Ch. 62/66). // Detecting a dead session faster than TCP: application heartbeats and TestRequest timers, TCP_USER_TIMEOUT / SO_RCVTIMEO, link-state events (ethtool/netlink); the heartbeat timer as a sequenced input to the deterministic core (Ch. 74). // Reconnect: backoff that respects venue reconnect throttles (Appendix K.4), avoiding reconnect storms when a gateway host with N sessions dies, re-logon with correct sequence numbers, servicing the venue's ResendRequest from the journal, gap-filling administrative messages. // Session takeover by a standby host: moving session identity (venue-side failover port vs VIP/gratuitous ARP), sequence-number handoff from shared/replicated state, pre-established backup sessions to a secondary gateway port (warm - Ch. 46/51), and the 'unknown in-flight' window handed to reconciliation. // Measure it: TCP-loss -> detected -> logged on -> first accepted order, p50/p99 for restart vs standby takeover; the cost of write-before-send (ns) on the hot path; cold vs pre-established backup session first-order latency (Ch. 46). // Pitfalls: sequence reset without gap-fill (silently dropped orders); resending non-idempotent orders on ResendRequest; two gateways logging on with the same CompID/session; heartbeats scheduled on the hot core; venue CoD firing before the standby reconnects.
  EXISTING: appendix-J-market-structure-primer.md J.3, J.5: Order lifecycle states and OUCH/FIX message types; no session layer (logon, sequence numbers, resend).; appendix-K-exchange-connectivity.md K.6 line 57: States that 'sessions are designed to reconnect and resynchronize (J.3) without losing order state' - J.3 contains no such material (dangling cross-reference).; 74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.2.1 line 37: One line: the gateway 'owns the venue session... sequence numbers, working-order state'.; 46-keeping-the-hot-path-warm.md §46.4.2: Pre-establishing and heartbeating connections to keep cwnd warm - warming, not session recovery.; 38-coroutines-async-models.md §38.4 line 123: Mentions 'FIX session state machines (Ch. 53)' as a coroutine use case; Ch. 53 has no session state machine.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il -E 'logon|logout|sequence reset|resend request|resendrequest|msgseqnum|session takeover|soupbin|session recovery|gap ?fill|test request|HeartBtInt' chapters/*.md -> only 53:57 (UDP reorder buffer) and 54:79 (market-data retransmission 'resend request'); zero order-entry session-layer hits. grep -n -i -E 'reconnect|disconnect' -> 46:133/142/166 ('reconnect' listed as a cold-start event), 58:135 (GPS), 74:37/127 (passing), appendix-K:57 (one sentence). grep -n -i -E 'TCP_USER_TIMEOUT|SO_KEEPALIVE|tcp_keepalive|VIP|gratuitous ARP' -> zero hits. grep -c -i heartbeat 53-*.md 54-*.md -> 0.

--- [resilience-ha #4] Order-State Recovery & Reconciliation After a Crash: Idempotent Submission, Duplicate Detection, Drop-Copy & Position Rebuild
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part XII / proposed Part XIII, right after the Session-Resilience chapter and before Sequencer & Replication; before Ch. 76. Prerequisites: Ch. 74 (gateway/risk roles), Ch. 75 (output journal), Ch. 25 (flat hash tables for dedup/order maps), Ch. 27/72 (integer safety in position math), Appendix J.3. | gap=absent | priority=high | area=other
  HFT: After any failover the firm's real position is whatever the exchange says it is, and the gap between that and local state is unhedged risk. Idempotent submission and fast, measured reconciliation turn 'we think we're flat' into 'we know', within a time budget that keeps the kill-switch window small.
  SCOPE: The unknown-state matrix after a crash: each in-flight order is in one of {never sent, sent-not-acked, acked, partially filled, filled, cancelled, replaced} and the recovering process cannot tell which; the two hazards (resend -> duplicate exposure; don't resend -> missing hedge) and the policy per order type (IOC vs resting). // Idempotent submission: ClOrdID/token generation that survives restart (monotonic, host/epoch-tagged, journaled before send), venue-side duplicate-ClOrdID rejection as the backstop, client-side dedup tables (open addressing - Ch. 25) sized for the session and pre-allocated (Ch. 23/24). // Three sources of truth and how to reconcile them: the local output journal (Ch. 75), the venue's OrderStatusRequest / order-mass-status replies, and the drop-copy session (an independent fault domain - Ch. 74) - conflict rules, lag tolerance, and the conservative risk posture (assume worst-case position) until reconciled. // Rebuilding working orders and positions: deterministic replay of the output journal through the core (Ch. 74/75) to recompute expected state, diffed against the venue's view; fills arriving for unknown orders; cancel-replace chains and partial fills that straddle the crash boundary. // Deciding mass-cancel vs reconcile: time budget, exposure, venue CoD state; the risk process (Ch. 74) as owner of the decision and of the position limits applied during the uncertainty window. // Measure it: reconciliation time vs number of working orders and drop-copy lag; hot-path cost (ns) of journaling each outbound order before send; duplicate-detection lookup cost (Ch. 25) on the order path. // Pitfalls: ClOrdID reuse after restart (different host, same counter); trusting a single source; reconciling on the hot core; drop-copy lag mistaken for missing fills; scaled-integer position arithmetic errors during rebuild (Ch. 27/72).
  EXISTING: appendix-F-glossary.md line 12 'Drop-copy': Defined and pointed at Ch. 74 - Ch. 74 never mentions drop-copy (dangling cross-reference).; 74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.4.4: Gateway cancels a dead strategy's working orders (kill-switch); no reconciliation, idempotency or unknown-state handling.; 65-transport-beyond-tcp.md §65.5 line 96, §65.6 line 120: 'Design for message semantics (sequence/idempotence - Ch. 53/54)' - those chapters cover market-data dedup, not order idempotency.; appendix-J-market-structure-primer.md J.3: Order state machine (New/Ack/Partial/Fill/Cancel) as domain background; nothing on recovering it.; 38-coroutines-async-models.md, 52-advanced-tcp-internals-tuning.md, 57-flow-steering-receive-scaling.md, 72-secure-programming-low-latency.md 38:123, 52:66, 57:67, 72:13: Drop-copy mentioned only as a type of connection to steer/tune/trust.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il -E 'drop.copy|reconcil|idempot|duplicate order|duplicate detection|dedup|orphan|clordid|order status|unknown order|in.flight order|mass status|position reconc' chapters/*.md -> drop-copy only as a connection type (38/52/57/72) plus glossary F:12 (-> Ch. 74, which has no hit for 'drop'); 'idempot' only 65:96/120; 'orphan' only shm segments (26/50) and 74:37 ('orphan orders', passing); 'dedup' only market-data A/B (53:171/236); zero hits for clordid, order status, reconcil, unknown order, mass status. grep -n -i -E '(position|working.order|open order).{0,60}(restart|recover|persist|crash)' -> only 74:37 and F:23 (gateway 'holds working-order state'); no recovery procedure.

--- [resilience-ha #5] State Checkpointing & Snapshot/Restore Mechanics for Fast Restart
  kind=expand-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 74 - new §74.4.6 'Checkpointing the core: consistent snapshots without stalling the hot path' (with §74.3's recovery table updated), cross-referenced from Ch. 75 §75.4.4 (which keeps only the storage/indexing side). Builds on Ch. 15/23/26 (page faults, COW, mmap), Ch. 9/53 (fixed-layout serialization), Ch. 46 (re-warm on restore). | gap=partial | priority=high | area=other
  HFT: Ch. 74 and 75 both say recovery time is 'bounded by snapshot frequency' but never show how to take one while the hot path keeps trading; a naive snapshot is itself a tail event. The mechanics decide whether a crash-only restart is 200 ms or 20 s at the open.
  SCOPE: The snapshot as a (state, last-applied-sequence) pair taken at a sequence boundary of the deterministic core; recovery = load snapshot + replay journal from seq+1 (Ch. 75) + re-warm (Ch. 46); RTO = detect + load(size) + replay(rate x interval) + warm - the curve to measure. // Taking a snapshot without stalling the hot path: fork()-based copy-on-write snapshot (the Redis approach) and its measured cost on the parent (COW page-fault storms, TLB shootdowns - Ch. 15, huge-page interaction) vs double-buffered state with an off-core serializer vs incremental dirty-page checkpoints (soft-dirty bits, userfaultfd). // Where the snapshot lives: a shared-memory segment that survives process death (crash-only restart just maps it - Ch. 26/74) vs disk/NVMe (survives host death; feeds the standby - ties the Replication chapter); header with magic, layout version, generation and checksum (ties the shm-reattach section). // Serialization that restores without allocating: fixed-layout zero-copy structs (Ch. 9/53) with explicit schema versioning for strategy/config evolution (Ch. 73.4.2); pre-faulted destination buffers (Ch. 23). // Choosing the checkpoint interval: snapshot cost x frequency vs replay catch-up time; snapshot size vs book depth/universe; checkpoints at natural quiescent points (pre-open, auctions) vs fixed cadence. // Measure it: p99.9 of the hot path during a fork() snapshot vs double-buffer vs dirty-page; restore time vs snapshot size; replay catch-up time vs journal length since checkpoint (a replayed open microburst - Ch. 53/75). // Pitfalls: snapshotting mid-apply (inconsistent cut); fork() in a process with mlock'd/huge-page memory; a snapshot format tied to a since-changed struct layout; snapshot written to the capture device (Ch. 75.5 shared fate); restoring cold and going live (Ch. 46).
  EXISTING: 74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.3 table ('bounded by snapshot frequency'), §74.3 lesson 2, §74.4.4: Names periodic snapshots as the bound on recovery; no mechanism for taking them.; 75-capture-persistence-replay-storage.md §75.2, §75.4.4, §75.5 'No snapshots -> unbounded replay': Index checkpoints and 'a state snapshot (the Ch. 74 core's state at a sequence point)' as storage artifacts; no consistency, COW or cost discussion.; 26-memory-mapping.md §26.2 line 25: MAP_PRIVATE = copy-on-write defined; not applied to snapshotting.; 50-inter-process-communication.md §50.3 code (lines 82-90): fork() used only to spawn the IPC demo consumer.
  EVIDENCE: grep -n -i -E 'checkpoint|copy.on.write|fork\(\)|incremental snapshot|consistent snapshot|snapshot (interval|frequency|without)' chapters/*.md -> 74:83/92 and 75:37/99/120/141 (all one-line 'take periodic snapshots'); copy-on-write only 26:25 (definition) and 31:124 (COW config pattern); fork() only 50:82/90. grep -c -i snapshot -> 74: 3 hits, 75: 4 hits, all without mechanics; the large counts in 35 (26) and 54 (22) are seqlock snapshots and exchange book snapshots. grep -n -i -E 'soft.dirty|userfaultfd|dirty page' -> zero hits.

--- [resilience-ha #6] Shared-Memory Crash Consistency & the Reattach Protocol
  kind=expand-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 74 - new §74.4.7 'Segment lifecycle: reattach, epochs and torn-write recovery' (the content Ch. 26 §26.4/§26.5 and Ch. 50 §50.2/§50.5/§50.6 explicitly defer to 'Ch. 74'). Two chapters already tell the reader this content lives in Ch. 74.
  SCOPE: Segment header discipline: magic, layout version, generation/epoch counter, owner PID + process start time (or pidfd), heartbeat slot, checksum; the reattach decision tree on restart - valid header? owner alive? epoch matches readers'? -> reuse, reconcile, or recreate. // Producer-died-mid-write: commit-flag / sequence-last ordering (Ch. 35) so a half-written slot is detectable; reader policy (skip/truncate to last committed); a writer epoch stamped on records so readers discard a dead epoch's tail; new writer continues the sequence vs bumps the epoch and forces a resync (Ch. 54's snapshot-resync shape). // Consumer position persistence: readers that must resume exactly where they stopped (order ring -> gateway) store their cursor in the segment; at-least-once vs at-most-once semantics per ring and the dedup that at-least-once requires (ties Order-State Recovery). // Cross-process locks and death: PTHREAD_MUTEX_ROBUST / EOWNERDEAD and robust futex lists for control-plane locks; why the data plane uses none (Ch. 74.5 shared fate) and how to audit for one. // Orphaned /dev/shm segments: ownership (the supervisor creates, pre-faults, mlock's and unlinks), tmpfs sizing and mlock accounting across restarts, huge-page-backed segments (Ch. 15) on reattach. // Measure/test it: kill -9 the writer at random points (ties the fault-injection section) and assert readers never consume a torn record; reattach time vs segment size (pre-faulted vs not - Ch. 26). // Pitfalls: reusing a stale segment with a live-looking header; PID reuse fooling the owner check; raw pointers in the segment (Ch. 74.4.1); readers blocked on a dead writer's futex (Ch. 50.5 lost wakeup).

--- [resilience-ha #7] Overload, Degraded Modes & Load Shedding: What to Drop at the Open
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part XII, after Ch. 74 (process topology) and before Ch. 75 - the degraded-mode state machine is owned by the risk/supervisor processes Ch. 74 defines; alternatively Part IX after Ch. 54 for the conflation half. Builds on Ch. 1 §1.2.1 (queueing), Ch. 53 §53.2.4 (microbursts), Ch. 54 (gap policy), Ch. 35/28 (latest-value snapshots, dirty bitmaps), Ch. 71/75 (ring full-buffer policy), Appendix K.4 (venue throttles). | gap=scattered | priority=medium | area=design-patterns
  HFT: The open and news bursts deliver more messages than any budget absorbs, and a strategy that falls behind trades on stale prices while believing it is current. Deciding in advance what to drop, conflate or pull - and measuring the latency the policy preserves - is the difference between a controlled degraded mode and a silent loss.
  SCOPE: Overload as a tail event: the 'falling behind' failure mode (consumer slower than the feed -> lag grows -> every decision is stale) and how to measure lag - sequence lag per channel and PTP-timestamped message age (Ch. 58) as first-class production metrics (Ch. 76). // Market-data conflation: coalescing multiple updates per instrument into the latest book state when behind - a seqlock latest-value slot (Ch. 35) plus a per-instrument dirty bitmap (Ch. 28) the strategy drains; when conflation is wrong (trade prints, order-level signals, sequence-dependent strategies). // Prioritization lanes: own fills/acks before market data, BBO before deep book, quoted symbols before the universe; priority rings in the shm data plane (Ch. 34/37/50) and the branch cost of lane selection on the hot path. // Shedding policy and the degraded-mode state machine: NORMAL -> CONSERVATIVE (widen/pull deep quotes) -> PULL (cancel all quotes, no new orders) -> KILL, with hysteresis and operator override; who decides (risk process - Ch. 74), how it is published (Ch. 73 atomic swap), logged (Ch. 71) and alerted. // Admission control at the gateway: a branch-light token-bucket order-rate limiter that respects venue throttles (Appendix K.4), reject-vs-queue semantics, cancel priority under throttle. // A per-stream drop policy table unifying what the book says piecemeal: lossless (capture - Ch. 75), overwrite-oldest (logging - Ch. 71), conflate (market data), never-drop-but-fence (orders). // Measure it: lag growth under a replayed open microburst (Ch. 53/75) with and without conflation; tick-to-decision p99.9 during shedding vs not; rate-limiter cost (ns) on the order path; detect-to-degraded transition time. // Pitfalls: conflating fills; shedding silently; oscillating between modes without hysteresis; degraded mode decided on the hot core; sizing the box for the mean and shedding at the open every day.
  EXISTING: 01-the-latency-mindset.md §1.2.1 lines 49, 261: Queueing theory: latency explodes near utilization 1; no technique for the overload itself.; 53-zero-copy-wire-market-data-decoding.md §53.2.4, §53.4.4: Hardware/driver side of microbursts (RX ring sizing, reorder buffers, drop detection); no application-level conflation or shedding.; 54-reliable-multicast-feed-recovery.md §54.4.3: Pause/continue/safe-state per instrument on a gap - a data-quality policy, not an overload policy.; 71-zero-overhead-logging.md, 75-capture-persistence-replay-storage.md, 37-the-disruptor-pattern.md 71:62/119/132, 75:116/138, 37:136/175: Ring full-buffer policy (drop-and-count vs overwrite-oldest; never block the producer); Disruptor wrap gating as 'natural backpressure'.; appendix-K-exchange-connectivity.md K.4 line 41: Venue message-rate throttles named; no client-side limiter.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il -E 'load.shedding|\bshed|conflat|degraded|graceful degradation|back.?pressure|brown.?out' chapters/*.md -> 'conflate' only as a verb (11:165, 23:17/26); 'graceful degradation' only fabric (64:88, 65:107) and Appendix H.6 (feature macros); 'shed' only 75:116 ('shed at the capture layer'); backpressure only ring policy (34/37/71/75) and 54:100. grep -n -i -E 'rate.?limit|token bucket|leaky bucket|order rate|orders per second|admission control' -> 54:79/111/121 (rate-limiting NAK requests), 59:121 (EDT replaced token buckets), K:41 (venue throttles); no order-rate limiter or admission control. grep -n -i -E 'degraded mode|safe.state' -> 'safe-state' only as the kill-switch end state in 54/74; no graduated modes.

--- [resilience-ha #8] Testing Failover: Fault Injection, Game Days & Resilience Gates
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 76 - new §76.4.4 'Fault injection and failover drills' alongside the latency-regression gate (§76.4.1) and simulation harness (§76.4.3); cross-referenced from Ch. 74.6 and the new HA chapters. Builds on Ch. 40 (stress/fuzz), Ch. 61 (eBPF), Ch. 74-75. | gap=scattered | priority=medium | area=debugging-diagnostics
  HFT: Every failover path that is not exercised rots, and the first real exercise is otherwise during the open with positions on. Fault injection makes recovery a measured, repeatable property - the same move the book makes for latency with the regression gate.
  SCOPE: A fault catalogue for a trading box: kill -9 / SIGSTOP (crash/hang) at random sequence points, tc netem delay/loss/partition, iptables drop of the venue session, link flap (ip link down / ethtool), NIC/port failover, PTP loss (Ch. 58), capture disk full (Ch. 75), OOM, clock step - and the blast radius each is expected to have. // Deterministic fault injection: faults as sequenced inputs to the Ch. 74 core / §76.4.3 simulator so a failover scenario replays bit-identically and can be bisected like any regression. // Injection tooling: in-process hooks behind a compile-time flag that compiles away (Ch. 18), LD_PRELOAD and strace -e inject for syscall faults, eBPF bpf_override_return / kprobes (Ch. 61) and the kernel fault-injection framework, switch/NIC-level faults (Ch. 60) for 'below the host' failures. // Assertions that matter: no duplicate or orphaned order beyond the CoD window, replica state hashes equal after promotion, recovery within RTO, survivors' p99.9 unchanged during the failure (Ch. 54's flat-distribution test generalized). // Game days: scheduled drills against venue test/certification sessions and a production-like box (Appendix C), runbooks, measuring MTTR and detect time, and feeding findings back into supervisor policy (Ch. 74.4.3). // Resilience gates in CI: the failover test as a pass/fail gate next to the latency gate (§76.4.1), with the same stability requirements so it is trusted rather than disabled. // Pitfalls: testing only clean exits (SIGTERM) and never SIGKILL/SIGSTOP; drills that never touch a real venue session; injecting faults the production monitoring cannot see; flaky resilience tests being turned off.
  EXISTING: 74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.6 exercises 1, 3, 5: Exercises inject a segfault, kill a process, make one hang - ad hoc, no tooling or methodology.; 76-production-profiling-end-to-end-case-study.md §76.4.3 line 90, checklist line 124: One bullet: simulation should 'inject the rare and the hostile... the failover (Ch. 74)'.; 40-concurrency-correctness-tooling.md §40.4.1-40.4.3: Stress testing, fuzzing and model checking for concurrency bugs; no system-level fault injection.; 61-ebpf-bpftrace-xdp.md §61.4.1: kprobes/uprobes for tracing; bpf_override_return / fault injection not covered.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il -E 'fault.injection|chaos|game.day|link.flap|\bdrill|failure.injection|inject(ed|ing)? --- [resilience-ha #9] Market-Data Liveness & Source Failover Beyond A/B
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 54 - new §54.4.4 'Silent feeds, stale-data kill and failing over to another source' (and a §54.2 extension: liveness as the fourth channel); ties Ch. 53 §53.4.3, Ch. 46 (warm standby feed handler), Ch. 51/57 (multicast joins, steering), Ch. 75 (cross-site capture), Appendix K.6. | gap=partial | priority=medium | area=health-monitoring-app
  HFT: Ch. 54 recovers from gaps you can see; a line that simply stops delivers no gap and no error while your book quietly ages. Detecting silence and switching sources - or pulling quotes - within a measured budget is what keeps a stale book from being traded as current.
  SCOPE: Detecting a silent feed (no gap, no packets): exchange heartbeat / 'no activity' messages on the multicast channel, per-channel last-packet timestamps (PTP - Ch. 58) with staleness thresholds adapted to the instrument's normal rate and session phase; distinguishing a quiet market from a dead line. // Stale-data kill: pulling quotes when the book is older than X us or the feed is silent beyond the heartbeat interval - the market-data counterpart of the kill-switch (Ch. 74), owned by the risk process and published via Ch. 73. // Both-lines-dead failover to a secondary source: a second venue gateway/colo feed, a consolidated/SIP feed or a vendor feed - different latency, different sequence domain; re-sequencing and snapshot resync (§54.4.2) across sources; marking decisions made on the secondary (Ch. 75) for later analysis. // Feed-handler process failover: a standby handler already joined to the groups (IGMP state and NIC filters - Ch. 51/57), warm (Ch. 46), writing the same shm ring layout with an epoch bump readers detect (ties the shm-reattach section). // Cross-site redundancy: capturing both lines at both sites (Ch. 75) and gap-filling from the remote site's capture; latency of the remote path vs trading blind. // Measure it: time-to-detect a silent feed vs heartbeat interval (false-positive rate at lunch vs at the open); failover-to-secondary latency; the extra tick-to-trade latency the secondary source imposes while in use. // Pitfalls: a staleness threshold tuned for the open firing mid-day; IGMP rejoin delay after NIC failover; trading on a vendor feed with different sequence/book semantics; treating the SIP as equivalent to the direct feed.
  EXISTING: 54-reliable-multicast-feed-recovery.md §54.2, §54.4.1-54.4.3: Three-channel gap recovery and per-instrument stale-on-gap policy; liveness of a silent channel and alternative sources are not covered.; 53-zero-copy-wire-market-data-decoding.md §53.2.3, §53.4.3, §53.4.4: A/B arbitration and drop detection at NIC/socket/application layers.; 51-socket-optimization-tcp-tuning.md §51.4.2: Multicast group joins and RX buffer sizing.; appendix-K-exchange-connectivity.md K.6: A/B lines over diverse physical paths.
  EVIDENCE: grep -n -i -E 'secondary|backup|consolidated|\bSIP\b|alternate (feed|source)|feed source|fail.?over|silent|no packets|liveness' 53-*.md 54-*.md -> zero relevant hits (53:38/59/220/224 are endianness/drops; 54:88/97 are stale-on-gap). grep -c -i heartbeat 53-*.md 54-*.md -> 0 each (heartbeats appear only in 46/51 for TCP warming, 74 for process liveness, 45, and K.5's PPS metaphor). grep -n -i -E 'IGMP|rejoin' chapters/*.md -> zero hits. grep -n -i -E 'stale (feed|data|market|quote|book)|market.data heartbeat' -> zero hits.

--- [resilience-ha #10] Clock Failover on the Host: Redundant Grandmasters, BMCA & Behavior When PTP Is Lost
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 58 - new §58.4.4 'Failover of the time source' after §58.4.3 (grandmaster holdover and oscillator grades); ties Ch. 17 (monotonic vs realtime, steps), Ch. 74 (time as an input), Ch. 75 (flagging capture). | gap=partial | priority=low | area=health-monitoring-os-net
  HFT: Ch. 58 treats loss of sync as 'an outage that invalidates data' but not as a failover the host must survive without stepping its clocks mid-session; a stepped CLOCK_REALTIME corrupts tick-to-trade measurements and compliance records at once. The section makes time-source failover a configured, measured behavior.
  SCOPE: Redundant grandmasters and the Best Master Clock Algorithm (BMCA): PTP domains, priority1/priority2 and clockClass, boundary-clock failover, and the offset discontinuity a master switch introduces - measured with ptp4l offset logs across an induced switch. // Host-side servo policy when the master disappears: ptp4l holdover/free-run, phc2sys slewing vs stepping CLOCK_REALTIME (step_threshold / first_step_threshold), why a step mid-session breaks interval math (Ch. 17), capture timestamps (Ch. 75) and MiFID II traceability - and how to configure 'never step during the session'. // Flagging and bounding uncertainty: marking journal records taken during holdover/desync with the estimated drift bound (oscillator spec, §58.4.3) so replay and compliance know the timestamp quality; the NIC PHC's own TCXO drift when unsupervised. // Application behavior: intervals on the TSC / CLOCK_MONOTONIC regardless (Ch. 17); the deterministic core sees time only as a sequenced input (Ch. 74) so a clock event never changes decisions retroactively; alerting as an outage (§58.5) with a defined degraded mode. // Measure it: offset excursion and settle time during a grandmaster failover; drift accumulated per minute of holdover on NIC PHC vs grandmaster OCXO/Rb; the effect of a 1 us step on a replayed capture's wire-to-wire numbers. // Pitfalls: two masters in one domain after a misconfigured BMCA; phc2sys allowed to step at the open; holdover drift silently exceeding the regulatory bound; correlating logs across hosts during desync.
  EXISTING: 58-clock-synchronization-ptp.md §58.4.1 'Monitor the sync', §58.4.3 'Grandmaster oscillator drift and holdover', §58.5, §58.6 ex. 4: Grandmaster-side holdover, oscillator grades and monitoring/alerting; no BMCA/redundant-master failover, no host-side step/slew policy, no capture flagging.; 17-timekeeping-tsc-rdtsc-clocks.md §17.2.2 line 57, §17.5 line 172: CLOCK_REALTIME can step (NTP/PTP corrections, leap seconds) - use monotonic for intervals.
  EVIDENCE: grep -n -i -E 'holdover|clock (step|jump)|leap second|BMCA|best master|step_threshold|clock_servo|ptp domain|domainNumber|redundant (grandmaster|master)|two grandmaster' chapters/*.md -> holdover only 58:90/113/122/135/143/145 (grandmaster side) and appendix-K:49; CLOCK_REALTIME steps only 17:57/172; zero hits for BMCA, best master, step_threshold, clock_servo, PTP domain, redundant grandmaster.

--- [resilience-ha #11] Site-Level Disaster Recovery & Multi-Colo Resilience
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Appendix K - new K.7 'Site redundancy & disaster recovery' (renumbering the current K.7 trade-offs to K.8), cross-referenced from the Sequencer & Replication chapter; stays an orientation section without benchmarks, consistent with Appendix K's framing. | gap=absent | priority=low | area=other

######## LENS algorithms-numerics — 12 proposals ########
SUMMARY: LENS: algorithms, data structures and numerics for the hot path. Method: read chapters/README.md in full, read Ch. 25, 27, 28, 29 and 53 in full, then grepped every candidate (4-8 distinctive terms each, grep -il / grep -n -i across chapters/*.md) and read the matching passages before judging.

VERDICT. Part V (27-29), Ch. 25 and Ch. 53 are strong on *representation and mechanics*: scaled-integer prices, denormals/FTZ-DAZ, <bit> intrinsics and occupancy bitmaps, SIMD width/downclocking, flat vs node containers, zero-copy struct overlay with bswap and bounds checks. What is largely missing is the *algorithmic layer* between those primitives and a trading application: (1) approximate transcendental math (exp/log/erf/normal-CDF, polynomial and table approximations) - zero hits anywhere; (2) hash *functions*, perfect hashing, dense-ID remapping and hardware checksums (CRC32C/PCLMUL) - Ch. 25 covers table layout only, CRC appears only as a constexpr demo and as Ethernet FCS; (3) timers/deadlines/timing wheels - zero hits, and Ch. 38's event-loop section has no timer at all despite TIF/heartbeat/reorder-window timeouts being needed in Ch. 53/54/74 and Appendix J; (4) streaming statistics (EWMA, Welford, Kahan, rolling windows, online quantiles) - EWMA appears once as a denormal source, Kahan once as a phrase; (5) allocation-free strings - std::string_view is never mentioned in the whole book, interning/fixed-capacity strings absent; (6) small-array search/sort (branchless binary search, Eytzinger, SIMD scan, sorting networks) - Ch. 3 explicitly promises Ch. 25 "revisits exactly this comparison" and Ch. 25 does not; (7) the limit order book itself is a ~600-word prose outline (§25.4.4) with no code, no measurement, no L2-vs-L3 distinction and no sparse-book design; (8) branch-free parsing/formatting and timestamp conversion - Ch. 53 names SWAR/digit-pair/decimal-to-scaled techniques in one-sentence bullets without code or measurement, and datetime handling is absent; (9) strong types for Price/Qty are referenced five times in Ch. 27 as "Ch. 19" but Ch. 19 contains only CRTP, expression templates and policy-based design - a dangling cross-reference. Position/P&L/fee/FX arithmetic, compression for capture, and RNG round out the list at medium/low priority. Lookup-tables-vs-computation (Ch. 13 §13.4.2 + Ch. 18 §18.4.2) and integer division by constants (Ch. 27/28) are adequately covered and are not proposed.

CONSISTENCY/ACCURACY FINDINGS in the lens chapters (stale cross-references from the renumbering; fix in the Phase-4 pass): Ch. 53 §53.3 results block cites "§48.2.2, §48.5" for the bswap overlay and "Ch.27" for from_chars/FAST (should be §53.2.2/§53.5 and Ch. 28), and decode.cpp comments cite "(Ch.20,27)" for bit_cast/bswap (Ch. 21/28); §53.5 cites "Ch.27" for bswap and "Ch.12" for predicted branches (Ch. 28/13). Ch. 28 §28.3 cites "<- §27.6" for the pdep/pext AMD warning (should be §28.6) and "Ch. 12" for branch mispredicts (Ch. 13). Ch. 29 simd.cpp comments cite "Ch. 20" for restrict and "Ch. 7" for SoA (Ch. 21/8); §29.5 cites "Ch. 26" for reassociation (27) and "Ch. 10 ILP" (11); §29.3 cites "Ch. 41" for the SMT sibling (43). Ch. 25 §25.5/§25.7 cite "§25.2-24.3" and "§25.3-24.4.1". Ch. 18's kTickSize comment cites "(Ch. 26)" and "Ch. 12 lookup tables, Ch. 27 bit tables" (should be 27, 13, 28). Appendix H attributes <bit> to Ch. 27 (28), saturating arithmetic to Ch. 60 (72), std::print to "Ch. 59, 71" (71), std::execution to Ch. 40 (39). Appendix J cites "Ch. 34's seqlock publication" (35), "Ch. 53, 60" for ITCH zero-copy parsing (53/72), "Ch. 56" for hardware timestamps (58); Appendix K cites "Ch. 68, 70" for FPGA-integrated NICs (69/70). README.md line 5 still says "seven appendices (A-G) follow" while twelve (A-L) are listed. Two unfulfilled forward promises: Ch. 3 §3.3 ("Ch. 25 revisits exactly this comparison" - binary search vs hash under realistic access) and Ch. 29 Ex. 5 ("SIMD market-data scan (preview Ch. 53)") - proposals 3 and 4 below close them.

--- [algorithms-numerics #1] Fast Transcendentals & Approximate Math for Pricing and Signals
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part V — Numerics & Data Parallelism, immediately after Ch. 27 (Fixed-Point & Floating-Point Arithmetic) and before Ch. 28; prerequisites Ch. 11 (dependency-chain latency), Ch. 27, Ch. 29; leads into Ch. 67 (GPU pricing) and the streaming-statistics chapter proposed below. | gap=absent | priority=high | area=other
  HFT: Options market makers and most signal computations call exp/log/sqrt/erf inside the per-tick loop (EWMA decay factors exp(-dt/tau), log-returns, volatility, Greeks, implied vol), and a glibc call with errno handling and special-case branches costs tens of nanoseconds with a data-dependent tail. A minimax polynomial or table with a known error bound evaluates in a few FMAs, branch-free and vectorizable, which decides whether a full quote surface can be recomputed inside the tick-to-trade budget.
  SCOPE: The cost of libm on the hot path — measured latency/throughput and tail of glibc `exp`/`log`/`pow`/`sqrt`/`erf`/`tanh` (scalar, errno handling, special-case branches); `-fno-math-errno` turning `sqrt` into one `vsqrtsd` (Appendix D.3); why a libm call is a ~20-50 ns data-dependent event inside a quote loop. // Range reduction + polynomial approximation — minimax (Remez) vs Taylor, Horner vs Estrin evaluation and its dependency-chain latency (Ch. 11), FMA-based evaluation, degree-vs-error tables, bounding absolute/relative error in ulps for a required precision. // Bit-level tricks — exponent manipulation for fast `exp2`/`log2` (Schraudolph), `rsqrtss`/`vrcp14ps` plus one Newton step, splitting mantissa/exponent with `std::bit_cast` (Ch. 21), and when a 1e-3 relative error is acceptable in a signal but not in a price. // Table-driven approximations — fixed-point index + linear/quadratic interpolation, sizing tables to stay L1-resident (Ch. 13 §13.4.2's LUT-vs-miss trade-off), generating them at compile time (Ch. 18). // Normal CDF/PDF and `erf` for Black–Scholes — Abramowitz–Stegun / Cody / Hart approximations; implied-volatility root finding (Newton with analytic vega, Brent/bisection fallback, Jäckel's 'Let's be rational'); iteration counts and worst-case latency as a tail event. // Vectorized math — SLEEF, glibc libmvec via `#pragma omp simd`/`-ffast-math`, Intel SVML, `std::simd` + hand-rolled polynomials; throughput per lane vs scalar libm, with the AVX-512 downclocking caveat (Ch. 29.3). // Accuracy vs determinism — approximations as part of the documented FP contract (Ch. 27.4.3), bit-reproducibility across builds and machines, FTZ/DAZ interaction, exhaustive error testing over the input domain. // When not to approximate — when scaled-integer math suffices (Ch. 27), when the value is recomputed off-path, and when the error can flip a trading decision. // Measure it: ns/call distribution for libm vs polynomial vs table vs SIMD on the reference machine; verify the codegen is straight-line FMA with no `call`.
  EXISTING: chapters/27-fixed-point-floating-point-arithmetic.md §27.2.4, §27.3 op-latency table, §27.5: div/sqrt latency and reciprocal-multiply hoisting; `vrcpps` + Newton refine shown only as a `-ffast-math` side effect (asm comment 'faster, approximate'). No exp/log/erf, no approximation design.; chapters/appendix-D-compiler-flag-reference.md D.3: `-fno-math-errno` lets sqrt inline to a single instruction; `-freciprocal-math` hazards. Flags only, no technique.; chapters/67-gpu-computing-cuda.md §67.4.3: Black–Scholes/Greeks/Monte-Carlo named as GPU batch workloads; no CPU-side math.; chapters/19-template-metaprogramming-zero-cost.md §19.4.2: Greeks/P&L vectors named as an expression-template use case only.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il across chapters/*.md: `polynomial` = 0 files; `minimax|remez` = 0; `horner|estrin` = 0; `taylor|pad[eé]` = 0; `\berf\b|erfc|normal cdf|cumulative normal` = 0; `libm|libmvec|svml|sleef` = 0. `approximat` hits only Ch. 27 L181 (asm comment), Ch. 43 L125 and Ch. 46 L123 (unrelated senses). `black[- ]scholes|greeks|implied vol` → Ch. 67 (4), Ch. 19 (1), Ch. 29 (1) — workload names only. `newton|bisection|brent|root[- ]find` → Ch. 27 L181 (asm comment) and Ch. 76 (unrelated). `std::exp|std::log|expf|logf` hits are `std::expected` (Ch. 20, 19 lines) and `std::experimental::simd` (Ch. 29).

--- [algorithms-numerics #2] Fast Hashing, Checksums & Key Design for the Hot Path
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part V, after Ch. 28 (Bit Manipulation & Integer Tricks) and before Ch. 29 (SIMD); builds on Ch. 25 (flat hash tables) and Ch. 28; ties Ch. 53 (symbol fields off the wire), Ch. 62 (userspace stacks must verify checksums themselves), Ch. 72 (hash flooding), Ch. 75 (journal record integrity). | gap=scattered | priority=high | area=other
  HFT: Every message a feed handler or gateway processes begins with a key lookup — symbol to book, order ID to resting order, session to connection — and the hash function is a fixed cost paid before the Ch. 25 table layout even matters. Checksum generation and verification (FIX, framing, journal records, userspace-stack UDP) is likewise per-message work that is one hardware instruction when done right and a byte loop when not.
  SCOPE: Hash functions vs hash tables — what the Ch. 25 toy FlatMap's `0x9E3779B97F4A7C15` multiplier is (Fibonacci hashing), avalanche/bias, why identity or multiplicative hashing suffices for exchange-assigned integer IDs but not arbitrary keys; measured ns/hash and probe-length distribution for identity, multiply-xorshift (splitmix/murmur finalizer), wyhash/rapidhash, xxHash3, CityHash, SipHash and `_mm_crc32_u64`-as-hash. // Keying by fixed-width symbols — load an 8-byte right-padded symbol as a `uint64` (one `cmp`, no `memcmp`), padding/case normalization, two-instruction hashing of 8/16-byte keys, 12–20-byte symbols via SIMD compare (Ch. 29). // Perfect and minimal-perfect hashing for the fixed instrument universe — compile-time (Ch. 18 `constexpr`) or startup-generated CHD/PTHash/BBHash tables, lookup = one multiply + one load with zero collisions, rebuild on reference-data reload (Ch. 73). // Dense-ID remapping — map symbol/venue/order IDs to small contiguous integers at session start so the hot path indexes arrays (Ch. 8) instead of hashing; exchange locate codes as natural dense keys; generational indices; 32- vs 64-bit ID width (Ch. 9). // Order-ID map design — sequential exchange order IDs with identity hash + power-of-two mask (Ch. 28.4.3) vs a mixing step; sizing for the open; measured probe lengths on a captured ITCH order-ID stream. // Hash flooding as a security concern (Ch. 72) — when a keyed hash (SipHash) is required for untrusted keys and what it costs; why trusted feed IDs do not need it. // Hardware checksums — CRC32C via SSE4.2 `crc32` (3-cycle latency, 1/cycle throughput, interleaved streams — Ch. 11), PCLMULQDQ-folded CRC32/CRC32C at >20 GB/s, Fletcher/Adler, the FIX `CheckSum(10)` mod-256 byte sum with SIMD; where each appears: FIX sessions, SBE/ITCH framing, Aeron/Chronicle frames, capture journal records (Ch. 75), userspace UDP/TCP checksum verification under kernel bypass (Ch. 62) vs NIC offload (Ch. 55). // Combining/incremental hashing for composite keys and streams; why `std::hash` (identity on integers, implementation-defined quality) is not a design. // Measure it: ns/hash by key size, probe distribution, CRC32C throughput scalar vs PCLMUL vs offload; verify codegen (`crc32`, `pclmulqdq`, `imul`+`shr` mixing).
  EXISTING: chapters/25-hotpath-hostile-stl-cache-friendly-containers.md §25.3, §25.4.1: Open-addressing/robin-hood/SwissTable table layout; the toy FlatMap uses a multiplicative hash with no discussion of hash choice or quality; names `absl::flat_hash_map`/`unordered_dense`. 'Look up symbols (or order IDs)' is the motivating workload but key design is not discussed.; chapters/18-compile-time-mechanics.md §18.1 L15, §18.4.2 L163: One bullet: compile-time perfect hash or sorted constexpr array for closed key sets. --- [algorithms-numerics #3] Branch-Free Parsing & Formatting: Integers, Decimals, Timestamps and FIX Tag Scanning
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part IX — Market Data, NIC & Fabric, immediately after Ch. 53 (as its text-protocol companion) and before Ch. 54; alternative home: Part V after Ch. 28.   HFT: Ch. 53 measures FIX text decode at ~10x the cost of binary overlay and then tells the reader to use 'optimized field scanning + branch-free number conversion' without showing it, yet most order-entry sessions and many venues still speak FIX and every outbound order formats integers and a timestamp into text. A fixed-width SWAR parse or itoa is a handful of multiplies replacing a data-dependent loop, and the timestamp field alone is a notorious source of hidden syscalls and locks on the order path.
  SCOPE: Measure the spectrum on one FIX message — `atoi`/`strtoll`, `std::from_chars`, digit-at-a-time with `[[likely]]`, 8-digit SWAR (multiply-shift trick, Lemire/Kelley), 16/32-digit SIMD (`pmaddubsw`/`pmaddwd` chain); branch-mispredict profile of variable-length vs fixed-width fields (Ch. 13). // Decimal prices straight to scaled integers — `123.45` → `int64` at a fixed scale without `double`, implied-decimal fields (ITCH 4/8 places), sign, leading zeros, rejecting malformed input at the gate (Ch. 53.6/72) with one compare. // Delimiter scanning for FIX — find `SOH`/`=` across 16/32/64 bytes with `pcmpeqb`+`movemask`+`tzcnt` (Ch. 28/29) vs `memchr`; tag dispatch via compile-time perfect hash or jump table (Ch. 14/18); repeating groups without allocation; `std::string_view` over the receive buffer and its lifetime rules. // Integer formatting — `std::to_chars` vs digit-pair tables vs 2-digits-at-a-time itoa (jeaiii/Alexandrescu), fixed-width zero-padded fields (OUCH/FIX require them), writing into a pre-sized `std::array<char,N>`; why `std::to_string`/`snprintf`/`std::format` stay off the hot path (Ch. 71). // Floating-point formatting when unavoidable — shortest round-trip (Ryu, Dragonbox, Schubfach — what `std::to_chars(double)` implements), fixed precision, cost relative to integer formatting. // Timestamps — TSC ticks to nanoseconds-since-epoch (Ch. 17) and formatting FIX `SendingTime`/`TransactTime` (`YYYYMMDD-HH:MM:SS.sssssssss`) from a precomputed midnight base and digit tables; never `localtime`/`gmtime`/`strftime` on the hot path (TZ lookups, locks, `stat("/etc/localtime")`); parsing ITCH 48-bit nanoseconds-since-midnight; day rollover and leap seconds as cold-path events. // Verify the codegen — the parse is `imul`/`shr`/`and` sequences and vector compares with no `call`; `from_chars` inlining differences across libstdc++/libc++ (Appendix H). // Fuzz and differential-test hand-rolled parsers against `from_chars` (Ch. 40/72).

--- [algorithms-numerics #4] Searching & Sorting Small Arrays: Branchless Binary Search, Eytzinger Layout, SIMD Scan and Sorting Networks
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 25 — new §25.4.5 between 'Intrusive containers and small-buffer types' and the LOB case study (whose sparse secondary level store depends on it), with a matching §25.3 measurement row and §25.5 pitfalls; this also discharges Ch. 3 §3.3's promise that Ch. 25 'revisits exactly this comparison'. Alternative: a short chapter in Part V after Ch. 28. | gap=scattered | priority=high | area=other
  HFT: Sorted small arrays are everywhere in a trading engine — the sparse price-level store Ch. 25 defers to, tick-size bands, venue quote ranking for a consolidated BBO, time-indexed lookups — and `std::lower_bound` pays a branch mispredict per level on data the predictor cannot learn. Branchless and SIMD search turn a 30–60 ns pointer-and-branch walk into a few nanoseconds of straight-line code, and sorting networks do the same for ranking a handful of quotes.
  SCOPE: Why `std::lower_bound` is slow on the hot path — ~log2(n) data-dependent mispredicts (Ch. 13) and dependent loads (Ch. 11); measured on 8/64/4096-element sorted arrays with uniform vs clustered (price-level-like) keys. // Branchless binary search — the `cmov`-based half-interval form (Khuong & Morin), prefetching both candidate halves (Ch. 10), fixed-iteration unrolled search for known sizes; verify the `cmov` in asm (Ch. 13.5) and watch for the lengthened dependency chain (Ch. 13.6). // Cache-oblivious static layouts — Eytzinger (heap order) with implicit prefetch, static B-tree/S+-tree blocks sized to one cache line or SIMD width; when plain sorted-array locality still wins. // SIMD linear scan for N ≤ 32–64 keys — `vpcmpgtq`/`vpcmpgtd` + `movemask` + `popcnt`/`tzcnt` (Ch. 28/29) finds the insertion point in one or two compares; this is the lookup of a `flat_map`-backed sparse price-level store. // Sorting small fixed-N arrays — sorting networks (Batcher / optimal networks for N ≤ 16) with `min`/`max` (`cmov`, `vpminsd`), insertion sort for nearly-sorted input, `std::sort`/pdqsort/radix-sort crossover points, stability requirements (price-time), and the consolidated-BBO use case (ranking 5–20 venue quotes by price). // Partial ordering — top-k with `std::nth_element`/`partial_sort`, small binary heaps vs sorted arrays, maintaining a sorted array under single insert/erase (memmove cost vs tree). // Measure it: ns/lookup distribution across N and key distribution; mispredict and L1-miss counters per structure; where each wins. // Pitfalls: oversized Eytzinger arrays blowing L1, sorting networks on already-sorted data, `std::stable_sort` allocating, branchless code that is slower on predictable data.

--- [algorithms-numerics #5] Timers, Deadlines & Timing Wheels on a Busy-Poll Core
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part VI — Concurrency, immediately after Ch. 38 (Coroutines & Async Models, whose §38.4.1 event loops have no timer story) and before Ch. 39; builds on Ch. 17 (TSC as the clock), Ch. 41 (busy-poll vs block), Ch. 24 (pools), Ch. 25 (intrusive lists); feeds Ch. 53/54 (reorder-window and recovery timeouts), Ch. 74 (heartbeats; timer fires as replayable inputs), Appendix J.3 (TIF/expiry). | gap=absent | priority=high | area=design-patterns
  HFT: Every order, quote and recovery request carries a deadline, and a busy-poll engine must check and expire thousands of them each loop iteration with no syscall, no allocation and no O(n) scan. Getting this wrong either stalls the loop on an expiry burst or leaks stale orders into the market when a cancel-if-stale timer fires late.
  SCOPE: Where timers live in a trading engine — order TIF/expiry (IOC/FOK/GTD), quote-refresh and cancel-if-stale deadlines, reorder-window timeouts (Ch. 53.4.3), retransmission backoff (Ch. 54), heartbeats/liveness (Ch. 74.4.3), throttles and rate limiters; why a loop may hold 10^4–10^6 pending deadlines. // The clock for deadlines — `rdtsc` polled every iteration (Ch. 17) vs vDSO `clock_gettime(CLOCK_MONOTONIC)` vs `timerfd`/`epoll_wait` timeouts (a syscall and a context switch — Ch. 41); testing 'now ≥ deadline' without a mispredict storm. // Data structures — array-based binary heap / `std::priority_queue` (O(log n), node-free, O(n) cancel), intrusive deadline-ordered lists for uniform-timeout classes (O(1) insert/cancel), hierarchical timing wheels (Varghese & Lauck; O(1) insert/cancel/expire, bounded cascade), hashed wheels (Netty/Kafka), calendar/radix heaps; all pool-backed (Ch. 24) with generation counters to defuse stale cancels (ABA — Ch. 34). // Measured insert/cancel/expire latency and tail for heap vs wheel at 10^3–10^6 timers; tick granularity vs cascade cost; cache footprint of the wheel (Ch. 7). // Integrating with the event loop — one poll loop drains NIC/queues then expires due timers (Ch. 38/47), timer coalescing, bounding expiry work per iteration so a burst cannot stall the hot path, callbacks as enqueued events rather than inline calls. // Determinism — timer fires as timestamped input events to the deterministic core (Ch. 74.2.2) so replay reproduces the timeline; simulated clocks in replay/backtest (Ch. 75/76). // Pitfalls — `std::chrono::system_clock` on the hot path, `nanosleep`/`sleep_for` for sub-millisecond waits (timer slack — Ch. 45), `std::map<time_point,…>` timers (Ch. 25's node-container tax), unbounded expiry bursts at session open, reused-slot ABA. // Verify: the per-iteration check compiles to a load, compare and predicted branch; no `call` into libc time functions.
  EXISTING: chapters/74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.4.3 (L115-119), §74.2.2 (L57): Heartbeats 'beyond a tuned timeout' — policy and detect-vs-false-positive tuning only; 'each timer fire is a record in the ordered log' states the determinism requirement with no implementation.; chapters/53-zero-copy-wire-market-data-decoding.md §53.4.3, §53.4.4: 'Bounded reorder window + timeout' as a tuning decision; no timer mechanism.; chapters/54-reliable-multicast-feed-recovery.md §54.4.1 L71: 'buffer briefly' before declaring a gap; no mechanism.; chapters/47-linux-native-io.md §47.4.1 L119, §47.5: Zero-timeout `epoll_wait` busy-poll; `epoll` timeouts otherwise unaddressed.; chapters/41-context-switching-mitigation.md §41.2, §41.4: The kernel timer tick as an interrupt source to eliminate — not application timers.; chapters/appendix-J-market-structure-primer.md J.3 L36: TIF definitions (Day/IOC/FOK/GTC); Ch. 9 L88 has a `tif : 3` bit-field. No expiry implementation anywhere.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il `timer wheel|timing wheel|hierarchical timer|hashed wheel` = 0 files. `priority[ _]queue|min-heap|binary heap|std::make_heap|pairing heap|radix heap` → only 65-transport-beyond-tcp.md L79 (Homa's network priority queues). grep -n -i `timer|deadline|timeout|expir|heartbeat` in 38-coroutines-async-models.md = 0 lines; in 47-linux-native-io.md only zero-timeout epoll_wait (L119/137/149/159); in 41 only the kernel tick (L41/43/123/124/146/166); in 17 only measurement-overhead subtraction; in 74 only heartbeat policy (L81/93/119/147/159/168) and 'timer fire is a record' (L57). `\bIOC\b|\bGTD\b|\bGTC\b|time[- ]in[- ]force|order (expir|timeout)|quote (refresh|timeout)|cancel[- ]on[- ]disconnect` → App J L36 definitions, Ch. 74 L37/L127 (cancel-on-disconnect as gateway duty), Ch. 9 L88 bit-field only.

--- [algorithms-numerics #6] Streaming Statistics & Rolling-Window Computation on the Hot Path
  kind=new-chapter | placement=Part V, after Ch. 27 and preceding the fast-transcendentals chapter proposed above (the two can be adjacent 'arithmetic of signals and monitors' chapters); builds on Ch. 11 (dependency chains), Ch. 27 (FP hazards/determinism), Ch. 28 (ring-index masks), Ch. 34 (ring buffers); feeds Ch. 76 (production monitoring) and Appendix L. | gap=absent | priority=high | area=other
  HFT: The strategy and risk layers of a tick-to-trade system are made of streaming statistics — moving averages, volatilities, imbalances, rolling extremes — recomputed on every book update, and production health monitoring needs the same machinery (EWMA of queue depth, online p99) at zero overhead. Done naively each is an O(window) rescan over allocating containers with drifting floating-point sums; done right each is a constant-time, allocation-free update whose numerical error is bounded and whose results replay bit-exactly.
  SCOPE: The O(1), zero-allocation primitives a tick loop computes — running sum/mean, EWMA (fixed-interval and irregular-time `exp(-Δt/τ)` forms), Welford/West online variance and covariance, rolling VWAP/TWAP, order-flow imbalance over a sliding window, rolling min/max via a monotonic deque, exponentially-decayed counters for rate limiting. // Fixed-memory windows — ring buffers of samples with running sums (Ch. 34), add-new/subtract-old drift in floating point and its fixes (periodic exact recomputation, Kahan/Neumaier compensated sums, integer/fixed-point accumulators — Ch. 27), windows by count vs by time. // Numerical stability — catastrophic cancellation in `E[x²]−E[x]²`, denormal decay in EWMA (Ch. 27.2.3, FTZ/DAZ), `double` vs `int64`/`__int128` accumulators, bit-exact determinism for replay (Ch. 74/75). // Online quantiles for monitoring — HdrHistogram (Ch. 3) as the per-stage recorder, P² and Greenwald–Khanna, t-digest and DDSketch for mergeable percentiles across processes/hosts, reservoir sampling; cost per record vs accuracy; publishing p99 estimates to a dashboard off-path (Ch. 71/76). // Approximate set/frequency structures and where they do and do not fit — bitmaps (Ch. 28) for dense universes; Bloom/cuckoo filters and count-min sketches only for sparse/unbounded key spaces; measured false-positive rate and cache cost. // Incremental update per tick vs vectorized batch recomputation (Ch. 29) — when recomputing a 64-element window from scratch beats maintaining running state. // Measure it: ns/update per estimator, error vs exact on captured data, accumulated drift over a session; verify the update is a few FMAs with no branches or calls. // Pitfalls — `std::deque` (allocating) for windows, `std::accumulate` over the whole window per tick, time windows that rescan on every update, EWMA with per-tick `pow`, estimators that silently reset on NaN.
  EXISTING: chapters/27-fixed-point-floating-point-arithmetic.md §27.2.3 L42, §27.6 L194: EWMA named only as a source of denormals; 'use Kahan/compensated summation' as a single phrase in the pitfalls list.; chapters/03-microbenchmarking-done-right.md §3.4.3: HdrHistogram as the tail recorder — the only streaming-percentile structure in the book.; chapters/76-production-profiling-end-to-end-case-study.md §76.2, §76.4.1 L73/81: Per-stage HdrHistograms in production and a 'trend dashboard' — no estimator design, no EWMA/rolling computation.; chapters/19-template-metaprogramming-zero-cost.md §19.3 Vwap demo (L81), §19.4.2 L158: The CRTP `Vwap` is a running sum, not a window; 'moving-window computations' named as an expression-template use case.; chapters/11-cpu-pipelines-and-execution.md §11.4.1-11.4.2: Accumulator splitting for reductions — the ILP mechanics, not the estimators.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il `ewma|exponential(ly)?[- ]weighted|moving average|exponential smoothing` → Ch. 27 (L42, denormal context) and App G (1 bibliography line). `welford|running variance|online variance|kahan|compensated|pairwise summ|neumaier` → Ch. 27 L194 only. `t-digest|ddsketch|p-?square|online percentile|reservoir|quantile` → Ch. 3 L251 only ('not just the quantiles'). `rolling|sliding window|monotonic (deque|queue)|vwap|twap` → Ch. 19 L158 (phrase) and the `Vwap` demo; all other 'rolling' hits are 'rolling out/rollout'. `bloom` = 0, `cuckoo` = 0, `hyperloglog|count-min` = 0. grep -n -i `ewma|rolling|moving|percentile estimat|sketch|aggregat` in Ch. 76 → only the trend dashboard lines (L73/81/122).

--- [algorithms-numerics #8] Position, P&L, Fee and FX Arithmetic in Scaled Integers
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 27 — new §27.4.4 after 'Cross-build determinism on the hot path', with a corresponding §27.3 measurement (int64/__int128 vs double vs `_Decimal64` on the same P&L update) and §27.6 pitfalls; cross-reference Ch. 72 §72.4.3 (checked arithmetic) and Ch. 74 §74.2.2 (deterministic core). | gap=partial | priority=medium | area=other
  HFT: A wrong position or P&L is a direct money and compliance bug, and the arithmetic sits on the order path (pre-trade risk) and in the deterministic core (replay must reproduce it bit-exactly). Ch. 27 settles the price representation and Ch. 72 the overflow checks, but neither shows how positions, average prices, sub-tick fees and FX compose in integer math without rounding or scale errors.
  SCOPE: The position/P&L state machine as integer arithmetic — signed position, average entry price (division with an explicit rounding rule), realized vs unrealized P&L on fills, mark-to-market against mid/last/settlement, all inside the deterministic core (Ch. 74) with integer-only math. // Scale management across quantities — price scale × quantity scale → notional scale; fees/rebates in sub-tick decimals (0.0030/share needs a finer scale than price); per-venue fee schedules; FX conversion for multi-currency books (the fractional rate forces a wider intermediate or a deliberate rounding boundary); `__int128` intermediates with overflow checks (Ch. 72). // Integer division semantics — C++ truncation toward zero vs floor for negative positions/short P&L (`(-7)/2 == -3`), round-half-even in integers, division by constant scales compiled to multiply-shift (Ch. 28.4.3), testing rounding at sign boundaries. // Lot accounting (FIFO/LIFO/average-cost) in fixed memory — intrusive lot queues from a pool (Ch. 24), when average-cost suffices, compliance/replay implications. // Pre-trade risk in integer math — exposure/notional/position limits checked with `__builtin_*_overflow` or saturating ops (C++26 `std::add_sat`, Appendix H), branch-predictable limit checks on the order path. // Decimal floating point measured — GCC `_Decimal64` (libbid/libdfp, software) vs scaled `int64` vs `double` on one P&L update: the number behind Ch. 27's 'too slow for the hot path' verdict; IBM POWER hardware DFP as the exception. // Pitfalls — mixing scales silently (ties strong types, proposed §19.4.4), `double` for fees 'because they are small', accumulating P&L in `double` across a session (drift, non-determinism), unsigned quantities meeting signed positions.
  EXISTING: chapters/27-fixed-point-floating-point-arithmetic.md §27.2.1, §27.2.2, §27.4.1, §27.6: Scaled integers vs decimal vs binary (decimal FP 'too slow, software-emulated' — one unmeasured bullet); tick rounding and rounding direction as a business rule; `price × qty` into `__int128`, 'P&L = Σ(price × qty)', convert at boundaries; pitfalls on accumulated error, mixing scales, overflow. No position/average-price/fee/FX composition.; chapters/72-secure-programming-low-latency.md §72.4.3 L93-100: Checked arithmetic on notional/position/exposure, headroom, signed/unsigned — the safety primitives only.; chapters/appendix-J-market-structure-primer.md J.1 L15, L18; also Appendix F L18: Maker-taker fees and rebates as market structure definitions, not arithmetic.
  EVIDENCE: grep -n -i `p&l|pnl|profit and loss|realized|unrealized` → Ch. 27 (2 phrases), Ch. 76 (backtest P&L, 5), Ch. 19/29/11/31 (passing examples). `\bfees?\b|\brebates?\b|\bFX\b|currency conversion|multi-currency|average (fill )?price|cost basis|mark[- ]to[- ]market|lot accounting` → only App J L15/L18 and App F L18 (definitions) — no arithmetic anywhere. `\bexposure\b` → Ch. 72 L98 only. `_decimal|decimal64|libdfp|ieee 754-2008|densely packed decimal` → Ch. 27 L226 (references) only. `catastrophic cancellation|accumulated error|loss of precision` → Ch. 27 (3 lines). `position.{0,20}(arithmetic|tracking|keeping)|net position|position (limit|check)` → Ch. 69 (FPGA pre-trade risk), App J/K only.

--- [algorithms-numerics #9] Strong Types & Units: Price, Quantity, Notional and IDs as Zero-Cost Wrappers
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 19 — new §19.4.4 'Strong types and units' after 'Policy-based design', with a §19.5 codegen listing proving register passing and identical asm; repoint Ch. 27's five forward references ('strong type … Ch. 19') at the new section; add an mp-units row to Appendix H. | gap=absent | priority=high | area=language-features
  HFT: Ch. 27's central advice — prices and quantities as scaled integers — is only safe if the compiler stops you from adding a price to a quantity or a 10^4-scaled value to a 10^8-scaled one, and Ch. 27 defers that mechanism to Ch. 19, which never delivers it. A correctly built strong type costs zero instructions and zero bytes, and proving that (register passing, identical asm) is exactly the verify-the-codegen discipline the book teaches.
  SCOPE: Why the type system is the cheapest bug filter on the hot path — the Ch. 27 failure modes (price+quantity, scaled+unscaled, ticks vs scaled units, OrderId vs ClOrdID vs SymbolId) caught at compile time for zero runtime instructions. // Building a strong type correctly — `explicit` constructors, `[[nodiscard]]`, defaulted `operator<=>`, an allowed-operations matrix (Price−Price→PriceDelta, Price×Qty→Notional, Qty±Qty, no Price+Qty, scalar rules), `constexpr` throughout (Ch. 18), trivially copyable so it works in `std::atomic<Price>` (Ch. 30), seqlock snapshots (Ch. 35) and `memcpy`/`bit_cast` wire loads (Ch. 53). // The ABI question — when a one-member wrapper is passed in a register exactly like `int64_t` under the SysV x86-64 ABI, what breaks it (non-trivial special members, two members, tag members without `[[no_unique_address]]`), verified in Godbolt (Ch. 4): call sites and arrays of wrappers produce identical code to raw integers (SoA/SIMD unaffected — Ch. 8/29). // Scale as part of the type — `Fixed<int64_t, Scale>` templates, compile-time scale conversion, `std::chrono::duration`/`std::ratio` as the model and as the unit type for time on the hot path (Ch. 17). // Libraries — mp-units (C++20/23), Boost.Units, `au`, `NamedType`/`strong_type`: compile-time dimensional analysis vs a 40-line hand-rolled wrapper; compile-time and error-message cost (Ch. 18, §19.6). // Strong IDs and handles — OrderId/SymbolId/VenueId/SlotIndex as distinct types, generational indices (Ch. 25.4.3), preventing index-into-wrong-array bugs in SoA layouts (Ch. 8); `std::hash` specializations for use as keys. // Boundaries — convert to raw integers only at the wire (Ch. 53) and the log (Ch. 71); `std::formatter` specializations off-path. // Pitfalls — implicit conversions that reintroduce mixing, `operator*` that silently double-scales, wrappers with destructors/virtuals that change the ABI.
  EXISTING: chapters/27-fixed-point-floating-point-arithmetic.md §27.4.1 L127, §27.6 L195, §27.7 Ex. 2 L204, checklist L211/L218: `struct Price { std::int64_t ticks; }` — 'a zero-cost abstraction, Ch. 19'; all five mentions defer to Ch. 19.; chapters/19-template-metaprogramming-zero-cost.md §19.1 L15, §19.2 L41, §19.4.1-19.4.3: Intro promises 'a `Price` type that's a zero-overhead wrapper around an `int64`'; §19.2 covers tag dispatch (a different technique); §19.4 contains only CRTP, expression templates and policy-based design — the promise is never developed.; chapters/25-hotpath-hostile-stl-cache-friendly-containers.md §25.4.3: Index handles/generational indices named.; chapters/17-timekeeping-tsc-rdtsc-clocks.md throughout: `std::chrono` used; no discussion of duration as a unit type for hot-path time.
  EVIDENCE: grep -n -i `strong(ly)?[- ]typ|strong typedef|named ?type|opaque typedef|phantom|newtype|tagged type` → 27-fixed-point-floating-point-arithmetic.md only (L127, L195, L204, L211, L218), each deferring to Ch. 19.
