--- [algorithms-numerics #10] Strings Without Allocation: Fixed-Width Symbols, std::string_view, Fixed-Capacity Strings and Interning
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 25 — new §25.4.6 (after the small-buffer discussion of §25.4.3 and the search section proposed above), with a §25.3 measurement (symbol compare/lookup via `std::string` vs `string_view` vs `uint64` key) and §25.5 pitfalls; cross-reference from Ch. 53 §53.2.2 (`char sym[8]` fields) and Ch. 71 (log arguments). | gap=absent | priority=high | area=language-features
  HFT: Symbols, order IDs and text fields appear in every message a feed handler or gateway touches, and `std::string` is the most common source of hidden allocations and 32-byte copies on paths that should be a single 64-bit compare. The book never mentions `std::string_view` and treats SSO in one sentence, so readers have no allocation-free string toolkit to pair with the zero-copy decoding of Ch. 53.
  SCOPE: The string types a trading system needs — fixed-width padded symbols (ITCH 8-byte, CME 20-byte, FIX variable), ClOrdID/ExecID text IDs, venue/account strings — and why `std::string` (heap beyond SSO: 15 chars libstdc++ / 22 libc++; allocation on copy/append; 32-byte object) is the wrong default on the hot path (Ch. 23's hidden allocations). // Fixed-width symbols as integers — `memcpy`/`bit_cast` (Ch. 21) a `char[8]` into `uint64_t`, compare with one `cmp`, hash in one multiply (proposed hashing chapter), normalize padding (space vs NUL, trailing vs leading), 12–20-byte symbols as two words or a SIMD compare (Ch. 29); measured vs `memcmp`/`std::string::operator==`. // `std::string_view` over the receive buffer — zero-copy field references (Ch. 53), lifetime rules (dangling once the RX ring slot recycles — Ch. 34/55), `string_view` as a map key with transparent hashing (`is_transparent`) to avoid temporary `std::string` construction on lookup. // Fixed-capacity inline strings — `std::array<char,N>` + length, `inplace_string<N>`/`static_string`/`etl::string`, `boost::static_string`, `std::pmr::string` on a monotonic buffer (Ch. 24), `constexpr` construction (Ch. 18); trivially copyable so they fit seqlock snapshots (Ch. 35) and binary log records (Ch. 71). // Interning — symbol → dense `SymbolId` at session start (reference-data load and hot reload — Ch. 73) so the hot path never touches characters; building the intern table (flat map / perfect hash) and the reverse table for off-path logging and formatting. // String building on the way out — `to_chars` into fixed buffers, `std::format_to_n` with a stack buffer, never `operator+`/`std::ostringstream`/`std::to_string`. // Measure it: ns per symbol compare/lookup/copy for `std::string`, `string_view`, `uint64` key and fixed-capacity string; allocation count via `operator new` override (Ch. 23); verify the `uint64` compare is a single `cmp`. // Pitfalls — `std::string` temporaries from `const std::string&` parameters receiving literals/`string_view`, `string_view` into a reused buffer, `std::unordered_map<std::string,…>::find(const char*)` allocating, locale-dependent comparisons, non-NUL-terminated `string_view` handed to C APIs.
  EXISTING: chapters/23-memory-management-fundamentals.md §23.1 L15, §23.4.1 L121, §23.5 L135: SBO threshold and `std::string` growth named as hidden allocations — a few sentences.; chapters/25-hotpath-hostile-stl-cache-friendly-containers.md §25.4.3: '`std::string`'s SBO' named alongside small-vector types; nothing further.; chapters/53-zero-copy-wire-market-data-decoding.md §53.3 AddOrder struct, §53.4.1: `char sym[8]` in the wire struct, never discussed as a string type; `to_chars` for outbound.; chapters/71-zero-overhead-logging.md §71.4.1-71.4.2: No `std::string`/`std::format` on the hot path; type-safe argument capture.; chapters/72-secure-programming-low-latency.md §72.4.2 L90: Fixed-capacity inline buffers with a capacity check, as a memory-safety measure.; chapters/36-safe-memory-reclamation.md §36.1, §36.3, §36.4.1 (also Ch. 16 L145, 31 L114, 73): 'Symbol tables' as replicated/published reference data — not as string→id structures.

--- [algorithms-numerics #11] Compression for Capture, Replay and Bandwidth-Constrained Links
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Ch. 75 — new §75.4.6 'Compression: codecs, framing and decompression-bound replay' after 'Feeding captures into replay'; plus a cross-reference paragraph in Appendix K.3 (microwave bandwidth) and Ch. 53 §53.2.1 (FAST as transport compression). | gap=partial | priority=medium | area=other
  HFT: Full-depth capture of a few venues is terabytes per day, so retention cost and research replay speed are bounded by compression ratio and decompression throughput, yet the book's only guidance is 'optionally compress off-path'. Choosing LZ4 vs zstd-with-dictionary vs domain delta encoding changes storage by an order of magnitude and decides whether a replay or backtest runs at line rate or ten times slower than the market it replays.
  SCOPE: Codec landscape measured on captured market data — LZ4 (GB/s decode, ~2–3× ratio), zstd levels 1–19 and `--fast`, zstd dictionaries trained on small messages (order-of-magnitude better ratio on short records), Snappy, zlib baseline; compression vs decompression throughput and CPU cost per MB on a housekeeping core (Ch. 42). // Domain encodings before general compression — delta/delta-of-delta timestamps (Gorilla), varint/LEB128 and zigzag deltas, columnar (SoA — Ch. 8) layout of captured fields, bit-packing (Ch. 28); why these multiply a general codec's ratio. // Framing for random access — per-block vs per-record compression, seekable zstd frames, block indexes keyed by sequence/timestamp so replay can start anywhere (ties §75.2 time-indexed storage), per-frame checksums (xxHash64 in zstd frames) for integrity. // Replay is decompression-bound — measured replay throughput (messages/s) against compressed vs raw journals, parallel decompression per segment, balancing `O_DIRECT`/NVMe read bandwidth against decode bandwidth (§75.4.3, Ch. 48). // Where compression must not run — never on the capture hot path (reinforcing §75.4.2); background compaction (§75.4.4), tiered retention, keeping the compressor off hot cores and out of the hot L3 partition (Ch. 42/44). // Transport compression — FAST (Ch. 53) and exchange 'compressed' feeds as decode-CPU-vs-bandwidth trades; compact internal encodings (SBE vs hand-packed structs vs FlatBuffers/Cap'n Proto for IPC — Ch. 50); top-of-book-only summaries for microwave links (Appendix K.3) where every byte is serialization time. // Pitfalls — compressing records individually without a dictionary, losing random access, single-threaded decompression on the replay critical path, format/version drift making archives undecodable (§75.5).
  EXISTING: chapters/75-capture-persistence-replay-storage.md §75.4.2 L81, §75.4.4 L100, §75.2: 'Optionally compress off-path … never on the capture path'; compaction 'merge/compress sealed journal segments'; time-indexed storage. No codec, framing, dictionary or decompression-throughput discussion.; chapters/53-zero-copy-wire-market-data-decoding.md §53.2.1, §53.3: FAST as 'compressed binary' with stop-bit/presence-map decode cost (~100-300 ns/msg).; chapters/70-smartnics-dpus.md §70.2/70.4: Compression listed as a DPU offload category.; chapters/appendix-K-exchange-connectivity.md K.3 L30, K.7 L64: Microwave has 'far lower bandwidth — only the most latency-critical, compact signals go over it'; no technique for compacting them.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il `\blz4\b|zstd|zstandard|snappy|zlib|deflate|brotli|lzo` = 0 files. `compress` → Ch. 75 (L81, L100 — two sentences), Ch. 53 (FAST), Ch. 70 (offload category), Ch. 28 (pext 'compress bits'), Ch. 74 (1), App F/J (FAST). `delta[- ]encod|varint|leb128|zigzag|bit[- ]pack` → Ch. 53 (one FAST sentence) only. grep -n -i `codec|zstd|lz4|dictionary|decompress|checksum|crc|integrity` in Ch. 75 → 0 lines except L115 'verify gap-free by sequence'. `flatbuffers|cap'?n ?proto|protobuf|msgpack` = 0 files (`serializ` hits are CPU 'serializing' and TCP contexts). --- [algorithms-numerics #12] Random Number Generation for Simulation, Benchmarks and Randomized Strategies
  kind=new-section-in-existing-chapter | placement=Appendix L — new §L.5 'Generating inputs: PRNG choice, seeding and keeping the generator out of the timed region'; plus a short 'RNG for Monte-Carlo' subsection in Ch. 67 §67.4.3 (cuRAND/Philox, per-path streams) and a cross-reference from Ch. 74 §74.2.2 (randomness as a replayable input). | gap=absent | priority=low | area=other
  HFT: Monte-Carlo pricing and risk (Ch. 67/68) are bounded by RNG throughput and need reproducible parallel streams, while the engine itself uses randomness for backoff jitter, sampling and order randomization and must still replay deterministically. The book's own benchmarks use `mt19937_64` and `rng() % N` throughout without ever discussing generator cost, bias or the variable latency of the standard distributions.
  SCOPE: Generator cost and quality on x86-64 — `std::mt19937_64` (2.5 KB state, cache footprint), xoshiro256**/xoroshiro128+, PCG64, splitmix64, wyrand, Lehmer/MCG128, `rdrand`/`rdseed` (hundreds of cycles — never on the hot path); measured ns/draw and state size; seeding discipline (`std::random_device` once; never `time(0)`). // `std::uniform_int_distribution` and `std::normal_distribution` are not O(1) — rejection sampling and the Marsaglia-polar two-value cache give variable latency and hidden state; Lemire's nearly-divisionless bounded integers; `% N` bias and the division it costs (Ch. 28); ziggurat/Box–Muller/inverse-CDF normals; vectorized generation (Ch. 29). // Counter-based RNGs (Philox/Threefry/Random123, cuRAND) for parallel Monte-Carlo — reproducible, independent per-thread/per-path streams without shared state (Ch. 31), identical streams on CPU and GPU (Ch. 67/68). // Randomness inside the engine — backoff jitter (Ch. 32.4.2), randomized order timing/sizing, 1-in-N sampling for tracing (Ch. 5/61): generated off the critical path or from a precomputed buffer; randomness as an input to the deterministic core (Ch. 74) with the seed captured for replay (Ch. 75). // Benchmark hygiene (the Appendix L point) — generate inputs outside the timed region, avoid `rng() % N` and the standard distributions inside the loop, keep generator state out of the measured cache footprint, fix seeds so runs are comparable (Ch. 3). // Pitfalls — `rand()`/`random()` (global state, glibc locks), reseeding per call, 32-bit seeding of `mt19937`, correlated streams from sequential seeds, `std::random_device` throughput assumptions.
  EXISTING: chapters/25-hotpath-hostile-stl-cache-friendly-containers.md §25.3 flatmap.cpp (also Ch. 1, 2, 3, 7, 10, 13-16, 28 benchmark code): `std::mt19937_64 rng(1)`, `keys[rng() % N]`, `std::uniform_int_distribution` used in harness code; never discussed.; chapters/32-spinlocks-backoff-contention-control.md §32.4.2 L143, Ex. 4 L176, checklist L185: 'optionally add randomization (jitter)' to backoff — no generator discussed.; chapters/74-process-topology-deterministic-state-machine.md §74.2.2 L56, §74.5 L142: Randomness and seeds as inputs to the deterministic core — requirement only.; chapters/67-gpu-computing-cuda.md §67.4.3 L113 (and Ch. 68): Monte-Carlo as a workload; no RNG, cuRAND or stream design.; chapters/appendix-L-benchmark-harness.md L.1-L.4: No input-generation section.
  EVIDENCE: grep -il `xoshiro|xorshift|\bpcg\b|splitmix|philox|counter-based` by name = 0 files (the 11 file hits for the pattern including `mt19937|mersenne` were all `std::mt19937` in benchmark code). `box[- ]muller|ziggurat|inverse transform` = 0. `random number|\brng\b|prng|rdrand|rdseed|uniform_real|normal_distribution` → benchmark `rng` variables plus Ch. 74 L56/L142 only. grep -n -i `random|rng|mt19937|seed|curand|philox` on appendix-L-benchmark-harness.md = 0 lines; on 67-gpu-computing-cuda.md only L113 ('random paths'); on 68 only 'placed randomly'.
